<meta>This page is the author's preface to his study on Palestinian folklore, originally published in the Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society. It follows the title and reprint information.</meta>

# PREFACE

The ancient characteristics of Palestine are disappearing so quickly that most of them will soon be forgotten. Consequently, it has become the urgent duty of every student of Palestine and the Near East—as well as those studying archaeology and the Bible—to lose no time in collecting as much material as possible regarding the folklore, customs, and superstitions currently found in the Holy Land. As we have begun to realize, such information is of the greatest importance for the study of ancient Eastern civilizations and the history of early religions. As a native of this country, I have felt it my special duty to assist in this scientific effort. However, since I do not claim to be a trained archaeologist, I am not attempting to do more than record the raw material I have collected, leaving the task of comparing it with other data to professional archaeologists and biblical scholars.

This change in local conditions is caused by the significant influence the West is exerting upon the East. This is due to the introduction of European educational methods, the migration of Europeans to Palestine, the movement of Palestinians to Europe and especially to America, and, most significantly, the influence of the Mandatory Power <note>The British Mandate for Palestine (1920–1948), which governed the region after World War I.</note>. The simple and raw, yet pure, traditional Palestinian atmosphere is fading away, and a European civilization—more sophisticated but also more artificial—is taking its place.

Our subject of study leads us into the most sacred and private aspects of the inhabitants' lives. It is not always easy to examine the structure of a sanctuary or the objects found within it; however, it is even more difficult to gain the confidence of the <term>fellâh</term> <note>original: "fellâh"; an Arabic word for a peasant, farmer, or villager.</note> to such a degree that he will speak freely and in detail about his religious practices and rites. Understanding these, as well as the nature and character of the local saints, is of the highest scientific interest. Thus, even for me, it was not always possible to uncover the origins of many beliefs.

<summary>In this preface, the author explains the urgent need to document Palestinian folklore and religious traditions before they are erased by Western influence and modernization under the British Mandate. He emphasizes that while he is providing the raw data as a local observer, the material is essential for the scientific study of ancient civilizations and religion.</summary>

<keywords>Taufik Canaan, Palestine, folklore, British Mandate, fellâh, cultural preservation, archaeology, religious studies, Westernization</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface to his study on Palestinian folklore, which began with a discussion on the rapid modernization of the Holy Land and the need to preserve local traditions before they disappear.</meta>

# PREFACE

The present work is based on a study of 235 shrines which I have examined personally, as well as 348 shrines for which exact information was available. I have also attended Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> festivals, devotional ceremonies <note>original: *dikers*, from the Arabic *dhikr*, meaning "remembrance" or "remembrance of God"</note>, celebrations of saints' birthdays <note>original: *môlads*, from the Arabic *mawlid*</note>, and other ceremonies. My research includes a large assortment of stories told about the saints, a large collection of verses sung by the people in honor of the saints <note>original: *awliâ*, the plural of the Arabic *wali*, meaning "saint" or "friend of God"</note>, and a very extensive collection of amulets. My voluminous collection of Palestinian proverbs and idioms has also helped me explain certain customs and practices connected with saints and shrines. Every superstition that might help explain or compare the text has been cited and analyzed. Yet, the subject is so vast and complicated that I cannot claim to have gathered more than a handful of grain from a large heap of corn. I hope that more of this unexplored material will soon receive the attention it deserves.

I have made as much use as possible of Arabic literature regarding this subject, since only a small part of it has previously been used systematically for comparison. The similarities found in these Arabic literary sources show that much of the existing folklore of Palestine is not unique to this country, but is common throughout the Arab world. Regarding European literature, I have used only those works that deal directly with Palestine.

A study of "Saints and Sanctuaries" brings the reader into direct contact with the daily life and customs of the inhabitants of Palestine. It explains much that would otherwise be obscure in popular belief and superstition. It affords a glimpse into the mysterious regions of local ceremonies and throws light on many dark corners in the workings of the popular mind. What is even more interesting is that it allows for a comparison with the customs, practices, and rites of ancient <note>original: "primitive"</note> times. It is remarkable how many ideas have remained virtually unchanged for thousands of years. The study of many current beliefs may disclose the clue to much that has remained unexplained in the religious practices of the ancient Near East.

The various ideas described in the following pages are common to both Muslims and Christians among the Palestinian peasantry. Where the two groups differ, the differences are only superficial.

I take this opportunity to thank Dr. W. F. Albright, the Director of the American School of Oriental Research, for his valuable advice and continued encouragement, and Dr. H. Danby for his help in completing the book.

JERUSALEM, May 15^th^, 1927 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; T. CANAAN

<summary>In this preface, T. Canaan outlines his extensive research methods, including the study of hundreds of shrines and local traditions, to document Palestinian folklore. He emphasizes that these local beliefs offer vital insights into the ancient history of the Near East and are shared by both Muslim and Christian peasants.</summary>
<keywords>shrines, folklore, Palestine, Muslim festivals, saints, dhikr, mawlid, awliya, proverbs, superstitions, W. F. Albright, H. Danby, T. Canaan</keywords>

# MUSLIM SAINTS AND SANCTUARIES IN PALESTINE

->T. CANAAN<-
->(JERUSALEM)<-

## A. SITES AND TYPES OF SANCTUARIES

### 1. SITES

A traveler in Palestine is struck by the barrenness of the hill country. Here and there, one encounters gardens, orchards, or vineyards, generally grouped near a village. During the spring and the first part of the summer, some patches of land are sown with various grains. Scattered here and there on the barren mountains or in the plains, a solitary large green tree or a small group of trees beautifies the surrounding region, giving it a fresh and lively appearance. They provide welcome shelter for the traveler, protecting him from the burning rays of the summer sun. These trees are sacred to Muslims <note>original: Mohammedans</note> since they indicate the presence of some <term>nabi</term> <note>original: nabî; a prophet</note>, <term>wali</term> <note>original: welî; a saint or "friend of God"</note>, or <term>sheikh</term> <note>original: šêh; a leader or holy man</note>. This sanctity was and remains the only reason why they escape the destruction that has been the fate of the forests of Palestine. It is a pity that we do not have countless sacred trees commemorating holy persons, for Palestine would then be more wooded and, consequently, healthier, more fertile, and more beautiful. If such a tree—and most of them can look back on centuries of life—could tell us all its experiences, we would know much more about the history and folklore of this country. I shall try to analyze the nature of Muslim sanctuaries in Palestine, of which trees are only one feature, and I hope to explain some religious problems through this study.

By sanctuaries, I do not mean only those places where a well-known Prophet or <term>wali</term> <note>original: welî</note> was buried, but every place—shrine, tomb, tree, shrub, cave, spring, well, rock, or stone—which is invested with some...

<summary>Dr. T. Canaan introduces his study of Palestinian Muslim sanctuaries, noting that sacred trees dedicated to saints are often the only greenery preserved in the barren landscape. He defines "sanctuaries" broadly to include both natural features and man-made structures associated with holy figures.</summary>
<keywords>Palestine, Muslim sanctuaries, sacred trees, saints, folklore, nabi, wali, sheikh, landscape conservation</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Muslim shrines in Palestine, specifically discussing the necessity of studying folk beliefs and the physical distribution of these sanctuaries across the landscape.</meta>

religious reverence, even if such reverence is based on superstition and is thus considered non-religious according to Quranic <note>original: Qoranic</note> teaching and creed. Only such a broadening of the field of research will enable us to approach many very important questions regarding comparative religion and primitive belief.

### a) *Their Relationship to Human Settlements*

Sacred shrines are countless in Palestine. We encounter them nearly everywhere—in the villages, on the mountains, in the valleys, and in the fields. There is hardly a village, no matter how small, that does not honor at least one local saint. Usually, however, every settlement boasts several. For example, Awarta <note>original: ‘Awartah</note> possesses fourteen, with eleven located in the village itself and three outside at some distance; Anata <note>original: ‘Anâtâ</note> has seven¹ (though one is not accepted by all inhabitants²); Jericho has six; the Mount of Olives has six;³ and Kolonia <note>original: Koloniâ</note> has five. 

These local saints are honored not only by the villagers themselves, but in many cases, their fame is widespread, and individuals or groups make pilgrimages to honor them. Some of these shrines are situated within or near the village. In such cases, one of them serves as a mosque where the residents perform their prayers.⁴ However, the majority of them lie outside, some even at a significant distance from the area occupied by the town or village. Thus, we encounter many holy places in the fields, far from any habitation. Since every village possesses lands that stretch for miles beyond the settlement itself, every shrine—

---

¹ The names of the various saints will be provided at the end of this study.

² The *sullah* <note>original: ṣullâḥ; plural of ṣâleḥ, meaning "pious man"</note> inhabiting the ruins at the western entrance of the village are not accepted by all as authentic. My guide, Mohammed of this village, recounted that some people had heard *iddeh* <note>original: ‘iddeh; religious music used in Sufi rituals</note> at this place on different occasions. A *fellah* <note>original: fellâḥ; a peasant or farmer</note> who urinated <note>original: passed water</note> at this spot was immediately afflicted with eye trouble. These pious men inhabit the ruins of a church. The son of <term>Sheikh</term> <note>original: eš-šêḫ</note> Abd es-Salam, <term>Sheikh</term> Sliman <note>original: Slîmân</note>, is also a less important <term>wali</term> <note>original: welî; a saint</note>.

³ A seventh holy place on the Mount of Olives was Harrubet el-Ashara <note>original: Ḥarrûbet el-‘Ašarâ</note>, a tree that grew on the western slope in a plot of ground currently belonging to a Latin Mission. The tree has since been cut down.

⁴ Such <term>mosques</term> <note>original: djawâmi‘; plural of jami</note>—especially those in villages located in the immediate neighborhood of large cities—are not used much. Many of the peasants come to the city on Friday to perform their midday prayer <note>original: ṣalât ed-djum‘ah</note> and to conduct their business.

<summary>This page discusses the density of Muslim shrines in the Palestinian landscape, noting that even small villages often host multiple sacred sites, some of which are located in remote fields.</summary>
<keywords>Palestine, Muslim shrines, local saints, Awarta, Anata, folk religion, sacred geography, walis, mosques</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the geographic distribution of Muslim shrines in Palestine, focusing on their relationship to villages and their common placement on high ground.</meta>

A shrine situated in such land belongs to that village and is also primarily honored by its inhabitants. There are exceptions to this rule. Sheikh es-Sidri <term>Eš-šêḫ es-Sidrî</term> in the lands of Anata <term>‘Anâtâ</term> is honored mostly by the semi-Bedouin living to the east of the village.

The following is an analysis of the locations of shrines taken from a few villages around Jerusalem:

| Name of the village | Number of sanctuaries | Those inside the village | Those outside the residential area |
|---------------------|:---------------------:|:-----------------------:|:---------------------------:|
| Anata <term>‘Anâtâ</term> | 7 | 3 | 4 |
| Isawiya <term>‘Êsâwîyeh</term> | 3 | 1 | 2 |
| Kolonia <term>Kolôniâ</term> | 5 | 1 | 4 |
| Awarta <term>‘Awartah</term> | 14 | 11 | 3 |

Some villages have their saints <term>awliâ</term> <note>plural of welî</note> only in the residential area itself or in its immediate vicinity. This is the case in Beit Hanina <term>Bêt Ḥanînâ</term>, Sur Bahir <term>Sûrbâhir</term>, and Shuafat <term>Ša‘fât</term>, each of which has four such saints.

### *b) High Places*

The shrines are mostly situated on an elevated place—the top of a mountain, a hill, or a small rise in the plain—thereby overlooking the entire surrounding landscape. Even shrines built on the sloping side of a mountain or just above a valley floor are placed so that they more or less dominate the surrounding area and are visible from afar. Relatively few shrines <term>welîs</term> are situated in valleys; but if one is, it is generally found near the junction of two valleys <term>wâdis</term> or in a place where the valley <term>wâdi</term> has widened, making them visible from a distance from various directions. Many a sacred place, although situated on high ground, is not easily seen because of the nature of the shrine <term>welî</term>, specifically when it lacks a building or a large tree. This is true of all sanctuaries found near caves, enclosures, springs, cisterns, rocks, or heaps of stones. Some shrines on the tops of mountains include:

| Name of Saint | Location |
|---|---|
| The Prophet Samuel <term>en-nabî Ṣamwîl</term> | Mizpah of Samuel |
| Sheikh el-Katrawani <term>eš-šêḫ el-Qaṭrawânî</term> | between Bir Zeit <term>Bîr Zêt</term> and Atara <term>‘Aṭârah</term> |
| Sheikh Ahmad el-Karaki et-Tayyar <term>eš-šêḫ Aḥmad el-Karakî eṭ-Ṭaiyâr</term> | Qastal <term>Qaṣṭal</term> |
| Abu Hurayrah <term>Abû Hurêrah</term> | Wadi esh-Sharia <term>Wâdi eš-Šarî‘ah</term> |
| el-Uzeir <term>el-‘Uzêr</term> | near Awarta <term>‘Awartah</term> |
| Sheikh el-Umari ed-Djbe'eh <term>eš-šêḫ el-‘Umarî ed-Djbê‘eh</term> | near Beit Anan <term>Bêt ‘Anân</term> |
| el-Mas'ad <term>el-Maṣ‘ad</term> | Mount of Olives |

The shrine of Sheikh el-Umari ed-Djbe'eh <term>eš-šêḫ el-‘Umarī ed-Djbê‘eh</term> is built on the summit of a high mountain. The view from this spot to the west is magnificent. The plain, Ramla <term>Ramleh</term>, Lod <term>Lydda</term>, Jaffa, and the sea are easily seen, especially when the weather is clear. Around the shrine <term>maqâm</term> <note>a "standing place" or sanctuary dedicated to a Muslim saint</note> is a ruin <note>original: Ḥirbet ed-Djbê‘eh</note> and many natural caves. No tomb is visible, and the room contains nothing but a prayer niche <term>miḥrâb</term> <note>a niche in the wall of a mosque or shrine indicating the direction of Mecca</note>. Two large carob trees withered away due to the severe winter of 1921–1922. Anyone who takes refuge in this saint's sanctuary <term>welî</term> is granted absolute protection.

By "elevated places," I do not mean only the very summit of a mountain, but any spot which is high and, to some extent, overlooks the surrounding area, such as:

This characteristic is very common, not only of Palestinian Muslim <note>original: Mohammedan</note> shrines, but also of sanctuaries elsewhere in the Islamic world. Lewis Bayles Paton's statement on this point—although not absolutely correct—is more exact than that of Chester C. McCown. The first writer says: <note>Paton, *Annual of the American School of Oriental Research in Jerusalem* I, p. 62.</note> “The majority of the alleged tombs of saints in modern Palestine are situated on the summits of high mountains.” McCown's <note>McCown, *Annual of the American School of Oriental Research in Jerusalem* II and III, p. 63.</note> statements in this respect are hard to understand. I shall, later on, discuss his first idea, namely: “A very considerable number of shrines are on hilltops because the cities or villages to which they belong sought such sites, not because the hill is sacred.” 

In systematically reviewing the villages of the Jerusalem district which I have visited for the purpose of this study, and noting exactly the position of the shrines, I found that in 26 villages, 70% of the shrines occupied the top of a hill or mountain, and 24% were on the sloping side of the...

mountain below its summit, and only 5% were in a valley or a plain. Of these shrines, 45% belong to the built sanctuaries <term>maqâm</term> <note>original: maqâm; literally a "standing place" or station of a saint</note>, 18% are tombs, and 37% are sacred springs, trees, enclosures, and caves. In other words, only 52% (45% built shrines and 7% holy trees) would be more or less easily seen, while the character of the other 48% lessens the possibility of them being seen from a distance.

I also do not agree with McCown's statement about Jerusalem. He writes: "There are vast numbers of shrines—several to every good-sized town—which are not easily seen because they are not on hilltops. Jerusalem is such a place." He does not appear to have considered the following very important facts, which make most of the shrines of Jerusalem difficult to see:

1. The built shrines of most of the saints <term>welîs</term> <note>original: welîs; plural of "wali," a Muslim holy person or saint</note> inside the city are low structures, and houses have been built on top of their roofs. Examples include Bairam Shawish <term>Bairam Šawîš</term>, Sheikh Rihan <term>eš-šêḫ Rîḥân</term>, and Sheikh Hasan el-Qerami <term>eš-šêḫ Ḥasan el-Qêramî</term>.

2. The crowded houses in the city proper hide shrines that have no buildings above them from view.

3. Most of the important sacred places in the sacred enclosure <term>ḥaram</term> <note>original: ḥaram; referring to the Haram al-Sharif or Noble Sanctuary, which contains the Dome of the Rock and Al-Aqsa Mosque</note> area are contained within the Mosque of Omar and the Al-Aqsa Mosque, and naturally cannot be easily seen from the outside.

On the other hand, the majority of the shrines situated outside the city walls are easily seen; for example, Sheikh Ahmad et-Tori <term>eš-šêḫ Aḥmad eṭ-Ṭôrî</term>, Sa'd and Sa'id <term>Saʿd u Sʿîd</term>, Sheikh Jarrah <term>eš-šêḫ Djarrâḥ</term>, Sheikh Ukasha <term>eš-šêḫ ʿOkâšeh</term>, and others.

This choice of location is not a new custom, for we read that the people of the ancient Orient used to choose such places for the building of their temples and the worship of their gods. In Ezekiel 6:2, we read: "And say, you mountains of Israel, hear the word of the Lord God; Thus says the Lord God to the mountains and to the hills, to the watercourses and to the valleys: Behold I, even I, will bring a sword upon you, and I will destroy your high places." It is interesting to see how these two verses¹ refer to mountains, watercourses, valleys, and green trees—in other words, "high places" combined with water and trees, a feature that still characterizes present-day shrines.

Mountains and hills seem to have always played a great role in human religion.² It is interesting to note that all the great divine...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the location of Palestinian shrines, moving from the specific layout of Jerusalem to the broader historical and religious significance of mountains as sacred sites.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

Significant works have, traditionally, been performed on mountains: Ararat and the ark of Noah, Moriah and Abraham's sacrifice, Sinai and the Law, and Ebal and Gerizim with the blessing and the cursing. It is the same also with Jesus: on a mountain he was tempted, was transfigured, preached, prayed, and was crucified; and from a mountain he ascended to heaven. The prophets and kings also preferred these lofty places for many of their important actions. Elijah received the word of the Lord on Mount Horeb;¹ the "schools of the prophets" were on hills and mountains;² on Mount Carmel, Elijah won the victory over the priests of Baal who worshiped their idols on this mountain;³ Moses died on Mount Nebo, from which he saw the Promised Land; Aaron died on the top of Mount Hor; and on this mountain, Eliezer was ordained as his father's successor. I do not need to provide more examples to illustrate the fact that mountains were, in ancient times, regarded as sacred to some degree.⁴ This idea was adopted by the Israelites from those who lived there before them and was transmitted by them to following generations.

A traveler through Palestine is struck by the many mountain tops which are covered with a prominent <term>weli</term> <note>original: weli; a tomb or shrine of a Muslim saint, or the saint himself</note>; still greater is the number of summits which bear shrines that cannot be distinguished from a distance. Does this not indicate that the present inhabitants still believe in the peculiar sanctity of mountains? McCown minimizes the importance of this idea; Curtiss⁵ and Paton stress it. Which view is correct?

Many primitive ideas have unquestionably persisted through thousands of years and can still be traced to the present day in one form or another among the inhabitants of the "immovable East." <note>This phrase reflects a common 19th and early 20th-century view that Middle Eastern cultures remained unchanged since biblical times.</note> The sacred character of mountains seems to have been a widespread concept in the ancient Orient. The modern Palestinian places most of his shrines on mountains and hills, regardless of whether or not these places are used for human habitation. Although most <term>awliâ</term> <note>original: awliâ; the plural of "weli," referring to saints or holy people</note>—

³ H. Zeller, *Biblical Dictionary* <note>original: Biblisches Wörterbuch</note>, pp. 146, 147.
⁴ Yahweh <note>The Hebrew name for God</note> appeared on high places, 1 Kings 3:4-5. It was forbidden for the Israelites to take part in the worship on high places like the heathens, Deuteronomy 12:2; Jeremiah 2:20; Ezekiel 20:28-29; etc.

Since these shrines are situated in the immediate vicinity of villages, it is striking that so many uninhabited mountains also have sanctuaries. This fact proves that the ancient idea regarding the sacredness of mountains has likely been passed down to the modern inhabitants of Palestine. Although they do not explicitly acknowledge it as such, the ancient practice continues nevertheless.

### c) *Relation of Shrines to Cemeteries*

It is very significant that the shrines or graves of many "holy men" are situated in the middle of cemeteries or directly adjacent to them. The following list provides a rough comparison between holy places (shrines, graves, etc.) found in connection with cemeteries and those that have no relation to cemeteries:

| Name of the village | Number of sanctuaries | In cemetery | Not in cemetery |
|---------------------|-----------------------|-------------|-----------------|
| et-Tûr | 6 | 3 | 3 |
| Jericho | 6 | 2 | 4 |
| Ša'fâṭ | 4 | 3 | 1 |
| Surbâhir | 4 | 1 | 3 |
| 'Anâtâ | 7 | 1 | 6 |

I should add the following details. In Surbâhir, the five tombs of ed-Dawâ'rî are counted as a single shrine. The three tombs of ed-Djarâhîd, which actually represent three distinct holy places, I have also considered as one. The same applies to the two graves of ed-Dawâ'ri in Ša'fâṭ, which can be seen in the cemetery.

The list above shows that 63% of the shrines are situated in a cemetery. However, the sanctuaries of some villages are not connected with burial places at all, so the general percentage of such a combination across the region amounts to only 30%. In some cases, a cemetery surrounds the shrine, while in other cases, only a few tombs are found nearby. This raises the question of whether the burial place was established around the shrine, or whether the tomb of a distinguished man was built in a cemetery that already existed. In most cases, the cemetery is more recent, as the presence of the holy place led people to choose that location for public burials. This is always the case when the shrine is an old one. But regarding <term>awliâ</term> <note>original: awliâ; Arabic plural for 'saints' or 'protectors'</note> of more recent origin, we almost always find that these men—who were viewed during their lifetimes as "blessed men of God"—were buried in the common cemetery and became <term>welîs</term> <note>original: welîs; plural for 'saints' or the shrines dedicated to them</note> after their death. Their tombs then began to enjoy private and...

...is much less used. The dead are ritually washed here before they are buried.

Shrines in whose neighborhood only one or a few tombs are found include:

| Name of Shrine | Location |
|---|---|
| <term>Sheikh Ahmad el-Tori</term> <note>original: eš-šêḥ Aḥmad eṭ-Ṭôrî</note> | Jerusalem |
| <term>Sheikh Badr</term> <note>original: eš-šêḥ Badr</note> | Jerusalem |

There are two reasons why some people prefer to bury their dead in the vicinity of the grave of a <term>saint</term> <note>original: welî</note>.<note>1 This custom is also common among some Bedouin tribes, according to Jaussen, *Coutumes des Arabes*, p. 99.</note>

1. The closer a person is buried to a <term>saint’s</term> <note>original: welî</note> or <term>sheikh’s</term> <note>original: šêḥ</note> tomb or <term>shrine</term> <note>original: maqâm</note>, the greater the blessing they may receive in the world to come. This is why so many Bedouin carry their important dead from great distances to be buried near a saint’s tomb. For example, the Bedouin of the <term>er-Rashaydiyeh</term> <note>original: er-Rašâydiyeh</note> tribe inter some of their dead around the <term>Sheikhs of the Jarahid</term> <note>original: šiûḥ ed-Djarâhîd</note> on the Mount of Olives, and the <term>Adwan</term> <note>original: ‘Idwân</note> tribe carry their dead to the shrine of the <term>Prophet Moses</term> <note>original: Nebî Mûsâ</note>.

2. The protection provided by the saint, due to the general respect he commands, is another reason for burying the dead close to the <term>saint's</term> <note>original: welî</note> tomb. This was practiced especially by prominent political families who were frequently in conflict with other families. When a leader died, they buried him near a sacred spot to protect his body from being dug up and dishonored by his enemies. The "man of God" is certain to protect everything placed under his care; no one dares to violate the sanctity of a person buried there. Such reasons led the <term>Abd el-Hadi</term> <note>original: ‘Abd el-Hâdî</note> family of Nablus to bury three of their members—<term>Mohammad el-Husein</term>, <term>Yusif Sliman</term>, and <term>Abd el-Karim</term>—near the <term>shrine</term> <note>original: maqâm</note> of <term>Our Lady of the Presence</term> <note>original: el-Ḥaḍrah</note>.

### d) Relation of Shrines to Ruins

Another interesting fact is that a large number of sacred sites are located within or near ruins. One should not always expect to find the remains of a large city; there may only be a few old rock-cut tombs, the remains of a few houses, several old cisterns, or some ancient pillars. Such a ruin in itself must have been a striking...

| <term>el-Mufaddel</term> <note>original: el-Mufaḍḍel</note> | <term>Awarta</term> <note>original: ʿAwartah</note> | a rock-cut tomb. |

## 2. STRUCTURE OF THE SANCTUARIES

Let us approach a common type of <term>shrine</term> <note>original: welî</note> and examine it more thoroughly. What do we find here? Of course, the same objects are not found in every case. We shall try to investigate every component separately, leaving the classification for later. For our purpose, we will take note of the following features: a building, a tomb, a tree (or a group of trees), a water reservoir (such as a cistern, well, spring, or basin), and a cave. It is quite difficult to give an absolute description of each one, since they vary so much across the different parts of Palestine that we rarely find two that are completely alike.

<summary>This page explores how ancient ruins, caves, and trees often inspire the creation of Palestinian shrines, providing a table of specific examples where saints are honored near historical sites. It also introduces the primary physical components—buildings, tombs, trees, and water sources—that define these sacred spaces.</summary>
<keywords>sanctuaries, Palestinian folklore, ruins, archaeological sites, weli, peasant beliefs, sacred landscape, cisterns, rock-hewn tombs</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed survey of Palestinian sanctuaries, moving from their general location near ruins to a specific description of their architectural features and the damage they sustained during World War I.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

### a) The Building

The building itself—the shrine (<term>maqâm</term> <note>Arabic: "place" or "station"</note>), the domed structure (<term>qubbeh</term>), or, as it was called in Biblical times, a "house of high places"1—is in most cases, and in all the simpler examples, a four-sided (quadrangular) building. We will consider only this form for now. The door—and there is only one—is low. There is generally one small window, but sometimes there may be more (<term>ṭâqah</term> or <term>šarrâqah</term> <note>Arabic: window or aperture/skylight</note>), though occasionally there is none at all. The roof is a simple vaulted dome2 with a long upright stone in the center, which is raised above the vault itself. This stone is sometimes cut in the shape of a crescent moon. Instead of such a stone, an iron bar with three balls—the lowest being the largest—and a crescent moon at the top may sometimes be found.3 This dome-form (<term>qubbeh</term>) is a very characteristic feature of Muslim shrines. It is found not only in the simple shrine of a <term>saint</term> <note>original: welî</note>, but also in the large and important shrines of the prophets as well as in common mosques. As McCown says,4 "The *qubbeh* is a characteristic feature of the Palestinian landscape." Very often the word *qubbeh* is used as a synonym for "shrine," although originally it referred to any vaulted building.5

The inside is always plastered and whitewashed, but since these buildings are often very old, everything is frequently in poor condition. A great many of the shrines are in a pitiful state of disrepair, mainly due to neglect, winter storms, and old age. The <note>First World</note> War was another cause of their ruin; examples include the shrines of **Sheikh Ahmad el-Karaki the Flyer** (*Qastal*), the **Prophet Samuel** (*Nebi Samwil*, also known as *Mizpah*), **Sheikh Hasan** (*Beit Iksa*), **el-Qatrawani** (North of *Bir Zeit*), **Abu el-Oun** (*Biddu*), and **Sheikh Abd el-Aziz** (near *Beit Surik*). During the war, some had to be leveled to the ground to prevent the enemy from using them as a target for their artillery (such as **Sheikh Nuran**, between *Shallaleh* and *Khan Yunis*). Doors and other wooden parts were nearly always taken away by soldiers and used as firewood (such as **Sheikh Anbar**, **Abd es-Salam**, and **el-Umari of ed-Djbe'eh**). In some cases, the villagers have replaced the lost doors with others and repaired the shrines in...

2 The *qubbeh* of the Bedouin is an imitation of the tent; see Jaussen, 102.
3 Such a decoration is a sign that the building was constructed recently.
4 *Annual II and III*, 50.
5 See the Arabic dictionaries *Muhit el-Muhit* and Hava, <note>original: s. v. (sub voce)</note> under the entry for that word.

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

...in a relatively simple manner, as seen in the cases of el-Qaṭrâwânî, Sheikh Hasan <note>original: eš-šêḫ Ḥasan</note>, and the Prophet Samuel <note>original: en-nabî Ṣâmwîl</note>.

<margin>Sheikh Badr <note>original: الشيخ بدر</note></margin>
Looking inside a proper shrine shows that one or more niches (also called a <term>ṭâqah</term>) are located in the wall—a feature common to all of them. These actually look like elongated cupboards. Occasionally there may be only a single niche, though generally more are found. In the small, simple, one-room shrine of Sheikh Badr <note>original: eš-šêḫ Badr</note>, which sits on a hilltop in the northwest part of Jerusalem, I counted eight. In the shrine of el-'Uzêr (at Awartah), there are dozens of them. These are built at different heights and are scattered irregularly across the four walls without any concern for symmetry. With few exceptions, they are dirty; the surrounding wall, and especially the area below, is often heavily smeared with oil. This unsightly effect is because these niches are where oil lamps, oil bottles, matches, and other small objects are kept.

The interior generally shows signs of having once been decorated with henna (<term>ḥinnâ</term>), indigo dye (<term>nîleh</term>), or both. The decoration consists of simple lines running roughly parallel to each other around the inside, forming a type of decorative border or frieze. Often this border is more complex. Some typical designs are shown in Plate I.

In addition to these borders, we find two other very important decorations: representations of the hand and imitations of palm branches (sometimes twigs or trees). Both of these are explained by folk beliefs. In Muslim tradition, the hands represent the "Hand of Fatima" (the daughter of the Prophet); in Christian tradition, the "Hand of the Holy Virgin"; and in Jewish tradition, the "Hand of God."¹ These symbolic decorations are believed to bring blessings. We find them very clearly and frequently on the two outer sides of the door (<term>ṣdâghât</term> <note>door jambs</note>), on the lintel (<term>šâšiyeh</term> <note>the top stone of the doorway</note>), and on the inner walls of the shrine, especially around the prayer niche (<term>miḥrâb</term> <note>the niche indicating the direction of Mecca</note>). These are generally imprints of a human hand dipped in blood, henna, or indigo. A dozen such imprints may be seen in some shrines.

These handprints are seen not only in shrines but also on the doors of private houses. They are intended to protect the inhabitants against the harmful effects of the "evil eye." Small charms shaped like hands—made of glass, mother-of-pearl, silver, gold, or other metals—are also used.

---

1 Canaan, *Superstition* <note>original German: Aberglaube</note>, pp. 64 ff.; Doutté, *Magic and Religion* <note>original French: Magie et Religion</note>, pp. 325 ff.

<summary>This page describes the interior architectural details of Palestinian shrines, focusing on the functional wall niches and the protective symbolic decorations, such as handprints (the Hand of Fatima) and palm branches, used to ward off the evil eye.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of decorative motifs found inside Palestinian shrines, specifically examining the use of handprints and palm tree symbols and their connection to local folk beliefs.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

Handprints made of blood are rarely found. I have seen them on newly built houses where a sheep was sacrificed before the house was occupied, as well as during the festival of Bairam <note>original: ʾîd ed-Ḍḥiyeh; the Feast of Sacrifice</note>. Only once have I observed blood handprints on the door of a shrine. This mark was made by a man who sacrificed a sheep he had promised to the saint <term>welî</term>.

The imitation of the palm tree (Plate I, Figure 8) is mostly used as an interior decoration. It consists of a vertical line with shorter side lines that meet at sharp angles pointing upward. The total number of side branches varies; however, in most cases, there is an equal number on either side. I examined these carefully to see if the number of branches on one side matched the sacred numbers 3, 5, 7, or a multiple of them, but in most cases, they did not.² In some instances—specifically at the shrine of al-Badriyah <note>original: el-Badrîyeh</note> in Sharafat <note>original: Šarâfât</note> and <margin>al-Badriyah <br/> Sharafat</margin> al-Qatrawani <note>original: el-Qaṭrâwânî</note> (north of Bir Zeit)—the numbers all coincided with the numbers three and five in the first case, and five and seven in the second. This feature is always explained as representing palm branches or palm trees (<term>naḥl</term>). We know that palm branches are carried in most funeral processions for wealthy or important men as a symbol of life. Muslim folk belief holds that palm trees were created from the same clay from which God made Adam.³ This is why this tree is said to have many similarities to humans.⁴ The Quran mentions it very often, as it is one of the chosen trees.⁵

Nonetheless, I would ask: is it not possible that these figures were formerly crude imitations of the hand, and that over time the distinct number *five* was lost, along with the original meaning?

Other decorations that may be found are shown in Plate I and Figure 5 of Plate II. The depiction of the serpent indicates long life.⁶ The word *hayyeh* <note>original: Haiyeh</note> (serpent) and *haya* <note>original: ḥayâ</note> (life) have

² Very few examples featured the number four.
³ *The Pearl of Wonders* <note>original: Ḥarîdatu-l-ʿAdjâyb</note>, page 102.

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

...from a similar-sounding root.<note>original: Canaan, *Aberglaube*, p. 85; refering to Canaan's work on Palestinian "superstitions."</note> I could not explain the meaning of the squares containing dots (Plate I, Figure 6). It is unlikely that these dots (Plate I, Figures 4 and 5) represent the "visiting cards" of pilgrims, as the scholar Paul Kahle suggests. In some shrines, I have seen simple representations of a mosque, a minaret, a ship, flowers, and other objects. The sole purpose of these figures is to beautify the <term>maqâm</term> <note>shrine or holy site</note>. Sometimes verses from the Quran or the names of God, the Prophet Muhammad, and some of the <term>ṣaḥâbeh</term> <note>the Companions of the Prophet</note> are written on the walls. The shrine of Sheikh Yasin <note>original: eš-šêḫ Yâsîn</note> is the best example; there, alongside the names "Allah" and "Muhammad"—which are surrounded by wreaths of leaves—we find the Muslim profession of faith <note>original: "There is no god but God, and Mohammed is the apostle of God"; the Shahada</note>, two flags (the Turkish flag and the green flag of the Prophet), a crescent moon, and many five-pointed stars. In the <term>miḥrâb</term> <note>prayer niche</note>, a censer and its chain are painted.

These decorations are made with henna (<term>ḥinnâ</term>), indigo (<term>nîleh</term>), or red lead (<term>sîraqûn</term>). Some peasants believe that indigo should never be used in holy places, considering henna the only appropriate material. When henna (<note>original: *Lawsonia inermis*</note>—the henna plant) is used as a red dye, it is kneaded into a paste and then daubed on the wall. Very often, <term>samneh</term> (clarified butter) is mixed with it, though not always, as the researcher Curtiss suggests. It is with this paste that the impression of the hand is so frequently made. While it is still on the wall, the paste has a dirty greenish-brown appearance, but when it falls off, it leaves a brownish-red stain. The <term>miḥrâb</term> and its immediate surroundings are decorated first. Most other decorations are made with indigo <note>original: nîleh (methylene blue)</note> and red lead <note>original: sîraqûn (minium)</note>.

In many of these simple shrines, though not all, there is a <term>miḥrâb</term>, which takes the usual form and points southwards <note>toward Mecca</note>. There is at least one in each of the larger sanctuaries, and some have several. For example, the Tomb of the Shepherd <note>original: qabr er-Râ‘î</note> near Nebi Musa has three. In some shrines dedicated to saints <note>original: awliâ</note>, the <term>miḥrâb</term> is only indicated on the southern wall with color or with a ridge-like frame made of projecting plaster. In the Christian church of Saint George <note>original: el-Ḥaḍr</note> (located between Beit Jala and the Pools of Solomon), which is—

<margin>The Prophet David <note>original: النبي داود — An-Nabî Dâhûd</note></margin>
Also honored and visited by Muslims, the prayer direction is marked by a large picture of Saint George. I have seen Muslims go in and perform their prayers by turning their faces toward the picture and therefore toward the south. All *mihrabs* <term>mihrâb: a niche in the wall of a mosque or shrine that indicates the direction of Mecca</term> are placed in the southern wall of the sanctuaries. The following three are the only exceptions I know of: a *mihrab* in the building below Al-Aqsa <note>original: el-Aqsâ; the mosque in Jerusalem</note>, a *mihrab* in the tomb of the Prophet David <note>original: nabî Dâhûd</note>, and one in the shrine of Al-Mufaddil <note>original: el-Mufaḍḍil</note> in Awarta <note>original: ‘Awartah</note>.¹ In the first case, it is said that the Prophet Muhammad prayed there during his night journey to Jerusalem, and when he had finished, the angel Gabriel ordered him in the future to perform his prayers facing Mecca. Thereupon, the Prophet turned his face in that direction and performed his second prayer.²

The floors of the poorer *maqams* <note>shrines</note> are mostly bare, but sometimes mats are provided. The larger and more important shrines have mats and often costly carpets.

While the previous description holds true for all simple *maqams*, we must still consider those which are larger, more important, and more elaborate. I shall try to describe them according to the various complexities of their structure. But before proceeding to this part of our subject, something should be said about the *qubbeh* or "cupola" <note>a small, dome-like structure on a roof</note>. This is one of the most important features of the *awlia* <term>awliâ: the plural of "wali," referring to Muslim saints or their shrines</term> and belongs to almost every typical shrine. In examining a *qubbeh*,³ we find two different types:

1. The simple type, where the *qubbeh* is built directly over the four walls of the shrine. It looks like a hemisphere placed on top of the walls.

2. The square space formed by the four walls is converted into an octagon <note>an eight-sided shape</note> near the roof by filling in the corners with pendentives <term>pendentive: a curved triangle of vaulting formed by the intersection of a dome with its supporting arches</term>. The octagon is raised slightly, and the hemispherical *qubbeh* rests on it. A vertical cross-section of such a building (cut diagonally) is shown in Figure 4, Plate II.

The *maqams* which possess two vaulted domes instead of one are as simple in character as those just described. In reality, such a building—

² It is curious that Abraham and Lot are thought to have performed their prayers facing Mecca (southward), even though they are figures from before the time of Islam.
³ For a short description of it, see *Palästina-Jahrbuch* VII, 92.

This represents two rooms which, by removing the separating wall, are joined to form one elongated space. A high, strongly built arch, which helps to support the roof, takes the place of the missing wall.

In the next category are sanctuaries which have a *rawaq* <term>rawâq: an open arched hall or portico</term> built in front or at the side. This may be composed of one arch, but more often of two. People gather here before and after their visits to the shrine. Sometimes meals are eaten and festivals are held in this place. In Beit Hanina <note>original: Bêt Ḥanînâ</note>, the inhabitants have recently built a three-vaulted hall to the southeast of the mosque <term>djâmi‘: mosque</term> of Sultan Ibrahim el-Adhami; it opens to the north and contains a *mihrab* <term>miḥrâb: a prayer niche</term>. Sheikh Salman el-Farsi (on the Mount of Olives) has such an open hall in front of the sanctuary itself. In el-Uzeir (el-Qariyeh) and Sheikh Hamad (Kolonia <note>modern Motza</note>), the hall is located at one side of the sanctuary.

<margin>Additional rooms</margin>
Even more complex are those shrines where one or more additional rooms are built beside or around the sanctuaries, opening into the vaulted areas. These serve as kitchens, living quarters for the servant <term>ḫaddâm: servant</term> or caretaker <term>qaiym: custodian</term>, and storage rooms. Sometimes—though only when the sanctuary is situated in or very near a village—one of these rooms may be used as a schoolroom <term>kuttâb or maktab: a traditional elementary school</term>, and occasionally another is occupied by the sheikh <note>original: šêḫ</note> or the preacher <term>ḫaṭîb: an orator or prayer leader</term>, who may act as the teacher. Some examples include:

| | |
|---|---|
| Sheikh Hamad | Kolonia |
| Sheikh I’mar | Beit Duqqu |
| El-Uzeir | Abu Ghosh |
| El-Anbia | Nablus |

In a few cases, one room is used for the ritual washing of the dead before burial, as in el-Azerat (Awarta) and the mosque of el-Uzeir (Abu Ghosh).

At times the guest chamber <term>maḍâfeh: a room for receiving guests</term> is connected with the shrine, as in the Prophet Shu’ah <note>original: en-nabî Šû‘ah</note>¹ (in the village of Shu’ah),² where it is a room built over the shrine. In the shrines of Sheikh Abu Ismail (Beit Liquya) and Sheikh Hsen (Beit Anan), the front room serves as a guest chamber. In both of these cases, we find the hearth <term>el-wdjâq: a fireplace or stove</term> in the center of the room, on which coffee is prepared for those present. The guest house of Sheikh Yasin (Deir Yasin) is situated opposite the shrine <term>maqâm: a sacred tomb or sanctuary</term> and separated from it—

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>
<sig>2</sig>

...next to an open area.<note>The text continues from the previous page describing the placement of guest houses.</note> These guest houses <term>maḍâfeh: a communal guest house for visitors</term> are used only during the cold months of the year; in the summer, people prefer to sit outside under a tree or in an open arched gallery <term>rawâq: an open-fronted porch or portico</term>. A rough sketch of Sheikh <note>original: eš-šêḫ</note> Hamad in Kolonia <note>modern Motza</note> clearly illustrates the type of building described above. See Fig. 1, Plate II.

Another category consists of holy sites where the main sanctuary is surrounded by numerous rooms. These rooms serve pilgrims who visit once a year and generally stay for several days. In these cases, the building usually has two, and sometimes three, stories. The lower floor is used for storage rooms, a kitchen, and stables, while the upper floor is reserved for visitors. A caretaker lives at the sanctuary year-round to guard it. These larger shrines are generally dedicated to prophets <term>anbiâ: prophets (singular: nabî)</term> rather than local saints <term>awliâ: saints or "friends of God"</term>. The shrine of the Prophet Moses <note>original: En-nabî Mûsâ</note> is the most famous example of this type. However, only a few prophets have such large complexes. The shrines of the Prophet Salih (in Ramleh), the Prophets (in Nablus), the Prophet Joseph (in Beit Ijza), and many others consist of fairly simple buildings. Meanwhile, el-Uzeir, el-Mansuri, and el-Mufaddil (all in Awarta), who are also considered prophets, have no buildings at all.

Some mosques <term>djawâmi‘: mosques (singular: djâmi‘)</term> and saintly shrines were certainly once ancient churches or old houses. For instance, I believe the mosque of Omar ibn el-Khattab (in Sur Baher) and the mosque of el-Uzeir (in el-Qaryeh <note>Abu Ghosh</note>) were originally churches. The shrines of Sheikh Abdallah (in Beit Surik), Sheikh Salih (in Deir Yassin), Sheikh Srur (in Awarta), and Sheikh en-Nubani (in Nablus) are simple rooms that were once used as homes. They lack a prayer niche <term>miḥrâb: a niche in the wall pointing toward Mecca</term> or a vaulted ceiling, and they show no signs of containing a tomb. At present, they are in a very poor state of repair.

Many built shrines <term>maqâm: a local sanctuary or "standing place" of a saint</term> are open sanctuaries where the roof rests on pillars. The best example of such a shrine is that of Hasan the Shepherd <note>original: er-Râ`î</note>, who was said to be the shepherd of the prophet Moses. Inside a rectangular enclosure built of stones...

...and mortar, we see an elongated and vaulted roof which rests on six pillars, three to the north and three to the south. Between these pillars is the large tomb. El-Maṣʿad (Mount of Olives) is an octagonal sanctuary with the sides closed up. Masâdjid sittnâ ʿÂišeh has a vault resting on four corner pillars, where the south side has been completely closed, and the eastern and western only partly built. The western and the southern walls of the shrine of Aḥmad es-Sarrîsî <margin>On the top of a mountain in Abû Għôš.</margin> are closed, while the two other sides are open.

I do not propose to give an exhaustive architectural description of all types of shrines. My only aim has been to give simple examples of the different classes. Descriptions of beautiful mosques like the Dome of the Rock <note>original: "eṣ-Ṣaḥrah"</note>, Al-Aqsa <note>original: "el-Aqṣâ"</note>, and others, need not be given here, since they may be found in convenient form elsewhere. <margin>Short descriptions are found in Baedeker, Meistermann and other guide-books; scientific descriptions are: Gressmann, *The Dome of the Rock in Jerusalem* <note>original: "Der Felsendom in Jerusalem"</note>, *PJ* IV, 54 ff.; R. Hartmann, *The Dome of the Rock and its History* <note>original: "Der Felsendom und seine Geschichte"</note>, 1909; De Vogüé, *The Mosque of Omar in Jerusalem* <note>original: "La Mosque d’Omar à Jérusalem"</note>, 1905.</margin> Many of the sanctuaries which are situated in a village serve at the same time as the <term>djâmiʿ: a congregational mosque</term> of that place, where the people assemble for prayer. Many a mosque was built in the immediate neighborhood of a <term>welî: a saint's shrine</term>, as in the case of Sheikh <note>original: eš-šêḫ</note> Jarrah, Sad u Said (both in Jerusalem), Salman el-Farsi (Mount of Olives), and others. Some sacred sites which are situated out in the fields, and which contain no tomb, serve for the passerby as a place in which to perform his prayers—for example, el-Imam Ali, on the carriage road from Jerusalem to Jaffa.

We often find in front of the sanctuary an elevated place, well-covered with large, smooth stone flags, called <term>mṣallâyât: prayer platforms</term>. They are generally in close contact with the sanctuary and serve for prayer. It is not necessary that a ritually clean cover be spread on them since they are always kept clean. Such "prayer platforms" are met with in Sheikh Saleh (Anata), Irjal Sufah (West of Der Ghassaneh), Sheikh Damrah, and Sheikh en-Nubani (both in Mazari en-Nubani). <margin>I am indebted for information regarding the last three places to Omar Effendi Barghuti.</margin>

Before passing on, it may be well to note that in some shrines there are inscriptions. They are generally found just above the door of the sanctuary or above that of the court, though occasionally...

<summary>This page describes various architectural forms of Palestinian shrines, noting how many serve as communal mosques or outdoor prayer platforms (mṣallâyât) for travelers and locals.</summary>

Inscriptions may be found above windows (at the shrine of Sheikh Jarrah <note>original: eš-šêh Djarrâḥ</note>), or above one of the pillars of a vault (at the shrine of Sheikh Hasan er-Rai <note>original: eš-šêh Ḥasan er-Râʻî</note>). Some examples of such inscriptions include:

1. Above the central door of the shrine of el-Hadrah¹ (Nablus):

*This prayer house was built during the reign of the Sultan, King Seif ed-Din Qalawan, the pious. May God grant him power; and his father, the Sultan, the pious King Ala ed-Din. May his victories be glorious.*

2. Inside the shrine <term>maqâm: a sanctuary or shrine marking a holy site</term> of el-Hadr (Nablus):²

*O Dasuqi, O Badawi. The shrine of el-Hadr, Ahmad el-Badawi,³ Abd-el-Qadir ed-Djilani.*

3. An inscription on velvet laid on the <term>cenotaph: a horizontal funerary monument or "empty tomb" placed over the burial site</term> of el-Anbia (Nablus):

*This is the tomb of our prophets, the noble prophets of God, the sons of our master Jacob: and they are Zebulun <note>original Rayâlûn: Canaan notes this is likely a scribal error</note>, Issachar <note>original: Jašadjar</note>, and Asher. On behalf of our Prophet, these men, and all other prophets, may there be the most efficacious prayers and the most complete peace.*

4. In the <term>rawâq: a portico or gallery open on one side</term> adjacent to the shrine of el-Anbia:

*Whenever Zacharias went into the chamber to visit her [Mary], he found provisions with her.*⁷

I cannot find the specific connection between this Quranic verse and the shrine of el-Anbia <note>the Shrine of the Prophets</note>, as the site has no traditional association with Zacharias and Mary.

*Muhammad Pasha, the doer of good deeds, has erected this blessed dome <term>qubbeh: a dome or domed shrine common in Islamic architecture</term> over Hasan the Shepherd—may God sanctify his secret <note>a Sufi blessing for the deceased</note>—as he (the Pasha) was returning from welcoming the Muslim pilgrims. He proceeded with the building but found no water. However, because of his great zeal—may God protect him—water was brought to the site² from the village of Jericho. Thus, he earned his heavenly reward. The 1st of Rabi’ [al-Awwal], 1110 <note>Approximately September 1698 AD.</note>.*

8. On the tomb of Sheikh Abu al-Halawah (Jerusalem):

<note>original Arabic: هو الحي الباقي. هذا قبر ولي الله الشيخ حسن ابو الحلاوة. لروحه الفاتحة. ١٣٠٥.</note>

*He is the Living, the Everlasting. This is the grave of the Saint <note>original: wali</note> of God, Sheikh Hasan Abu al-Halawah. For his soul, recite the Opening Chapter <note>original: al-Fatiha; the first chapter of the Quran, often recited for the dead</note>. 1305 <note>Approximately 1887–1888 AD.</note>.*

## b) The Tomb

Inside the shrine, and generally in the center of the room, we find the tomb of the holy person whose name it bears. Before providing a description of the tomb itself, one point should be clarified: the relationship between the tomb and the building. The tomb is often not located inside the shrine, but outside of it: on the stone bench <note>original: *maṣṭabah*</note>, in the portico <note>original: *rawâq*</note>, or in the garden adjacent to the sanctuary <note>original: *maqâm*</note>. However, it is not at all necessary for a tomb to be directly or indirectly connected with the site for it to be considered a shrine, and there are many tombs that do not have a dome <note>original: *qubbeh*</note>. Both of these features will be discussed at length in another part of this work. We may classify tombs according to their position as follows:

1. **Those connected with a sanctuary <note>original: *maqâm*</note> may be situated:**
   a) In the shrine itself; such as el-Badriyeh (in Sharafat), Bishr el-Hafi (in Nablus), etc.;
   b) In the portico <note>original: *rawâq*</note>; such as the master Ahmad et-Taiyar (in Sharafat);
   c) In the garden adjacent to the sanctuary; such as the prophet Annir (in Deir 'Ammar) or the Sheikh Yusif (in Harbatha).

2. **Those which have no shrine building but are situated:**
   a) In a cave; such as the Sheikh es-Sidri (in 'Anata) or the Lady Rab'ah (on the Mount of Olives);
   b) Outside in the fields or in a cemetery; such as the Sheikh Muhammad el-Baqqani (in Nablus), the Sheikh Ramadan, the Sheikh 'Bed (both in Qatanna), el-Uzer (in 'Awarta), etc.;
   c) Inside the village, among the houses and not attached to any sanctuary <note>original: *maqâm*</note> or cemetery; such as the Sheikh Suwan and the Sheikh Isma'il (both in Sur Bahir).

<summary>This page describes various religious inscriptions found in Palestinian shrines and categorizes the physical locations of tombs, noting that a "shrine" (maqâm) can exist with or without a built structure or a dome.</summary>

<keywords>maqâm, tomb classification, Palestinian shrines, Sharafat, Nablus, Islamic architecture, rawâq, qubbeh</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the positioning of tombs relative to the sanctuary buildings, focusing on secondary tombs belonging to the saint's family members and servants.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

It often happens that in addition to the main tomb or tombs located inside the shrine, there are one or more outside that are closely linked to the life story of the main <term>welî</term> <note>original: *welî*; a Muslim saint or holy person</note>. These may be situated at various distances and still maintain their association. Some examples include:

In the neighborhood of Sheikh <note>original: *eš-šêḫ*</note> Abd es-Salam, the tomb of his son Salman can be seen outside the <term>maqâm</term> <note>original: *maqâm*; a sanctuary or sacred place</note>. <margin>El-Badriyeh <note>original Arabic: البدرية</note></margin> El-Badriyeh has the tomb of her husband, Master <note>original: *es-sayid*</note> Ahmad et-Tayyar, outside her shrine in the adjacent hall. Sheikh Ahmad el-Bustami and his brother Sheikh Murad (in Nablus) have their Black servant buried outside of the sanctuary. El-Qatrawani's shepherd lies buried near the sanctuary of his master.

The number of tombs in a single sanctuary varies. In most cases, there is only one, but some have two, and others even more. One of the tombs—generally the largest—is of more importance than the others. It contains the primary saint, and therefore the sanctuary takes his name. The other graves belong to his close relatives: his wife, his brother, his male descendants, and sometimes his servant. The shrines of Sheikh Anbar and Sheikh Badr each contain two tombs: one for the saint and one for his wife. In the case of Sheikh Badr, both tombs are in the same room, while in that of Sheikh Anbar, the tomb of the saint's wife is in a small adjacent room. <margin>El-Badriyeh <note>original Arabic: البدرية</note></margin> In el-Badriyeh, one tomb in the shrine itself is said to be hers; the other to the north is that of her children, while the tomb outside her shrine is believed to be that of her husband. 

Cases where we find more than two tombs are even more interesting. At Sheikh Hamad (in Kolonia), there are five: the tomb of the saint, two for his two wives, one for his son, and a fifth for his servant (who was actually a Black slave, or <term>abd</term> <note>original: *'abd*</note>). The tomb of the servant is shown partly inside and partly outside the shrine, but the two halves do not align with each other. We often find tombs arranged side by side that belong to one of the following categories:

1. The graves of members of the same family, like the Dawari in Sur Bahir and the Yamin family in Beit Anan. All are regarded as <term>sullah</term> <note>original: *şullâḥ*; plural of *şâleḥ*</note>, or "righteous men." In Shu’fat, there are also two tombs belonging to the Dawari family. At Irjal el-Amud (in Nablus), we find many tombs outside the sanctuary which are supposed to be the graves of the servants of the <term>awlia</term> <note>original: *awliâ*; saints or "friends of God"</note> buried here.

<summary>This page describes the varying layouts of Palestinian shrines, noting that secondary tombs for a saint's family members or servants are often found both inside and immediately outside the main sanctuary building.</summary>
<keywords>welî, maqâm, saintly families, tomb architecture, El-Badriyeh, Sheikh Badr, religious servants, Palestinian folklore</keywords>

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

2. The tombs of <term>mujahidin</term> and <term>shuhada</term>. Both words mean "martyrs." The first denotes those who have fallen in a holy war. In Hebron, we are shown the tombs of the *Shuhada* <note>original: *eš-Šuhadâ*</note>. Local tradition says that after they were beheaded, their heads rolled down the hill shouting *našhadu, našhadu, našhadu* ("We witness, we witness, we witness"). In Ramleh, the tombs of the *mujahidin* <note>original: *el-Mudjâhidîn*</note> are arranged in several rows, not far from the shrine of the prophet Saleh. Some large tombs are said to contain the remains of more than one saint, as seen in the sanctuary of the Prophets <note>original: *al-Anbiâ*</note> in Nablus. There, the <term>darih</term> <note>original: *darîḥ*; a monumental tomb or headstone</note> is supposed to be built over the remains of the prophets Raiyalun, Yashjar, and Ashar, the sons of Jacob. An inscription on silk hanging over the tomb reads: 

> "This is the tomb of the prophets of God, the Glorious, the sons of our master Jacob—namely Raiyalun, Yashjar, and Ashar. On behalf of our Prophet, and for these and all other prophets, may there be the most efficacious prayers and the most complete peace." <note>The tradition that Zebulon, Issachar, and Asher are buried in Nablus may originate with the Samaritans.</note>

In Abu Ghosh, we find that the tomb of Sheikh Ismail el-Inbawi, which lies by the north wall of the shrine, has a structure connecting it with the wall. This structure is said to be the tomb of his son, Sheikh Nasir.

<summary>This page details the tombs of martyrs and the sons of Jacob in Palestine, providing a comparative table of how many family members or servants are buried alongside primary saints in various regional shrines.</summary>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical structure and layout of Palestinian tombs, moving from the specific contents of the shrines to their external construction materials and geographical orientation.</meta>

# CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

The tombs are built of stone. In most cases, the entire structure is simply plastered over and whitewashed. Even if they are located in the open air (such as in cemeteries or fields) and have no protection from storms, they are treated in the same simple way. Other tombs are constructed of well-cut <note>original: "hewn"</note> stones, which is usually a sign of a more recently built tomb. <note>original: "of recent origin."</note> The tombs of al-Uzayr, al-Mufaddil, and al-Mansuri are very well maintained. In the case of al-Uzayr (Figure 2, Plate II), I could identify three layers of well-made plaster (<term>qasara</term> <note>original: *qṣâra*; a type of traditional lime plaster</note>). The general structure of these tombs—and of the <term>cenotaphs</term> <note>a monument erected in honor of a person whose remains are elsewhere</note> of many important <term>walis</term> <note>original: *welîs*; Muslim saints or holy people</note>—consists of an upper peaked <note>original: "gable"</note> section placed on top of a lower rectangular base. Figure 3 of Plate II shows a cross-section of such a monument.

The orientation of the tomb is generally from East to West—that is, the orthodox orientation of all Muslim tombs in this part of the Islamic world. The dead are laid on their right sides with their heads to the west and their feet to the east, so that their faces are turned toward the <term>qibla</term> <note>original: *qibleh*; the direction of the Kaaba in Mecca, toward which Muslims pray</note>. There are a few exceptions to this rule. Sheikh Ghanim of Jericho, located in the western cemetery, is the best example of such an exception, since his tomb is built from North to South. This saint belongs to the holy ad-Dawari family. We have already seen that some of this family are buried in Sur Bahir and others in Shafat. In the case of Sheikh Zayd (in Anata), it is somewhat difficult to determine how the tomb is intended to lie. The wall runs from North to South, and in the middle of it there is a <term>shahid</term> <note>original: *šâhid*; a headstone or grave marker</note>, but only one; there are no signs of any enclosure running from east to west to define the tomb's direction. It is generally said that graves facing this direction belong to the pre-Islamic prophets, but this explanation, while true for some, does not apply to the two cases mentioned. The graves of the prophet Samuel (<term>Nabi Samwil</term>), al-Uzayr, al-Mufaddil, and the Prophets <note>original: *al-Anbiâ*</note> run approximately north to south; the grave of Lot (in Bani Na'im) also has a North-to-South direction.

---

<note>1. Regarding the general construction of modern Muslim tombs, I refer the reader to Boehmer's article, "In the Muslim Cemeteries of Jerusalem" <note>original German: *Auf den muslimischen Friedhöfen Jerusalems*</note>, *Journal of the German Palestine Society* <note>original: *ZDPV*</note>, 1909—10.</note>
<note>2. *Palästina-Jahrbuch* VII, 86; Jaussen, *Customs* <note>original: *Coutumes*</note>, p. 99.</note>
<note>3. Both located in Awartah.</note>
<note>4. Located in Nablus.</note>
<note>5. It is curious that while the direction of the tomb is North-South, this prophet is reported to have turned his face toward the south while praying.</note>

<summary>This page describes the physical construction and orientation of Palestinian Muslim tombs, noting that while most face Mecca (East-West), several prominent prophetic tombs are oriented North-South.</summary>
<keywords>tomb construction, plaster, qasara, cenotaph, qibla, tomb orientation, Sheikh Ghanim, Nabi Samwil, Prophet Lot, Awartah, Nablus</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the physical characteristics of Muslim cenotaphs in Palestine, specifically discussing the upright stones at the head and foot of the graves and their varying dimensions.</meta>

# Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society

In shape, the <term>cenotaphs</term> <note>a monument erected in honor of a person whose remains are elsewhere</note> are elongated with a rounded top, flat, or with a triangular cross-section rising to a single edge. The tomb of el-Badrîyeh (Figure 1, Plate III) has a line running from end to end of the top, parallel to the center axis, which divides the cenotaph into two parts. It appears as though two tombs were intended, but popular tradition recognizes only one. At both ends, we find upright stones, known as a <term>naṣb</term> <note>original: "a stone set up"</note> or <term>šâhid</term> <note>literally "witness"; a memorial stone or headstone</note>, marking the head (to the west) and the feet (to the east). Very often only the head <term>naṣb</term> is found, sometimes carved in the shape of a turban (such as at Sheikh Abû Ḥalâweh in Jerusalem); in other cases, neither the head nor the feet are marked at all (as with Sheikh Šaddâd and Sheikh Ṣâleḥ). 

Female saints (such as el-Badrîyeh and Râbʿah) and "gigantic" tombs (such as el-ʿUzêr and el-Mufaḍḍil) have no <term>šawâhid</term> <note>plural of šâhid; memorial stones</note>. In many cases where we do not find such stones, a careful investigation shows that the cenotaphs once had them but have since lost them (for example, Sheikh ʿNênî in Sûrbâhir). Where more than one saint is believed to be buried in the same grave, we may find multiple markers, as in el-Anbiâ (Nablus). There, three <term>šawâhid</term> stand, one for each of the three sons of Jacob who are supposed to be buried there. According to the scholar Jaussen, these two upright stones are symbolic, representing the two angels who visit the dead <note>original: referring to Munkar and Nakir, the angels who, according to Islamic tradition, question the deceased in the grave</note>. I could not personally verify this statement, and I have been unable to find such an explanation in Arabic books.

The tomb may be as high as 1 to 1.5 meters, but some are very low. The tombs of Sheikh Badr, his wife, and Sheikh Rîḥân are not raised at all above the surrounding floor. Those built outside a <term>maqâm</term> <note>a shrine or sanctuary built over the burial place of a saint</note> are generally elevated, while the lower ones are almost always found inside buildings. The sizes of tombs vary enormously. While most are of normal size, some have exceptionally large dimensions. The following are the largest that I have seen:

| Name of Saint | Location | Length | Breadth | Height |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| El-ʿUzêr | ʿAwartah | 564 cm | 362 cm | 385 cm |
| el-Manṣûrî | ʿAwartah | 440 cm | 235 cm | — |
| el-Mufaḍḍil | ʿAwartah | 468 cm | 264 cm | — |
| es-Sulṭân Ḥalîl Qalawânî | Nablus | 458 cm | — | — |

<note>The dictionary *Muḥîṭ el-muḥîṭ* (original: "The Circumference of the Ocean," a famous 19th-century Arabic dictionary) and the Hava dictionary do not give a specialized funerary meaning for "naṣb," defining it simply as "a stone set up."</note>

Many <term>cenotaphs</term> <note>monuments built in honor of a person whose remains are elsewhere</note> have a small niche (*ṭâqah*) in one of their walls, where oil lamps (*srâdj*, plural *surdj*), matchboxes (*'ilbit kabrît* or *tšaḥḥâteh* <note>original: *kaḥḥâteh*; the "k" is pronounced as "tš" (ch) in some local dialects</note>), and other items are kept. These niches may be located on different sides of the tomb: on the northern side, as seen at Sheikh Suwan <margin>In Surbahir.</margin> and Sheikh Imbarak; <margin>In Bet Iksa.</margin> on the southern side, as at ed-Dawari, <margin>In Surbahir.</margin> ed-Djarahid, <margin>On the Mount of Olives.</margin> Sheikh Zed, <margin>In Anata. In this case, the niche (*ṭâqah*) was created by removing a stone from the wall that runs from north to south.</margin> or Sheikh Ismail; <margin>In Surbahir.</margin> or on the western side, as with Sheikh Hamdallah <margin>In Biddu.</margin> and Sheikh Anbar. <margin>In Isawiya.</margin>

Some tombs possess more than one niche, such as those of the <term>sheikhs</term> <note>original: *šiuḫ*; plural of sheikh, meaning elders or leaders</note> of ed-Djarahid on the Mount of Olives, where three tombs stand in a single line. <note>The scholar Kahle mentions only two tombs, but there are actually three; see *Palästinajahrbuch* VII, 90.</note> The tomb in the center has two niches: one on the west and one on the south. This latter niche is fitted with a wooden door. In one niche, I found a lamp and tins of oil, while in others, I found water, matches, and burned incense. <note>The middle and southern tombs are connected at the head by a small wall. It is believed that anyone who lies between them will be cured of their illness. See also Kahle, *Palästinajahrbuch* VII, 91.</note> Sheikh ez-Zughbeh (located near the tomb of el-Mansuri in Awartah) has several such niches—on the southern, western, and eastern sides.

In shrines where a tomb is housed inside a building, incense is generally burned in one of these wall niches. <note>At the shrine of Sheikh Hamad, the oil lamps were placed directly on the tomb.</note> However, for tombs located in open fields or cemeteries without a surrounding building, lights and incense are placed directly into these cenotaph niches.

Some cenotaphs have a circular, cup-like cavity on top, which may be shallow or deep. These are used for water, or more frequently, for flowers. Some people believe that the soul of the deceased visits the tomb once a week, on Friday, and expects to find water there to quench its thirst. These...

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical features of Palestinian tombs, specifically focusing on "cup-like holes" and the various folk beliefs and rituals associated with them.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

Cup-like holes are found more frequently in the tombs of common people than in those of the **pious men** <note>original: *ṣullâḥ*, plural of *ṣâleḥ*</note>.

Based on what I have gathered from various people, these cups are used for one of the following purposes: <margin>It is curious that the scholar Kahle gives only one explanation for these cups in the *Palestine Yearbook* (PJ VII, 90).</margin>

1. Water and flowers are placed in them. The purpose of the water is to keep the flowers alive for a long time. This is the explanation provided by the more educated classes.

2. The water in the cup is for birds to drink **"for the benefit of the soul of the deceased"** <note>original: *'ann rûḥ el-maiyet*</note>. The idea behind this explanation is that the birds will thank the soul of the dead for this act of kindness and will, if necessary, testify to this good deed. Such an explanation is given by people of the middle class. <margin>For the same reason, as Kahle suggests, about 450 kg of grain and a **large water jar** <note>original: *zîr*</note> are placed on the roof of the **shrine** <note>original: *maqâm*</note> of Imam ash-Shafi'i in Cairo on his feast day.</margin>

3. The water in the cup <note>At times there are two such cups; they are even found on the tombs of commoners.</note> serves to quench the thirst of the soul of the dead. I have heard this idea from peasants and some simple Muslims in Jerusalem.

Flowers, water, and other offerings are generally brought on Thursday afternoon, which is the day when cemeteries are usually visited.

Another custom that points to the belief mentioned in the third point is the fact that relatives of the deceased often read the **opening chapter of the Quran** <note>original: *fâtiḥah*</note> for his soul if his widow becomes engaged to another man. At the same time, an egg and a small jar full of water are buried at the head of the tomb. The water is supposed to quench his thirst and wet his mouth, while the egg is intended to burst apart in the dead man's place when his wife's remarriage becomes known to him. <note>This custom is dying out.</note>

Up to now, only complete tombs have been mentioned, but parts of tombs are also found. A short description of one will suffice. In Surbahir <note>The author notes hearing both "Sûrbâhir" and "Sûrbâhil."</note>, just behind the guest house, there is a rectangular depression in the rock about 4 by 2 meters in size, with a depth of 50 to 60 centimeters. Two steps lead down into it. In the middle of the western
