<meta>This page continues the description of Muslim burial sites in Palestine, transitioning from the folk rituals involving "cup-holes" to the architectural features and decorative traditions of specific tombs and shrines.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

# CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

From the wall there protrudes a small, very simple building resembling one half of a common Muslim tomb. Inquiring about the significance of this, I was told that **Sheikh** <note>original: *eš-šêḫ*</note> Ismail was buried in a small cave in the rock, and that this building is intended to cover his feet, which stuck out since the cave was not long enough for his entire body. In the east side of this partial tomb, there is a **niche** <note>original: *ṭâqah*</note> which serves for light and incense.

Very often tombs are decorated. **Henna** <note>original: *ḥinnâ*</note>, **red lead** <note>original: *sîrâqûn*</note>, and **indigo** <note>original: *nîleh*</note> are used. Palm branches, hands, lines, and dots are frequently encountered. Red lead produces a beautiful red color. It is curious that all representations of palm branches made on the graves of the Dawari (in Sur Bahir) had either five or seven branches. Sometimes verses from the Quran or the names of God are written with these colors. In the case of the three tombs of the **Sheikhs** <note>original: *šyûḫ*</note> of Jarahid (on the Mount of Olives), I noticed a red line running across every tomb, beginning at the lower middle of the northern side and ending at the lower middle of the southern side. In many cases, an inscription may be found connected with the tomb. The inscribed stone is on the sides or on the top of the **cenotaph** <term>A tomb-like monument to a person whose remains are elsewhere, or a decorative structure built over a grave.</term>.

Lastly, it should be noted that a great number of the tombs situated inside a **shrine** <note>original: *maqâm*</note> are covered with one or more **covers** <note>original: *stârât*, plural of *stârah*</note>. Generally, it is a greenish cloth, often with a border or embroidery in other colors. <note>Green is traditionally associated with Islam and is often used for the shrines of holy figures.</note> Sometimes the covers are decorated with verses from the Quran. A turban and sometimes a **rosary** <note>original: *masbaḥah*</note> are placed on the **headstone** <note>original: *râsîyeh*</note>. This last item may, as in the case of Bayram Shawish in Jerusalem, be placed around the whole tomb. In many cases, the cover itself is not put directly on the tomb, but on a wooden cage, which is made in the form of the tomb and encloses the grave. Such cages are especially used where the tombs are very low, as in al-Badriya and the already mentioned Bayram Shawish. In many cases, an inscription laid on the cover and embroidered on a piece of velvet informs us of the name or names of those buried in the grave, as seen on the tombs of Rijal al-Amud, al-Anbiya, Sheikh Ahmad al-Bistami, and Sultan Badr al-Ghafir (all in Nablus). Sometimes the tomb is encircled with an iron frame, as with Sheikh Salman al-Farsi.

¹ Also **cover** <note>original: *sitrat*, plural of *sitreh*</note>. This expression is not used much for these covers.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of Muslim tomb types in Palestine, moving from masonry structures to primitive stone heaps and exploring the historical and spiritual significance of trees at these sanctuaries.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

All the tombs described so far have been built of masonry. However, there are others made of an elongated heap of stones surrounded by a stone enclosure.¹ Such graves closely resemble the simple tombs used by modern peasants. We find them especially among the Bedouins (for example, Sheikh Zughbeh,² in Jericho). In some cases, there is no surrounding enclosure, which points to the most primitive form of tomb worship. The only example of this kind that I have seen is that of **Sheikh Hues** <note>original: eš-šêh Ḥuês</note>³ of **Biddu** <note>original: Biddû</note>. No **niche** <note>original: *ṭâqah*</note> is associated with it.⁴ In other instances, we do not find a heap of stones, but only a vertical stone at the head and another at the foot to mark the position of the **grave** <note>original: *qabr*</note>, as seen with **Sheikh Sabbah** <note>original: eš-šêh Ṣabbâḥ</note> at Jericho. This supposed tomb is surrounded by an **enclosure** <note>original: *huwêṭah*</note>.⁵

### c) *Trees*

Trees are a very important element of most shrines. This is not a new custom, as many of the "high places" mentioned in the Old Testament were associated with "green trees." I have no doubt that, with few exceptions, every **Muslim** <note>original: Mohammedan</note> sanctuary is, or once was, characterized by one or more trees. **Saints' shrines** <note>original: *Welîs*</note> of recent origin, however, are generally treeless, like **Sheikh Abu Halawi** <note>original: eš-šêh Abû Ḥalâwî</note>. A large number of these trees were cut down during the war <note>The author likely refers to World War I.</note>, while many others have died of old age or been uprooted by storms (such as the **terebinth** <note>original: el-Buṭmeh</note> tree in **Beit Safafa** <note>original: Bêt Ṣafâfâ</note>). These are undoubtedly the main reasons why so many shrines are currently treeless.

I have very often heard the following statement: "The **shrine** <note>original: *welî*</note> has no tree at present, but I remember very well that during my childhood a large tree stood there." In many cases where an old tree was cut down, the villagers to whom that particular saint belongs have planted a new one of the same species. This was done, for example, at **Sheikh Anbar** <note>original: eš-šêh ‘Anbar</note>. The huge fig tree that once grew there was cut down and burned by soldiers whose camp was nearby. The people of **Isawiya** <note>original: ‘Êsâwîyeh</note> have planted another

---

¹ Doutté, **Magic and Religion** <note>original: *Magie et Religion*</note>, p. 432.
² Not Zu‘beh, as given by Kahle, **Palestine Yearbook** <note>original: *PJ* (Palästinajahrbuch)</note>, 1911, p. 88.
³ In 1922, the inhabitants of the village heaped stones together to build the tomb.
⁴ Kahle describes another example of this category, namely **Sheikh Mohammed Darir el-Qadri** <note>original: eš-šêh Moḥammed Ḍarîr el-Qâdrî</note> (**Palestine Yearbook** <note>original: *PJ*</note>, 1911, p. 87).
⁵ In the vicinity, there is a **balsam** <note>original: *zaqqûm*</note> tree (a kind of **myrobalan** <note>original: myrobalm; a plum-like fruit-bearing tree</note>).

<header>CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

in its place. At times it happens that a tree grows up some distance from the **shrine** <note>original: *welî*</note>. If someone from a nearby village states that it was revealed to him in a dream that this tree belongs to that same saint, this claim is enough to protect the tree completely. A **Mediterranean hackberry** <note>original: *mês* tree (*Celtis australis* L.)</note> growing very near **Sheikh Abdullah** <note>original: *eš-šêḫ* 'Abdallah</note> in **Shafat** <note>original: Ša'fât</note>, and a fig tree growing above the cave of **Sheikh Abd es-Salam** <note>original: *eš-šêḫ* 'Abd es-Salâm</note> in **Anata** <note>original: 'Anâtâ</note>, are currently regarded as belonging to these saints.

According to my data from all the shrines where I could obtain definite information about trees, they were present in 60% of the cases. From an analysis of the different species of trees growing near these places, we find that sanctity is not attributed to any one species more than others. This fact indicates that it is not the tree itself which makes the place holy, but rather the tree derives its sanctity from the **saint** <note>original: *welî*</note> to whom it is dedicated. In some cases, there might appear to be proof to the contrary, but see the discussion below. The following analysis may be of interest: out of 128 cases where trees were found near sanctuaries, 30 were **oaks** <note>original: *ballût*</note> (1), 25 were **figs** <note>original: *tîn*</note> (2), 21 were **carobs** <note>original: *ḥarrûb*</note> (3), 16 were **olives** (4) <note>original: *zêtûn*</note> (5), 14 were **mulberries** <note>original: *tût*</note> (6), 12 were **lote trees** <note>original: *sidr*</note> (7), and 10 were **terebinths** <note>original: *buṭum*</note> (8). Other trees occasionally found include:

---

(1) **Kermes oak** <note>original: *Quercus coccifera* L.</note>. In this connection, I wish to express my thanks to Mr. Dinsmore for his kindness in providing the exact botanical names.

(2) **Common fig** <note>original: *Ficus carica* L.</note>.

(3) **Carob tree** <note>original: *Ceratonia siliqua* L.</note>.

(4) **Olive tree** <note>original: *Olea europea* L.</note>.

(5) A few words about the role played by the olive tree in Palestinian folklore may be of interest. In the various commentaries on the Quran, the olive tree is called **"the blessed tree"** <note>original: *eš-šadjarah el-mubârakeh*</note>. It is said to come from Paradise and is considered the most noble of all plants (**Fakhr al-Razi** <note>A famous Persian Sunni Muslim theologian and philosopher (1149–1209 AD).</note> VI, 264; VIII, 458). A common proverb compares the olive tree to the Bedouin (who can live anywhere in the desert and requires very little for his living), the fig tree to the **peasant** <note>original: *fellâḥ*</note> (who has more needs), and the vine to a **concubine** <note>original: *sirrîyeh*</note> (who requires a great deal of attention). Christians believe that olive trees kneel down on the night of the feast of the Holy Cross.

(6) **Black mulberry** <note>original: *Morus nigra* L.</note>.

(7) **Christ's Thorn Jujube** <note>original: *Zizyphus Spina Christi* L.</note>.

(8) **Palestine terebinth** <note>original: *Pistacia palestina* Boiss.</note>.

Trees that naturally dominate the plains—such as mulberries, palms, and sycomores—are more frequently found at shrines located in those areas.

In some instances, a solitary tree serves to beautify the shrine; in others, a small or large grove is dedicated to the holy person. I believe that many of these sacred trees are the last survivors of ancient woods that once covered the area. These trees now serve as living witnesses to the former "forest glory" of Palestine. The shrines of **Sheikh al-Qatrawani** <note>original: *Eš-šêh el-Qaṭrawânî*</note>, **Rijal Abu Tuh**¹ <note>The Men of Abu Tuh</note>, **Sheikh Ahmad**², and **Sheikh Abu Lemun**³, illustrate this view.

It is not necessary for all the trees in a group dedicated to a **saint** <term>welî: a holy person or "friend of God" in Islamic tradition, often associated with a shrine</term> to be of the same species. The following list shows that different types of trees can be associated with the same holy site:

| Name of Saint/Shrine | Location | Types of Trees |
|---|---|---|
| **Abu Lemun** | Beit Iksa | terebinth and oak trees; |
| **al-Mansuri** | Awarta | mulberry and grapevine; |
| **al-Uzeir** | Awarta | terebinths, a palm, and a carob; |
| **Trees of the Forty** <note>original: *šadjârât el-Arb’în*</note> | Qubeibeh | figs, oak, and terebinths; |
| **al-Uzeir** | Bethany <note>original: *Êzarîyeh*</note> | pomegranate, cypress, and a lemon tree; |
| **Salman al-Farsi** | Mount of Olives | Aleppo pine, cypress, olive, and pomegranate; |
| **Abu Tuh** | Beit Likiya | olive, oak, terebinth, carob, and several other kinds. |

The trees are generally located very close to the sanctuaries. In very rare cases, the building actually encloses the tree, or rather

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

...part of the trunk. However, it is common for the tree to be found at some distance from the *welî* <note>original: "welî" — a saint or the shrine of a saint</note>. In cases where a holy man has several trees dedicated to him, one may grow near the *maqâm* <note>original: "maqâm" — literally "place," referring to a sanctuary or shrine</note>, while others are located a considerable distance away. 

The best example of this is al-Badriyeh, a female saint who has an oak, two olive trees, and a lemon tree in her sanctuary. She also has another large oak tree to the east of the *maqâm*, a third oak in the valley, one on the way to el-Malha, and a fifth which once stood east of er-Ram. This last tree was cut down during the war. Sultan Ibrahim el-Adhami (of Beit Hanina) has a mulberry tree and, at some distance, two oaks and a *mêseh* <note>original: "mêseh" — a hackberry or European nettle tree</note>. The last one died recently and was cut down.¹

Holy trees that are not connected with any *qubbeh* <note>original: "qubbeh" — a domed shrine</note> or tomb will be described later. All holy trees, whether they are near to or far from the shrine, are revered and respected; even those that are not connected to any shrine at all enjoy the same reverence. 

If the holy tree is a fruit tree—such as a mulberry, fig, vine, cactus, etc.—it is regarded as a *sabil* <note>original: "sabîl" — literally "pathway," here meaning a public gift or charity</note>.² This means that anyone passing by is permitted to eat as much as they choose, but nothing must be carried away. It is said that anyone who breaks this rule will be severely punished by the saint of that particular tree. Almost everyone who takes advantage of this privilege will recite the *fatihah* <note>the opening chapter of the Quran</note> before plucking the fruit. 

In other cases, the <margin>the servant of the shrine</margin> *qayim* or *haddam* (the responsible servant or caretaker of the shrine) reserves for himself the exclusive right to gather the fruit of such trees, as well as those from the *waqf* <note>property dedicated to religious or charitable purposes</note> gardens belonging to the shrine. This is the case with Sittna el-Hadra in Nablus. In the case of el-Mansuri (in Awartah), the large grapevine is rented to an inhabitant of the village, who has the sole right to harvest the grapes. The income from the fruit is used to repair the *maqâm*.

The following story about el-Mansuri illustrates how severely a saint will punish anyone who steals from his property. A gendarme <note>a military policeman</note> happened to pass through Awartah. He rested under the mulberry tree beside the sanctuary. Seeing the beautiful...

---

¹ Other examples include Sheikh Hamad (at Kolonia), who has a mulberry in the sanctuary and an oak at a distance; Sheikh Abdallah (at el-Qubeibeh) also has a mulberry and, on the hill to the south, a carob tree known as *el-kharroubeh ed-djdideh* <note>the new carob tree</note>.

² *Sabil* is also used to refer to a water reservoir or public drinking fountain, as will be explained elsewhere.

...grapes in the sanctuary <note>original: "maqâm" — a shrine or holy place</note>, he cut a few bunches, despite the repeated warnings of the village people. Before long, the gendarme began to vomit blood incessantly. Nothing helped or relieved him until he bought a sheep and offered it to the Victorious One <note>original: "el-Manṣûrî" — a name for a saint</note>, thereby appeasing his wrath and atoning for his offense.

Another well-observed rule is that no one dares to cut any branch, however small it may be, from any of these trees. Furthermore, the saint will not allow anyone to gather and take away the broken or withered branches. They may only be used for cooking such meals as are offered in fulfillment of a vow, or meals prepared for the festivals of that particular saint <note>original: "welî" — a holy man or his shrine</note>. Sheikh Breik <note>original: "Eš-šêḫ Brêk" — some pronounced it Brêtš</note>, located south of Yalo <note>original: "Yâlô" — a biblical village near the Ayalon Valley</note>, had many trees that were cut down by some of the village inhabitants and converted into charcoal. According to local belief, the saint took revenge for this infamous act by slaying every one of the trespassers. The people always believe that locusts cannot injure the holy trees. Most of those I asked about this subject assured me that while all other trees in the village were completely eaten up by this frightful curse in the year 1915, the holy trees remained untouched. This can be taken as an excellent illustration of the simple, childlike faith of the peasants; in reality, only those species of trees which locusts generally do not attack elsewhere were spared in the case of the saints' shrines.

One additional point should be mentioned in connection with trees. The sacredness of the trees and the respect shown to every saint is the reason why peasants of the neighboring fields deposit their grain and wood, their plows, and other agricultural implements under these trees for one night or longer, feeling sure that the saint will protect them. More will be said later about this subject.

Not to be confused with holy trees associated with saints are those inhabited by demons. It is very difficult to give any definite rule by which a stranger can differentiate between the two. The following points appear to be characteristic:

1. I have never heard that a tree supposed to be inhabited by demons was hung with pieces of cloth. Every person whom I asked about this answered the same way, and so I cannot verify the...

<meta>This page transitions from discussing trees protected by holy saints (welis) to those inhabited by malevolent spirits (djinn), specifically focusing on the folklore surrounding the carob tree and its association with King Solomon.</meta>

...statement of Mills, quoted by Goldziher in his *Muslim Studies* <note>original: "Mohammedanische Studien"</note>.¹

2. While any tree can be sanctified by a saint <note>original: "welî"</note>, the spirits <note>original: "djinn" — supernatural beings in Islamic folklore that can be good or evil, though often feared as demons</note> seem only to inhabit certain kinds of trees, especially the carob tree <note>original: "ḫarrûb"</note>. Several stories illustrating this belief may be found in my book *Superstition* <note>original: "Aberglaube"</note>.² This is why an Arabic proverb says, “Sleeping below a carob tree is not recommended,”³ since it is thought that these trees are not only preferred by the demons as a home, but that they assemble here from time to time. Therefore, a simple peasant farmer <note>original: "fellâḥ"</note> will not tie his donkey to a carob tree without first asking the spirits for permission. Superstition tells us that this tree was the cause of the ruin of King Solomon’s kingdom. The misfortunes attached to it may arise from the idea that the carob⁴ belongs to the misfortune-bringing planet Saturn.⁵ Black fig trees are also thought to be preferred by the demons.⁶

When a tree is inhabited by a demon, it cannot belong at the same time to a saint. This is different with springs, where a good and a bad spirit may dwell in one and the same watercourse.⁷

The story of the carob tree and the ruin of King Solomon’s kingdom runs as follows: One day in the temple courts, King Solomon noticed a young plant unknown to him. He asked this plant for its name. “Carob,” was the answer. “Of what use are you?” continued the king. “To destroy your works,” replied the plant. The king then asked God that his death, whenever it should occur, might be hidden from the spirits until all mankind should be aware...

<meta>This page continues the folk legend of King Solomon and the carob tree, explaining how it led to his death and the end of his control over the demons. It then transitions into a discussion of the role of water sources at holy sites.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

of it. After praying in this way, Solomon dug up the carob and planted it in his garden. To prevent any harm from coming from it as much as possible, he watched it closely until it had grown into a strong sapling. He then cut it down and made it into a walking stick.

Many years before, Balqîs <note>original: Balqîs — the Arabic name for the Queen of Sheba</note>, Queen of Sheba, had come to test the king with difficult questions. One of these was how to pass a silk thread through a bead that had a spiral-shaped <note>original: "screw-like"</note> hole. Solomon asked all the animals, birds, reptiles, insects, and worms for help. Only a small white worm took on the task. It succeeded by taking the end of the thread in its mouth, crawling into one end of the hole, and coming out the other. In return, Solomon granted the worm's request to live in any plant it chose and feed on it. Without Solomon knowing, the worm had found a home under the bark of the carob <note>original: *harrûb*</note> tree, which had been made into his staff, and it had eaten its way to the very center of the wood.

The time arrived for the king to die. He happened to be sitting as usual, leaning on his stick, when the Angel of Death came and took away his soul. The demons <note>original: "djinn"</note> did not know he was dead and continued their forced labor according to the king's instructions for forty years. At last, however, the worm hollowed out the entire staff, which suddenly broke, and the king’s body rolled to the ground. Only then did the evil spirits realize that their tyrant was dead.¹

### d) *Water Courses*

Another feature of most holy places is the presence of water. This is either rainwater stored in cisterns (<term>bîr</term>, plural <term>biâr</term>) or <term>hrâbât</term> (the plural of <term>hrâbeh</term>, which is a cistern-like hole that is not lined with plaster), or "living" <note>meaning fresh or flowing</note> water from wells and <term>baiyârât</term> (the plural of <term>baiyârah</term>, which are deep wells found especially on the plains), and lastly, running water from springs and brooks. Of course, not all shrines have water near them, but it is found at the majority of sites. Such a spring or cistern is considered more or less sacred to the holy man whose shrine is nearby, and it may derive supernatural power from him. If this power is known, it is used...

¹ The story is found in the *Encyclopedia of Knowledge* <note>original: *Dâiratu-l-ma'ârif*, Volume VII</note>; part of it is mentioned in *The Glorious History of Jerusalem and Hebron* <note>original: *al-uns ed-djalîl*</note>, Volume I, page 121; and in Hanauer’s *Folklore of the Holy Land*, pages 49–50. This text is taken mostly from the last source.

use by the <term>fellâḥîn</term> <note>original: *fellâḥîn* — peasant farmers</note>. This subject will be dealt with later in greater detail.

In many cases, the cistern or the well is in a ruined condition and therefore does not hold water.¹ At shrines situated on or near the tops of high mountains, cisterns are more common. In the western plains, wells, cisterns, <term>baiyârât</term> <note>original: *baiyârât* — irrigated orchards or plantation wells</note>, and <term>ḥrâbât</term> <note>original: *ḥrâbât* — cisterns or underground ruins</note> are generally found. However, in the hill country, where the holy places are situated on the sloping sides of the mountains, springs are more common.

In a few cases, a <term>sabîl</term> <note>original: *sabîl* — a public drinking fountain or reservoir</note> is attached to the sanctuary. In this context, *sabîl* means a reservoir built by the public road and filled at regular intervals with water, so that every thirsty passerby may benefit from it. A cup is always left in these places. Among the <term>welîs</term> <note>original: *welîs* — saints or their shrines</note> with *sabîls*, we may mention al-Imâm ‘Alî and Sheikh Jarrah. The latter is surrounded by a <term>zâwiyeh</term> <note>original: *zâwiyeh* — a Sufi lodge or small monastery</note>. Outside the <term>maqâm</term> <note>original: *maqâm* — a sacred shrine or "station"</note>, a new mosque was built. On its inner walls hang <term>ṭbûl</term> (drums), <term>ṣnûdj</term> (brass castanets), spears (<term>ḥarbeh</term>, plural <term>ḥarbât</term>²), long sharp spits or <term>sîâḫ</term>, and <term>šîâš</term> (another type of ritual spit).³

The word <term>misqây</term> <note>original: *misqây* — a place for providing water</note> is used in some places instead of *sabîl*. Some shrines, like our master Sa‘d el-Anṣâr and Sheikh Ḥamdal-lah, have one or more large earthenware jars (<term>zîr</term>), which are kept full of water. The pious pilgrim and the passerby find water there for their ritual washing and refreshment.

The shrine of Ḥamdal-lah⁴ is situated in the immediate neighborhood of the western cemetery of Biddû. It consists of a four-sided enclosure built of stone and mortar. The door is on the northern side. Around the tomb, an oak tree and a rose bush grow, and another rose shrub can be seen outside the enclosure. A jar similar to the one mentioned above was placed in the outer southwest corner, but it was broken when I visited the shrine in 1922. A <term>miḥrâb</term> <note>original: *miḥrâb* — a prayer niche indicating the direction of Mecca</note>, set into the southern wall, marks the direction for prayers. Some rags were fastened to the tree <note>This was a common folk practice where pilgrims tied pieces of clothing to sacred trees to leave a part of themselves with the saint or to seek healing</note>. To the north of this shrine, there is a large water basin carved into the rock.

# Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society

Sometimes running water and a tree are the only indications of the sacredness of a place. At other times, we find only watercourses which, although not connected with any shrine, grave, or holy tree, are considered sacred and are assigned to some holy person. In one case, *el-Maṭba'ah*, there is a swamp connected with a <term>welî</term> (holy man or saint). This marsh has a widespread reputation for relieving rheumatic pains and is at the same time supposed to cure sterility.

Sixty-five percent of all sanctuaries recorded in this connection possessed a source of water (flowing or standing) in the neighborhood.

### e) *Caves*

The last feature to be noted is the presence of a cave in or about the shrine. We must consider three quite different kinds of caves:

1. Sacred caves connected with a sanctuary, either a tomb or a <term>maqâm</term> (sacred site or shrine).
2. Sacred caves which have no connection with any shrine.
3. Simple caves, having no apparent connection with the sacredness of a shrine, though situated near one.

It is interesting to note how many holy places are directly or indirectly connected with one or another kind of cave.

Sacred caves sometimes lie inside the *maqâm* itself and appear rather like a shallow cistern with a wide opening.<note>As is the case, for example, in the sanctuary of *en-nebî Luṭ* (Prophet Lot) in *Banî N'êm*.</note> In such cases, we seldom find a tomb in the shrine, and the people believe that the tomb is inside the cave itself.<note>S. Kahle, *Palästinajahrbuch*, 1911, p. 92.</note> Of course, no one has ever dared to descend into the cave to look for the grave. The mouth of such a cave is generally closed.

The following story illustrates this belief. The <term>qaiym</term> (guardian) of *eš-šêḫ* Moḥammad, whose shrine lies in *wâdî ed-Damm* <note>original: "wâdî" meaning a valley or dry riverbed; "ed-Damm" meaning "of the blood"</note>, to the south of *ed-Djôrah*, once ventured to descend into the cave (<note>original: "el-ghâr"</note>) of the shrine. There he saw the *welî* with a bloody sword in his hand. This sword was that of the Mohammedan leader who fell here while leading the troops who finally conquered Ashkelon. As soon as the guardian climbed out, he fell sick and died in a few days.

More often the caves are outside the building, either nearby or some distance away. Occasionally people relate that the holy man

...has been seen leaving his shrine and walking to the cave, where, after staying for some time, he returns to his *maqâm* <term>shrine or sacred site</term>. Caves connected with holy persons are always respected. Some have never been entered; others are approached only during the daytime, as their *wahrah* <note>original: "wahrah"; a condition of inspiring awe or dread</note> is very strong. In many cases, oil lamps are lit and incense is burned in the cave itself. No animals are allowed to enter such a cave, for it is believed that the spirit of the holy man will sooner or later inflict severe punishment upon such an animal. Many peasants say that they have seen a greenish light in these caves, which is extinguished as soon as a human being approaches the place.

In most respects, the above description also applies to sacred caves not connected with any specific shrine. Fuller details regarding this type of holy place will be given later. Among such caves, we may mention: one on the left side of the carriage road leading from Jerusalem to Kolôniâ, just opposite the last house of Liftâ, which is situated on the right side of the road.^1 There is also a cave in the garden of the Leper Hospital in Jerusalem.^2

In addition to these two groups of caves, there are many instances where caves are located near sanctuaries but have no direct connection with them. Often they are found within a ruin. Shepherds may keep their flocks there during the night. Examples include the caves found around the shrines of the Sheikh (*eš-šêḫ*) 'Abd es-Salâm, the Sheikh es-Sidrî, and the Sheikh 'Anbar. The caves of the first are in the ruins (*ḫirbet*) of 'Almît; those of the second are in the ruins of Dêr es-Sidd; and those of the third are in the ruins of Ibqû' eḍ-Ḍân.

<margin>The wilderness <note>original: البرية (al-Bariyah)</note></margin>

The caves below the shrine of the Lady (*es-sitt*) el-Badrîyeh in Šarâfât are used for storing crushed straw (*tibn*).

These caves are of two types—either natural or hewn into the rock. Most of the latter are ancient rock-cut tombs, the entrances of which have been enlarged. It is sometimes observed that old, damaged, and partly buried vaults are counted as caves. I have especially noticed this in 'Awartah.

The three caves belonging to this class are, in my opinion, the simplest and most primitive type of sanctuaries. They were ruined, dark, dirty, and unattended. The shrine of Sheikh (*Eš-šêḫ*) Srûr is situated inside the village and is made—

...of a low, dirty, dark, and half-ruined room which was, at the time of my visit, full of lime. El-Adjami <note>original: "El-‘Adjamî"</note> is a very low, narrow, and dirty opening in the habitation of a peasant. The *maqam* <term>shrine or sacred site</term> was filled with firewood. A second El-Adjami has a shrine consisting of a room-like cave, situated below a building and vaulted over.

Although these sanctuaries are of such a crude type, they are honored and respected by the peasants. Oil lamps are lighted in them, and oaths and vows are made in their name.

Among sanctuaries having a sacred cave in their vicinity are: El-Uzeir <note>original: "El-‘Uzêr"; referring to the biblical Ezra</note> near Awarta, es-Sakhrah <note>original: "es-Ṣaḫrah"; the sacred Rock</note> in the Mosque of Omar <note>This name was historically used by Westerners to refer to the Dome of the Rock in Jerusalem.</note>, Sheikh Ahmad el-Hweis in Biddu, and Sheikh es-Sidri near Anata.

The first two will be described more fully later. The tomb of Sheikh Ahmad el-Hweis¹ lies in the common cemetery, while the cave, which is more highly honored, lies at the very edge of the village. It is an ancient tomb hewn into the rock which becomes partly filled with water during the winter time. All vows and lights are offered to this saint in this place. He has also been seen walking out of the cave.

The Sheikh es-Sidri shrine has already been described.

Sacred caves which are not connected at all with a tomb or a masonry *maqam* will be discussed below. Among caves which, although found in the neighborhood of holy places, have no connection with the sanctity of the *maqam*, are:²

...empty, except for straw brooms, an earthen oil jar, and a copy of the Qoran. Outside of this room there are several caves, both small and large, which do not share the sacredness of the *weli* <term>original: *welî*; a Muslim saint or the shrine dedicated to them</term>. To the northeast, we see the tomb of his son, Sheikh Suleiman <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Slîmân*</note>. A fig tree grows in the rock adjacent to the shrine. Some relate that the father planted it; according to others, God made it grow directly from the rock to prove the authenticity of the saint.

Up to this point, we have dealt only with those caves whose nature as a cave is obvious. Very often, however, people say that beneath or beside a *weli* there is a hidden cave, inside of which the tomb of the holy person is situated. This feature is found at the shrines of Sheikh Ahmad the Flyer of Kerak <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Aḥmad el-Karakî eṭ-Ṭaiyâr*; "et-Tayyar" means "the flyer," a title often given to saints believed to travel miraculously</note> in Qastal, Our Lady the Green <note>original: *sittnâ el-Ḥaḍrâ*</note> in Nablus, Sheikh el-Qatrawani (between Bir Zeit and Atarah), the Prophet Samuel <note>original: *en-nabî Ṣamwîl*; associated with the biblical Mizpah</note>, Sheikh Abdullah in Qubeibeh, and others.

The shrine of Our Lady the Green <note>original: *Sittnâ el-Ḥaḍrâ*</note> illustrates this class. Three doors lead to an elongated room spread with carpets, with the middle door serving as the main entrance. The walls, especially the southern one, are decorated with rough paintings and verses from the Qoran, and they are hung with the musical instruments and weapons used by dervishes <term>members of a Muslim religious order known for devotional practices</term>. The *mihrab* <term>original: *miḥrâb*; a prayer niche in the wall of a mosque or shrine indicating the direction of Mecca</term> is beautifully decorated. A door in the western wall leads to a small, dark room known as "Jacob’s Sorrow" <note>original: *ḥuzn Ja‘qûb*</note>, as it is believed that Jacob wept here for the supposed death of his beloved son Joseph. The *sheikh* <term>a religious leader or caretaker</term> relates that this room is built over a cave that was once open. It is said that fifty-two steps used to lead down into it. This cave is thought to be the actual place where Jacob spent his days of mourning.¹ The sanctuary is surrounded by beautiful gardens.

It is a mistake to confuse the sacred caves described above with those inhabited by *djinn* <term>spirits or genies in Islamic mythology</term>, who appear in different shapes—mostly during the night—and always try to harm the passerby. Such caves include the Cave of Abu Farah <note>original: *Mghârit Abû Farḥ*</note> and the Cave of Marj el-Badd <note>original: *Mghârit Mardj el-Badd*</note>, both in Abu Dis. In the first one, the *djinn* appear sometimes in the form of animals and sometimes in the shape of human beings. At the second cave, the demons take the appearance of a rooster.

Places and caves regarded as holy by Christians and Jews may, at the same time, be considered by Muslims to be the...

Sliman Mustafa, a peasant from Malha, was returning one evening from Jerusalem. As he was caught in heavy rain, he sought shelter in a cave located near Bir el-Balat, not far from the Monastery of the Cross <note>original: "convent of the Holy Cross"</note>. The monks of this monastery are said to have buried their dead here in former years. As soon as he sat down on a stone, a male goat came close to him. The peasant, happy at this unexpected gift, struck a match but could see nothing. As soon as the light of the match went out, he saw the goat again. Frightened by the repeated appearance and disappearance of this animal, he rushed out of the cave saying the words, "In the name of the Gracious, Merciful God" <note>This is the *Basmala*, a common prayer for protection</note>. This freed him from the demon, which was following him in the shape of a goat.

### 3. TYPES OF SANCTUARIES

We have now considered all the characteristic elements of these sanctuaries—with the exception of stone circles, stone heaps, and rocks, which may also be found. We now proceed to deal with the various combined features which may make up a *weli* <term>original: *welî*; a Muslim saint or the shrine dedicated to them</term>. Attention must first be drawn to a constant factor affecting the importance of the different features of a shrine. The two most important parts of a sanctuary are without doubt the *maqam* <term>original: *maqâm*; a sacred "station" or shrine, often marking a spot where a saint stood or stayed</term> and the tomb; trees and water-courses are ranked second, with other features generally being of minor significance. I hope, however, in the following pages to make it clear that even these minor features are sometimes granted a high degree of sanctity. No place can be considered holy—that is <note>original: *i. e.*</note>, inhabited by a holy person—unless two conditions are fulfilled:

1. The performance of religious acts there, such as swearing oaths, making vows, lighting lamps, and burning incense.
2. The occurrence of supernatural phenomena there, such as hearing religious music, seeing a light that lit itself, or a severe punishment falling upon someone who trespassed.

These points will be considered in a later chapter. Let us now study the different features which may constitute a shrine in the broad sense of the word. They may be divided into nine classes:

->I. Sanctuaries consisting of a *maqam* and a tomb, along with all or<-
->most of the other features;<-
->II. A *maqam* but no tomb;<-

<summary>This page describes a folklore account of a goat-demon near Jerusalem and begins categorizing the different types of Palestinian Muslim shrines (welis) based on their physical features and evidence of holiness.</summary>
<keywords>djinn, Malha, Monastery of the Cross, weli, maqam, folklore, sanctuary classification, supernatural phenomena</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the classification of Palestinian sanctuaries, moving from the individual natural and architectural elements to the specific configurations these shrines take.</meta>

III. A tomb without a shrine <term>original: *maqâm*; a sacred station or building</term>;
IV. A cave, with or without a tomb;
V. A simple circular enclosure made of stone, without a tomb;
VI. A spring or a well;
VII. A single tree or a group of trees;
VIII. A heap of stones;
IX. A single large stone or a rock.

The types listed in I through VI may or may not be combined with one or more of the following features: a tree, water, a ruin, or a cave.

### I. Sanctuaries with a shrine <term>original: *maqâm*</term> and a tomb.

These are the most complete and highly developed forms of sanctuaries. We usually find them dedicated to important, well-established, and highly honored saints. In such cases, the tomb of the holy person was the original part of the shrine, and over time, an individual or a village built the sanctuary building around it. The more important the holy man, the more complex the building becomes. Prophets <term>original: *anbiâ*</term> enjoy the largest shrines. However, even many simple sheikhs <term>original: *šyûḫ*</term> have shrines that fall into this group—for example, Sheikh et-Tori and Sheikh ‘Anbar. Some of these are elaborate structures, such as Sheikh I‘mar in Bet Duqqu, Sheikh Hamad in Kolonia, and the Men of the Column <note>original: *Irdjâl el-‘Amûd*</note> in Nablus. Excellent examples of large and complicated buildings are those of the Prophet Moses <note>original: *en-nabî Mûsâ*</note> and Ali son of Elim <note>original: *Alî ibn ‘(E)lêm*; located north of Jaffa</note>.

The shrine of Sheikh I‘mar <note>This name is a local corruption of the name "Omar"</note>, the son of Sheikh Saleh, is situated on the mountain where the village of Bet Duqqu is built. The sanctuary consists of three rooms, a cistern, and an open area to the north of the rooms. This open area is surrounded by a massive wall and has a palm tree on its east side. The two western rooms are connected to each other. In the southern room are the tombs of the saint <term>original: *welî*</term> and his wife, while his son, Sheikh Dahud, is buried in the northern room. These tombs were decorated with henna and red ochre <term>original: *ḥinnâ* and *maghrî*</term>. On each side of the door that leads from this room to the open courtyard, we see a tomb. The eastern one covers the remains of Sheikh Qasim, the son of Ali, who was the son of Marar, while the western tomb belongs to this same Marar, the...

## II. Shrines without a tomb

These are sanctuaries built in a village that bear the name of a <term>jami</term> <note>original: djâmi'; a mosque</note> <note>The author notes that this is not an absolute rule, as some mosques, like the Jami el-Amari in Deir Aban, do contain tombs.</note>, such as the Jami el-Arba'in (in Isawiya), Jami Omar ibn al-Khattab <note>This was likely a church that was later converted into a mosque.</note> (in Sur Bahir <note>The name Sur Bahir is also sometimes pronounced Surbahil.</note>), Jami el-Uzer <note>Likely referring to Ezra or Lazarus; this also was likely a converted church.</note> (in el-Qaryeh), and others.

The Jami el-Uzer lies to the east of the French Benedictine church and convent. The shrine consists of an open courtyard, to the south of which there is a <term>rawaq</term> <note>original: rawâq; a portico or arched gallery</note> with two arches and a prayer room containing two beautifully decorated <term>mihrabs</term> <note>original: mihrâbs; prayer niches that indicate the direction of Mecca</note>. To the west of the open court is a small room where the dead are ritually washed before burial. A spring and a palm tree are found in the open courtyard. Around the two prayer niches of the prayer room and around the one in the portico, there are handprints and depictions of palm branches, some of which have nine leaves and others seven.

The local people are well aware that the holy man for whom the mosque is named was not buried here. They explain the connection of his name to the place by the fact that during his lifetime he was so pious and honored that a prayer niche was erected in every place where he was believed to have prayed—and he never missed any of the five daily prayers. Later, a mosque was built around these sites. This explanation was told to me by the <term>mukhtar</term> <note>original: muhtâr; the village chief or headman</note> of...

The names given to mosques in Sur Bahir and by the people of Soba are commonly associated with the Caliph <term>Omar ibn al-Khattab</term> <note>original: 'Omar ibn el-H̲aṭṭâb; the second Caliph of Islam (r. 634–644 AD)</note> and <term>Sultan Ibrahim al-Adhami</term>¹ <note>original: es-sulṭân Ibrâhîm el-Adhamî; an early Sufi saint and former King of Balkh who lived as an ascetic</note>. Many mosques bearing the name of the Caliph are called *Umari*. This may account for some of these sites, but, of course, it cannot explain all the <term>mosques</term> <note>original: djawâmi', plural of djâmi'</note> of this category. In many cases, it is believed that the holy man lived, taught, or appeared after his death in that specific spot, and therefore a mosque was built in his memory. A third explanation was given to me by the <term>mukhtar</term> <note>original: muh̲târ; the head or leader of a village</note> of Anata. He said that every time a new mosque is built, it is dedicated to a saint, who is not necessarily chosen from among the most important ones. However, the present writer believes that the basis of such a dedication is a legend connecting the man of God in question with that specific locality.

The following stories will serve as illustrations.

In the room known by the name <term>The Mourning of Jacob</term> <note>original: ḥuzn Ya'qûb</note>, situated in the shrine of <term>Our Lady the Green</term> <note>original: sittnâ el-H̲aḍrâ; a site in Nablus</note> in Nablus, Jacob is supposed to have mourned for the death of his beloved son Joseph.

<term>Uzair</term> <note>original: El-'Uzêr; often identified with the biblical Ezra</note> came to el-Qaryeh to worship Almighty God. He fastened his donkey to a pillar beside the spring and prayed. His devotion was performed with such intensity that it lasted one hundred years, yet he thought it had only been a few minutes. As he turned back to the place where he had fastened his animal, he found that only the skeleton of the donkey remained.²

<term>Sheikh el-Qatrawani</term> <note>original: Es-šêh̲ el-Qaṭrâwânî</note> lived in the village of Qatrah, north of Gaza. According to one version of the story, he left his village—since he could not fulfill his religious duties there—and came to the lonely...

...the spot of Dahrit Hammudeh,¹ a high hill between Bir Zeit and Atarah. Here he lived in a state of prayer and self-denial. According to another version of the story, when his body was being carried for burial, he flew up off the shoulders of the pallbearers and descended onto the hill where his shrine stands today.²

In the room leading to the so-called Stables of Solomon, "the crib of Christ" (<term>the bed of our Lord Jesus</term> <note>original: srîr saiydnâ ‘Isâ</note>) is shown to visitors. It is told that the Virgin Mary used to place her child here.

Beneath the Holy Rock of the **"Mosque of Omar"** <note>The Dome of the Rock in Jerusalem</note>, visitors are shown specific spots where David, Solomon, Abraham, Elijah, and Muhammad are believed to have prayed. Each of these locations is considered holy.

Near some shrines of this first group, a <term>mosque</term> <note>original: djâmi‘</note> has been built recently, bearing the name of the holy person honored nearby. However, such a mosque serves only for regular prayer, while all traditional honors and rituals continue to be directed toward the old shrine. Examples of this include the shrines of Sheikh Jarrah and Sa‘d and Sa‘id³ (both located in Jerusalem).

Hitherto we have only dealt with shrines of this class where it is absolutely certain—according to both general belief and outward appearance—that no tomb exists. However, there is a subdivision of this class that acts as a link between this and the previous group. These are sanctuaries where no tomb is visible and where there is no external sign suggesting a tomb could be there, yet local tradition insists the saint was buried on-site, either beneath the building or in a cave that was later sealed. Such sanctuaries include el-Qatrawani, Sheikh Ahmad el-Karaki (in Qastal), Sheikh Husen (in Bet Surik), Sheikh Abu Isma‘il (in Bet Likia), Sheikh Abdallah (in Qubebeh), and others.

The shrine of Sheikh Abu Isma‘il, which lies in the center of the village, consists of two rooms. The front room serves as a <term>guest-house</term> <note>original: maḍâfeh</note>, while the second is the actual shrine of the saint. No tomb is visible anywhere. However, it is said that the <term>saint</term> <note>original: welî</note> is buried in a cave lying beneath the shrine. In the middle of the guest-house is the <term>fireplace</term> <note>original: udjâq</note> where coffee is prepared. In the outer courtyard...

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

An example of this <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of sites where a saint is believed to be buried despite the lack of a visible tomb.</note> is a *sidreh* (a lote tree <note>original: *sidreh*; the *Ziziphus spina-christi* tree, often considered sacred in local folklore</note>) which provides a protective shadow, where guests gather during the summer months.

Another category includes the group to which the *Qubbet el-Arba‘in*¹ ("The Dome of the Forty") belongs. Every Muslim knows that "the Forty" <note>A group of forty saints or martyrs common in Islamic hagiography.</note> were not buried in this sanctuary; nevertheless, a long, rectangular stone frame in the middle of the floor, running east to west, serves as a symbolic tomb.

### III. A tomb without a building

There is hardly a village that does not possess at least one such *wali* <note>original: *welî*; a Muslim saint or "protector"</note>. In some places—such as Surbahir and Jericho—this type of shrine is by far the most common. These holy places may consist of a single tomb or a whole set of graves. Where several tombs are found side-by-side, the individuals buried there generally belong to the same "holy family." Examples of this include the *shuyukh* <note>original: *šyûh*; the plural of sheikh, meaning elders or leaders</note> of the al-Ja‘abri family² (Hebron), Hajj Ibayd (St. John), the Men of Sufah (Dayr Ghassaneh³), Sheikh Abu Yamin (Bayt ‘Anan), the Martyrs (*ash-Shuhada* of Hebron), and the Holy Warriors (*al-Mujahidin*, the fighters in the holy war, in Ramleh). In many cases, the descendants of these saints are still living.

The shrine of Sheikh Abu Yamin is surrounded by an enclosing wall. The tombs of the Sheikh and his son are inside the *maqam* <note>A shrine or sacred station</note>, while the graves of his descendants are in the open courtyard surrounding the building, within the wall. A pomegranate tree, a palm tree, and a fig tree belong to the saint. It is said that he is often seen flying while his band of musicians is playing. According to local belief, he and all his descendants were chosen men of God.

With the exception of a few such places—like those of the Martyrs and the Holy Warriors—most examples of this class are from more recent times. They generally fall into one of the following categories:

1. A living sheikh of a holy family dies. His tomb receives roughly the same honors as those of his ancestors. Examples include Sheikh Bhet, Sheikh Hilu, and Sheikh Saleh of the ad-Dawayri family (Surbahir).

³ Information provided by Omar Effendi al-Barghouti.

<summary>This page describes different types of Palestinian shrines, focusing on symbolic tombs like the "Dome of the Forty" and open-air graves belonging to "holy families" or recently deceased sheikhs.</summary>
<keywords>Qubbet el-Arba'in, lote tree, wali, maqam, holy families, Hebron, Sheikh Abu Yamin, Surbahir, Palestinian folklore, Muslim saints</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of holy tombs without formal buildings, specifically looking at how certain graves in communal cemeteries become shrines and how new holy sites are identified through visions.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

These *shuyukh* <note>original: *šyûḫ*; plural of sheikh, meaning elders or saints</note> of the ad-Dawari family are buried in the common western cemetery of the village. Their tombs are enclosed by a rectangular wall which is broken in many places. The most important of them are Sheikh al-Neni and Abu Mita. Although their tombs are smaller than the three mentioned above, they enjoy greater respect and honor. All except the tomb of al-Neni have stones at the head and foot and a niche in which oil lamps are lit. The newer tombs are decorated with henna <note>original: *ḥinnâ*</note> and red lead <note>original: *sîrâqûn*; a bright red mineral pigment</note>. Broken oil jars and oil bottles are scattered around the tombs.

2. If a famous holy man or a dervish <note>original: *derwîš*; a member of a Muslim mystical/Sufi order</note> dies—one who had founded or was a prominent member of a *tariqa* <note>original: *ṭarîqah*; a Sufi spiritual path or order</note>, or who used to heal the sick during his life—his tomb tends to become sacred and he himself is considered a *wali* <note>original: *welî*; a saint or "friend of God"</note>. The best example of this is Sheikh Abu Halawi.

He is buried in the cemetery which runs along the east wall of Jerusalem. The tomb is situated on rising ground to the north of St. Stephen's Gate <note>Also known as Lions' Gate.</note>. The lamp <note>original: *qandîl*</note>, described by Kahle <gloss>¹</gloss> and shown in the photograph which he took of the place, was stolen during the war. Flowers are very often placed on the tomb. This sheikh was highly honored during his life. Sick people and those in trouble used to seek healing and help from him. While his dead body was being carried to the place of burial, it supposedly flew away and descended on the spot where the soul of the saint chose to have his remains interred. His tomb is honored, and the sick very often tear a piece from their garments and bind it around the headstone <note>This practice, known as *ma'aqad*, is a way of "binding" the illness to the saint's protection or leaving a petition for healing.</note>.

3. A night vision experienced by a villager may reveal that a specific place, in or near his own neighborhood, is sacred as the burial place of a saint. The people of the village will then probably build a tomb there, as was done in the case of Sheikh Suwan.

A peasant of Sur Bahir, who lived in a small cave, lost one member of his family after another through death. No one could explain his misfortune. One night a venerable sheikh appeared to him in a vision and, reproaching him severely, said:

> "Why do you not respect my home? If you will not atone for your past neglect, I will cause the remainder of your family to die."

---
¹ *Palästinajahrbuch* <note>original: *PJ*; the German "Palestine Yearbook"</note> Volume VI, page 67.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how holy sites are identified, transitioning from a story about the discovery of Sheikh Suwan's burial place to a technical description of the various types of simple enclosures built around such tombs.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

The frightened man asked: “Who are you, my Lord?” The reply was: “I am Sheikh Suwan <note>original: *šêḫ Ṣuwân*</note> and I am buried here.” Early the next morning, a tomb was built, the cave was cleaned, and the family moved elsewhere.

In many cases, a low circular stone enclosure, or *huwetiyeh* <note>original: *huwêtîyeh*</note>, surrounds the tomb. Even in high structures, it is never vaulted. Generally, it is constructed of simple, unhewn stones, as in the following cases:

| | |
|---|---|
| Sheikh Sabbah <note>original: *Eš-šêḫ Ṣabbâḥ*</note> | in Jericho, |
| Sheikh Zughbeh <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Zughbeh*</note> | in Jericho, |
| Sheikh Hamdallah <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Ḥamdallah*</note> | in Biddu <note>original: *Biddû*</note>, |
| Sheikh Imbarak <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Imbârak*</note> | in Beit Iksa <note>original: *Bêt Iksâ*</note>, |
| Sheikh Teiri <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Ṭêrî*</note> | in Deir Ghassaneh <note>original: *Dêr Ghassâneh*</note>,¹ |
| Sheikh el-Habil <note>original: *eš-šêḫ el-Habîl*</note> | in Deir Ghassaneh. |

Sometimes this enclosure is built more solidly, using hewn stones and mortar. The walls may be high and surround the entire group of objects: the tomb, trees, the *mihrab* <note>original: *miḥrâb*; a prayer niche</note>, and an open court. This open space surrounding the tomb is often paved with stone slabs, especially in the case of important saints <note>original: *welîs*</note> like el-Uzeir <note>original: *el-’Uzêr*</note> (Figure 2, Plate II), el-Mufaddil <note>original: *el-Mufaḍḍil*</note>, and al-Mansuri <note>original: *al-Manṣûrî*</note> (all in Awarta <note>original: *‘Awartah*</note>). In the case of al-Mansuri, the wall surrounding the tomb is constructed on three sides with beautiful small vaults (Figure 3, Plate III).

In the case of simple enclosures, an opening like a door is sometimes left on one side. This door is often made of two large side stones set upright with another on top, and it is rather low. Although visitors are supposed to enter through this door, they seldom do.

Many Bedouin saints <note>original: *welîs*</note> are of this type.

Sheikh Sabbah and ez-Zughbeh <gloss>²</gloss>—both in Jericho—are good illustrations of the type mentioned above. The first has a very low door, while in the second, a breach in the low wall serves the purpose. A visitor must crawl if he wishes to enter through the door of the enclosure of Sabbah in the traditional, orthodox manner; <gloss>³</gloss> therefore, everyone prefers to jump over the wall.

In most cases where a tomb has an enclosure, it is said that at different times people proposed to build a *maqam* <note>original: *maqâm*; a shrine building</note>, but the saint

---

¹ Information from Omar Effendi el-Barghuti <note>original: *el-Barghûṭî*</note>.
² Kahle, *Palästinajahrbuch* <note>original: *PJ*</note>, 1911, pp. 88. Not Zu‘beh but Zughbeh.
³ Crawling through the door, and thus humbling oneself, is regarded with more favor by the sheikh than jumping over the wall.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of local traditions regarding the construction of saints' tombs, specifically focusing on instances where saints are said to have resisted the building of structures over their graves, and begins a new section describing sacred caves.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

The saint himself refused to have a tomb and pulled down during the night what was built during the day, throwing the stones far away.

The tomb of Sheikh Darwish <note>original: *eš-šêh Darwîš*</note> of Beit Surik <note>original: *Bêt Sûrîk*</note> is surrounded by a high enclosing wall. Every time the peasants of the village tried to build him a suitable tomb and to put a roof on the four walls, the saint showed his dissatisfaction by pulling down their work, until they were obliged to give up their idea. The same is said of el-Mansuri <note>original: *el-Manṣûrî*</note>, el-Mufaddil <note>original: *el-Mufaḍḍil*</note>, and others.

In some cases, we are shown a tomb, but exact investigation will fail to disclose anything—even a heap of stones—which might mark the existence of a grave. Under the terebinth tree <note>A species of sumac tree common in the region.</note> of Sheikh Mustafa <note>original: *eš-šêh Muṣṭafâ*</note> (Soba <note>original: *Ṣôbâ*</note>), irregularly scattered stones were said to represent the tomb of the saint <note>original: *welî*</note>. But I could not distinguish any characteristic of a tomb. The scattered stones were shaped like ordinary field stones. <note>See McCown, in the work cited <note>original: *op. cit.*</note>, p. 56.</note>

### IV. A cave with or without a tomb

There are two types of sacred caves: those with a tomb and those without one. The first type is rare, and its best representative is Sheikh es-Sidri <note>original: *eš-šêh eṣ-Ṣidrî*</note>. <note>According to some, his first name is Muhammed; according to others, it is Abdallah.</note> A careful description of this sanctuary may suffice to explain this point more exactly. The saint's shrine <note>original: *welî*</note> is situated in the ruin of Deir es-Sidd <note>original: *Dêr es-Sidd*</note>, south of Sheikh Abd es-Salam <note>original: *eš-šêh 'Abd es-Salâm*</note>, on the top of a low hill. In the ruin, there are many caves <note>These are used at present for cattle.</note> carved into the rock, with stairs leading down to them. Many cisterns, mostly broken, are scattered here and there. Bir ez-Zqaq <note>original: *Bîr ez-Zqâq*</note> provides water for shepherds. The saint’s tomb is situated in a rather large, natural cave with a low roof. The entrance is built of good, carved stones and surrounded by a square outer court, which lies lower than the western part. There is no door to close the cave. At the north-north-east end, we find the tomb erected on an elevated square platform with two pillar fragments on the front corners. On both of them, as well as around the tomb, we find oil lamps, candle stubs, matches, broken jars, bottles, etc. Besides this tomb, there is no other building. At present, no tree is connected with this sanctuary, but formerly a large terebinth tree adorned the empty space.

<summary>This page describes Palestinian folklore regarding saints who miraculously prevent the construction of roofs or tombs over their graves and provides a detailed archaeological description of the sacred cave-shrine of Sheikh es-Sidri.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of sacred caves in Palestine, providing specific examples of shrines where caves and tombs are associated with famous saints, including those shared by different religious traditions.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

Other examples of this type are: Sheikh Ahmad el-Gharib <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Aḥmad el-Gharîb*</note> (north of el-Mujeidil near Nazareth); Sheikh Salman el-Farsi <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Salmân el-Fârsî*</note> (on the Mount of Olives), who used to have his tomb in a cave; and the Lady Rab’ah <note>original: *es-sayidî er-Râbʿah*</note>.

A beautiful domed shrine <note>original: *qubbeh*</note>¹ was built by the cave of Sheikh Salman el-Farsi.

Formerly, a narrow passage <note>original: *dahlîz*</note> used to lead to the cave. In front of the pretty shrine, several trees were planted: a cypress, a pine, two pomegranates, and an olive tree. A cistern is also connected with the place.

The Lady Rab’ah² (not Rahba, as stated by Meistermann,³ nor Rahibet as stated in Baedeker⁴)—who has her sanctuary near the Place of Ascension <note>original: *el-Maṣʿad*</note> and below the As’adiyyeh Sufi hospice <note>original: *Zâwiet el-Asʿadîyeh*</note>—is honored by Muslims, Christians, and Jews. The Christians and Jews do not revere her as Rab’ah,⁵ but as Saint Pelagia⁶ and the prophetess Huldah,⁷ respectively. Twelve steps lead from the upper room to the cave where the tomb is shown, all carved into the solid rock. A small room near the grave is said to be the place where she used to perform her daily prayers. It is said that Rab’ah used to kneel a thousand times daily, saying: "I ask for no reward, but only to please Almighty God." In the upper room, there is a cistern whose water is said to have a particularly pleasant taste.

There are some caves where tombs are found in front of them, and these two features are closely linked with each other. It is said that the saint <note>original: *welî*</note> has occasionally been seen walking from his tomb to the cave. As illustrations, we may cite Sheikh Ahmad el-Huways <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Aḥmad el-Ḥuwês*</note>, which has already been described, and Sheikh Asfur <note>original: *eš-šêḫ ʿAsfûr*</note>⁸ to the south of Deir Ghassaneh <note>original: *Dêr Ghassâneh*</note>.⁹ In the first case, all honors are paid to the cave, where it is supposed that the soul of the saint lives.

The second category—a cave without a tomb, and supposed to be inhabited by a saint—has many examples in Palestine. These caves are either natural formations or rock-cut tombs. They are situated on the edge of a village, or outside in the fields. They are rarely found within residential areas. Among caves which are considered to be the habitation of saints are:¹

| Saint or Cave Name | Location |
| :--- | :--- |
| el-Hadr <note>Saint George/Elijah</note> | Mount Carmel,² |
| Sheikh Ali Qaitun <note>original: *eš-šêḫ ‘Alî Qaitûn*</note> | Hebron, |
| Sheikh Ghreyib <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Ghreyib*</note> | el-Hadr, near Beit Jala, |
| The Sheikh's Cave <note>original: *Mghâret eš-šêḫ*</note> | Kufr ‘Aqab, |
| The Forty Men <note>original: *Irdjâl el-Arb‘în*</note> | Biddu, |
| The Forty Warriors <note>original: *el-Arb‘în Mghâzî*</note> | Beit Likia, |
| Sheikh Isa <note>original: *eš-šêḫ ‘Îsâ*</note> | Beit Likia, |
| The Men of Abu Tuh <note>original: *Irdjâl Abû Ṭûḥ*</note> | Beit Likia, |
| Sheikh Yusif <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Yûsif*</note> | Beit Hanina, |
| Sheikh Abdallah <note>original: *eš-šêḫ ‘Abdallah*</note> | Mizpah of Samuel. |

Six³ steps lead down to the Cave of the Forty Men <note>original: *Mghârit Irdjâl el-Arb‘în*</note>⁴ of Biddu. The cave is small, somewhat round, with a low roof. During the winter months, part of it is full of water. In front of this cave, two oaks, an olive, and a terebinth tree grow side by side. In their shade, the *hatib* <term>ḥaṭîb: the religious leader or preacher of the village</term> teaches the children. No tomb is attached to this "Forty" <note>referring to the forty saints</note> anywhere.

The "Men of Abu Tuh" <note>original: *Irdjâl Abû Ṭûḥ*</note>, whose exact number is unknown, inhabit a small cave situated in a rather large grove.⁵ The entrance to the cave <term>mghârah: cave</term> is so small that no one can enter. Broken jars, oil bottles, oil lamps, and burned incense are scattered around the opening. These saints are very highly respected, and no one dares to cut even a twig from their groves.

The different names used for caves, irrespective of whether they belong to this group or not, are *mghârah*, *ghâr*, *šqâf*, and *ḥikf*. These different expressions do not mean the same thing. The expression *ghâr* is used only for cave-like cisterns, which are situated

in the *maqâm* <term>maqâm: a Muslim shrine or sanctuary, often marking the site of a saint's tomb</term>. They are always treated as the most holy places, and nobody dares to enter them. A *ḥadîth* <term>ḥadîth: a record of the traditions or sayings of the Prophet Muhammad</term> says: "Every prophet has a cave assigned to him" <note>original Arabic: *mâ minn nabiyn illa walahu ghâr*</note>. In many cases (for example, the Prophet Samuel <note>original: *en-nabî Ṣamwîl*</note>), the visible tomb found in the shrine is said not to be the true one but to sit above the actual tomb, which is in the *ghâr* <term>ghâr: a cave or grotto</term> and cannot be seen or visited. *Hikf*¹ mostly refers to a small cave covered by a large stone slab, like the shrine of Sheikh al-Umari <note>original: *eš-šêḫ el-‘Umarî*</note> east of Hizma. The other expressions generally denote ordinary caves. As has been already observed, small, low, vaulted rooms are sometimes called caves (*mghârah*). I encountered this peculiarity in Awarta. In Soba, the "Forty Warriors" <note>original: *Arb‘în Mghâzî*</note> are represented by a small, shallow hole in the old masonry. This was also called a *mghârah*. Here, lights and incense are burned.²

Of course, all honors are paid to such a cave just as to any shrine. It is lit up, offerings and even sheep may be vowed to it, a pious woman will never enter any of them while she is ritually impure, and no animals are allowed to defile the holy place by entering.³ Not infrequently, the cave is connected with a tree, a grove, or a well, as in the following cases:

| Saint or Site Name | Location |
| :--- | :--- |
| Sheikh Ahmad Ghreyib <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Aḥmad Ghreyib*</note> | in el-Mujeidil near Nazareth,⁴ |
| Sheikh Musa <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Mûsâ*</note> | in Harbata,⁵ |
| The Men of Abu Tuh <note>original: *Irdjâl Abu Ṭuḥ*</note> | in Beit Likia, |
| The Forty Men <note>original: *Irdjâl el-Arb‘în*</note> | in Biddu. |

The following story illustrates how a simple cave may eventually come to receive the honors of a shrine.

The Mohammedan leper Djum‘ah⁶ (Friday), from Abu Dis, while in the leper asylum "Jesus-Hilf" <note>Jesus-Help</note> in Jerusalem, used to live during the—

<summary>This page explains the religious status of sacred caves (ghâr) in Palestinian Islam, noting they are often considered the true resting places of prophets and are subject to strict purity rules. It also lists specific sites where caves are linked to saints and begins a narrative about a leper named Djum‘ah.</summary>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Djum’ah, a guard who witnessed spiritual manifestations in a cave, and shifts focus to broader categories of sacred sites, including famous caves in Jerusalem and Bethlehem, and the introduction of stone enclosures.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

During the summer months, he lived in a tent in the institution's large garden to guard it from thieves. One Thursday evening, while he was saying his prayers, he clearly heard the prayers and religious music of dervishes <term>dervishes: members of a Muslim religious order, often known for their ritual devotion, songs, or dances</term>. Djum’ah immediately left his tent and walked slowly toward the place where the *madîḥ* <note>original: *madîḥ* (religious song)</note> was coming from: a cave where a greenish light was burning. Djum’ah did not dare to enter. Staying outside the cave, he waited until these mysterious visitors had finished their prayers. Afterward, he noticed the same thing happened every Thursday evening (*lêlatu-d-djum’ah* <note>literal: "the night of the gathering/Friday," as the Islamic day begins at sunset on Thursday</note>). From that time on, he kept the cave and its surroundings clean, believing that *ṣullâḥ*^1 <term>ṣullâḥ: pious or righteous men</term> lived or gathered every Thursday evening in this cave to perform their prayers. Djum’ah was too poor to provide a lamp <note>original: "offer a light"</note> every week, as common belief suggested he should have done.^2

The preceding story also illustrates the fact that many places are considered sacred by only a few private individuals; their reputation has not yet spread to the wider public.

We must not overlook the most important sacred cave for Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note> in Palestine, located beneath the Holy Rock in the Dome of the Rock <note>original: "Mosque of Omar"</note>. The various parts of this cave, which are deeply revered by every Muslim, will be described in the section dealing with sacred stones. Even Christians believe in certain holy caves, such as the "Milk Grotto"^3 in Bethlehem. Tradition claims that several drops of the Virgin Mary's milk happened to fall there while she was nursing the Christ Child. The healing powers of this place will be described later.

## V. A simple stone enclosure

Such an enclosure may be very small, with a diameter of no more than 30 to 40 centimeters, though they are sometimes much larger. The circular enclosure (*ḥuwêṭiyeh* or *ḥuwêṭah*^4)—sometimes also called a *ḥôḍ*^5 or...

---

^1 When no specific saint can be named, vague expressions like *ṣullâḥ* (pious ones), *awliâ* (saints), *darâwîs* (dervishes), or *a‘djâm* (strangers or holy men) are used.
^2 The holiness of this cave has been forgotten since Djum‘ah left the institution long ago.
^3 This cave will be described later.
^4 The common expression *hauwaṭak billâh*, "I ask God to be a wall around you" (meaning "may God protect you"), comes from the same Arabic root *hauwaṭa*. See the dictionary *Muḥîṭ el-Muḥîṭ*, volume 1, page 477.
^5 *Ḥôḍ* literally means a watering trough.

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of sacred sites in Palestine, moving from sacred caves to simple stone enclosures known as ṣîreh, explaining their construction, the rituals associated with them, and their occasional transformation into more formal buildings.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

A stone enclosure <note>original: *ṣîreh*</note> was, in every instance known to me, made of simple, unshaped stones set irregularly side by side with no attempt at symmetry. Often, a gap is left in the circle to serve as a doorway. In the case of the Sheikhah Imbarakeh <note>original: *eš-šêḫah Imbârakeh*</note> of Kalandia, the female saint is said to gather clean pieces of broken pottery and arrange them as a small wall <note>original: *huwêṭah*</note>, leaving a small gap for the entrance. Since the war, it has been observed that she does not replace the old pieces with new ones as she always used to do. Somewhere in the inner wall of these circles, there is usually a niche <note>original: *ṭâqah*</note> where oil lamps and matches are placed, and where incense is burned.

In Biddu, we find the western cemetery on a small elevation. At its north-eastern corner is a small, crudely built enclosure in which Sheikh Ali et-Tallal <note>original: *eš-šêḫ 'Alî eṭ-Tallâl*</note> is honored. Near this holy spot grow an olive tree and a fig tree, and a cistern was recently discovered there. All of these belong to the saint. Two sides of the elevation are made of old masonry. This place is a good example of a sacred enclosure combined with trees, a cistern, and ruins.

In the case of Sheikh Fredj <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Frêdj*</note> of Bet Hanina, an old petroleum tin partly covers the sacred enclosure to protect the light from being blown out. The fact that saints <term>awliâ: Muslim saints or "friends of God"</term> belonging to this class are not kept clean and are not well cared for suggests that they are not as highly honored as others. We hear of cases where villages have tried to erect a shrine <term>maqâm: a holy site or sanctuary, often housing the tomb of a saint</term> for one or another Sheikh of this group, but where the holy men prevented the completion of the work in the same way we saw with Sheikh Huwes <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Ḥuwês*</note>. Some of the sanctuaries belonging to this group have been transferred to the more formal category of buildings by the erection of a structure in place of the stone enclosure. The Mosques of our Lady Aisha <note>original: *Masâdjid sittnâ 'Âišeh*</note> in the neighborhood of the Prophet Moses <note>original: *nabî Mûsâ*</note> shrine illustrate this point. A simple, square, handsome building—with the northern side completely open and the east and west sides partly open—now stands on the site of the old enclosure. No tomb, cistern, or tree is connected with this place.

---

<note>1</note> *Ṣîreh* actually means an enclosure for cattle. Arabic dictionaries do not give this word, nor the word *ḥôḍ* (basin/enclosure), the specific meaning used in the text: a sacred enclosure.
<note>2</note> The name is not *eṭ-Ṭalalî* as recorded in McCown, page 59. "The depressed spot" refers to the enclosure itself and not the grave of the saint <note>original: *welî*</note>. Sheikh Hasan Abu-l-Alamen of Biddu is not mentioned in the list provided by McCown.

A few meters to the north of el-Mansuri (at Awartah), one is shown a very crudely built enclosure said to mark the tomb of Husha, the son of el-Mansuri. A large windproof kerosene lantern is placed in the center. Regarding this saint and the other important figures of Awartah, it is said that they do not want any buildings to be erected over their tombs.

Interestingly, even Christians have similar enclosures that they respect and honor. On the left side of the stony road leading from Beit Jala to el-Khadr—passing el-Marah and going through esh-Sharafeh—just before reaching the latter, there is a small enclosure where a passer-by may throw offerings of bread, figs, or grapes. It was always kept clean. The peasants of Beit Jala tell how when St. George (known locally as el-Khadr) came from the north to the village of el-Khadr (where a church is built in his honor), he walked with gigantic strides, one of which happened to land on this spot.<margin>¹ This is the only enclosure I know of that is honored by Christians.</margin>

### VI. A watercourse (spring or cistern)

We know that nearly all the springs of Palestine are thought to be haunted by spirits. These spirits are supposed to belong to the class of demons. <note>original: 1; referencing Taufiq Canaan, "Haunted Springs and Water Demons," in the *Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society* (JPOS), vol. I, p. 153, and his work *Aberglaube* (Superstition).</note> But at the same time, there are watercourses definitely assigned to some holy man. Their number is much less than those inhabited by demons. Paul Kahle <note>original: 2; *PJ* (Palästinajahrbuch), vol. VI, p. 93 f.</note> thinks that two conditions must be fulfilled to make a spring holy: 1) the source should be somewhat mysterious, such as a dark canal or a large cavity; and 2) the spring must play an important role in the water supply of the nearby village. Although many springs fulfill both conditions, the majority fulfill only one, whereas many springs inhabited by demons satisfy these same two conditions.

For our purpose, it is necessary to study specifically the differences between sacred springs and those haunted by *jinn* <note>original: *djinn*; supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology that can be benevolent or malevolent</note>. The following is a comparative table of the differences:

->Springs inhabited by<-

| Holy Men | Jinn (demons) |
|----------|----------------|
| 1. May be situated in the neighborhood of a *weli* <note>original: *welî*; a saint or a saint's shrine</note>. | 1. Never. |
| 2. Prayer and religious music may be heard, especially on Thursday evening. | 2. Never. |
| 3. A light with a greenish flame may be observed appearing and disappearing. | 3. Never. |
| 4. The water may be used to treat different ailments. | 4. In exceptional cases. |
| 5. The inhabiting saint appears as a venerable sheikh <note>original: *šêḫ*</note> (wearing a white, red, or green headdress) or a pious *sheikha* <note>original: *šêḫah*; a female saint or elder</note>. | 5. The *jinn* take the shape of an animal, a Black man, a monster, or a bride. |

---

1 Canaan, *Haunted Springs and Water Demons, JPOS*, vol. I, p. 153 etc. and *Superstition* (original: *Aberglaube*).
2 *PJ* <note>Palästinajahrbuch</note>, vol. VI, p. 93 f.
