| Holy Men | Jinn (demons) |
|----------|----------------|
| 6. People who accidentally fall into a cistern or well inhabited by a holy man are generally protected from any injury, especially if they call for the help of the sheikh <note>original: šêh; an Islamic religious leader or elder</note>. | 6. On the contrary, the jinn <note>original: djinn; supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology that can be benevolent or malevolent</note> may even injure the victim. |
| 7. The saint enjoys hearing the name of God and prayers. | 7. The demon trembles before these powerful words and is usually driven away by them. He may injure the person who uttered them in revenge. |
| 8. A miraculous sign may appear. | 8. Never. |

While these statements are true for all watercourses directly or indirectly connected to a shrine, they also apply to those which—though having no connection at all with sanctuaries or the tombs of saints—are nevertheless believed to be inhabited by the spirit of a holy person. I will focus only on this latter category. Often such water sources have a tree growing nearby; since both may be holy, it is sometimes difficult to know which is more significant: the watercourse or the tree.

El-Matba'ah <note>original: El-Maṭba' ah; located between Sheikh Ibreik and Tell esh-Shammam</note> is a marshy pool said to cure all sorts of rheumatic ailments. No ritually impure <note>original: nidjsih</note> women may approach the holy spot. Once, a barren woman made a pilgrimage to this place, hoping to find help. It happened that at the moment of her arrival, her period unexpectedly started <note>original: itwassaḥ râshâ; literally "her head was soiled"</note>. Being pious, she waited far away from El-Matba'ah until she was purified <note>original: ṭihrat</note>; then she took some mud and rubbed her body with it. Scarcely a year had passed before she conceived and bore a child.

Ain esh-Sharif <note>original: Ên eš-Šarîf; "The Noble Spring"</note>, located just above Ain esh-Shamiyeh <note>original: 'ên eš-Šâmiyeh; "The Damascene Spring"</note> in Kolonia, is a newly discovered spring and a newly found shrine <note>original: welî; the tomb or spirit of a saint</note>. A few weeks after the spring was discovered, a sheikh <note>original: šêḥ</note> wearing a green turban appeared in a night vision to Muhammad Ali. He ordered Ali to tell the inhabitants of the village that they should not defile his shrine, the newly

# CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

discovered spring. “What is your name, my sheikh?” <note>original: šêḫ; a title for a religious leader or a spiritual being</note> asked Mohammed. “Al-Sharif” <note>original: Eš-Šarîf; meaning "The Noble One"</note> was the answer.

Some waters derive their power from the fact that they mix once a year with the water of the holy well Zamzam in Mecca.^1 At such a period the water is curative. Wells of this type with a special reputation are: Spring of the Mother of the Steps <note>original: ‘Ên Imm ed-Daradj</note> in Siloam,^2 the Bath of Healing <note>original: Ḥammâm eš-Šifâ</note>^3 in Jerusalem,^4 and the cistern in the shrine of al-Nubani in Nablus.

This overflow of the water of Zamzam takes place generally on the tenth of *Muharram*, which is also known by the name *Ashura*. It is thought to be the anniversary of the death of Husayn, the son of Fatimah and grandson of the Prophet Mohammed. The connection between the overflowing of the spring and the memory of Husayn is quite interesting. According to some, the water of this holy well at Mecca mixes on this day with all springs in Muslim countries, thus giving every Muslim the opportunity to drink from Zamzam.

The sanctity, as well as the curative action of other waters, is said to be derived from various holy figures: Job, Jesus, Saint George <note>original: el-Ḫaḍr; the "Green One," a figure often identified with St. George or Elijah</note>, the Lady Mary <note>original: Sittî Mariam</note>, and others. Springs connected with Job will be described later on. Since it is believed that Jesus sent the blind man—whom he healed by earth moistened with spittle^5—to the Spring of the Mother of the Almond Tree <note>original: ‘ên Imm el-Lôzeh</note>^6 to wash his eyes there,^7 some Christian women^8 believe that this water

At last a sorceress <note>original: sâḥreh</note> assured her that the inhabiting jinn had taken her jewels. She gave the wife a written talisman and ordered that for three days, one-third of the talisman should be burned in the bath each day. This was done and, behold, on the third day the wife of the official found her lost jewels in the place where she had left them. <meta>This page continues the discussion of water sources in Palestine believed to have supernatural properties, specifically focusing on those inhabited by holy figures or dual spirits (good and evil).</meta>

->Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society<-

has still the power to cure inflamed eyes. The Muslims of Nablus believe that Saint George <note>original: el-Ḥadr; the "Green One," a figure often identified with St. George or Elijah</note> takes a bath every Thursday evening in the basin found in the Bath of the Steps <note>original: Ḥammâm ed-Daradjeh</note>. This is why it is thought to be inhabited or visited by this holy man. The sick go there at this time to take a bath, burn incense, and light candles. In the Bath of Our Lady Mary <note>original: Ḥammâm Sittî Mariam</note>¹ (near St. Stephen's Gate, Jerusalem), women struggling with infertility bathe in the hope of becoming pregnant. It is believed that Saint Mary once took a bath in this place; so candles, oil, and flowers are offered as part of a vow.²

A connecting link between waters haunted by demons and those inhabited by saints is found in cases where people believe that both a good and a bad spirit haunt the same spring. This is a special characteristic of periodical <note>seasonal or intermittent</note> springs. Thus, the Bubbling Spring <note>original: ‘ên Fauwâr</note>³ is thought to be inhabited by a "free man" <note>original: hurr</note> (a master) and a "servant" <note>original: ‘abd; traditionally a spirit depicted as a Black slave</note>. The first is a white person and the other a Black person, as the words themselves indicate. The following are the springs⁴ which may be grouped in this class:⁵

*   **The Walnut Spring** <note>original: ‘Ên ed-Djôz</note> (Ramallah), inhabited by a white and a black sheep.
*   **The Spring of Artas** <note>original: ‘Ên Artâs</note> (Artas), inhabited by a white and a black sheep.
*   **Ona’s Well** <note>original: Bîr ‘Ônah</note> (Beit Jala), inhabited by Saint Mary and sometimes a servant <note>original: ‘abd</note>.
*   **The Spring of the Stone** <note>original: ‘Ên el-Ḥadjar</note> (Deir Ghassaneh), inhabited by the Faithful Lady <note>original: es-sitt Mu'mineh</note> and sometimes by a giant spirit <note>original: mârid</note>.

I have never heard of a spring inhabited by a being that might at times be a saint <note>original: welî</note> and at other times change into a genie <note>original: djinn</note>, as the scholar Samuel Curtiss was told regarding the hot springs of Zerqa Ma‘in. In all probability, this spring belongs to the group mentioned above and is supposed to be inhabited by two separate spirits—a good one and a bad one. They are distinct beings, and one never changes into the other.

It is often reported that these two classes of powerful, opposing spirits are continually fighting each other. In the case of the Bubbling Spring...

This goat-like hair was an inheritance from her mother, who was a female genie <note>original: djinnîyeh</note>.

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the dual spirits (the noble and the servant) who control the flow of water and provides a detailed list of holy springs and wells throughout Palestine.</meta>

We are told that when the noble spirit <note>original: ḥurr; literally "free man"</note> wins the victory, he allows the water to flow for the benefit of thirsty mankind. But it is not long before the servant spirit <note>original: ‘abd; traditionally depicted as a dark-skinned slave</note> recovers and resumes the battle. As soon as he overpowers the noble spirit, he shuts off this blessing of God and thus takes revenge on the human race. This opposition of

->good against evil,<-
->white against black,<-
->light against darkness,<-
->angels against devils,<-
->upper against lower world,<-
->God against Satan<-

is a very old idea in Semitic religions, and we could not have it better represented than in the current simple folklore of a Palestinian peasant <note>original: fellâḥ</note>.¹

| The Well of the Leaf <note>original: Bîr el-Waraqah</note> | Jerusalem | leads to paradise,³ |
| The Monk's Spring <note>original: 'Ên er-Râhib</note> | Nablus | monk,⁴ |
| Ein Karem <note>original: 'Ên Kârim</note> | Ein Karem <note>original: 'Ên Kârim</note> | the Virgin Mary, |
| The Well of Ona <note>original: Bîr 'Ônâ</note> | Beit Jala <note>original: Bêt Djâlâ</note> | the Virgin Mary, |
| The Spring of Gabrianus <note>original: 'Ên Kibriân</note> | West of Beit Jala <note>original: Bêt Djâlâ</note> | St. Gabrianus, |
| The Spring of the Mother of the Almond Tree <note>original: 'Ên Imm el-Lôzeh</note> | below Job’s Well <note>original: Bîr Ayûb</note> | cures eye troubles.⁵ |

Both Muslims <note>original: Mohammedans</note> and Christians believe that these saints try to save anyone who happens to fall into a well. The following story illustrates this. A child from Isawiya <note>original: Êsâwîyeh</note> happened to fall into a ruined cistern. Soon afterward, his parents pulled him out. The boy reported that two men came to his aid while he was falling and carried him gently to the bottom. One of them was a venerable old man; the other wore clothes similar to those of the villagers of the surrounding district and had two old-fashioned pistols in his belt.⁶

4 This spring stops flowing once a week on Sundays, as the monk is said to fulfill his religious duties on this day.
5 Samuel Ives Curtiss and Paul Kahle provide few examples of holy springs.
6 Other stories are provided in the *Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society* <note>original: JPOS</note>, in the place cited above <note>original: l. c.; Latin: loco citato</note>.

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of supernatural beings associated with water sources, specifically concluding a story about a dervish in a cistern before transitioning to the biblical precedents for sacred springs and beginning a new section on solitary holy trees.</meta>

The old people of the village remembered that a dervish of this description had fallen into the cistern many years before.<note>This refers to the story mentioned on the previous page regarding the identity of a spirit or person seen in the water.</note>

The belief in sacred springs, inhabited springs, and curative waters can be traced back to the Old and New Testaments. Naaman was cured of his leprosy by washing himself seven times in the Jordan River.<note>2 Kings 5:1–14</note> The blind man sent by Jesus to Siloam came back with his sight restored after he had washed his eyes.<note>John 9:6–7</note> The pool of Bethesda cured every disease, "for an angel went down at a certain season into the pool and troubled the water: whosoever then first after the troubling of the water stepped in was cured of whatever disease he had."<note>John 5:1–5</note> Names like "Spring of the Sun" <note>original: Ên Shemesh</note><note>Joshua 15:7</note> point to the fact that these springs were originally dedicated to gods.

## VII. A solitary tree

I do not propose to include under this heading any tree which—though situated in absolute solitude, far from any shrine or grave—nevertheless belongs to a saint <note>original: weli</note> who has a sanctuary somewhere in the vicinity. Thus, for example, the Sheikh Hamad <note>original: eš-šêḫ Ḥamad</note>, located in the middle of the village of Colonia <note>original: Kolôniâ</note>, has a tree on the opposite mountain to the southeast of the village, on the old road leading to Jerusalem. Another case is the Lady Badriyeh <note>original: el-Badrîyeh</note>.<note>A female saint mentioned earlier in the text.</note> I have already mentioned the various trees belonging to this holy woman.

---

<note>1</note> I will not describe here the "Springs of Retention" <note>original: ‘iûn (plural of ‘ên) el-ḥaṣr, referring to springs believed to cure the retention of urine</note>, since they are generally not connected with any shrine or the name of a saint <note>original: weli</note>. They are not revered religiously. I think that what Curtiss says about the stones which cure backache is truer of these springs: that their therapeutic use is based on the belief in a magic power—the supernatural powers of good spirits. For more on these springs, see *Superstition* <note>original: Aberglaube</note> and the *Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society* <note>original: JPOS</note>, as cited previously. Minute questioning of the people of Soba <note>original: Ṣôbâ</note> during my last visit resulted in them saying that the "Spring of Retention" of this village was also called "Moses’ Spring" <note>original: ‘ên Mûsâ</note>, and that they have sometimes seen two beautiful young ladies sitting beside the water and combing their hair. They disappeared as soon as they knew they were being watched. Some peasants attributed the name Moses to that of the Prophet Moses. If this is true, then the spring belongs to the category already mentioned, where both good and bad spirits haunt the same spring.

Only those trees will be described which, although considered to be the dwelling place of some saint, have absolutely no connection with any *shrine* <term>original: maqâm; a sanctuary or tomb of a holy person</term>. Jaussen <gloss>1</gloss> seems to include in this group trees which are connected with a holy spring and a holy *stone heap* <term>original: rudjm; a cairn of stones often marking a sacred spot</term>. I have tried to avoid such cases, since it is very difficult to say which of these features was originally considered sacred. There are several cases belonging to this group, and it is at times difficult to explain the reason why such trees were granted their sanctity. This question, which often arises, will be dealt with below in another context. Among sacred trees of this type, which receive honors like other *saints* <term>original: welîs; "friends of God" or holy men</term>, are: <gloss>2</gloss>

# CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

Other trees will be mentioned below. A few observations must still be made regarding some unique characteristics of these trees. The Tree of the Father of Fire <note>original: Šadjarat Abû Nâr</note> has a menstrual period <note>original: biṭḥid; "it menstruates"</note> every time she is irritated by a trespasser. A thick, viscous fluid is excreted. <gloss>1</gloss>

Under the Tree of Happiness <note>original: Šadjaret es-Sa'âdeh</note>, Sheikh Hasan al-Aruri was ordained as a leader <term>original: quṭub; literally a "pole" or spiritual axis in Sufi tradition</term> by several saints. This is, of course, a sufficient reason for a tree to be considered sacred. <gloss>2</gloss> It derives spiritual power from the man with whom it came in contact and is able to help those in need with this power. <gloss>3</gloss>

Sheikh Mustafa and Sultan Ibrahim of Soba belong to this category. The first has a terebinth, an almond, and a *quddeb* tree. The stones scattered irregularly under these trees are supposed to represent a tomb, but they have no actual connection with a grave. Sultan Ibrahim's shrine consists of a square, open enclosure with a niche <term>original: ṭâqah; a small aperture or window</term>, a prayer niche <note>original: mihrab</note>, a small low-walled enclosure <term>original: ḥuwêṭah</term>, and a fig tree. I believe that this type of sanctuary serves as the link between the specific class of tree-shrines we are discussing and larger sanctuary enclosures.

In critically reviewing the names of the saints <term>original: welîs; "friends of God" or holy protectors</term> belonging to this group, we observe that some have the simple name of the tree as their own holy name. We never find a person's name assigned to such trees. For example: <gloss>4</gloss> "Our Lady of the Laurel" <note>original: Sittnâ el-Ghârah</note> <gloss>5</gloss> is located to the east of Beit Nuba. There are also two holy terebinth trees, each of which bears the name "The Saintly Terebinth" <note>original: el-walîyeh el-Buṭmeh</note>. One is north of Beit Nuba and the other is in el-Qubeibeh. It is said that the Laurel Lady appeared during the British attack in 1917. She was seen standing on top of the tree, wearing a greenish garment and a light head-shawl, holding a sword in her hand that dripped with blood. Every time the British troops advanced, she drove them back.

---

<gloss>1</gloss> This is the only case I have been able to find of a tree having a menstrual flow. For this condition occurring with demons, see *Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society (JPOS)* I 153, etc. I owe this information to the kindness of Omar Effendi al-Barghuti.
<gloss>2</gloss> This story was related by Omar Effendi al-Barghuti.
<gloss>3</gloss> Curtiss <note>Samuel Ives Curtiss, a scholar of Semitic religion</note> mentions another such case.
<gloss>4</gloss> The following holy trees were not included in the previous list.
<gloss>5</gloss> Healing can be achieved by treating a sick person with the smoke from the burning leaves of this tree.

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of holy trees in Palestine, focusing on their unique characteristics and the structures associated with them, such as mosques and niches (ṭâqah) built on or near the sites of sacred trees.</meta>

->Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society<-

In Bêt Ṣafâfâ, a mosque <note>original: *djâmi‘*</note> was built on the site of the holy terebinth tree <note>original: *Buṭmeh*</note>,¹ whose sacred tree had been uprooted by a violent storm. The ruins of this mosque are still known as the Mosque of the Terebinth <note>original: *djâmi‘ el-Buṭmeh*</note>. This sacred tree was believed to be inhabited by pious spirits <note>original: *ṣullâḥ*</note>.²

Rags of all possible colors are fastened to most of these trees. Even stones, as will be mentioned later, are sometimes placed on the twigs.

If both a tree and an enclosure are found together, as in the case of Sheikh <note>original: *eš-šêḫ*</note> Frêdj in Bêt Hanînâ, I believe the tree is the more important feature. However, when a tree and a spring together represent the sacred place, it is very difficult to determine which of them takes priority.³

We generally find a niche <note>original: *ṭâqah*</note> connected with these sacred trees. It may be a crack in a nearby rock, a low enclosure covered with tin or a stone slab, a hollow in the tree itself, or a built structure. Oil lamps are lit and incense is burned in this niche. An excellent example of a built niche beside a sacred tree is that of *Ḥarrûbet el-‘Ašarah* near el-’Êsâwîyeh. Right next to the tree, a low, room-like niche was recently built. I would not consider this one-cubic-meter building a domed shrine <note>original: *qubbeh*</note>, as Kahle does.⁴ In the shrine of Sheikh Abdallah (Ša‘fâṭ), a kerosene tin serves as the niche.

Even some Christians in Palestine believe to some extent in the sacredness of certain trees, though they do not burn lights or incense for them. Among trees of this type are:

***

¹ Near the Mamilla pool, there used to be a terebinth tree. It was a common belief that when it was cut down or withered away, the rule of the Turks would depart from Palestine. It so happened that during the last year of the World War it dried up, and soon afterward Jerusalem was taken by British troops. This tree was also known by the name *el-Buṭmeh*.

² A man from Bethlehem was allowed to take the wood of this tree for use in an oil press <note>original: *badd*</note>. In exchange, he had to build a mosque <note>original: *djâmi‘*</note> with a portico <note>original: *rawâq*</note> and a cistern in its place. However, because he did the work so poorly that it collapsed a few years later, the saint living in the tree punished him very severely, and his entire family died one by one. Under the *el-Buṭmeh* tree, the people of the village used to gather for gossip and to entertain their guests, as they would in a guest house <note>original: *maḍâfah*</note> (as related by the prayer leader <note>original: *imâm*</note> of Bêt Ṣafâfâ).

³ In the case of Sheikh <note>original: *eš-šêḫ*</note> Husên, there is both a tree and a spring dedicated to him. I think that the tree is the more important feature, although the saint is sometimes seen sitting near the spring. The spring used to be called the Spring of the Walnut <note>original: *‘ên ed-Djôz*</note>.

⁴ *Palästinajahrbuch* <note>original: *PJ.*</note>, as cited above.

We also find parallels to such trees in the Bible, especially in the case of the Burning Bush.⁶ This bush was not connected with any shrine; it was holy in itself because the Lord spoke from it. The same may be said of the mulberry trees of David. Their sanctity was revealed through "the sound of marching <note>original: "a going"</note> in the tops of the trees."⁷ This was a sign from God. Abraham built his first altar and received the first revelation God made to him under the terebinth of Moreh (Genesis 12:6–7). The next altar he built was under the terebinth of Mamre. In Beersheba, he planted a tamarisk tree and called on the name of Jehovah (Genesis 21:33). Under an oak tree, the angel appeared to Gideon (Judges 6:11, 24 — S. Curtiss).

### VIII. Heaps of stones

When one stands at such a spot, it is a cause for wonder to look around in every direction and find nothing to suggest the idea of sanctity except mere heaps of stones, which, of course, differ in size and form in different places. It should be noted that a stone heap <term>rdjûmeh (plural *rudjm*)</term> may also be inhabited by spirits <term>djinn</term>. Thus, for example, one of the stony mounds <note>original: *tumuli*</note> in el-Baq'ah (the Plain of Rephaim) is thought to be inhabited by a hen with her chicks.

***

¹ A man who cut it down was punished with death.
² See *Canaan, Superstition* <note>original: *Aberglaube*</note>, page 63.
³ *Superstition* <note>original: *Aberglaube*</note>, page 87.
⁴ The oil from these trees is sold for a high price. Rosaries are made from the olive pits.
⁵ It is said that the angel appeared to the shepherds at the spot where this tree is growing. Some peasants who once tried to burn the tree noticed, to their great astonishment, that the fire did not affect it. This proved the sacredness of this olive tree to everyone (related to me by Louise Baldensperger).

<meta>This page continues the classification of sacred stone heaps (rudjm) in Palestine, providing specific examples of different structural types and exploring their origins as either ancient tombs, memorials for the fallen, or "witness-places" (mašâhid) used by pilgrims.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>

->Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society<-

Such heaps of stones, or burial mounds <note>original: *tumuli*</note>, are of the following types: 

1. Quite isolated with no other features, except that occasionally a few stones are set up to form a small niche <term>ṭâqah</term> for oil lamps.
2. The stone heap <term>rudjm</term>, or its summit only, is completely surrounded by an enclosure of stones.
3. Very rarely, the stone heap may be placed within a cave. 

As examples of these forms, we may cite:

| | Name | Location |
|---|---|---|
| 1. | Sheikh Obed <note>original: *eš-šêḫ I’bêd*</note> | In Sataf <note>original: *Ṣaṭâf*</note> |
| | Sheikh Sad <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Sa‘d*</note> | In Wadi Hadr <note>original: *wâdî Ḥaḍr*</note> east of Abu Dis |
| | Sheikh Ahmad <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Aḥmad*</note> | Khirbet el-Qusur <note>original: *Ḥirbet el-Qṣûr*</note>, opposite Jorah <note>original: *ed-Djôrah*</note> |
| 2. | Sheikh Abd el-Muhsin <note>original: *eš-šêḫ ‘Abd el-Muḥsin*</note> | Jibiah¹ |
| | Sheikh El-Birdâq² | Beit Rima <note>original: *Bêt Rîmâ*</note> |
| 3. | Sheikh Murjan <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Murdjân*</note>³ | Jorah (near Ein Karim) |

Naturally, one asks what the stone heap represents and what its purpose is. We often hear expressions indicating that there is a tomb under the heap. We also know that the ancient Palestinians⁴ used to pile large heaps of stones on the tombs of their important dead; even today, most peasant <note>original: *fellaḥ*</note> tombs are marked either by a small enclosure of stones or an elongated, low stone heap. Jaussen⁵ reports that the Bedouins still mark the places where someone has been killed—whether in war or by treachery—with a heap of stones. In the case of some of the stone heaps <note>original: *rdjûmeh*</note> that I am now describing, this explanation may be true, but certainly not in every case.

A special class of stone heaps must still be mentioned: the witness-places <term>el-mašâhid</term> (the plural of <term>mašhad</term>). These are recent heaps of stones placed irregularly in different locations. The word *mašhad* may express one of the following meanings:

1. The place from which something is seen.⁷
2. Since at such places the pilgrim always utters, first of all, "I testify that there is no god but Allah" <note>original: *ašhadu annâ lâ ilahan illâ allah*</note>, thus testifying to the unity of God, the places may be named *mašhad* after this testimony <term>šhâdeh</term>.⁸

---

¹ The *sheikh* appears as a Black man <note>original: "negro"</note> with a sword in his hand.
² Inhabited by foreigners <note>original: *Adjâm*; usually referring to Persians or non-Arabs</note>. Only the top of the large hill is surrounded by an enclosure.
³ A Black saint <note>original: "negro"</note>.
⁴ Joshua 7:26; 8:29.
⁵ *Customs of the Arabs* <note>original: *Coutumes des Arabes*</note>, p. 336.
⁶ Not often used in the singular.
⁷ From the root *šâhada*, meaning "to behold."
⁸ From the root *šahida* or *šhâdeh*, meaning "to give testimony."

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of stone heaps (rudjm) in Palestine, moving from burial markers to "witness stones" placed by pilgrims near holy sites like the shrine of the Prophet Moses (Nabi Musa).</meta>

# CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine <note>original: "Mohammedan Saints..."</note>

3. Stones which are believed to be witnesses before God, testifying that the person who erected them visited that sanctuary and offered a prayer. It is believed that on the Day of Judgment, people may ask animals, plants, or stones to testify on their behalf. Thus, these stones—piled up by the pilgrim while reciting a prayer and the *Opening* <note>original: *fâtiḥah*, the first chapter of the Quran</note>—may bear witness¹ both to his piety and to his visit to the holy place. They will also remind the holy man, in whose honor the pilgrimage visit <note>original: *ziârah*</note> was made, to help and to intercede for the pilgrim.² Even in the Old Testament, we see a heap of stones set as a witness, as in the story of Laban and Jacob.³

When traveling from Jerusalem to Jericho, there is a road branching to the right a few kilometers after passing el-Hod <note>original: *el-Ḥôḍ*</note>. This road leads to the Shrine of the Prophet Moses <note>original: *Nabî Mûsâ*</note>. The hills where the shrine is first seen are covered with these stone heaps. Every Muslim who passes by—whether during the festival or at any other time—throws one or more stones onto such a heap or creates a new one. As he does so, he recites the aforementioned *Testimony of Faith* <note>original: *šihâdeh* or *Shahada*: "There is no god but God, and Muhammad is His messenger"</note> and the *Opening* prayer. Few heaps are large; most consist of only a few stones. The lowest stone is the largest, and the top one is the smallest. These small heaps may be made up of two, three, four, or five stones.

Generally, on every road leading to the sanctuary from any direction, "sign-stones" <note>original: *nawâṣib*, the plural of *naṣb*</note> are erected. I observed such heaps on the four roads leading to the Prophet Moses's shrine, on three roads to el-Hadr <note>original: *el-Ḥaḍr*</note> (Beit Jala), and on two...

---

¹ See Luke 19:40; Hebrews 3:11 <note>original: Heb. 3:11. The author likely intended Habakkuk 2:11: "For the stone shall cry out of the wall."</note>.

² Such a belief is not recent. In *The Pleasing Gift Concerning Jerusalem's Traditions* <note>original: *et-tuḥfatu-l-marḍiyah fil-aḫbâr el-qudsiyah* by ‘Abd el-Majid ‘Ali</note>, we read on page 62 that a man, while on Mount Arafat, took seven stones and said:
> "Oh stones, witness that I believe and say: there is no god but God, and Muhammad is his prophet."
That night, he dreamed that on the Day of Judgment he was tried, found to be a sinner, and sent to hell. As he approached the first gate of hell, one of the stones blocked the entrance. All the angels of the lower world were unable to remove this obstacle. The same thing happened at every one of the seven gates of hell. Consequently, he was brought back to the heavenly Judge, who allowed him to enter heaven since the stones had borne witness in his favor.

³ Genesis 31:45 ff.

⁴ Not *nasib* <note>original: *naṣîb*</note> as McCown writes. *Nasib* means "lot" or "luck," while *nasb* <note>original: *naṣb*, plural *nawâṣib*</note>, meaning "stones set up as a sign," comes from the same root as *nusb* <note>original: *nuṣb* or *nuṣub*, plural *anṣâb*</note>, meaning "idols" or "sacrificial altars." See the dictionary *The Encompassing of the Ocean* <note>original: *Muḥîṭ el-Muḥîṭ*</note>.

<summary>This page describes the practice of creating small stone heaps (nawâṣib) as religious "witnesses" for pilgrims in Palestine, specifically near the shrine of Nabi Musa. It explains the theological belief that these stones will testify for the believer on Judgment Day, drawing parallels to Biblical traditions and Islamic folklore.</summary>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of stone heaps (rudjm) in Palestine, specifically focusing on "sign-stones" or "witnesses" (šawâhid) located on paths leading to various shrines, and the specific locations where these practices are observed by both Muslims and Christians.</meta>

leading to Sheikh al-Umari of Jubeia <note>original: *eš-šêḫ el-‘Umarî ed-Djbê‘î*</note>, and also on the two roads to Hasan the Shepherd <note>original: *Ḥasan er-Râ‘î*</note>.¹

This custom of making small piles of stones applies only to a relatively few sanctuaries. Christians are also familiar with these "arches" <note>original: *qanâṭir*; singular: *qanṭarah*—a third name for these heaps</note>, and they pile stones when reaching ash-Sharafeh <note>original: *eš-Šarafeh*</note> on their way from Beit Jala <note>original: *Bêt Djâlâ*</note> to al-Khadr <note>original: *el-Ḥaḍr*</note>. They do this because from this point they can see the Monastery of Saint Elijah <note>original: *Mâr Eliâs*</note> on one side and the convent of Saint George on the other.

These "witnesses" <note>original: *šawâhid*</note> do not closely resemble the stones that mark the boundaries of fields, as the scholar McCown² thinks. Landmarks are generally made of large stones placed separately at a distance from each other, running more or less in a straight line, usually between fields. When they are made of stones, they differ from the "arches" <note>original: *qanâṭir*</note> because they use far larger and fewer stones.

"Arches" <note>original: *qanâṭir*</note> may also serve quite different purposes. Among these minor purposes we may mention:

1. Heaps which are raised in a place where a holy man is supposed to have rested, such as in the neighborhood of the rock-cliff of Sheikh Asfur <note>original: *šqîf eš-šêḫ ‘Asfûr*</note>.³

2. Sometimes a traveler, after climbing a high mountain, raises a heap of stones or throws a stone on an existing heap, while saying a prayer as a mark of thanks to God that he has overcome a difficulty. Heaps of this sort are generally known by the name "The Place of Success" <note>original: *el-Mafâzeh*</note>.⁴ At the top of the ascent of Farhah near Salfit <note>original: *Salfît*</note>, such "places of success" <note>original: *mafâzât*</note> may be seen. Similar cases are described in the Book of Joshua, where the Israelites erected stones at Gilgal as a memorial after crossing the Jordan River and thus overcoming the difficulties of their long wandering.⁵ Samuel also, after subduing the Philistines,

> "took a stone and set it between Mizpah and Shen and called it Ebenezer, saying, 'Hitherto has the Lord helped us.'"⁶

---

**1** Other sanctuaries where such stone heaps are set up include: the Prophet Lot <note>original: *en-nabî Lûṭ*</note>, the Prophet Yaqin <note>original: *en-nabî Yaqîn*</note>, Ali son of Alem <note>original: *‘Alî bin ‘Lêm*</note>, Ezra <note>original: *el-‘Uzêr*</note>, etc.
**2** *Annual of the American School* II and III, page 66.
**3** Heard from Omar Effendi al-Barghuti <note>original: *‘Omar Effendi el-Barghûṭî*</note>.
**4** *Mafâzeh* literally means "desert." Here it denotes success in overcoming a dangerous difficulty. Source: *The Encompassing Ocean* <note>original: *Muḥîṭ el-Muḥîṭ*, a famous Arabic dictionary</note>.
**5** Joshua, chapter 4.
**6** 1 Samuel 7:12.

<summary>This page describes the locations and varied purposes of ritual stone heaps (qanâṭir) in Palestine, used by both Muslims and Christians to mark sightings of holy sites or to commemorate overcoming travel difficulties. The author distinguishes these religious heaps from agricultural landmarks and links the practice to Biblical traditions like those of Joshua and Samuel.</summary>
<keywords>stone heaps, qanâṭir, witness stones, šawâhid, Palestine shrines, Christian pilgrimage, al-Khadr, Saint George, el-Mafâzeh, Biblical parallels, Ebenezer, Gilgal</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on stone heaps (qanâṭir) and transitions to the category of holy sites consisting of single large stones or rocks. It provides specific examples from the Palestinian landscape, such as sites near Al-Qastal and Beit Ijza.</meta>

3. In places where prominent village or district chiefs were killed, passersby raise heaps of stones <gloss>1</gloss> and say prayers for their souls. These "arches" <note>original: *qanâṭir*—literally "arches," but used here for stone heaps</note> are smaller and much less numerous than those found near a sanctuary. We find such heaps in Maqtal al-Mashayikh <note>original: *Maqtal el-Mašâih*—The Place of the Sheikhs’ Slaughter</note> <gloss>2</gloss> on the road to Beit Rima. These types of stone heaps serve as a connecting link between prominent historical figures and holy men, a topic I will explore later.

In concluding the discussion of this group, I must observe that while the stone mounds <note>original: *rdjûmeh*</note> are regarded as holy places and the dwellings of saints, the "witnesses" <note>original: *šawâhid*</note>, "arches" <note>original: *qanâṭir*</note>, and "places of safety" <note>original: *mafâzât*—heaps built by travelers</note> do not enjoy this same level of dignity.

On the southern road leading from Al-Qastal to Soba, we find a large heap of stones built in a circular form. It is about 1.5 meters high and 2 meters in diameter. In the center of the upper part, a vertical stone sticks out. There is no niche <note>original: *ṭâqah*</note>, tree, or cistern connected with it. The people I asked for an explanation said that a saint <note>original: *welî*</note> had appeared or revealed himself <note>original: *ašhar nafsuh*</note> at this spot, claiming it as his own. Out of piety, the peasants erected this heap.

## IX. A single large stone or a rock.

There are only a few examples in this category. With the exception of the Noble Rock <note>original: *aṣ-Ṣahrah*</note> inside the "Mosque of Omar" <note>The author refers here to the Dome of the Rock in Jerusalem</note>, which is highly honored not only by the Muslims of Palestine but by the entire Islamic world, other holy stones and rocks in Palestine receive far less respect and religious reverence than other types of shrines and sanctuaries <note>original: *maqâmât*</note>. The holiness assigned to them is generally minor. A description of the Noble Rock is of primary importance and will be provided at the end of this chapter.

Some holy places in this category include:

Araq al-Ajami <note>original: *'Arâq el-Adjamî*—The Rock of the Persian</note> <gloss>3</gloss> in Beit Ijza is a natural rock that is not particularly prominent, nor is it connected with any sanctuary, tree, cave, or cistern. In the middle of it is a small, man-made depression where, I was told, offerings are placed.

***

<gloss>1</gloss> These resemble in some respects the heaps of stones mentioned in the Bible in Joshua 7:26, 8:29, and 2 Samuel 18:17.
<gloss>2</gloss> When I saw this place in 1921, there were only a few heaps remaining.
<gloss>3</gloss> I found no niche <note>original: *ṭâqah*</note> connected with this place.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of stone heaps (qanâṭir) to the category of holy sites centered around single large stones or natural rock formations. It provides specific geographical examples of sites associated with prophets and saints, particularly in the regions of Samaria and Judea.</meta>

# Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society
->Page 78<-

In the neighborhood of Deir Ghassaneh <note>original: Dêr Ghassâneh</note>, there are some rocks called the Rocks of the Foreigners <note>original: *Nuqqâr el-ʿAdjâm*</note>.^1 They are situated between two hills and are attributed to the "Foreigners" <note>original: *el-ʿAdjâm*, often referring to Persians or non-Arabs</note>. In passing, the peasant farmer <note>original: *fellah*</note> will recite the opening prayer of the Quran <note>original: *fâtiḥah*</note> to these saints <note>original: *awliâ*</note>, just as he would at any other shrine. No vows, offerings, or oaths are made to or by them.

In the village of Sheikh Sa'd <note>original: Šêḫ Saʿd</note> in the Hauran region, there is a stone called the Rock of Job <note>original: *Ṣaḫret Ayûb*</note>, on which it is said that the prophet Job used to lean during the days of his suffering. The stone is an ancient monument <note>original: *stela*</note> with hieroglyphics from the time of Ramesses II.^2

On the height of Mount el-Martum <note>original: el-Marṭûm</note>,^3 near the ruins of Beni Dar <note>original: Benî Dâr</note>,^4 and to the south of the village of Bani N'em <note>original: Banî Nʿêm</note>, a shrine <note>original: *maqâm*</note> is built for the supposed prophet Yaqin <note>original: Yaqîn</note>.^5 In the room, we notice a rock encircled with an iron frame. This rock shows the impressions of two feet and of two hands. It is related that Abraham was ordered by God to come to this place, where he could observe the destruction of Sodom and Gomorrah. It is curious that although Abraham is known to be a pre-Islamic prophet, the impressions show that he performed his prayer with his face turned toward Mecca.

Outside this shrine, there is another rock also showing the impressions of two feet. They are said to be those of Lot. This rock is surrounded by a small stone enclosure <note>original: *ḥuwêṭîyeh*</note>.

The Stone of el-Aruri <note>original: *Ḥadjar el-ʿArûrî*</note>^6 to the south of Salfit is a large piece of rock, beside which Sheikh el-Aruri is said to have rested. The Fortress of er-Rifai <note>original: *Qalʿat er-Rifâʿî*</note> to the west of Deir Ghassaneh is supposed to be the place where the saint er-Rifai used to rest. In the cemetery of the Gate of Mercy <note>original: *Bâb er-Raḥmeh*</note>, situated along the western wall of Jerusalem to the south of St. Stephen's—

***

^1 The *nuqqâr* are composed of several large stones raised from the ground. The Sufi dervishes <note>original: *ṣuffîyeh darâwîš*</note> are afraid to pass in their neighborhood, especially during the night. The night between Thursday and Friday is the most dangerous, according to Omar Effendi el-Barghuti <note>original: ʿOmar Effendi el-Barghûtî</note>. These features are true of many other holy places. A complete description of them will be given in another chapter.

^2 Journal of the German Society for the Exploration of Palestine <note>original: *ZDPV* XIV, 147</note>. I have not seen this place.

^3 The view from this high mountain, especially to the east, is excellent. The Dead Sea and the mountains of Moab are clearly seen.

# CANAAN: Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine
->Page 79<-

Gate, there is a broken pillar,¹ beside the tomb of Sheikh Shaddad <note>original: "eš-šêḫ Šaddâd"</note>. It is believed that by rubbing one’s back on this pillar one will be cured of any backache.²

<margin>The Forgotten Sheikh in the place of Prophet David <note>original: "الشيخ المنسي في النبي داود"</note></margin> El-Hajar el-Mansi <note>original: "El-Hadjar el-Mansî"</note>, “the forgotten stone,” is supposed to be the grave of a Christian who converted to the Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> faith just before his death. Some inhabitants of the Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> quarter on “Mount Zion”³ observed on various occasions a light shining from this spot. This proved the sanctity of the place to them.⁴ A light used to be burned every Thursday evening for this “forgotten man.”⁵

In the Al-Aqsa <note>original: "el-Aqṣâ"</note> Mosque there are two pillars, between which it is believed that no illegitimate child <note>original: "bastard child"</note> can pass.⁶ This is one of the many so-called “ordeals of God.”⁷

Inside El-Mas'ad <note>original: "el-Maṣʿad"</note>, the Chapel of the Ascension (on the Mount of Olives), we are shown the impression of the right foot of Jesus on a stone. This place belongs to the Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note> but it is also held sacred by the Christians, who celebrate mass here on certain days. The sacred stone is surrounded by a beautiful octagonal building with a vaulted roof.⁸

Christians honor other stones and rocks as well. I need not mention Golgotha and the pillar where Christ was bound and scourged, but I will limit myself to a description of the sacred stones of St. George, the “Milk Grotto,” and the rock on which Elijah is supposed to have rested.

The holy stone of St. George (el-Khadr <note>original: "el-Ḫaḍr"</note> between Beit Jala <note>original: "Bêt Djâlâ"</note> and the Pools of Solomon) still plays an important role. The story, as it is recorded in the author’s *Superstition* <note>original: "Aberglaube"</note>, is as follows. While a Greek priest was officiating at the Holy Communion in the Church...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sacred stones in Palestine, focusing on local legends associated with St. George, the Virgin Mary, and the Prophet Elijah, culminating in the description of the Noble Rock in Jerusalem.</meta>

of Mar Jirius <note>Saint George</note>, a few drops of the sacred wine were spilled. They soaked through his foot and cracked the stone upon which they fell. The priest’s wound never healed, and he died as a punishment for his carelessness in handling the Blood of the Savior <note>This refers to the consecrated wine used in the Christian Eucharist</note>. The stone received a supernatural healing power from the sacred wine, which benefited every sick person who happened to kneel on it. Its reputation soon spread throughout Palestine, and great numbers of sick people flocked there. Even the Tsar <note>original: "Czar"</note> of Russia heard of the wonderful virtues of this stone and sent a warship <note>original: "man-of-war"</note> to Jaffa to take it away. The stone is said to have been brought to Jaffa in a solemn procession. But St. George did not allow it to be transported any further. Every time the boat carrying this precious treasure moved some distance from the shore, Al-Khadr <note>original: "el-Ḥaḍr", the "Green One," a figure in Islamic tradition frequently identified with St. George or Elijah</note> brought it back with his spear. Everyone recognized the folly of disobeying the wishes of the saint, and so the stone was returned to its place.¹

The white stones of the Milk Grotto are used by Christians and Muslims <note>original: "Moslems"</note> of Bethlehem and the surrounding district as amulets to increase the flow of a mother's milk. It is believed that a few drops of the Virgin Mary's milk fell on the floor.

Opposite to Mar Elias <note>the Monastery of St. Elijah</note> on the western side of the main road, there is a depression in the rock. It is related that St. Elijah slept in this place while escaping from his pursuers.²

But the most important rock is the *Sakhrah* <note>original: "eṣ-Ṣaḥrah", meaning "The Rock"</note>, which measures 17.7 by 13.5 meters and is situated in the center of the "Mosque of Omar" <note>A common historical name for the Dome of the Rock</note>. The rough surface of the rock stands in great contrast to the beauty and harmony of the interior of the most beautiful mosque in the East <note>original: "Orient"</note>. The rock is itself sacred and is protected from visitors by a railing. Its sanctity is due to its connection with so many prophets,

---

1 See Hanauer, *Folklore of the Holy Land*, p. 52.
2 From the area between Mar Elias and Tantur, a piece of rock was carried to Beit Jala. It is said that while Mary was traveling from Bethlehem to Jerusalem carrying her child, she passed people <note>original: "Jews"; the text reflects local folklore</note> threshing beans on the rock east of Tantur. Christ cried for some, and she asked the people to give her a handful. They refused and said that they were not beans but only stones. Immediately, they turned into small stones. The workers at once followed her and accused her of being a witch. She hurried to escape, and when she was on the point of falling into their hands, she asked a rock to hide her. At once the stone opened and sheltered her. In vain did her pursuers search for her. This stone bears the name *Srir es-Sayyideh* <note>original: "ṣrîr es-Saiydeh", meaning "The Lady's Pebbles" or "The Lady's Cradle"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of sacred stones in Palestine, shifting focus from Christian and local folklore to the specific Islamic traditions surrounding the Sacred Rock (Sakhrah) within the Dome of the Rock in Jerusalem.</meta>

# CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

...especially Mohammed <note>The Prophet Muhammad</note>. When the Prophet ascended to heaven, the rock would have followed him if the angel Gabriel had not held it down. On two occasions the rock spoke, once to Mohammed and again to the Caliph ‘Omar. I shall not describe all the beliefs connected with this stone since they can be found in other sources. On, around, and below it, we find the following sacred places:

### I. On the rock itself: <gloss>1</gloss>

1. The impressions of the fingers of the angel Gabriel, who kept the rock from following the Prophet while he ascended to heaven (located on the western side).
2. The footsteps of Idris <note>The prophet Enoch</note> (on the east).
3. The footsteps of Mohammed, twelve in total. The Prophet is said to have walked over that part of the rock. The stone softened under his feet, and so the impressions remained.

### II. Below the rock:

4. Before entering the cave below the Sacred Rock, one is shown the "tongue of the rock." It is said that during the night journey <note>original: *lêlatu-l-Mi'râdj*, the miraculous ascent of Muhammad from Jerusalem to heaven</note> of the Prophet, he greeted the rock: "Peace be with you, O Rock of God" <note>original: *as-salâmu 'aleikî yâ ṣaḥrati-l-lâh*</note>, and it answered at once: "At your service, and peace be with you, O Messenger of God" <note>original: *labbeik wa'aleik as-salâm yâ rasûl allah*</note>.
5. Fifteen steps lead to the cave below the Sakhrah <note>original: *ṣahrah*, the Arabic name for the Sacred Rock</note>. To the right of it, we find the prayer niche of King Solomon.
6. Still further to the right is the place where Mohammed prayed; since he was tall and the roof was low, he would have struck his head, but
7. The rock yielded in that place, and we are shown the impression of his head <note>original: *ta'djit eṣ-ṣahrah*</note>.
8. The prayer place of Al-Khidr <note>original: *al-Ḥaḍr*, the "Green One," a legendary figure often associated with Saint George or Elijah</note>, at an elevated point.
9. The "Resting Place of Gabriel" <note>original: *Masnad Djubrâîl*</note> is the spot where the angel waited until the Prophet finished his prayer.
10. The "Prayer Niche of Abraham the Friend" <note>original: *Miḥrâb Ibrâhîm el-Ḥalîl*</note>, to the left of the staircase.
11. The "Prayer Niche of David" <note>original: *Miḥrâb Dâhûd*</note>.
12. The rock is perforated in the middle, and it is said that the Prophet ascended through it directly to heaven.

---

<note>1 Kahle, *Palästinajahrbuch* VI, 93, mentions only 20 places (grouped under 19 headings).</note>

13. Just below the center of the cave, it is believed that the Well of Souls <note>original: *Bîr el-Arwâḥ*</note> is located, which is covered with a marble plate. The souls of the dead are said to gather in this cistern twice a week to perform their prayers.

### III. Directly around the sacred Rock:

14. The footprint of the Prophet <note>Muhammad</note>, which is displayed on a separate stone set upon small pillars at the southwest corner.
15. The hairs from the Prophet's beard are kept in a silver case just above entry number 14. <margin>These are shown to the public on the 27th of Ramadan, which is believed to be the Night of Power <note>original: *lêlatu-l-Qadr*, the night the Quran was first revealed</note>.</margin>
16. The banners of the Prophet and of 'Omar <note>the second Caliph</note> are kept in a box near entry number 15.
17. Two prayer niches connected with Hamzeh <note>the uncle of the Prophet Muhammad</note>.
18. A prayer niche <note>original: *miḥrâb*</note> on the northern side of the sacred Rock <note>original: *ṣaḥrah*</note>, where some of the prophets used to pray; it is known as the "Prayer Niche of the Prophets" <note>original: *miḥrâb el-Anbiâ*</note>. <margin>Tradition says that the Prophet Muhammad prayed here with the other prophets during the Night of the Ascension <note>original: *lêlatu-l-Mi'râdj*</note>.</margin>

### IV. At a distance from the Sacred Rock <note>original: *eş-Ṣaḥrah*</note>:

20. The Gate of Paradise <note>original: *Bâb ed-Djanneh*</note>, which is the northern door. <margin>The four doors of the mosque are the western (Gate of the West or Gate of the Women), the northern (Gate of Paradise), the eastern (Gate of David or Gate of the Chain), and the southern (Gate of the Qibla, facing Mecca). The historical text *The Solace of the Illustrious* <note>original: *Uns ed-Djalîl*</note> calls the eastern door the Gate of Israfil <note>the angel of the trumpet</note>.</margin>
21. The Black Paving Stone <note>original: *El-Balâṭah es-Sôdah*</note>, which was removed by Jamal Pasha <note>an Ottoman military leader</note>, is also known as the "Paving Stone of Paradise" <note>original: *Balâṭit ed-Djanneh*</note>. It is said that Muhammad drove nineteen golden nails into this stone. From time to time, God would send an angel to remove a nail, and when all have been removed, the Last Day will be at hand. One day the devil succeeded in removing some of them. While he was taking them out, he was driven away by the angel Gabriel, who found that only three and a half nails remained. <margin>The tomb of Solomon is said to lie beneath it.</margin>
22. Near the southern door, we see the prayer niche of Abu Hanifa al-Nu'man <note>original: *miḥrâb Abî Ḥanifatu-n-Nu'mân*, the founder of one of the four main schools of Sunni Islam</note>. <margin>This site is not considered as important as the others.</margin>
23. On one side of the eastern door, we find a prayer niche bearing the name of the "Seclusion of Ali" <note>original: *Hiluet 'Alî*</note>, which was the private place of prayer for Ali <note>the Prophet's cousin and son-in-law</note>. <margin>This site is also not considered as important as the others.</margin>

# CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine 83

The number of kneelings to be performed at these sites is specifically indicated. Mujir al-Din <note>original: *Mudjîr ed-Dîn*, a 15th-century historian of Jerusalem</note> mentions only locations 1, 4, 11, 14, 21, and the cave <note>original: *al-ghâr*</note>.² The scholar Imam Abu Bakr ibn al-Arabi³ claimed to have seen the Rock floating in the air without any support. A modern belief, which has a parallel in *The Glorious Fellowship* <note>original: *al-uns ed-djalîl*, the title of Mujir al-Din's history</note>,⁴ is that four streams flow from under this Rock:⁵ to the south the Healing Bath <note>original: *Ḥammâm eš-Šifâ*</note>, to the east Siloam <note>original: *‘ên sittî Mariam*, or "The Spring of Our Lady Mary"</note>, to the north the Pilgrims' Spring <note>original: *ên Ḥadjdjî*</note> and the Spring of the Barracks <note>original: *ên el-Qašleh*</note>, and to the west the Sultan’s Bath <note>original: *Ḥammâm es-Sulṭân*</note>.⁶

Before closing this chapter, I should also mention *Abu ed-Dhur* <note>meaning "Father of Backs"</note>, a rock situated on the left side of the carriage road from Jerusalem to Jericho, after passing the Spring of the Trough <note>original: *‘ên el-Ḥôḍ*</note>.⁷ This rock has a widespread reputation for curing backaches. After a patient has rubbed his back against the rock, he places a stone upon it. When I last saw this "Father of Backs," it was loaded with a large heap of stones. It is not currently assigned to any specific saint <note>original: *welî*</note>, and I cannot explain its widespread medicinal <term>therapeutic</term> use except by assuming that it must have once been connected in some way with a holy man or an object of worship.⁸ I do not think that Curtiss <note>Samuel Ives Curtiss, a scholar of Semitic religion</note> is correct in saying that such stones act by their own magical power. Neither the color nor the substance of this rock differs in any way from the surrounding rocks so as to attract special attention.⁹

We also note in this connection that this idea of honoring stones is not just a trait of the modern inhabitants of Palestine; it was well known in the ancient Orient. In Genesis 18:18 and following, we read:

"Jacob rose up early in the morning, took the stone he had used as a pillow, and set it up as a pillar, pouring oil on top of it. He named that place Bethel."¹ In Isaiah 57:6, we read about "the smooth stones of the stream," to which the Israelites "poured out a drink offering" and "offered a grain offering."² <note>original: "meat offering"</note>

Before moving on from this section, we should highlight those rare sacred sites that lack the typical features mentioned earlier—meaning they have no tomb, <term>maqam</term> <note>a "standing place" or shrine dedicated to a saint</note>, tree, cave, enclosure, or rock. It is difficult to believe that such neglected and unassuming places have ever drawn the interest of the local people. Generally, we find no clues to explain why these spots were ever dedicated to a <term>weli</term> <note>a Muslim saint or holy person</note>. Among sites of this nature, I should mention:

**Sheikh Salman** (<term>Beit Surik</term>): This is merely a small, cupboard-like hollow in a garden wall. The nearby pomegranate trees do not actually belong to the <term>weli</term>. Inside this opening, I observed oil lamps.

**El-Ajami** (<term>Awarta</term>): An open area flanked on two sides by the remains of two old, sturdily built walls. No tomb, cistern, or tree is associated with this <term>weli</term>. In the western wall, there is a <term>taqah</term> <note>a small niche or window</note> where oil is burned and incense is offered.

**El-Ajami** (a second saint of the same name in <term>Awarta</term>): A ruined building with no surviving signs of a <term>mihrab</term> <note>a prayer niche indicating the direction of Mecca</note> or a tomb. The villagers say the place was originally the house of a peasant. It seems that most examples in this category are connected with ruins. For instance, the site purchased by the Russians in <term>Anata</term> contains the ruins of a building with several pillars, likely the remains of a church. The <term>fellahin</term> <note>peasant farmers</note> of this village believe the site is haunted by the <term>sullah</term> <note>the "righteous ones" or spirits of the holy</note>, though no one knows who they specifically are.

Not all of these places are well-tended. They are often dirty and neglected, overgrown with thistles and other weeds, and filled with old tins, stones, and rubbish. This was particularly true of the two sacred sites in <term>Awarta</term>, as well as **Banat esh-Sheikh Salah**³ and **el-Umari** (both in **Jericho**). Of these last two sites,⁴ the former consists only of a simple outline...

## B. RITES AND PRACTICES

In our study of the different forms and categories of Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> shrines in Palestine, we have laid the foundation for our further investigations. Without a thorough knowledge of these forms, the study of the rituals will be difficult and incomplete. In the present chapter, I intend to address the question: what is done in these holy places?

Acts connected with a shrine may be performed in the <term>maqam</term> <note>a shrine or "standing place" of a saint</note> itself, outside of it, or at a distance from the sanctuary. These include simple acts as well as complex ones. The following classification includes the most important acts performed in a holy place, arranged according to their degree of complexity:

1. Religious acts
    I. Utterance of simple protective words
    II. Repetition of prayers
    III. Reverence
    IV. Religious service as in a mosque
    V. The <term>barakah</term> <note>divine blessing or grace; original: "barakeh"</note>
2. Placing private property under the protection of the <term>weli</term> <note>a Muslim saint; original: "welî"</note>
3. Tying of rags
4. Healing
5. Making oaths
6. Paying vows
7. Celebration of feasts, <term>mawasim</term> <note>seasonal religious festivals or pilgrimages; original: "mawâsim"</note>
8. Processions.

Some less important acts will be mentioned under the headings listed above.

---

¹ I found tin oil-lamps in this so-called shrine.

# 1. RELIGIOUS ACTS
## I. Uttering simple protective words

No devout peasant ever approaches or enters a <term>maqam</term> <note>a shrine of a saint; original: "maqâm"</note> without first asking the permission of the <term>weli</term> <note>a Muslim saint; original: "welî"</note>. The general expression used is "with your permission" <note>original: "dastûr"</note>¹ ("O our lord <note>original: "yâ sîdnâ"</note>² Abd es-Salam"³). It is a common Arabic—and especially Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note>—custom never to enter a <term>harim</term>⁴ <note>women's quarters or the women of a household; original: "ḥarîm"</note> without asking permission or, more accurately, notifying the women that a man is approaching.⁵ "Get permission before you enter" <note>original: "Dastîr qabl mâ tudḫul"</note> used to be a rule strictly enforced on every male servant.⁶ It is customary to use the word for permission <note>original: "dastûr"</note> alone or combined with a word for "being on guard" <note>original: "ḥaḍûr"</note>, as well as with different titles for God.⁷ Even when someone intends to contradict or criticize another person’s statements, they begin with "By your leave" <note>original: "dastûr"</note>⁸ ("the matter is not like that" <note>original: "el-mas'alih miš hêk"</note>).

This custom is much more significant, and therefore more frequent, when a person approaches the home of a supernatural being, such as a demon, a spirit, or a saint. No one would approach an—

---

¹ This is a Persian word derived from *dast* ("rule, order") and *uwr* ("owner") (see *The Circumference of the Ocean* <note>original: "Muḥîṭ el-Muḥîṭ"</note> I, 650). The direct meaning of "permission" does not appear in literary Arabic, but it is understood this way in common speech.
² My lord <note>original: "Sîdî"</note> means "my master" or "my lord." In Palestinian Arabic, it also means "my grandfather" <note>original: "djiddî"</note>.
³ Abd es-Salam el-Asmar came from Morocco to Palestine and lived in the village of Hirbet Almit, which is now a ruin. Because of a conflict between the government and the residents of this village, the government destroyed the rebels' houses. Abd es-Salam moved to Anata, bought the site, and lived there. His descendants are the peasants of that village. The <term>sheikh</term> <note>a leader or holy man; original: "šêḫ"</note> had six children, one of whom, Qasim, died childless. The other five were Alawi, Ibrahim, Ali, Abdallah, and Abd el-Latif. Alawi fathered Hamdan, the father of Mohammed, who was the father of Ahmad, who is still living. I owe this story and chronology to this Ahmad. Sheikh Abd es-Salam belonged to the Rifai Sufi order <note>original: "Rifâ'î"</note>.
⁴ The word <term>harim</term> <note>original: "Ḥarîm"</note> also means "wife" ("my wife" <note>original: "ḥarîmî"</note> or "my wives"). Many use the word for "my family" <note>original: "'êltî"</note> in the same way.
⁵ Even when a man entered his own house, he would unfailingly announce his entrance, as there might be female guests present.
⁶ This custom is more prevalent in cities than in villages. The inhabitants of Hebron, Nablus, and Gaza used to be very strict about it.
⁷ "O Concealer" <note>original: "Yâ sattâr"</note>; "O Keeper" <note>original: "yâ ḥâfiẓ"</note>; "O Faithful" <note>original: "yâ amîn"</note>, etc.
⁸ The aforementioned word for "being on guard" <note>original: "ḥaḍûr"</note> comes from a root meaning "to be ready" or "be on your guard" <note>original: "ḥaḍḍara"</note>. This word is also used when rocks are being blasted.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ritual of asking permission (dastûr) when approaching supernatural sites, extending the practice from human dwellings to the realms of spirits and saints.</meta>

inhabited cave, spring, or tree, or to draw water from such a spring without asking permission.1 The irritated genie <note>original: "djinn"</note> may otherwise injure the person. For the same reason, one should never put out burning coals with water without a direct and loud request for permission:2 "With your permission, take care, O inhabitants of the fire," <note>original: "dastûr ḥaḍûr yâ sukkân en-nâr"</note>.3 In other parts the expression is: "O inhabitants of the earth, we are under your protection" <note>original: "yâ ahl el-arḍ iḥnâ fîl-'arḍ"</note>.

Cemeteries are the dwelling places of the souls of the dead, and in former times, they were never entered without asking permission.4 Similarly, holy places are only approached or entered after asking for permission <note>original: "dastûr"</note>.5 This act expresses not only respect for the saint <note>original: "welî"</note>, but also reverence. In the case of genies <note>original: "djinn"</note>, respect and fear are the reasons for asking permission. Through such behavior, one both gains the favor and help of these men of God and avoids the danger that may befall them from evil spirits.

Not only are such precautions taken when approaching a shrine <note>original: "maqâm"</note>, but also whenever the name of an important saint is mentioned; people are afraid to disturb a saint <note>original: "welî"</note>.6 The following example illustrates this custom. When I asked a peasant of Anata <note>original: "'Anâtâ"</note> about the Sheikh <note>original: "eš-šêh"</note> Abd es-Salam, he told me a story to convince me of the importance and power of this leader <note>original: "šêh"</note>. He began: "Once a boy rode on the tomb of Sheikh Abd es-Salam—with your permission, O our lord Abd es-Salam..." <note>original: "marrah rikib walad 'alâ qabr eš-šêh 'Abd es-Salâm—dastûr yâ sîdnâ 'Abd es-Salâm..."</note>.7

3 Another sentence is "With your permission, take care, disperse, otherwise you will be burned" <note>original: "dastûr ḥaḍûr itfarraqû la tiḥtirqû"</note>.
4 This custom is now dying out.
5 The Kurds say on such occasions: "Holy and praised is our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the spirit" <note>original: "quddûs ṣubbûḥ rabbunâ wa rabb el-malâ'ikah war-rûḥ"</note> (heard from Mr. St. H. Stephan).
6 Saints are always at work praying or helping others; therefore, they should never be disturbed. Asking permission to call upon them ensures their consent.

The frightened boy promised the saint <note>original: "šêh"</note> a gift if he brought him down, and at once the tomb began to descend until it was back on the ground. <page-num>88</page-num>

As we have seen in this example, the name of the saint is added to the word *dastûr* <note>meaning "permission"</note>. Sometimes, instead of the name itself, the expression "O friend of God" is placed after *dastûr*: "Permission, O friend of God" <note>original: "dastûr yâ welîy allâh"</note>. Very rarely is the word "permission" <note>original: "dastûr"</note> used alone. Regarding the Prophet Moses <note>original: "nebî Mûsâ"</note>, I heard a woman from Jerusalem use the following expression: "Permission, O son of Amram <note>original: "yâbn 'Imrân"</note>, whose fire comes from his stones." <margin>1</margin> <margin>2</margin> Upon entering the shrine of the Prophet Saleh <note>original: "nebî Sâleḥ"</note>, the phrase used is: "Permission, O Prophet Saleh, O shepherd of the she-camel" <note>original: "dastûr yâ nebî Sâleḥ yâ râ‘î en-nâqah"</note>. <margin>3</margin> <margin>4</margin> For Ali son of Alim <note>original: "‘Alî ibn ‘Alêm"</note>, the saying is: "Permission, O Ali son of Alim, O you who bridle the sea with a hair" <note>original: "dastûr yâ ‘Alî ibn ‘Alêm yâ lâdjîm el-baḥar ibša‘rah"</note>. <margin>5</margin> <margin>6</margin>

This custom is not only found among Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note>, but Christians also practice it. St. George enjoys a great reputation in Palestine, and it is especially in connection with him that "permission" <note>original: "dastûr"</note> or "peace be upon him" <note>original: "es-salâm ‘alêh"</note> is used by Christians.

On rare occasions, someone who enters the enclosure of a shrine—the arcade <note>original: "ruâq"</note> or portico in front of the actual sanctuary <note>original: "maqâm"</note>—and says "permission" <note>original: "dastûr"</note> may receive the following answer from people sitting there: "Your permission is with you" <note>original: "dastûrak ma‘ak"</note>. This means, "you have your own permission," or effectively, you cannot enter the shrine. Such an answer is given when women are in the sanctuary and the presence of a man is not permitted. Conversely, this same expression means the exact opposite—"you may enter"—when it is said to a man asking permission to enter a house.

Besides asking for permission, we continually encounter cases where the saint <note>original: "welî"</note> is invoked in every phase of daily life. No peasant...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on religious etiquette in Palestine, specifically detailing the common invocations and prayers used by Muslims to seek divine help or the protection of saints during daily tasks and in times of distress.</meta>

# CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

<page-num>89</page-num>

...will begin any work without asking the help of the Almighty or that of a saint. The usual phrases are: "In the name of God" <note>original Arabic: *bism allâh*</note>; "In the name of the Virgin" <note>original Arabic: *bism el-’Adrā*</note>; "O prophet of God" <note>original Arabic: *yâ nabîy allâh*</note>; "O green Saint George" <note>original Arabic: *yâ Ḥaḍr el-Aḥḍar*; Khidr (the "Green One") is a figure often identified with St. George or Elijah</note>; "O prophet Reuben" <note>original Arabic: *yâ nabî Rubîn*</note>, and so on. The most common expression among Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note> is the first one. No meal is taken, no work begun, no food handled, no flour kneaded, and no wheat measured without saying this short prayer. It is believed that the *djinn* <note>spirits or demons in Islamic mythology</note> will take possession of everything upon which the name of God has not been uttered.1 According to Al-Tirmidhi <note>a famous collector of Islamic traditions</note>, the Prophet ordered: "When one of you eats, he must mention (remember) God."2 Such a precaution will not only assure a blessing but will also banish all demons, which are always ready to hurt human beings.

Whenever a person passes a shrine—even if he is some distance from it—and meets with a difficulty or inconvenience, he begs the assistance of that saint; "he urges him by his honor" <note>original Arabic: *biynhâh* or *bistadjîr fîh*</note>. Usually, the nearest saint <note>original term: *welî*, a holy person or "friend of God"</note> is called upon for help. Many prefer their local saints, whose power they have tested. All believe in the Prophets: Abraham, the Friend of God <note>original: *Ḥalîl allâh*</note>; Moses, the Interlocutor of God <note>original: *Kalîm allâh*; referring to him speaking directly with God</note>; David, the Prophet of God <note>original: *Nabîy allâh*</note>; Mohammed, the Messenger of God <note>original: *Rasûl allâh*</note>; and Jesus, the Spirit of God <note>original: *Îsâ (Rûḥ allâh)*</note>. The usual expressions are: "I implore you, O apostle of God" <note>original: *daḥîl ‘alêk yâ rasûl allâh*</note>; and "I am your neighbor, O my lady, O Badriyeh" <note>original: *anâ ṭanîb ‘alêki yâ sittî yâ Badrîyeh*</note>.

---

1 L. Einsler, *Mosaics*, p. 2 ff.; *Superstition*, p. 11.
2 Sheikh Abd el-Majid Ali, *The Pleasing Gift regarding the Holy Accounts* <note>original Arabic: *at-Tuḥfatu l-Marḍiyah fil-Aḥbâr el-Qudsîyah*</note>, etc., p. 4.
3 The *h* is to be pronounced.
4 *Khalil* = "friend," the surname of Abraham.
5 *Kalim* = Interlocutor, the surname of Moses.
6 *Nabiy* = Prophet, the surname of David.
7 *Rasul* = Messenger or Apostle, the surname of Mohammed.
8 *Ruh* = Spirit, the surname of Christ. This name is based on Surah 21 <note>of the Quran</note>, where we read: "And remember her (the Virgin Mary) who preserved her virginity, and into whom we breathed of our spirit."
9 "I implore you," "I implore your protection," and "I am under your protection" <note>original: *Daḥîl ‘alêk, daḥîl ḥarîmak, daḥîl ‘arḍak* and *daḥîlak*</note> are expressions used when one beseeches another for help.
10 *Tunub* means "tent rope" or "tent peg"; "my neighbor whose tent ropes are near mine" <note>original: *ṭanîbî*</note> (referencing the Arabic dictionary *The Encompassing Ocean*, II, 1297; *Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society* II, 43); "I beseech you to accept me as your neighbor (and thus to give me your help)" <note>original: *ṭanîb ‘alêk*</note>.
11 A man is obliged according to Arabic rules to help, protect, and safeguard his neighbor; "a neighbor is responsible for his neighbor, even if the neighbor acts wrongly toward him" <note>original Arabic: *ed-djâr lad-djâr u-law djâr*</note>.

<page-num>90</page-num>
->Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society<-

Often, people use simple cries for help, such as: "O Sultan Ibrahim, heal my son!" <note>original Arabic: *yâ sulṭân Ibrahîm (išfî ibnî)*</note> or "O Father of the Prophets!" <note>original Arabic: *yâ Abû l-anbiâ*, referring to the patriarch Abraham</note>. Every peasant <term>fellâḥ</term> firmly believes that a saint will respond immediately to a call for help. In Lebanon, I heard a muleteer <note>Muleteers, coachmen, porters, and boatmen were often known for their rough language; an Arabic proverb says, "Like the muleteer, he only remembers God when he is struggling under a heavy load."</note> use the following expression when his animal collapsed under its cargo: "O Saint Elias, you shall have half of it—as long as it isn't injured!" <note>original Arabic: *yâ mâr Elyâs ilak nuṣṣuh walâ ihuṣṣuh*. The word *ihuṣṣuh* was explained as "injured," though the author notes it might linguistically relate to "becoming less."</note>

I will cite several cases where help is requested to illustrate this belief. For instance, a camel belonging to a poor camel driver <term>djammâl</term> might slip on a rock, risking a broken leg and the loss of a precious animal. <note>At this time, a camel was often a family's only source of income and their entire capital.</note> Or, a donkey carrying a heavy sack of flour might fall under its load. <note>A man who drives donkeys carrying grain to a mill is called a *tarrâs*. While this term is used in Egypt to mean a "hirer of donkeys," in Jerusalem and the surrounding districts, it specifically refers to someone who loads animals very heavily.</note> A traveler might lose his way at night and find all efforts to find the right direction are useless. 

The following story illustrates this point: Dr. Djad from Haifa was invited to a friend's wedding in the village of Yabad. While there, he suffered an attack of fever. When a coachman leaving for Haifa refused to take him along, the doctor begged the Saint Mesar <note>original term: *ṣaḥâbeh*, meaning a Companion of the Prophet Muhammad. Specifically Mesarah bin Masruq el-Absi, whose shrine is located on a mountain peak between Jiffin and Baqah.</note> to punish the driver. Consequently, the coachman's wheel broke on the journey. This same physician also prayed to the saint to return his brother, who had been deported by the Turks; his brother was eventually brought back in a way that seemed miraculous.

The assistance of saints is sought not only for minor inconveniences but also during great hardships. As we examine the nature of religious vows, we shall...

...see that these are really nothing but cries for help combined with promises. I will share another story <note>original: Heard from various residents of this village.</note> here to better illustrate this point. The Bedouins of the Tayâhâ tribe once attacked Beit Jala <note>original: Bêt Djâlâ</note> during the night. The terrified inhabitants, who were far fewer than the Bedouins, called out to Saint Nicholas <note>original: Mâr Inqûlâ; they called for help, or *istandjadû*</note> to assist them. Suddenly, the Bedouins began to flee in confusion, shouting: "I seek refuge in God from a village whose trees throw stones!" <note>original Arabic: *a'ûdu bil-lâh min balad šdjârhâ turšuk ḥdjâr*</note>. The village peasants gathered in the saint's church to thank him for delivering them so miraculously from their fierce enemies. To their great surprise, they saw that the icon <term>qûneh</term> <note>original: *qûneh*, a religious image; other instances where a saint's image exhibits a miraculous sign will be discussed in a later section</note> was dripping with sweat. This was taken as another sign of his power. <note>Since that time, Saint Nicholas has been regarded as the protector of the village.</note>

## II. Repetition of prayers

Upon entering a shrine <term>maqâm</term>, a pious peasant <term>fellâḥ</term> will recite "The Opening" <note>original: *fâtiḥah*</note>, which is the first chapter <term>sûreh</term> of the Quran. Reciting this prayer is believed to bring the blessing of both the saint and God. A peasant recites this prayer not only when entering the sanctuary, but every time he passes by one—especially at night.

When a pilgrim is traveling to visit a prophet's shrine and reaches a high vantage point where the sanctuary is visible, he stands still and says, "I testify that there is no god but God" <note>original Arabic: *ašhadu anna lâ ilâhan illâ-llâh*</note>. Travelers often do the same even if they do not intend to visit the shrine, simply upon seeing the sanctuary <term>maqâm</term> from a distance. In both cases, the passerby raises his hands and then wipes them down over his face to receive a blessing <term>et-tabarruk</term>. <note>The writer Abd al-Rahman al-Kawakibi suggests in his work *The Nature of Despotism* (*Ṭabâi'u l-Istibdâd*) that this action is a remnant of the Christian custom of making the sign of the cross.</note> In the first instance, several stones might be piled into a heap, which is called a memorial or "place of testimony" <term>mašhad</term>. <note>The author notes that he has never heard the terms *karkûr* or *rakûbeh*—mentioned by Edmond Doutté in his book *Magic and Religion* (*Magie et Religion*, p. 420 ff.)—used to describe these stone heaps.</note>

When visiting a prophet, the visitor says, "Blessings and peace be upon you, O Prophet of God" <note>original Arabic: *aṣ-ṣalâtu wassalâmu 'alêka yâ nabîy allâh*</note>. Such a prayer is never used for an ordinary saint <term>welî</term>. A pilgrim...
