<meta>This page continues the discussion of prayers and rituals performed at the shrines of saints, focusing on specific invocations and the physical acts of reverence required of visitors.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

...says when visiting Ahmad al-Rifa’i <note>original: Aḥmad er-Rfâʿî</note>: "O Ahmad, O Rifa'i, protect us from every creeping thing <note>original Arabic: *yâ Aḥmad yâ Rfâʿî iḥmînâ min kull šî sâʿî*</note>" (referring to reptiles).²

Prayers offered in a shrine are generally intended for the benefit of the saint; it is believed that God will count these prayers toward the spiritual merit of the saint <term>wali</term> <note>original: *welî*; a Muslim holy person or saint</note>. This is why in some places one hears: "Recite the Opening <term>Fatiha</term> <note>original: *iqra l-fâtiḥah*; the first chapter of the Quran</note> for the soul of the sheikh <note>original: *eš-šêḥ*</note>..." or "Recite the Opening for the sheikh..."

Many visitors preserve their names by writing a verse from the Quran on the wall of the shrine <term>maqam</term> <note>original: *maqâm*</note> or on the cenotaph <note>a tomb-like monument for someone buried elsewhere</note>. A typical sentence is: "I entrust to this shrine my testimony that there is no God but Allah <note>original Arabic: *audaʿtu fî hâda l-maqâm šihâdatî anna lâ ilâhan illa-llâh*</note>." After finishing the visit, the pilgrim often makes the request: "Accept my visit, O [Saint's name]... <note>original Arabic: *itqabbal zyârtî yâ . . .*</note>."³

### III. Reverence

The respect shown toward holy places is universal and shown in various ways. Today, these customs are not observed nearly as strictly as they once were. A devout peasant will not enter the shrine of any important saint <term>wali</term> without taking off his shoes.⁴ He either leaves them outside or carries them with him.⁵ In some instances—such as the shrines of Sheikh al-Turi <note>original: *eš-šêḥ eṭ-Ṭôrî*</note> and Sheikh Abd al-Salam <note>original: *eš-šêḥ ʿAbd es-Salâm*</note>—I was not allowed to enter unless barefoot. However, in most other places—such as Sheikh Abdallah in Qubeiba <note>original: *Qubêbeh*</note>, Sheikh Salman al-Farsi on the Mount of Olives, Hasan al-Ra'i near the Prophet Moses <note>original: *Nebî Mûsâ*</note> shrine, or Sheikh Hamad in Kolonia <note>modern Motza</note>—no objections were raised at all.

---

¹ Ahmad al-Rifa’i <note>original: Aḥmad er-Rfâʿî</note> was famous for healing patients bitten by snakes. His followers still practice as snake-charmers.
² Ahmad al-Rifa’i was a spiritual pillar <term>qutub</term> <note>original: *qutub*; a high-ranking saint in Sufism</note>. His shrine in Palestine is in Beni Zeid <note>original: *Benî Zêd*</note>, about an hour east of Deir Ghassaneh <note>original: *Dêr Ghassâneh*</note>. It is believed that there is a cave <term>ghar</term> <note>original: *ghâr*</note> beneath the shrine where his tomb is located.
³ Christians write: "Remember, O Lord, your servant... <note>original Arabic: *udkur yâ rabb ʿabdak . . .*</note>"
⁴ Because shoes become ritually unclean by walking on the street—which is full of impurities, including human and animal waste—they must be removed.
⁵ At the Dome of the Rock <note>original: *Omar Mosque*</note>, the Al-Aqsa Mosque, the Prophet Moses <note>original: *Nebî Mûsâ*</note> shrine, and other sanctuaries, those who do not wish to remove their shoes may put on slippers or sandals to visit the sites.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual respect shown toward holy sites in Palestine, specifically focusing on the requirement of ritual purity for those entering shrines or approaching sacred natural features like springs.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>

Many people will not enter a shrine unless they are in a ritually clean condition. For this reason, many peasants would not enter the shrine <term>maqam</term> <note>original: *maqâm*; a local saint's shrine</note> with me, but waited outside. Even when I asked them questions about various things, they answered from outside without stepping in.^1

These customs of the modern Palestinian remind us of ancient times, when the Lord called to Moses out of the midst of the Burning Bush: "Take off your sandals from your feet, for the place where you stand is holy ground."^2 The ancient Hebrew worshippers were also not allowed to approach the sanctuary without first purifying themselves, for only those who were ceremonially clean were accepted.^3

No "unclean" woman^4 ever approaches or enters a shrine, touches a holy tree, draws water out of a sacred well, or comes near a consecrated spring. There is nothing that will irritate a "man of God" (a saint) as much as such a reckless act.^5 In the midst of the vineyards of Betunia <note>original: Bêtûniâ</note> is the source of Khirbet Nuta <note>original: Ḥirbet Nûtâ</note>, which is guarded by the soul of Sheikh Saleh <note>original: *eš-šêḫ* Ṣâleḥ</note>. From time to time, the water becomes scarce and may even stop flowing. This is always thought to be the result of an unclean woman approaching the opening. Once the water stopped flowing, and as the inhabitants of Betunia searched in vain for the impure woman, a sheep was offered as a sacrifice to Sheikh Saleh. The spring was then thoroughly cleared out, and the water flowed again, even more abundantly than before.^6 Nor are springs inhabited by spirits <term>demons</term> <note>original: *demons*; likely referring to jinn or guardian spirits</note> to be approached by any menstruating woman or a woman with any persistent bleeding. The spring of Jifna <note>original: Djifnâ</note>, inhabited by a "bride," is an—

This disrespectful act irritated the man of God so much that he punished the evildoer with paralysis, which did not disappear until the saint <term>wali</term> <note>original: *welî*</note> was reconciled by the offering of two sheep.
^2 Exodus 3:5.

This is not the place to discuss the exact effects—both good and evil—that a woman is supposed to cause while she is menstruating.<sup>1</sup>

Those who enter an important shrine remain silent, devout, and humble. When someone speaks, the voice is kept low; no smoking, laughing, or any other disrespectful behavior is permitted.

Before continuing, I must emphasize that the reverence described above is regularly shown only to the prophets and the important <term>awliâ</term> <note>original: *awliâ*; the plural of *walî*, meaning saints or "friends of God"</note>. It is practiced much less at other shrines. While visiting the <term>djâmï' el-Arb'în</term> <note>original: *djâmï' el-Arb'în*; the Mosque of the Forty</note> in <term>'Êsâwîyeh</term> <note>Isawiya, a village near Jerusalem</note>, I found a tax collector from Jerusalem sleeping, eating, and conducting his business within the holy place. We were offered coffee which was brewed on the spot, and everyone smoked their cigarettes without being troubled at all.

The holy men are extremely offended when anyone commits adultery while inside or in the neighborhood of the shrine. It is believed that the entire mountain upon which the shrine of <term>Nebî Mûsâ</term> <note>Prophet Moses</note> is built shakes violently when such an unholy act is performed.

No animals are ever permitted to enter a shrine. Some peasants strongly object to hitching a horse in the immediate vicinity of a <term>maqâm</term> <note>a saint's shrine</note>. This latter custom, however, is slowly disappearing. Many stories are told to illustrate how the <term>awliâ</term> <note>saints</note> have punished trespassing animals with death. The following is an example:<sup>2</sup> One day, while heading to their daily work in the fields, some inhabitants of <term>'Êsâwîyeh</term> found a dead jackal with an oil lamp in its mouth. It lay directly in front of the shrine of <term>eš-šêḫ 'Anbar</term> <note>Sheikh Anbar</note>. This clearly showed that the beast had entered the shrine and carried away the oil lamp, for which it was immediately punished. Sometimes the saints are patient and give animals enough time to stop desecrating the sanctuary. But eventually—especially when human beings begin to doubt their power—they take revenge. <term>'Ên Qînâ</term> <note>Qina Spring</note> is inhabited by the <term>welî</term> <note>saint</note>...

---

<sup>1</sup> Some effects have been mentioned in *Superstition* <note>original: *Aberglaube*; referring to Canaan's book on Palestinian superstitions</note>, p. 36 ff. Others include: if a menstruating woman walks through a green field, the grass will wither. Some foods should not be prepared by a woman in this state, as they may spoil; milk, for example, will sour. Anyone who sleeps with a menstruating woman will become a leper.

<sup>2</sup> Other stories include: <term>Abû Ṣâlḥiyeh</term> (<term>Bêt 'Anân</term>) kills every gazelle that eats the leaves of his olive trees. Any animal that climbs on the building of <term>eš-šêḫ Yâsîn</term> <note>Sheikh Yasin</note> falls down dead. Whenever a herd of goats or sheep approaches <term>el-Qubbeh</term> <note>The Dome</note> (to the east of <term>Dêr Djrîr</term>), the <term>ṣullâḥ</term> <note>original: *ṣullâḥ*; "the righteous ones," referring to pious spirits or saints</note> drive them away. See also Jaussen, p. 308.

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

Abul-Enein. Every Thursday night, the peasants used to light an oil lamp in his honor, but jackals would regularly drink the oil. This irritated the owner of the land surrounding the spring, Mohammed Abdallah, so much that he lit an oil lamp for the saint and said: "If you cannot protect your own property, we will never light your lamp again." The next morning, a jackal was found dead with the oil lamp in its mouth. This, of course, conclusively proved the power of the <term>saint</term> <note>original: *welî*; a Muslim saint or "friend of God"</note>.

A woman used to place a <term>bowl</term> <note>original: *ṭôz*, also pronounced *ṭôṣ*</note> of butter under the protection of the saint Abu Ndjem. The next day, she noticed that part of it had been stolen. Praying for the saint to paralyze the thief’s hand, she set out another bowl. The next day, she found a <term>jackal</term> <note>original: *wâwî*</note> with a paralyzed front leg still dipped in the bowl. Abu Ndjem is located on the top of a hill one hour south of Artas. He was the prophet's <term>herald</term> <note>original: *naddjâb*</note>. A <term>herald</term> <note>original: *naddjâb*</note> is one who travels ahead of a prophet (or the <term>leader</term> <note>original: *šêḫ*</note> of a <term>Sufi order</term> <note>original: *ṭarîqah*</note>) in a procession to the next village to announce his coming and the arrival of his followers. Sheikh Abed (near Sataf) is said to kill any goat or sheep that enters his cave. There are exceptions where animals are intentionally allowed to enter the door of a shrine, but no further. These cases will be mentioned later.

The shrine and its surroundings are always supposed to be kept clean. However, this is rarely the case in reality. We should, however, clarify what the peasants mean by cleanliness. <term>Ritual impurity</term> <note>original: *Nadjâseh*</note> refers to anything considered ritually unclean, such as human and (to a lesser extent) animal waste,² corpses, and unclean food or drink.³ <term>Ritual cleansing</term> <note>original: *Et-tadjmîr*</note>—specifically, the practice of cleaning the penis against walls after urinating—is strictly forbidden. In this sense, holy places are kept more or less clean, but not in terms of modern sanitation, as large amounts of rubbish are found in and around places like Banat esh-sheikh Salah and el-Umari.

² Urine, fecal matter, pus, menstrual blood, spit, etc. A Turkish officer once relieved himself under the tree of Sheikh Abu Rish (Bet Anan). A stranger who spent the night in the <term>mosque</term> <note>original: *djâmi'*</note> of Abu l-On (Biddu) passed gas in his sleep. The offended <term>saint</term> <note>original: *welî*</note> immediately threw him out of the holy place.
³ Pork and all alcoholic drinks.

Sheikh Nuran,<note>original: šêḫ Nûrân</note>^1 and cut down a tree belonging to <term>Sheikh Abu Hureira</term> <note>original: eš-šêḫ Abû Ḥurêrî</note>.^2 F. The peasants knew that this was the expected punishment from el-Badriyeh.^3

### IV. Religious services in a shrine as in a mosque

In some shrines, daily prayers are performed; that is, they serve as a mosque. All such shrines have a <term>prayer niche</term> <note>original: *mihrâb*; a wall niche indicating the direction of Mecca</note>, and they are located within villages or not far from them. The inhabitants of villages near Jerusalem do not have many such <term>mosques</term> <note>original: *djawâmi*</note> because the peasants come to the city on Friday^4 to perform the noon prayers <note>original: *ṣalât ed-djum'ah*</note> in the <term>Mosque of Omar</term> <note>A common historical name for the Al-Aqsa Mosque compound or the Dome of the Rock</note>. In villages further away from Jerusalem, such mosques are common. These mosques are visited especially on <term>Friday eve</term> <note>original: *lêlat ed-djum'ah*; in the Islamic calendar, the "night of Friday" begins at sunset on Thursday</note> and on Friday. In some places, <term>Monday eve</term> <note>original: *lêlat et-ṯnên*; Sunday night</note>^5 also receives this honor. The same may be said of feast days. The visits during the <term>seasonal festivals</term> <note>original: *mawâsim*</note> will be described below.

In this connection, I wish to observe that some Christian churches are respected and visited by <term>Muslims</term> <note>original: Mohammedans</note>. Churches dedicated to <term>Saint George</term> <note>original: St. George</note>—especially in the village of el-Khader near Bethlehem—frequently enjoy this privilege. <term>Lady Mary</term> <note>original: Sittî Maryam; the Virgin Mary</note> comes next. The Chapel of the Ascension, the Church of the Nativity, the Milk Grotto, and <term>Saint Elias</term> <note>original: Mâr Elyâs</note>^6 follow. The last two enjoy only the respect of the neighboring Muslim villages.^7 According to <term>Mujir al-Din</term> <note>original: Mudjîr; a 15th-century historian and judge in Jerusalem</note>

### V. The Barakeh

The peasant does not visit a shrine only to fulfill a vow, to swear an oath, or to be cured of a disease. Very often, they go to these places to receive a "blessing." *Barakeh*² means even more than a simple blessing; as the scholar Kahle says,³ it denotes a benevolent power that radiates from the holy place to everyone who comes into contact with it. In order to receive such a blessing, the visitor touches the tomb, its coverings, the prayer beads, and other items, and then passes their hands over their face and down their body. In this way, they distribute the blessing transmitted through their hands to their entire body.⁴

Others will kiss one of these objects, or even take a piece of the <term>tomb covering</term> <note>original: *stârah*; the ornamental cloth draped over a saint's cenotaph</note> and wrap it around a part of their body. One receives a more lasting *barakeh* from the oil of the lamps, which may be used to rub the hands and the face. Water from sacred cisterns may be drunk as a blessing; at the same time, the devout peasant believes that it has an especially beneficial effect on the body. Many <term>pilgrims</term> <note>original: *hadj*</note> bring back one or more bottles filled with water from the <term>Zamzam Well</term> <note>A holy well in Mecca</note> and distribute small quantities of it to their friends. I have also been honored with such water.

Many visitors will take some souvenir of the shrine as a *barakeh*. A person might carry it on their body or hang it in their house, believing that it will bring good fortune. The most important object in this category is the <term>"pear"</term> <note>original: *en-ndjâṣah*</note>,⁵ which is said to be made of earth from the <term>Kaaba</term> <note>original: *el-Ka'beh*; the sacred building at the center of Islam's most important mosque in Mecca</note> mosque, dipped in the blood of sacrifices, and shaped like a pear.⁶ Nearly all pilgrims bring several of these and distribute them to their friends, who hang them in their houses.⁷

---

1. See Volume II, page 411.
2. I do not think that *barakeh* means the opposite of the "evil eye," as Edmond Doutté states in *Magic* <note>original: *Magie*</note>, page 440.
3. *Palestine Yearbook VII* <note>original: *PJ VII*; Palästinajahrbuch</note>, pages 104 and following.
4. The same custom exists in some Eastern Churches after the <term>offertory</term> <note>The part of a religious service where offerings are made</note> (Stephan).
5. Canaan, *Superstition* <note>original: *Aberglaube*; referring to his book *Aberglaube und Volksmedizin im Lande der Bibel*</note>, page 86.
6. Mr. Stephan calls my attention to the fact that such earth "blessings" are sold from the shrines of Karbala, Najaf, and Kadhimayn <note>Major Shi'ite holy cities in Iraq</note>.
7. The same belief exists among Christians, who take with them Jordan River water, leaves from the olive trees of Gethsemane, or keep icons that have been sanctified by being placed on the Holy Sepulchre or in the Church of the Nativity.

<summary>This page explores the concept of 'barakeh' (divine blessing/power) and the various ways Palestinian pilgrims seek to obtain and transport this power from holy sites through touch, sacred water, and symbolic objects.</summary>
<keywords>barakeh, blessing, shrines, pilgrimage, folk medicine, holy water, Zamzam, Kaaba, en-ndjâṣah, Palestinian customs</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of barakeh (blessings) and the physical ways people seek to obtain it from holy sites and objects.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

As all the examples mentioned above have shown, it is not only the unseen souls of the holy men which possess this power, but everything which belongs to the <term>shrine</term> <note>original: *maqâm*; literally the "abiding place" of the soul</note> has it and is able to radiate it to human beings.¹ Doubtless a part of the body of the saint himself,² or an object connected directly with his life history, possesses more of this miraculous virtue. Thus, the hairs from the beard of the Prophet <note>Muhammad</note>³ which are kept in the Mosque of Omar <note>The Dome of the Rock</note> are visited every year on the 27th of <term>Ramadan</term> <note>The holy month of fasting; this specific night is Laylat al-Qadr, the Night of Power</note>.⁴ A piece of the "true cross"⁵ of Christ is carried by many Christians for the same reason.

Besides these ways of deriving a blessing from a <term>saint</term> <note>original: *welî*</note>, many peasants try to gain this benefit by visiting different important shrines. Thus, many of the pilgrims to <term>Nabi Musa</term> <note>The Tomb of the Prophet Moses</note>, after the <term>festival</term> <note>original: *môsam*</note> is over, pay visits to the <term>Sanctuary of Hebron</term> <note>original: *Ḥaram el-Khalîl*</note>, the Prophet Samuel <note>original: *Nebî Șamwŷl*</note>, the Prophet Saleh <note>original: *Nebî Șâleḥ*</note>, etc. Many a pilgrim makes his way back across Palestine and Egypt merely to <term>obtain a blessing</term> <note>original: *et-tabarruk*</note> from the different <term>saints</term> <note>original: *awliyâ*</note>. A repentant sinner believes that through such visits and prayers he will receive a blessing which will take off all his burdens.⁶ The soul of the saint, which is pleased with such actions, is always ready to help.⁷

In every important shrine, the visitor is guided by the so-called <term>shrine servants</term> <note>original: *ḥuddâm el-maqâm*</note>, who are attached to the different holy places and advise him what prayer is to be said in each spot. But there are now also small guidebooks for the holy places of Jerusalem and Hebron. The best known is *The Visitor’s Guide and the Directory for the Ritual Rituals and Visits to the Holy Places of Jerusalem and Hebron* <note>original: *al-muršid liz-zâir wad-dalîl fî manâsik wâ zyârât amâkin el-Quds wal-Khalîl*</note>,⁸ a larger one than that mentioned...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how pilgrims and devotees seek holy blessings (barakeh) by visiting various shrines across Palestine and by interacting with living holy men.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

by Kahle (*The Rites of Jerusalem the Noble*¹ <note>original: *manâsik el-Quds eš-Šarîf*</note>). These guide-books² state exactly what chapter <note>original: *sûreh*</note> and what prayer is to be said in every holy place.

The sheikhs <note>original: *šiyûḫ*</note> returning with their followers from the festivals <note>original: *mawâsim*</note> of the Prophet Moses <note>original: *Nebî Mûsâ*</note>, the Prophet Reuben <note>original: *Nebî Rûbîn*</note>, the Prophet Salih <note>original: *Nebî Ṣâleḥ*</note>, etc., to their villages try to visit as many of the local shrines as they can pass on their way. If any saint's sanctuary <note>original: *welî*</note> lies at a distance from the road, the procession stops, the musical troop <note>original: *saiyârah*</note> plays, and the sheikh <note>original: *šēḫ*</note> recites the Opening <note>original: *fâtiḥah*; the first chapter of the Quran</note>.

The blessing <note>original: *barakeh*</note> is not only derived from dead saints, but living holy sheikhs may also radiate it. Sheikh Abu Halawy had wide renown for this during his lifetime. People kiss his hand, touch his garments, eat some of the sheikh’s food, or perform similar actions to obtain this blessing. The following story illustrates the idea:³ A., the husband of Umm F. <note>original: *Imm F.*</note>, was away in Constantinople. His wife, having received no news from him, went with her daughter, who was eight years old, to Sheikh Abu Halawy. Before entering his room, they heard him calling, “Welcome, daughter of the Spiritual Pole <note>original: *el-quṭub*; a high-ranking Sufi saint</note>, the descendant of the Great Succor <note>original: *el-Ghôṣ*</note>.” They were astonished that he had recognized them before they entered. Seating Umm F. at his side, he took some bread and sugar from his breast-pocket⁴ and offered them to her, and she ate them to receive a blessing <note>original: *barakeh*</note>. Abu Halawy proceeded at once to tell the following story: “While we were journeying on the sea, we lost our way and reached an unknown and uninhabited island. During the night, the storm drove our boat ashore. In vain did we labor to get the ship afloat. While everyone was mourning our desperate condition, your ancestor, the Spiritual Pole and Succor <note>original: *quṭub el-Ghôṣ*</note>, appeared. He floated the ship, showed us the direction of our journey, assured us of a safe arrival, and disappeared as miraculously as he had appeared. This all happened last Wednesday.” Several days later, A. arrived. He told them how the Spiritual Succor had saved their lives. Now believing in the power of the sheikh,⁵ he visited him to seek a blessing <note>original: *et-tabarruk*</note>.

I will not describe in this chapter the healing powers of the saints, which some¹ attribute to their spiritual blessing <note>original: *barakeh*</note>, but which, as I will show later, is due to other powers.

## 2. PLACING PRIVATE PROPERTY UNDER THE PROTECTION OF A SAINT <note>original: *welî*</note>

Many of the saints' shrines are located out in the fields, far from the villages. Since a peasant's work is mostly in the fields—cultivating his land, reaping his harvest, quarrying stones, or gathering thorns to burn lime—he lives under the protection of these holy men. He may personally need their help, especially when he must leave some of his property behind in the fields. In such a case, he places everything he cannot carry home under their guard, feeling absolutely certain that no one will dare to touch his belongings. The saints are very protective in this regard, severely punishing anyone who steals their own property or anything placed in their care. 

Various stories are told to demonstrate this. The village headman <note>original: *muḫtâr*; the local leader of a village</note> of Shu'fat <note>original: *Šu'fâṭ*</note> told me about the following incident that happened to him during his childhood. Some inhabitants of the village had spread their olives on the roof of the shrine of Sultan Ibrahim el-Adhami <note>original: *es-sulṭân Ibrâhîm el-'Adhamî*; a famous 8th-century ascetic and Sufi saint</note> so they would ripen quickly in the heat of the sun. He climbed up during the night and filled his pockets and the bosom of his robe <note>original: *'ibb*; the fold of a garment used as a large pocket</note> with olives. The saint did not interfere the first or second time, but when the boy climbed up the third time, a venerable old man appeared to him. He was dressed in white, with a white beard and a spear in his hand, and he said: "By God, I shall cut your life short and cripple you if you dare steal another time." The frightened boy answered: "By God, I repent, O Sheikh" <note>original: *wallâh tubt yâ šêḫ*</note>. 

Another story illustrates the point in a different way. Once, some thieves broke into the cattle enclosure <note>original: *ṣîreh*</note> belonging to the neighbors of Sheikh Zakari <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Zakarî*</note> and stole the cattle. They had barely gone a few meters from the place when the saint <note>original: *welî*</note> struck them with blindness. Being unable to see their way and recognizing the severe punishment inflicted upon them by this man of God, they returned the cows to their place. At once their sight returned, and they escaped further punishment by running away.

---

¹ Kahle and Doutté.

Two anecdotes illustrate these supernatural interventions: a boy warned by a vision of a saint and thieves miraculously blinded until they returned stolen cattle.</summary>

<page-num>103</page-num>

A variety of objects are left at these sites for safekeeping. At the shrines of Sheikh Abdallah <note>original: *eš-šēḫ ’Abdallāh*; 'Sheikh' is a title for a religious leader or saint</note> in Shu’fat, Sheikh Ubeid in Deir Yasin, and Sheikh Ramadan, I saw large piles of thorns.^1 When I visited the cave of Sheikh Isa in Beit Liqya and the shrine <note>original: *maqām*</note> of Sheikh Ahmad et-Tayyar in Qastal, I found straw <note>original: *tibn*; crushed straw or chaff used as animal fodder</note> stored there. I also found three sour milk <note>original: *laban*; a staple yogurt-like drink</note> pitchers placed in front of the cave of Rijal Abu Tuh in Beit Liqya. A peasant from the village of el-Jib deposited his lime in the Mosque of the Forty <note>original: *Djâmi’ el-Arb’in*</note>. At the shrine of Sheikh Hamid in the same village, two donkey-loads of pottery were placed under the protection of the saint <note>original: *welî*</note>. In the shrine of Sheikh Ahmad at Khirbet Qaryet Sa'ideh, I saw farming tools. At Sheikh Abdallah es-Sidri in Khirbet es-Sidd, the Bedouin deposit some of their household belongings. However, it is not only the items left within the actual holy area that are protected by these holy men of God; even the property of those living near a sanctuary is guarded, as is clearly shown by the story related on the previous page.

The description and examples above show clearly how strictly the saints <note>original: *awliâ*</note> uphold the ancient Arab customs which are still practiced by the inhabitants of Palestine.^2

### 3. TYING OF RAGS

Tying rags to holy objects is a very old custom that is still found all over the Middle East. Rags are fastened to sacred trees, to the iron bars of sanctuary windows, to door handles, to the hooked staff <note>original: *miḥdjân*</note>, and to the headstone <note>original: *râsîyeh*</note> of the tomb. The hooked staff^3 is the stick (with a curved handle) associated with the saint <note>original: *wēlī*</note>. Tying rags to the staff or the tomb itself is seen much less often than the other practices. These "visiting cards" are sometimes so numerous that every inch of the window bars and every reachable twig of a sacred tree is covered with them.

They are generally fastened by visitors with one of the following intentions:

1. As a sign that they have visited the shrine <note>original: *maqām*</note> and fulfilled their religious duties.
2. The piece of rag acts as a reminder to the saint <note>original: *welî*</note> not to forget the visitor and their specific requests.

---

^1 These are gathered by the women and stored to be used as fuel during the winter.
^2 See also Goldziher, *Muslim Studies, Volume II* <note>original: *Moh. Studien II*</note>, page 301.
^3 See *The Circumference of the Ocean* <note>original: *Muḥîṭ el-Muḥîṭ*; a famous 19th-century Arabic dictionary</note>, Volume I, page 353.

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the practice of tying rags to holy objects as a form of "contact magic" to transfer sickness or seek a saint's blessing.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

3. Very often, a sick person tears a small piece from his clothes and ties it with the words, “I have thrown my burden (that is, 'my sickness') on you, O man of God.”¹ It is firmly believed that the saint will banish the disease.² In Beit Unia <note>original: *Bêt Ûnîâ*</note>, the relatives of a person suffering from fever put two baskets of stones on a certain tree, believing that the disease will soon disappear.³

The fundamental principle at the base of these three ideas is what we call “contact magic.” That is to say, everything which has been in contact with someone or has belonged to him will never completely lose its relation to that person. Thus, these pieces of cloth always keep their connection with the person from whom they came. They represent him, and anything done to them will happen to their owner. They represent the visitor, reminding the holy man of the visit performed, imploring help, and begging for a cure. Through their direct contact for a long period with the holy place, they gain some of the power of the saint <note>original: *welî*; a Muslim holy person or saintly protector</note>, which is transmitted to the person from whom they come and to the one who unfastens and carries them. This superstitious belief is well established in Palestinian folklore, and we shall have occasion again to speak about it.

In the sanctuary of el-Mansuri <note>original: *el-Manṣûrî*</note> in Awartah <note>original: *‘Awartah*</note>, I saw five pieces of cloth the size of large handkerchiefs hanging down from vines and mulberry trees there. Two of them were green, one red, one bluish, and the fifth yellowish. I was told that they were *sitar* <note>original: *sitâr*, plural of *sitreh*</note> or “coverings” of the tomb. It was argued that, as it is impossible for a man to vow a real cover for the huge tomb (which measures 440 by 235 centimeters), these small pieces were promised instead. The prophet is satisfied and accepts them as though they were real, large coverings.⁴

The same idea is expressed by placing small or large stones on the grave of a saint or on a holy tree. This custom is much less common than tying rags. I have seen stones on the tomb of Hasan er-Rai <note>original: *Ḥasan er-Râ‘î*</note>, Sheikh Ghanim <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Ghânim*</note>, Abu Hurayrah <note>original: *Abû Ḥurêrah*</note>, the mosques of our Lady Aisha <note>original: *masâdjid sittnâ ‘Âišeh*</note>, Sheikh Abdallah <note>original: *eš-šêḫ ‘Abdallâh*</note>, and others. In such cases, the stones are generally a sign...

² Compare to *Superstition* <note>original German: *Aberglaube*</note>, page 25.

...of having visited the sanctuary. In the case of the Prophet <note>original: *en-nabî*</note> Yaqin near Beni Na'im <note>original: *Benî N‘êm*</note>, the door handle and the railing surrounding the holy rock—which shows the footprints of Abraham—are covered in these rags. Bracelets, beads, and small necklaces were also found among the rags.<gloss>1. I observed the same practice at the shrine of Abu Hurayrah <note>original: *Abû Ḥurêrah*</note>.</gloss> Many people take some of these rags and carry them, believing that they will receive a blessing <note>original: *barakeh*</note> from the saint <note>original: *welî*</note>.<gloss>2. Referencing Samuel Curtiss, *Primitive Semitic Religion Today*, Chapter 7.</gloss> This is particularly practiced by the sick. In every such case, a new rag must be put in place of the one that is removed. This custom undoubtedly resembles the one recorded in the Bible in Acts 19:12:

> "So that even handkerchiefs or aprons that had touched his skin were carried away to the sick, and their diseases left them and the evil spirits came out of them."

The Bedouin and semi-nomadic peoples often fasten their head-bands <note>original: *‘uqâl*; the cord used to hold a headcloth in place</note> to a holy tree. They believe this is highly effective because it is a complete piece of clothing and represents the owner more fully. In very rare cases, an amulet <note>original: *ḥidjâb*</note> is tied to a holy object. The idea is that it gathers more power, which is then added to the strength of the magical or Quranic formula written inside. I have only seen this custom practiced in connection with the Prophet <note>original: *en-nabî*</note> Danian.

Some trees where rags may be seen hanging include:

I have never personally found the practice of attaching meat to trees, though scholars like Jaussen¹ and Doughty (as mentioned by Curtiss) have observed it. This custom seems to be more characteristic of the Bedouin.

## 4. HEALING

It is wonderful what a profound belief in the power of the saints still exists in the Middle East. We have seen how the peasant comes to these "demigods" during every phase of his life. He comes seeking help, but he also comes to thank them. When a child is severely ill, the mother implores a saint:
> “I beg you, O Prophet David, to cure my son.”
Or in a more humble way, 
> “I am your servant, O Friend of God, save my only child!”²

We have already noted that the present inhabitants of Palestine try to obtain the special favor and help of the saints by promising them offerings if they answer the prayer and cure the sick. But they are not satisfied with these means alone. We observe that there is still a deep-rooted belief among them—as there was among their ancestors—that everything which belongs to or comes in direct contact with a saint or his shrine receives some of his power, which may then be transmitted to others.³ Thus the trees, grass, stones, water, earth from the tomb, the tomb coverings, oil, and even sweepings possess supernatural power by virtue of the saint <note>original: *welî*</note> to whom they belong. This belief leads the person to use such objects, hoping to obtain some of the saint's power to guard him from misfortune, ease his...

³ The people of Beit Jala <note>original: *Bêt Djâlâ*</note> believe that they will sell their goods better if they put some earth from the "Bed of the Lady" <note>original: *srîr et-Saiydeh*; likely referring to a shrine of the Virgin Mary</note> on them.

...pains and hasten his recovery. In examining these curative “medicines,” we are astonished by their great number and variety. We may divide this material into the following classes:

1. Objects taken from the sanctuary and used in a protective or curative way.
2. Acts performed in a sanctuary to cure a disease.

**Regarding 1:** The material used may be of plant <note>original: vegetable</note>, mineral, or liquid origin. The grass which grows around the shrine is gathered and dried if necessary, then used to fumigate <note>The process of using smoke for purification or healing</note> a person who has a fever. <margin>Even better is straw taken from an ant-hill situated near a shrine.</margin> The common word for “fumigate” is *da'aq*. Leaves of trees are used in the same way. Occasionally a decoction <note>a concentrated liquid made by boiling plants</note> of plants is made as a drink <note>original: draught</note>.

Small pieces of wood are cut from holy trees and carried as amulets. The *mês* trees <note>Mediterranean Hackberry, *Celtis Australis L*</note> which grow in the Mosque of Omar area <margin>See *Superstitions* <note>original: *Aberglaube*</note>, pp. 62, 63.</margin> are the most important source of such amulet wood. They are carried as a protection against the evil effect of the "bad eye" <term>evil eye</term>. One sees many children—and sometimes animals—wearing this amulet. 

The most active *‘ûd mês* <note>hackberry twig</note> is that which is cut on the 27th of Ramadan <margin>The 27th of Ramadan is the *Night of Power* <note>original: *lêlatu l-Qadr*</note> mentioned in the 97th Chapter <note>original: Sûrah</note> of the Quran. According to most Muslims, it is one of the last ten nights of this month. In Palestine, the 27th is fixed as the specific night. This is due (according to *The Pleasing Gift regarding the Jerusalem Chronicles, the Prophetic Traditions, and the Creeds of Monotheism* <note>original: *at-Tuḥfah l-Marḍîyah fî l-Aḫbâr el-Qudsîyah wal-Aḥâdît an-Nabâwîyah wal-’Aqâ’id et-Tawḥîdîyah*</note>, by ‘Abd el-Majîd ‘Alî) to the phrase *Night of Power* appearing three times in this chapter. As this phrase contains nine letters in Arabic, the number of letters of this word in this chapter <note>9 letters x 3 appearances = 27</note> indicates which night of Ramadan is intended.</margin> after sunset and before daybreak <margin>Most people cut the twigs just after midnight.</margin>, since the Quran says: “It is peace until the rising of the morn” <margin>From George Sale’s 1734 translation of the Quran.</margin> (the last verse of Chapter 97). 

This night <margin>On this night, the angel Gabriel gave his first revelation to the Prophet Muhammad.</margin> is chosen because everyone believes that the heavens then open, the angels descend, and God grants every wish and hears all prayers. <margin>All sins are forgiven if requested.</margin> Muslims believe that the divine decrees for the ensuing year are annually fixed and settled on this night. <margin>According to the text *The Pleasing Gift*, the Battle of Badr took place on the morning of the following day.</margin> The *mês* trees were planted, according to popular legend, by the *djinn* <term>jinn</term> <note>supernatural spirits or genies</note> as a present to King Solomon for the protection...

of his temple.^1 The small twigs almost always have the shape of a fork with two spikes.^2 Christians, who also believe in the power of this tree—though to a lesser extent than Muslims—cut small twigs from the *mês* tree <note>Mediterranean Hackberry</note> near the Well of the Three Kings.^3 It is believed that the current tree is the descendant of the one under which Mary rested when she was pursued by the Jews.^4 If possible, a twig in the shape of a cross is carried.

Dates are brought from Mecca as a *barakeh* <note>original: *barakeh* (a blessing or spiritual grace)</note> and as a special way to help children begin speaking sooner. But they offer another benefit as well: if young children are allowed to suck on such a date, they will become eloquent speakers with sweet voices.^5 Christians believe that a date from the date palm of *Mâr Sâbâ* <note>Saint Sabas, a monastery in the Judean desert</note>^6 is the best cure^7 for infertility <note>original: sterility</note>.^8 Cut-up palm leaves are used in the "Peel of Thursday" <note>original: *qišret el-Hamîs*; likely referring to Maundy Thursday rituals</note>, which will be described later.

Leaves from the Prophet's olive tree, gathered on the Feast of the Flags <note>original: *djum'et el-'alêmât*</note>^9—which corresponds to Good Friday in the Greek Orthodox Church—help to cure fever and stomach problems. This tree stands between the Al-Aqsa Mosque and the Dome of the Rock <note>original: Omar Mosque</note>, just to the west of the water basin known as "The Cup" <note>original: *el-Kâs*</note>. Every year, the Prophet and his Companions <note>original: *Ṣahâbeh*</note> inhabit this tree at that time. This supernatural event is shown by a quivering vibration in the twigs. Olive trees are held in special honor in Palestine.^10 Christians^11 take small...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal and ritual use of sacred plants in Palestine, transitioning from the use of olive branches in Muslim traditions to Christian practices involving blessed mixtures and the legendary origins of medicinal herbs.</meta>

<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

...olive twigs carried during the Foot-washing procession and mix them with the "Peelings of Thursday" <note>original: *qišret el-Hamîs*</note>.¹

Next, we turn to a purely Christian practice which resembles the customs already described in many respects. The "Peelings of Thursday" has been described already in my book *Superstition* <note>original: *Aberglaube*</note>, and this account may be repeated here with some additions. *Qišret el-Hamîs*, which means literally “the peelings of Thursday,” is composed of the capsules of *maḥlab* (St. Lucie cherry, or *Prunus mahaleb*,² an aromatic grain) mixed with cut-up leaves of palms, olive trees, and some cut flowers. The *maḥlab* capsules are dipped in the water in which the Patriarch of the Greek Orthodox Church washes the feet of the Bishops in the ceremony of Maundy Thursday. The water is perfumed with rose-water and other scents. This sack of ingredients is dried and placed that very evening on the pulpit from which the gospels are read. The olive leaves are taken from the olive branches used in the ceremony of the Foot-washing. The flowers come from the "Mid-Lent Sunday" <note>original: *ḥadd en-nuṣṣ*</note>³ or from the “Feast of the Cross” <note>original: *‘îd eṣ-ṣalîb*</note>. On these two days, the cross is laid on a large plate <note>original: *sinîyeh*</note> covered with flowers. Prayers are said over it, and the flowers are then distributed to the congregation. The palm leaves are from Palm Sunday. Very often, one finds salt and alum mixed with the *Qišret el-Hamîs*. This mixture is used to fumigate <note>To burn as incense so the smoke can treat a person</note> a sick child, as it is considered the best means to prevent the negative effects of the "evil eye." When no salt and alum are mixed in beforehand, they are added just before the fumigation takes place.⁴

Three-lobed sage <note>original: *Mêramîyeh* (*Salvia Triloba*)</note> gets its name from Mary <note>original: Mariam</note>, or Saint Mary. It is said that while Mary was walking once on a hot summer day, she perspired profusely; she plucked a plant to wipe her face, and ever since, this plant has carried her name and is used to cure many diseases.

In examining those objects which belong to the mineral kingdom, we find that plaster <note>original: *qṣârah*</note>, stones, and sweepings from many shrines are used medicinally. The stones are carried as charms, or the water in which they have been soaked is used as a remedy...

¹ It is a widespread custom to place a green olive twig or another type of branch along with a silver coin beneath a threshold, as it is believed that this act symbolizes permanence and prosperity. For the same reason, a large green twig (an olive branch is always preferred) is placed where a tiled roof is being built.

³ The Sunday which falls in the middle of the Lenten fast.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual healing practices in Palestine, transitioning from the use of blessed plants and "Thursday peelings" to the medicinal use of sacred stones, earth, and water from various holy sites.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

The water in which these stones have been placed is then drunk. The black stones of the Prophet Moses <note>original: *Nebî Mûsâ*</note> are considered a very powerful protective amulet <note>original: *ḥirz*</note>. They demonstrate their special power when burned. They contain a bituminous <term>bituminous: containing bitumen, a naturally occurring oily or asphalt-like substance that burns easily</term> substance and are therefore black. Very often they are cut into square or triangular shapes, inscribed with a protective talisman, and carried as a charm <note>original: *ḥidjâb*</note>. Both Christians and Muslims use the soft, whitish stones from the Milk Grotto in Bethlehem to increase a mother’s breast milk. These stones are rubbed into water and given to nursing women to drink. It is believed that the Holy Family took refuge in this cave, and a drop of the Virgin Mary’s milk fell upon the floor. In many cases, a specific number of stones from a particular holy place must be worn for them to be effective. I have always found the number seven to be the standard. For example, seven stones taken from Sheikh Ghreiyb <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Ghreiyb*</note>, the Sheikhs of el-Masalmeh <note>original: *šiyûḫ el Masâlmeh*</note>, or Sheikh Abu Yamin <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Abû Yâmîn*</note>, etc., are used to cure fever.

The sweepings <note>The dust and dirt gathered from the floor of a sanctuary</note> of Sheikh Shnet <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Šnêṭ*</note> in Hebron are said to cure sterility,¹ and those of the Prophet Saleh <note>original: *en-nabî Ṣâleḥ*</note>, Sheikh Qaitun in Hebron <note>original: *šêḫ Qaiṭûn*</note>, and Sheikh Ramadan <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Ramaḍân*</note>,² etc., are used to heal fever. Some of the earth <note>original: *ṭrâbeh*</note> of Sheikh ez-Zughbeh in Malhah <note>original: *eš-šêḫ ez-Zughbeh (Mâlḥah)*</note>, when prepared with oil into a paste, can cure sores on the head. Earth gathered from the Tomb of the Shepherd <note>original: *qabr er-Râʿî*</note>³ and dissolved in water is given to cattle to protect them from disease.

The water from many holy places is also used for curative purposes. Healing is achieved either through a bath or by drinking the water. In most cases, the water is used to heal fever or to prevent sterility. People suffering from fever may take a bath in the Spring of Siloam <note>original: ‘Ên Silwân</note>, the Damascene Spring <note>original: ‘Ên eš-Šâmiyeh</note>,⁴ the Spring of the Prophet Job <note>original: ‘Ên en-nebî Aiyûb</note>, or the well of Sheikh Ibrahim <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Ibrâhîm*</note>,⁵ or they may drink from the Cistern of the Martyrs <note>original: *eš-Šuhadâ*</note>.⁶ In the case of Siloam, it is not a simple bath that cures the fever. The bath must be taken on Friday during the midday prayer, after which water is poured over the patient.

seven consecutive times. When an infertile <note>original: sterile</note> woman seeks a cure for her barrenness, she takes seven gold coins <note>original: *mašâḥiṣ*, the plural of *mašḥaṣ*¹</note> to this spring. She also brings seven keys from doors that open toward the south, and seven cups of water, each taken from a different cistern whose opening is never touched by sunlight.² She places the keys and the gold coins in the water, washes herself with it, and then pours the water from the seven cisterns over herself. These coins <note>original: *mašâḥiṣ*</note> are old gold coins that feature two human figures <note>original: *šaḥṣ* = person</note> on one side.

The Pressing Place <note>original: *El-Maṭbaʿah*</note>, a swamp in the Plain of Jezreel <note>original: Esdraelon</note> between Sheikh Ibreik <note>original: *eš-šêḫ Ibrêk*</note> and Tell esh-Shammam <note>original: *Tell eš-Šammâm*</note>, is famous for curing rheumatism, nerve pains, and infertility. It is believed that Sheikh Ibreik gives this place its healing powers. After an infertile woman has taken a bath in the Pressing Place, she washes herself in Isaac’s Spring <note>original: *ʿÊn Isḥâq*</note> and then goes to the shrine of Sheikh Ibreik to offer a gift. The Prophet Job <note>original: *En-nabî Aiyûb*</note>, located east of Harbata, also helps to cure infertility. Many inhabitants of Jerusalem believe that sitting in the stone basin <note>original: *djurn*</note> of Lady Mary <note>original: *sittî Mariam*³</note> banishes barrenness. It is supposed that the Virgin Mary once took a bath in this basin. Infertile women believe that they may receive children through the blessing of these holy figures, the saints <note>original: *awliâ*</note>. The ancient Hebrews of the Old Testament⁴ shared this same belief.⁵ Washing inflamed eyes with the water of the Spring of the Mother of Almonds <note>original: *ʿÊn Imm el-Lôz*</note>, located below Silwan, is supposed to cure them. Christian women believe, as previously mentioned, that Christ sent the blind man to wash his eyes with its water.⁶

Several springs where the Palestinians believe that Job bathed and was thus cured of his disease are still used for all sorts of skin conditions. Some of these places are:

To this list, I may add that the people of Gaza and the villages to its north and south believe that Job was cured of a severe skin eruption by bathing in the sea on the Wednesday preceding Greek Easter. Therefore, this day is known as *Job’s Wednesday* <note>original: *arba‘ât Aiyûb*</note>, or *Job’s Recovery* <note>original: *ibriyet Aiyûb*</note>. All animals suffering from skin diseases are brought to the sea and bathed on Tuesday evening (which is considered the beginning of Wednesday²). The best place to obtain a cure is near the shrine of *Our Lady the Green One* <note>original: *sittnâ el-Ḥaḍrâ*</note> near the site of Ascalon.

In the vicinity of the springs of Abû Zêd and the Spring of Abu Fakkah <note>original: *‘Ên abû Fakkah*</note> at Bet Zakariya <note>original: *Bêt Zakarîyâ*</note>, certain herbs grow that cure the ill effects of *fear* <note>original: *el-ḥôfeh*</note>. This curative power is attributed to the *Prophet Zechariah* <note>original: *nabî Zakarîyâ*</note>.⁴

The waters of the *Springs of Retention* <note>original: *‘iyûn el-haṣr*</note>—so named for their use in treating the inability to urinate—are renowned throughout Palestine as the best cure for the suppression of urine. Among all the springs in this category, I have found only one supposed to be inhabited by a *saint* <note>original: *welî*</note>: the *Well of Sorcery* <note>original: *Bîr es-Saḥar*</note> in Der Tarif, which is said to be the home of the *Saint Shueib* <note>original: *el-welî Šu‘êb*</note>. The question of how these springs acquired their healing properties remains unanswered.⁵

Not only are items physically part of a shrine used for medicine, but even the offerings left there are employed to cure disease. For example, we find that lamp wicks (from oil lamps), incense, rags tied to a tree or a window, and tomb coverings are believed to possess healing powers. Patients suffering from rheumatic or neuralgic⁶ pains rub their foreheads...

In keeping with this, the priests in the Tabernacle were commanded to perform their daily service from evening until morning. There are many references in the Bible pointing to this method of calculation. See James Neil, *Palestine Life*, pages 1 and following.

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of folk remedies and ritual cures associated with Palestinian shrines, moving from the healing waters of Job to the use of oils, wicks, and sacred objects from the tombs of various saints.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>

<header>CANAAN: Muslim <note>original: Mohammedan</note> Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

People rub their heads and joints with oil taken from shrines. Sometimes this oil is mixed with dried herbs, as is the case with *Sheikh Shnet* <note>original: eš-šêḫ Šnêṭ</note> and *Sheikh Kanfush* <note>original: eš-šêḫ Kanfûš</note> (both located in Hebron), or it is mixed with soil from the shrine, such as at *ez-Zughbeh* in Malhah <note>original: Mâlḥah</note>. This paste is used to treat skin conditions. Whenever oil is taken from any shrine, other oil must be given in its place; if this obligation is neglected, the holy oil may produce the opposite result of what was intended.

The wicks of the *Prophet Moses* <note>original: Nebî Mûsâ</note> (and sometimes those of the *Prophet David* <note>original: Nebî Dâhûd</note> or the *Sanctuary* <note>original: Ḥaram</note> in Hebron) are swallowed by sterile women who believe they will cure their condition. Others carry these wicks as an *amulet* <note>original: ḥidjâb</note> to ward off the same trouble. A patient with a fever is expected to recover if they are treated with the smoke of incense taken from *Our Lady the Green One* <note>original: sittnâ el-Ḥaḍrâ</note> (near ed-Djorah) or with the straw of the mats <note>original: qašš ḥaṣîreh</note> of *Shihab ed-Din* <note>original: Šhâb ed-Dîn</note> (near Jaffa). 

If small children wear a rag taken from a holy tree or a sacred shrine, they are protected against the harmful effects of evil spirits <note>original: djinn</note>. Fumigation with such a rag is believed to cure all diseases caused by these spirits. Whenever a rag is taken from a site, another must be fastened in its place. Rags from the *Tree of Happiness* <note>original: Šadjaret es-Sa'âdeh</note> (located between Yamun and Jenin) are famous for their effectiveness. Women also try to cut a piece off the tomb-covering of the *Prophet Moses* <note>original: Nebî Mûsâ</note> to make into a cup for a sickly child, or for a mother who has lost several of her male children. 

A thread that has been wrapped around the tomb of Moses—thereby matching the length of its circumference—is worn around the waist of a woman suffering from frequent miscarriages to prevent such tragedies in the future. The rosaries of many saints are also used to speed up a difficult labor. As soon as a woman hangs such a rosary around her neck so that it rests over her womb, normal contractions begin, and her pains and troubles are soon over. These miraculous rosaries are called *rosaries of ease* <note>original: masâbiḥ yusur</note>.¹

Among such rosaries are that of *el-Bakri*² (in the possession of Hasan of Der Ghassaneh), that of *Sheikh Abu Yamin* (from Bet Anan, currently held by Sheikh Yusif), and that of *Sheikh el-Arami*. This same miraculous help can also be obtained from the tomb-coverings of the Sheikh—

---

¹ Some use this name to refer to a specific type of rosary.
² O. S. el-Barghuti.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of sacred objects used for healing in Palestinian folk tradition, specifically focusing on holy bread and personal items belonging to saints, before transitioning to ritual actions performed at shrines.</meta>

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

Mohammed al-Khalili.<note>original: Moḥammed el-Ḥalîlî</note>¹ The bread of our master the Sheikh Abu Madian,<note>original: sîdnâ eš-šêḫ Abû Madian</note>² which is prepared from the grain of the religious endowment <term>original: waqf; a charitable endowment under Islamic law</term> of this holy man during the month of Ramadan, is considered a blessing <term>original: barakeh; spiritual grace or divine energy</term> as well as a remedy. In the case of illness, it is hung above the head of the sick person or placed under their pillow. During the bread-making process, the Quran is recited. Generally, the following parts are read while the dough is kneaded:

| | |
|---|---|
| The whole Quran | once |
| The Opening <note>original: Al-Fâtiḥah; the first chapter of the Quran</note> | seven times |
| Surah 112 | ten times |
| Surah 113 | three times |
| Surah 114 | three times |

As it would take a very long time for one person to read the whole Quran alone, as many people as the Quran has parts <term>original: ḥizb; one of sixty sections of the Quran</term> assist in reciting the Holy Book. Some of this bread is sometimes prepared in the month of Shaban <note>original: Šaʿbân; the month preceding Ramadan</note>. However, only that which is made during Ramadan possesses the spiritual power mentioned above.³ Holy bread (the *quddaseh* <note>original: quddâseh; sacramental bread used in the Christian liturgy</note>) is also used by Christians. It should be eaten only while one is ritually clean.

The slippers (*babuj*, plural *bawabij*) of al-Majdub <note>original: el-Madjdûb; literally "the attracted," referring to a Sufi ecstatic</note>⁴ (from Deir Ghassaneh) cure facial paralysis <note>original: miltâḥ</note> by being used to strike the affected side of the face. The slippers of al-Bakri (from Jamma'in), if worn by sterile women, are believed to cure their condition. Regarding the "trembling bowl" <note>original: ṭâsit er-radjfeh; a metal bowl inscribed with prayers used to cure fright</note>, I refer to my article in JPOS III, 122.

**2. Ritual Actions.** We shall now deal with actions connected with a holy place that are performed by the sick person to obtain relief. Such actions are strictly connected with the most holy part of the shrine, generally the tomb. The following examples will illustrate: A man with a fever tries to rid himself of his disease by walking seven times around the tomb of Sheikh Hreish. After each turn, he picks up a stone and places it on the tomb. While the sick person is walking around...

...at the grave, his relatives say prayers. Others believe that taking stones and placing them on the tomb <margin>the Dajani <note>original: الدجاني</note> family</margin>¹ while saying the words, "Carry the fever in my place, O Sheikh Shnet," <note>original: šēḫ Šnēt</note> is enough to remove the fever.²

In Jericho, a fever patient is carried by his relatives and laid on the tomb of Sheikh Ghanim <note>original: eš-šēḫ Ghânim</note>. The relatives withdraw, leaving the sick person alone, for they believe that the soul of this man of God converses with the patient and ultimately heals him.³ In Nablus, those who are very sick are carried to the shrine of the Prophets <note>original: el-Anbiâ</note>. They are placed near the tomb and left alone. If they begin to sweat, it is believed that the disease is improving. In the case of Sheikh Said <note>original: eš-šēḫ Saʿîd</note> in Idnah, those suffering from fever lie within the shrine’s enclosure. Backaches are cured by rubbing the back against Abu edh-Dhur <note>original: Abû eẕ-Ẕhûr</note> and placing a stone upon the rock. This same power is attributed to the broken column placed at the head of a small tomb beside Sheikh Shaddad <note>original: eš-šēḫ Šaddâd</note> in the Cemetery of the Gate of Mercy <note>original: Bâb er-Raḥmeh</note>.⁴ In Hebron, mothers encourage their children to run over the tomb of Sheikh Shnet <note>original: šēḫ Šnēt</note> to recover from their ailments.⁵

A man suffering from impotence must wash himself in a shrine <term>original: welī; a term for both a saint and their sanctuary</term> or on the threshold of el-Ateri <note>original: el-ʿAṭērî</note> in Deir Ghassaneh <note>original: Dêr Ghassâneh</note>. A written amulet <term>original: ḥidjâb; a protective charm containing sacred text</term> prepared by the sheikh of el-Hadra <note>original: el-Ḥadrâ</note> in Nablus and worn by the man, after he has prayed in the room known as Jacob’s Mourning <note>original: Ḥuzn Yaʿqûb</note>, will cure his condition.

A child suffering from fever is taken by his mother to the Cup <note>original: el-Kâs</note>⁶—also known as the Basin <note>original: Djurn</note>—which is a water fountain located between the al-Aqsa Mosque <note>original: el-Aqṣâ</note> and the Dome of the Rock <note>original: eṣ-Ṣahrah</note>. A *mafâzeh* is, as we saw on page 76, a heap of stones found on a mountain peak. A child walks three times around the basin during the midday prayer on Friday. During this process, his mother continually throws sweets on the ground, and the child is taken home without turning or looking back.

Talismans<note>1</note> <note>original: A talisman is an object, often inscribed with magic signs or names, believed to possess protective powers.</note> are also made in a sanctuary and worn by people as a protection against disease, or are used to fumigate <note>To purify or treat an area or person using smoke or medicinal vapors.</note> a sick child. The seal of the Mosque of Hebron <note>The Cave of the Patriarchs in Hebron, a site sacred to Jews, Christians, and Muslims.</note>, inscribed with the names of the Patriarchs and those of their wives who were buried there, is used against fever (compare with *Superstition* <note>original: Aberglaube; likely referring to the author's work, *Aberglaube und Volksmedizin im Lande der Bibel* (Superstition and Folk Medicine in the Land of the Bible).</note>, p. 130). This paper is cut at the dotted lines marked A, B, C, and D. The child is treated with medicinal smoke <note>fumigated</note> every day with one piece. When reading the amulet, read from right to left.

Similar seals are also issued by some of the sheikhs <term>original: šêhs; religious leaders or elders</term> of the Mosque of Omar. One such paper <gloss>1. I have such talismans on white, red, and green paper.</gloss> contains circular seals with various inscriptions and representations of hands, swords, and balances. 1. Within the image of the sword:

->The Path.<note>original: الصراط</note><-
->Shehadeh.<note>original: شحادة</note><-

5. Abraham's seal (circular):

->The Messenger of the Mighty, All-Knowing King, Abraham, the Friend of the Merciful, peace and blessings be upon him.<note>original Arabic: رسول الملك العزيز العلام ابرهيم خليل الرحمن عليه الصلوة والسلام</note><-

6. On a round seal we read the "Throne Verse" <note>original: *âyatu l-kursî*; a famous protective passage from the Quran</note> (Sûrah II, 250). <note>The author likely refers to verse 255 of the second chapter of the Quran.</note>

7. In the representation of the hand:

->And give good tidings to the true believers, O Muhammad; assistance from God and a speedy victory.^1<note>original Arabic: وبشر المومنين يا محمد نصر من الله وفتح قريب</note><-
->There is no God but God, and Muhammad is the messenger of God.<note>original Arabic: لا اله الا الله ومحمد رسول الله</note><-
->There is no champion but Ali, and no sword but Dhu l-Fiqar.<note>original Arabic: لا فتّا الا علي ولا سيف الا ذا الفقار</note><-
->O Compassionate, O Beneficent, O Merciful, O God.<note>original Arabic: يا حنان يا منان يا رحمان يا الله</note><-
->The Manifest, the Hidden, the First, the Last.<note>original Arabic: الظاهر الباطن الاول الاخر; these are among the 99 Names of God</note><-

One large seal contains twelve circles, while another contains seven. These circles contain powerful <note>original: efficacious</note> verses. When such papers are issued, we notice that each one includes several of the figures mentioned above.

Often, when a child is sick and the mother believes that only a protective amulet <term>original: hidjâb</term> will help to cure him, but she cannot get one written at that moment, she places a small bag of salt in a hole in the eastern wall and says, "This is for the bag of al-Rifa'i" <term>original: 'alâ kîs er-Rfâ'î</term>. Very early the next morning, she visits a sheikh <term>original: šêh; a religious leader or elder</term> of the Rifa'i order and asks him to write her an amulet <term>hidjâb</term> for the sick child. Her pious intention—shown by the act described—ensures the favor of this man of God, even before she receives a talisman from one of his representatives.^2

In analyzing the diseases which are cured by remedies taken from a saint's shrine <term>original: welî</term>, we find that two are most common: fever and sterility. The following ailments come next: mental troubles, inability to urinate, and skin diseases. The reasons why fever and sterility predominate are the following: in the East, "fever" refers to every ailment which is accompanied by a high temperature, and where no...

^1 Taken from Sûrah 61 <note>Verse 13</note>.
^2 A custom in Deir Ghassaneh <note>a Palestinian village north of Ramallah</note>, heard from O. S. Barghuthi.

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page transitions from a discussion of protective amulets to the specific medical conditions and social pressures—such as fever and sterility—that drive people to seek the intervention of saints in Palestine. It begins a catalog of "specialist" saints associated with particular cures.</meta>

<page-num>119</page-num>

<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

...other symptoms—like bleeding (from the lungs or gastrointestinal tract), diarrhea, coughing, and so on—predominate. Thus, illnesses such as malaria, typhoid, typhus, and recurrent fever are called simply *shûneh* or *hummâ* <note>original: fever</note>. We know also that malaria is a very widespread disease, found all over Palestine. In this way, "fever" may be considered the greatest Palestinian plague. 

Sterility—or in other words, marriage without children, especially sons—is considered a severe punishment, causing a deeply felt disgrace. A man's honor is best expressed by calling him not by his own name, but by that of his oldest son, preceded by "father of." *Abû 'Alî*¹ <note>original: "the father of 'Alî"</note> is the honorific name of a person who is never called by his actual name, but always *Abû 'Alî*. In order to disguise the true condition of a childless man, he is called "father" of his own father's name;² for example, if Hasan has no children while his father's name is Mohammed, he is called *Abû Mohammed*, or *Abû Ibrâhîm* if Ibrâhîm is the name of his father. This is the reason why every barren woman seeks help everywhere to escape her despised condition.

It is a very interesting fact that some saints <note>original: *awliâ*</note> have a special power over certain diseases; that is, they are specialists for particular types of ailments. The following list identifies the diseases and the saints who cure them: 

*   **For mental troubles:** People seek help from el-Hader,³ the Men of Sufah <note>original: *Rdjâl Ṣûfah*</note> (of Der Ghassaneh), en-Nubani, Sheikh Djabr (of Rafat), and Our Lady the Green <note>original: *sittnâ el-Ḥaḍrâ*</note>. Among these, el-Hader <note>a figure often associated with St. George or Elijah</note> enjoys the greatest reputation of all. 
*   **For skin conditions:** Every place where it is supposed that the prophet Job <note>original: *Ayyûb*</note> took a bath is renowned for curing skin troubles (see above). 
*   **For head eruptions:** The Sheikhs of al-Aruri (of Der Ghassaneh) have the power of curing a special eruption of the head called *shawwâtah* (also known as *ra''âyeh*). They treat it by cauterizing it with fire, while an ointment made of certain herbs that grow near the shrine is given to the patient.⁴

The saint Er-Rifa'i possessed, while on earth, the power of healing the bites of venomous serpents. He begged the Almighty to give him the "horn of asafoetida" <note>original: *qarn el-ḥaltît*; a pungent resin used in traditional medicine</note>...

---

¹ It is a widespread custom among Muslims and Christians in Palestine to give their first son the name of his grandfather (the father's father).
² A *hadith* <note>a traditional saying of the Prophet Muhammad</note> says, "The best names are those that praise God or signify servitude to Him." <note>original Arabic: *khairu l-asmâi mâ ḥammada au 'abbad*</note>. The latter names are always followed by a name or attribute of God, such as Abdullah (Servant of Allah).
³ I have transcribed this as both *Hadr* and *Hader*, as both pronunciations are commonly heard.
⁴ For severe menstrual bleeding, the inhabitants of the Samaria district go to the "Daughters of Jacob" <note>original: *Banât Ya'qûb*</note> for help.

<summary>This page discusses the cultural perceptions of disease in Palestine, particularly the social stigma of childlessness and the naming customs used to mitigate it. It also begins a list of saints who specialize in curing specific ailments like mental illness and skin diseases.</summary>
<keywords>malaria, sterility, honorific names, kunya, healing saints, awliâ, el-Hader, Prophet Job, traditional medicine, Er-Rifa'i, Palestinian folklore</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of "specialist" saints in Palestine, focusing on those who provide protection against poisons and mental illness, as well as the significance of specific holy times for healing rituals.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

(called in some places the *horn of the halhit* <term>original: *qarn el-ḥalḥît*</term>) an antivenomous power. Since his death, the milk in which the "horn" <note>original: *qarn*</note> has been rubbed is believed to cure every poisoned person. The sheikhs <note>original: *šiyûḫ*</note> of the Rifa'i order <note>original: *er-Rfâʿî*; a Sufi order known for its practitioners' supposed immunity to snakes and fire</note> claim to be immune to every snake bite.

There is a very widespread belief that certain cures are more certain at specific times. Thus, bathing in the "Bath of the Spring" <note>original: *Hammâm el-ʿÊn*</note> and springs dedicated to Job during the night of *Ashura*¹ (the tenth of *Muharram*²) is more effective than bathing at any other time. The sea is most curative on the day of "Job’s recovery" <note>original: *ibrîyet Aiyûb*</note>. The best hackberry wood <note>original: *ʿûd mês*</note> is that which was cut on the "Night of Power" <note>original: *Lêlatu l-Qadr*; the 27th of Ramadan</note>. Acts performed around the time of the noon prayer <note>original: *ṣalât ez-zuhr*</note> are more effective than others, and so forth.

I shall close this section with a study of the shrines of *el-Khader* <note>original: *el-Ḥaḍer*; literally "The Green One," identified with St. George</note>, the most renowned saint-physician for nervous and mental troubles. This man of God, who is honored by all faiths in Palestine, possesses many sanctuaries. Suppliants come to every one of them, but some are more popular than others. I have been able to gather the following list of shrines connected with this saint:

| Place | Quarter | Description |
|-------|---------|-------------|
| Jerusalem | Armenian quarter | Belongs to the Greek Church |
| Jerusalem | Beside the Franciscan Convent | Belongs to the Greek Church |
| Jerusalem | Inside the Jaffa Gate | Belongs to the Copts³ |
| Jerusalem | Outside the Jaffa Gate | Belongs to the Qurt family, and is honored by Muslims and Christians⁴ |

1 It is said that al-Husayn <note>The grandson of the Prophet Muhammad</note> died on this day while very thirsty. Therefore, many will drink water with their eyes shut and from an opaque vessel so as not to see the water.
2 Not the ninth, as Hava states in his Arabic-English Dictionary.
3 These three churches are not used by Muslims.

The saint repeated his request and finally said: "You both shall remain unable to speak until my sanctuary is built." Frightened, the man got up, woke his son, and—to their shock—both were mute. The next day the work began, and they indeed found the opening of a cave at the spot he had described. To their astonishment and that of all the onlookers, the calf which they had lost a year ago while ploughing this very place was found inside the cave, well-grown and well-fed. Straw, barley, and water had been brought to it in a miraculous way by St. George. They also found the following inscription:
<note>original Arabic: عجائب الخضر كثيرة ما هي قليلة هنا محل القديس العظيم في الشهدا جورجيوس اللابس الظفر المستشهد في الحروب</note>
> "The miracles of el-Khader are many, not few. Here is the place of the great saint among the martyrs, George, clothed with victory, the martyr of the wars." 

The fame of this place spread. Even the Sultan granted it a royal decree <term>original: Farmân</term>. Patients began to flock here, the most recent in the year 1923. No sooner was the work of restoring the sanctuary <note>original: maqâm</note> finished than the power of speech was restored to George Qurt’s grandfather and father. (An abbreviated version of this story is given by Kahle, *PJ* VI, 88).

There are some saints <term>awliâ</term> bearing the name **Sheikh Khader** <term>eš-šêḥ Haḍr</term> (such as the one west of **Rammun**) which probably have nothing to do with Saint George. There are many other shrines dedicated to this "Saint of God" <term>walîy allâh</term> that are not included in this list. The most important of these places are the one on **Mount Carmel** <note>Called by some **Saint Elias** (Mâr Eliâs).</note> and the shrine near **Beit Jala**. The latter is situated on an elevated spot between the village and the **Pools of Solomon**. The beautiful church, which is surrounded by a monastery, lies in a village bearing the name **el-Khader**. The inhabitants of the settlement are all Muslims. The entire area around the sanctuary used to belong to the monastery, which is directly dependent on the **Greek Patriarchate in Jerusalem**. 

A large part of the sanctuary was rebuilt a few years before the war. This change is of great importance, as proper rooms were constructed for those suffering from mental illness, <note>The present government has forbidden the acceptance of the insane in this place.</note> and thus a large part of the old practice for curing the afflicted was changed. We shall describe the way in which patients used to be treated before this change took place.

As the reputation for the wonderful cures of this saint spread throughout the country, the sick of all creeds were brought to the shrine from all directions. As soon as they arrived, the priest chained them in the vestibule <term>narthex</term> in front of the church. The heavy iron chain was fastened to an iron ring around the neck of the unfortunate person, while the other end of the chain was drawn through one of the two windows on either side of the main portal and fastened inside the...

church. In case three patients were sent at the same time, the third one was placed in a small room built just west of the dome. In this instance, the chain passed through a small window in the dome, thereby connecting the patient with the church. During the cold winter months, the patients were kept inside the church.

The following story, taken from my father's written notes, beautifully illustrates the strong belief in the miracles of St. George and shows how such ideas are spread by the priests themselves. The Greek Orthodox priest Ibrahim el-Awwa <note>From the village of Beit Jala.</note> went as usual on Saturday afternoon, accompanied by Ishaq Tuma <note>From Beit Jala. He was later ordained as a priest.</note>, to the monastery of St. George to lead the evening and Sunday morning masses. They found a violent and mentally ill Bedouin sheikh <term>šêh</term> of the Tayaha tribe there, chained inside the church. His condition was so severe that he tore all his clothes. Whatever the director <term>raiys: the superior or head of the monastery</term> put on him was soon torn to pieces. As the priest and his assistant Ishaq rose during the night to perform their duties, they saw the shadow of a human being moving cautiously on the roof of the church. Frightened, they woke the monk. A thorough investigation showed that the naked sheikh was crouching in a corner, shivering from the cold and extremely exhausted. At once, the head of the monastery gave him clothes and, taking him into a room, made a fire and began to rub him until he felt better. The director <term>raiys</term> asked the sheikh how he got out of the church while the doors were still locked. He answered: "A gentle-looking man with a spear in his hand, riding a horse, appeared to me and ordered me: 'Get up and go to your tribe.' He touched the chain with his spear and it fell from my neck. I climbed up the chain from which the church lamp hung. Swinging myself back and forth, I reached a window in the dome of the church, and from there I got out." The sheikh was completely sane from that moment on. He pledged himself to offer seven goats every year to his healer, el-Khader <term>el-Hadr</term>, a promise he is said to have kept. <note>This story was told to the author's father by the aforementioned priest, Ishaq Tuma.</note>

The patients received no medical treatment at all but were expected to be cured by the miraculous intervention of St. George. The director <term>raiys</term> of the church very often found it necessary to speed up the cure by

...driving out the devil. This was done by thorough beatings and prayers. It is no wonder that these poor creatures were furious when the priest fell into their clutches. Whenever a patient's condition became somewhat normal, the priest would secretly unfasten the chain from the church and tell the patient that the saint had declared him cured.

Only a simple straw mattress was given to them. The two who were bound in front of the church had not the least protection from the frightful summer heat or the cold of the winter. Their food consisted of bread—sometimes very hard—and water. Both were given in very limited amounts. The odor of their waste <note>original: "evacuations"</note> used to make the place unbearable.

At present, the sanatorium is built a short distance south of the church. It is composed of twelve rooms, in each of which there is a chain firmly fastened to the wall. The hygienic conditions of this place are in every respect better than in the old system. This new hospital was connected, when I saw it last, by a wire to the church. In this way, the healing power of the saint is transmitted to the sick.

An analysis of the treatment described above is of great interest. The sick are placed in the direct neighborhood of the saint to be near his blessing and his field of action; the chains transmit the curing power. As soon as the hospital was moved to a distance, a connecting wire was run between it and the church to carry the saint's miraculous power. Prayers and beatings are considered the best way of driving away a devil. This practice is still common among the *sheikhs* <term>šiyûḫ: the plural of sheikh; here referring to religious or tribal leaders</term>. Whenever a sick person shows any nervous symptoms—like hysterical fits, strokes <note>original: "apoplexy"</note>, epilepsy, and even convulsions resulting from fever—a *sheikh* <term>šêḫ: a title for a religious or tribal leader</term> is called for treatment. He writes a talisman <term>a charm or amulet inscribed with religious or magical symbols</term>, recites prayers, spits on the patient, and massages the body.1 This massage is always done in such a way that the hand of the *sheikh* moves from the upper parts of the body downwards. The reason for this direction of movement is so the devil should be massaged out from the important organs to the less important ones and eventually be thrown...
