1 An insane girl, who received no help from all the consulted physicians, went to a Chaldean priest. He, after fasting three days and three nights, made the sick child kneel down and pray with him, and behold, a cloud of smoke came out of her mouth while she lost consciousness. The priest put her in a bath and began to massage her. The moment she awoke, she was cured.

Massaging a demon upwards may cause an irreparable weakness in the heart, eyes, tongue, brain, etc.</note> The massage develops in most cases into violent beatings, which may be carried out with the hand, with a stick, a shoe, <note>original footnote 2: See the case described in *Superstition* (original: *Aberglaube*), pp. 117 f.</note> or even with a holy object, like the wooden shoe of the *Madjdûb* <term>a person "attracted" by God, often perceived as a holy fool or mentally ill</term>.

It is easier to drive out devils when the bodily condition of the possessed person is at its lowest. <note>original footnote 3: This is an older belief; see *The Pleasing Gift* (original: *at-Tuhfatu l-Mardîyah*), p. 4.</note> It is a common belief that the *djinn* <term>supernatural spirits, often blamed for illness or possession</term> prefer well-built, corpulent patients. This explains why the patients are fed so poorly. Neglect of cleanliness leads to the same result. <note>original footnote 4: See on this subject also Stephan, “Lunacy in Palestinian Folklore,” *Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society (JPOS) V* pp. 1—16, on which the following remarks may be made: — It would have been more helpful if the original meanings and uses of all the synonyms on pp. 2 and 3 had been given. — I do not think that “lunacy is considered as something divine” (p. 2). On the contrary, it is currently regarded, as it used to be in Biblical times, as the work of Satan. Although *madjdûb* is used at present for beginning lunacy, its real meaning is quite different from *madjnûn* (insane/possessed). — The real causes of lunacy following the writing of black magic (original: *siḥir aswad*), drinking spirits (p. 6, footnotes 3, 4), or naked sexual intercourse (original: *coitus nudus*) (p. 7) are nothing but the *djinn* and should not be put in another category.</note>

## 5. OATHS

It is a widespread custom to call on God or a saint to witness and attest to one’s affirmation, or to assert one’s innocence. This assurance is given by means of an oath in the name of a holy man, generally in his shrine, or in the name of God and in a shrine of some *welî* <term>a Muslim saint or holy person</term>. Simple oaths in which the name of God alone is used, and which may be uttered on any occasion, are innumerable and will not be discussed. Simple oaths are so common that they are a part of the daily life of a peasant. <note>original footnote 5: I will mention some of the most important simple oaths (original: *aqsâm*, plural of *qasam*) which are much used in the daily life of the peasant. They belong to one of the following two categories: a) The name or an adjective of God, that of a *welî* or a sanctuary are connected with the oath. b) The oath (original: *yamîn*) mentions some natural phenomena. The following examples will illustrate both kinds:</note> If he is telling a story or an...

When someone recounts an adventure and feels the need to emphasize a point—perhaps because someone might doubt his statement—he says: “By the truth of Saint George the Green, I went and came back” <note>original Arabic: *u-ḥaqq el-Ḫaḍr el-Aḫḍar innî ruḥt udjît*</note>. Or, he might say: “By the truth and honor of the Prophet...”

Category A: Oaths related to God and Religion

| Modern English Translation | Modern Meaning / Context |
|----------|----------|
| By God and Muhammad, the Messenger of God | An oath invoking the central figures of Islam. |
| By God, for there is no one more powerful than God | Emphasizing God's ultimate authority. |
| By the life of this branch and the worshipped God and the Prophet David | <note>The speaker often holds a stick or branch while swearing this.</note> |
| By the living cross | A common Christian oath. |
| By the truth of the Islamic direction of prayer | <note>original: *Qibla*</note> |
| By the truth of this church and the saints dwelling within it | Invoking the spiritual presence in a holy building. |
| By the gate of the East, the prayer direction of the Christians | Referring to the sunrise and the direction in which Christian dead are buried. |
| By the Kaaba | <note>The sacred building at the center of Islam's most important mosque in Mecca.</note> |
| By the honored Church of the Nativity | The site in Bethlehem where Jesus is believed to have been born. |
| By the Quran | <note>original: *Mus-haf* (the physical copy of the Quran)</note> |
| By the beard of the Prophet | Swearing by a symbol of the Prophet Muhammad's dignity. |

Category B: Oaths related to Nature and Honor

| Modern English Translation | Modern Meaning / Context |
|----------|----------|
| By the truth of the sun’s disk, which sets in the west in obedience to her Lord | The sun is personified as a "small eye" following God's command. |
| By the life of this mustache | Swearing by one’s masculinity, honor, and physical presence. |
| By the existence of the water which purifies the living and the dead | Referring to ritual washing and the washing of bodies for burial. |
| By the existence of the flowing water and the high heavens | Invoking the purity of nature and the majesty of the sky. |
| By the existence of this tree which drinks through its roots and praises its Lord | A poetic view of nature as a worshipper of God. |
| By the existence of this green carpet that grew silently from the earth | <note>Referring to the grass</note>; the original Arabic implies it grew "deaf and dumb" (without making a sound). |
| By the truth of the One who knows how many leaves are on this tree and how many hairs are on this animal | The speaker points to a specific tree and animal to emphasize God's infinite knowledge. |

Some of these formulas were taken from my father's written notes.

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Palestinian oaths, moving from general verbal oaths to those sworn specifically at the shrines of saints to settle legal disputes and accusations of theft.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

"By the honor of the Prophet Moses, I..." <note>original: *Udjâh en-nabî Mûsâ ...*</note> These oaths can be made in any location and do not need to be restricted to a sanctuary. It is customary to use the name of a local saint or an important well-known <term>welî</term> <note>original: *welî*; a Muslim saint or holy person</note>. All oaths made during a <term>môsam</term> <note>original: *môsam*; a religious festival or seasonal pilgrimage</note> of a saint, or in the neighborhood of a sanctuary, are performed in the name of that specific saint or prophet.

More important are the oaths made within the shrine itself. The reasons for such an act are either major or minor legal matters. When a man is accused of stealing something, but no direct proof can be provided, the accuser asks him to go to a saint’s shrine and swear to his innocence. The accused, followed by the accuser, enters the shrine. The accused lifts up his hands and says: "By God, the Great and Almighty One <note>original: This is sometimes repeated three times</note>, who has no greater power above Him, I have not stolen, nor even seen this female mule, nor do I know who has taken her" <note>original Arabic: *Wallahi el-azim wa-lladhi ma a'zam minhu 'azim inni ha-l-baghla la saraqtuha wala akhadhtuha wala bi-'ilm min akhadh-ha*</note>. The accuser must be satisfied with this oath, and the case is then regarded as closed. In some cases, the accuser may not require the accused to travel all the way to a sanctuary. The accused may instead be allowed to simply turn his face in the direction of the designated saint’s shrine and swear to his innocence with uplifted hands.

The legal principle *el-yamîn 'alâ niyet el-mhallif*—which means "the oath is to be fulfilled according to the intention of the person requiring it"—has two meanings. First, it means the accuser has the right to choose the sanctuary, the time, and sometimes the specific part of the sanctuary where the accused must place his hand while swearing (such as the tomb, the <term>mihrâb</term> <note>the niche in a mosque indicating the direction of Mecca</note>, or the Quran). Secondly, it means the oath given by the accused is interpreted according to what the accuser intended. A wording that might be technically true on the surface, but is essentially a lie, is considered a false oath. 

For example, "A" is accused by "B" of stealing his mare from a locked stable. "A" was actually the thief, having stolen the mare with the help of "C," who is "B's" servant. "C" opened the stable, untied the mare, and led it outside of "B's" property where "A" was waiting. "A" then galloped off with the horse, and after selling it, gave "C" his share of the money. "B" suspects "A" and asks him to swear an oath...

<summary>This page describes the ritual and legal process of swearing oaths at the shrines of saints in Palestine to settle accusations, particularly in cases of theft. It explains the principle that an oath's validity is based on the accuser's intent to prevent the accused from using deceptive wording.</summary>

<keywords>oaths, shrines, welî, legal disputes, theft, Palestinian folklore, religious law, mihrâb, Prophet Moses</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of legal oaths taken at Palestinian shrines, specifically focusing on the rituals performed to ensure the truth or to provoke divine punishment for perjury.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

The latter says: 

> “By God, the Almighty, I never entered the premises, unlocked the stable, untied the mare, or took it away.” 

His oath is technically true, and the accuser is obliged to accept it. However, because he tried to cheat through the specific wording of the oath, God and the saint will punish him most severely. When the accused is ritually pure, he enters the shrine; if he is not, he stands outside the door of the sanctuary, facing the interior, and performs his oath. In the case of a holy cave, one stands at the entrance. Many Bedouin of Transjordania step over the tomb of the <term>welî</term> <note>original: *welî*; a Muslim saint or holy person</note> (an act called <term>yufšuq</term>) and then swear. They believe that the saint—already irritated by the disrespectful act of someone stepping over his tomb—will act even faster to punish the accused if they have given a false oath.

If the accused is guilty but does not dare to acknowledge his fault openly, and is at the same time afraid of the consequences of lying under oath (perjury), he may try to escape his difficulty in the following way, as told to me by <term>Ṣofiyâ</term> of <term>Turmus'aiyâ</term>. The accused wears his garment turned inside out, fastening seven needles into it and carrying three silver bracelets in his pocket. All of this is done in secret. With this protection, he believes that no harm will come to him, even if he swears a false oath. By such an act, he intends to secretly acknowledge to the "man of God" (the saint) that he has committed the fault, while promising the saint that he will return the stolen items secretly or through a middleman.

When an accuser loses his case because of a false oath by the accused, he tries to provoke the anger of the saint, believing that he can thus hasten the punishment of his guilty opponent. There are different ways to provoke the saint. He may hold both ends of a mat from the sanctuary with both hands and, swaying it up and down to shake its dust onto the accused, say: 

> “O my Lord, O <term>Sêf ed-Dîn</term>, get me my rights from this lying scoundrel.” 

Sometimes the mats of the shrine are turned over with the words: 

> “I turn the mats of <term>el-Hauwâṣ</term> over on you, O scoundrel!”¹ <note>original Arabic: *Qalabt 'alêk ḥuṣr el-Hauwâṣ yâ ẓâlim.*</note> 

The mats remain upside down until a passer-by or the <term>qaiym</term> <note>original: *qaiym*; the attendant or custodian of a shrine</note> turns them back. Generally, however, they are not touched until the person who sought justice from the saint turns them over again himself. This is usually done when the relatives of the person who made the false oath beg for it. Turning the mats upside down is supposed to—

---

¹ “I turn the mats of el-Hauwâṣ over on you, O wrongdoer.” <note>original: *Qalabt 'alêk ḥuṣr el-Hauwâṣ yâ ẓâlim.*</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual actions performed at Palestinian shrines to ensure justice, specifically focusing on how the oppressed or falsely accused might provoke a saint’s spirit to intervene against a wrongdoer.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>

<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine 129</header>

...transfer the anger of the saint to the guilty person. These two acts, known as the "turning of the mats" <note>original: *qalb el-ḥuṣur*</note>, may be performed not only when a person has lied under oath, but also whenever a person is unjustly oppressed. It is widely believed that these acts will provoke the saint to react immediately in favor of the oppressed person.¹ In some regions, the oppressed person goes to the sanctuary and covers themselves with a mat while lying on the floor, or they place the sweepings from the shrine onto the tomb. Both of these acts are believed to irritate the saint, who will then immediately protect the one who has been mistreated.²

When an innocent person is accused and forced to swear an oath, they feel insulted by the suspicion directed at them and attempt to make the saint avenge them in one of the following ways. They may throw a small handful (about a quarter to a half-handful) of wall plaster <note>original: *qṣârah*</note>³ at their opponent and say: "May the result of the oath you forced me to take return upon you" <note>original: *Yamînak yiqlib ‘alêk*</note>. The accuser then answers,⁴ "It will return upon the liar" <note>original: *yiqlib ‘alâ l-kâdib*</note>.⁵ Others take stones or earth and throw them on the grave or on the shrine <note>original: *maqâm*</note>, thereby arousing the spirit of the holy man to keep his sanctuary <note>original: *maqâm*</note> holy and free of false accusations.⁶

An exceedingly rare custom involves going to a sanctuary and tying a piece of the oppressed person’s clothing to the headstone <note>original: *mṣîbeh*</note> while saying: "Attack So-and-So, who has oppressed me, O Madjdûb!" <note>original: *ṭûr yâ Madjdûb fî fulân illî ẓalamnî*</note>. To my knowledge, this custom is practiced only by the women of Deir Ghassaneh.⁷

A final resort to prove one’s innocence and demand revenge from God is to turn a copy of the Quran <note>original: *maṣḥaf*</note> upside down while saying to the accuser: "Behold, a Quran is turned against you" <note>original: *ḥaiy qalbêt maṣḥaf ‘alêk*</note>.

While the simple oaths <note>original: *ymân ‘urḍah*</note> for minor crimes that we have discussed may be sworn at any saint's shrine <note>original: *welî*</note>, more solemn oaths...

---

¹ Deir Ghassaneh <note>A historic village in the West Bank</note>.
² I heard both of these customs from the people of Abu Ghosh <note>A village west of Jerusalem</note>.
³ Plaster coating <note>original: *Qṣârah*</note> refers to the plaster used on the walls of rooms.
⁴ Arabic: "It will turn against the liar" <note>original: *yiqlib ‘alâ l-kâdib*</note>.
⁵ The accuser and the accused, who arrived together, return separately by different roads.
⁶ If the accused is a descendant of a saint and is innocent, he tries to irritate the saint by putting filth on the tomb or by shouting that the saint is asleep, powerless, or indifferent (see 1 Kings 18:27 <note>A biblical reference where the prophet Elijah taunts the prophets of Baal</note>).
⁷ Heard from Omar el-Barghuti <note>A Palestinian scholar and author</note>.

<summary>This page describes various rituals used in Palestinian folk religion to provoke a saint's intervention, such as turning mats or the Quran upside down to demand justice for oppression or false accusations. These acts are intended to irritate the saint's spirit so that they will immediately punish the guilty or protect the innocent.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual practices and oaths taken at Palestinian sanctuaries, specifically focusing on the types of crimes that require solemn oaths and the role of "irritable" saints in enforcing truth.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

<margin>X</margin>
Solemn oaths <note>original: *mughallaẓ*</note> for major crimes like murder,<gloss>1</gloss> rape,<gloss>2</gloss> and illegal entry <gloss>3</gloss> must be performed in the shrine of an important saint. Manṣûr of Liftâ assured me that an "irritable" or "hot-tempered" saint <note>original: *welî ḥišr* or *nizq*</note> should be chosen, since he will punish a liar immediately.<gloss>4</gloss> In such significant cases, it is not the accuser, but the judge who orders that such an oath be made if no absolute proof can be produced regarding the defendant's guilt. The judge—if he is unable to go himself—sends representatives to report the result. The oath of the accused must be supported by one notable person and sanctioned by three others.<gloss>5</gloss>

We have seen that all oaths taken in a sanctuary are made in the name of God and not in the name of a prophet. The name of a prophet may be mentioned in some cases after that of God, but no important oath is ever made in the name of a saint alone. Fridays and Mondays are generally chosen for an important oath. Monday is selected because it is said that the Prophet Muhammad was born on a Monday.

The material provided in the previous section shows the great honor and fear people have for the saints. Few indeed dare to swear a false oath in a shrine, for the vengeance of the saints is most severe. This revenge may manifest in the person himself, his family, his property, or his animals. Story after story is told to illustrate this point. In some cases—as with the saint *Šhâb ed-Dîn* of *Ṣaffâ*—the man of God marks the houses of those who swear a false oath. Early in the morning, they may find the corpse of some animal in front of their door. If no such thing is found, everyone knows that the oath was a true one.

### 6. VOWS

The practice of taking on voluntary obligations to a deity upon the fulfillment of certain conditions—such as recovery from disease, escape from death or danger, success in business, the birth of an heir, and similar blessings—is of extremely ancient date and common in all religious systems. There are many references in the Bible dealing with vows,

<gloss>2</gloss> Arabic: *‘arḍ* (literally "honor").
<gloss>3</gloss> Arabic: *hadjseh* refers to a stranger entering a respectable house for the purpose of either robbery or adultery.
<gloss>4</gloss> All those I asked confirmed this statement.
<gloss>5</gloss> See also O. el-Barghûṯî's paper, *Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society* II, 51.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of local religious customs in Palestine, transitioning from the fear of swearing false oaths to the widespread practice of making vows to saints for protection and healing.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>
<sig>9*</sig>

This fact shows us how widespread this practice was. The Prophet Muhammad gave some rules concerning it in the Quran, as well as in the <term>Hadith</term> <note>original: *Hadîṯ*; the recorded sayings and traditions of the Prophet Muhammad</note>. Vows, as they are performed today, have kept most of their ancient features.<gloss>1</gloss> This custom is still found among the peasantry as well as among city-dwellers, among Muslims <note>original: Mohammedans</note> and Christians, and among the rich and poor alike. All believe in its necessity, its effectiveness, and the certain blessing it brings. Every difficulty or misfortune in the daily life of the local people brings them nearer to God and to the saints. Holding that these holy men are closer to God than they are themselves—and closer to them than God is—they believe the saints are able to help. The person tries to gain their protection, favor, and help, believing he will be more successful if he rewards the saints. Therefore, he promises an offering to one or more saints, to be given in their names and in their honor.

The opportunities for making vows are innumerable: during sickness, when a friend goes on a journey, when bad news is received about an absent family member, when a woman is barren, during a poor agricultural year, in the face of immediate danger, after the repeated loss of children, during difficult labor, or when cattle suffer from infectious diseases. Among all these causes, illness is the most significant, and most vows are fulfilled in connection with it. Many saints are called upon for help. The father, mother, wife, brothers, sisters, children, other relatives, or even friends of the sick person make the vows. Usually, each of them calls on a different saint, thus ensuring the help of all the most important saints. If the condition of the sick man allows it, he will promise an offering himself. The value of the offering varies more or less according to the importance of the sick person within the family. The father, husband, or an only son are generally the most prioritized in this respect. The wife, daughters, or elderly women of the family come last. I witnessed the following event which illustrates this perfectly, as described in my book *Superstition* <note>original: *Aberglaube*, p. 70</note>. Abu Amin al-Betuni, the eldest member of his <term>hamuleh</term><gloss>2</gloss> fell ill with pneumonia. He had such a severe relapse that the course of the disease was greatly prolonged. When one day his condition became...

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Kahle, *Palästinajahrbuch* VIII, pp. 148 ff.; Canaan, *Superstition* <note>original: *Aberglaube*</note>, pp. 70 ff.; Jaussen, Doutté, Curtiss, *throughout*.
<gloss>2</gloss> A <term>hamuleh</term> <note>original: *Hamûleh*</note> is the "family" in the broad sense, almost a "clan," while the narrower meaning of a single household is expressed by the term *‘eileh*.

<summary>This page describes the enduring tradition of making vows to saints in Palestinian culture to seek divine help during times of crisis, particularly illness. It highlights how these vows are practiced across all social classes and religions, prioritizing male family members in the offerings made.</summary>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of local religious customs in Palestine, transitioning from the general practice of making vows to a specific case study of a family in crisis. It explores the hierarchy of spiritual appeals, where saints are often prioritized over God due to their perceived accessibility and human understanding.</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

The situation was very critical, and I explained the matter to his brothers and relatives. One of his brothers vowed that he would give thirty francs <note>original: "francs"; at the time, twenty francs were equivalent to one pound</note> worth of candles to the sanctuary of Abraham in Hebron, to the Mosque of Omar in Jerusalem, and to the shrine of Moses. At the same time, he begged Almighty God to heal his brother and to inflict the disease instead upon his own young son, who was only eight months old. <note>The baby fell sick a few days later. The mother remarked that "the gate of heaven was open" (original: *bâb es-samâ kân maftûh*), an idiom used when a wish is fulfilled immediately.</note> He was even ready to lose his child if the Almighty would save his brother. <note>Such a request is not called a "vow" (*nidr*), but rather a "petitionary curse" or intense prayer (original: *da'ueh*).</note> A second brother vowed an animal sacrifice <note>original: *dbîhah*</note> for the saint <note>original: *welî*</note> Zêtûn in the village of Bêtûniâ, and a second sheep for a picnic <note>original: *šathah*</note> for his friends and relatives. The wife promised the Prophet Samuel <note>original: *Nebî Samwŷl*</note> a sheep, while the sick man himself promised to offer a sheep and a "basket" of rice <note>original: *quffet ruzz*; a large measurement equal to 100 kilograms</note> to Abraham. The other relatives made smaller vows. 

According to Palestinian Arabic belief, God is the Almighty One who stands higher than all saints. This is the teaching of the Quran: if He chooses, He is able to do everything, whether possible or impossible. However, the saints are often preferred by the people. They are easier to access and feel closer to humanity—as they were all once human beings themselves. Because they understand human needs, ailments, and weaknesses so well, belief in them and fear of them has spread so widely among Palestinians that they have gradually taken the place of God in daily practice. People view them as minor deities, and I do not doubt that in many places, superstition has elevated them to a rank equal to that of God. This is the reason why nearly all vows are made to saints and not to God directly. The number of saints to whom vows are brought is innumerable. Generally, local saints <note>original: *welîs*</note> are preferred, but often others are chosen, either because they are prophets and thus favorites of the Almighty, or because they have gradually gained the complete confidence of the peasants through their miracles. Even in a single village where there are several saints <note>original: *awliyâ*</note>, one of them usually enjoys the greatest popularity because he is believed to be particularly powerful.

---

^1 Twenty francs or one pound are called a *nêrah* or *lêrah*. The difference between a French, English, Egyptian, or Turkish pound is expressed by adding the name of the nation: *fransawîyeh* (French), *inglîzîyeh* (English).

^2 The baby fell sick a few days later. The mother said "the gate of heaven was open," meaning that when the father expressed his wish, God heard it at once. This idiom is always used when a desire is quickly fulfilled.

^3 Such a desire is not called a vow <note>original: *nidr*</note>, but a petitionary curse or prayer <note>original: *da'ueh*</note>.

^4 A "basket of rice" <note>original: *quffet ruzz*</note> equals 100 kilograms (about 33 *rotl* <note>a local unit of weight</note>).

...be more powerful than the others. This is why, when inspecting several shrines in the same area, we observe that some are cleaner, better maintained, and wealthier than others. Besides the great prophets—such as Moses, Abraham, David, Christ, Reuben <note>original: Rûbîn</note>, Samuel, Saleh, and others—who are held in higher regard than the rest, there are certain <term>welîs</term> <note>local Muslim saints or holy men</note> like the Badriyeh, Salman al-Farsi, al-Qatrawani,¹ and others, who enjoy a widespread reputation.

Some saints do not like to see a woman entering their sanctuaries, even to fulfill a vow made in their name. This is especially true of Saint Sabas <note>original: Mâr Sâbâ</note>. Consequently, some women of the Greek Orthodox Church never make offerings to him, since he will not allow any of them to enter his sanctuary. The following story illustrates this belief:² A Russian lady, who had offered him a golden lamp, wished to bring it herself. She wore men's clothes and entered the monastery church unrecognized, placing the lamp in front of his icon. An unseen power threw the lamp away and spilled the oil. Every time the lamp was replaced, it was thrown even further away. The astonished <term>raiys</term> <note>head of the monastery</note>, who observed everything that was happening, searched for the reason. As soon as he found out that the donor was a distinguished woman, he begged her to leave the monastery, assuring her that Saint Sabas does not accept any offerings from women and even prohibits females from entering the monastery.³

Vows are not only made to sanctuaries where a <term>maqâm</term> <note>a sacred station or memorial</note> and a tomb are found, but every other shrine combination we have studied also enjoys this privilege. Naturally, what is vowed to these shrines—such as stones, caves, trees, and springs—is generally of much less value than what is offered to the <term>anbiyâ</term> <note>prophets</note>. Offerings to supposed holy stones, trees, and waters serve as another connecting link to ancient, primitive religions.

The tomb is believed to be in a cave beneath the building. A heap of <term>shid</term> <note>lime or plaster</note> was placed in the northeast corner of the room. To the west of the sanctuary lies the tomb of his servant.</note>

Vows may even be made to living persons. Generally, these privileged men belong to one of the following classes:

1. The descendants of a holy man. A thorough examination of the present saints <note>original: *awliyâ*</note> shows that some of them are recently recognized and that their descendants are still living. Cases in point are Hajj <note>one who has completed the pilgrimage to Mecca</note> Obeid,¹ Abdallah,² and the holy men <note>original: *irḍjâl*</note> of Sufah.³ Simple things are promised to the living sons of these saints, such as tobacco,⁴ a hen,⁵ or a cloth garment <note>original: *tôb*</note>.⁶
2. Sheikhs of a Sufi order <note>original: *țarîqah*</note> or those who are considered especially pious, such as Sheikh Abu Halawy.
3. Servants of a shrine or a priest. Vows of this class are especially made by Christians. The following case illustrates this type: A man of Jifna whose son was sick vowed:
> "O Saint George, if my child gets well, I shall offer curtains⁷ (literally, cloths) for your church and a vestment for the priest."⁸
4. Mentally impaired men. In Ain Arik, there used to live an insane⁹ man who was silent most of the time and walked only backwards. Not only the inhabitants of this village but also those of the surrounding places considered him a saint <note>original: *welî*</note>. They thought that by his behavior one could foretell the future of the village. If he shouted during the night, rain fell; and if he ran aimlessly to and

---

1 In Ain Karim. The sanctuary <note>original: *maqâm*</note>, which is at the same time a mosque <note>original: *djâmi‘*</note> with a minaret <note>original: *mêdaneh*</note>, is inside the village. Adjacent to the shrine there is a fruit garden. Anyone who takes a cutting from these trees and plants it finds that it will not grow.
2 West of Sheikh Ghet (near Deir Ghassaneh). His sanctuary was originally a cave which was changed into a small room with cloisters in front. The tomb of his wife (es-Slahiyeh) is beside his. His family all belong to the house of Mustafa Shaniur (O. S. Barghuti).
3 The sanctuary is situated on the top of a hill to the west of Deir Ghassaneh and contains three tombs for the male descendants (el-Majdub, Ibrahim, and Ali) and two for the female descendants (daughters of el-Majdub). Outside the sanctuary, there are four other tombs, one for Sheikha <note>a female holy leader or saint</note> Salhah and the other for her maid, Hanur. I owe this information to O. S. Barghuti.
4 Tobacco <note>original: *tunbâk*</note> used in the water pipe <note>original: *argîleh*</note>.

8 It is the official liturgical suit worn by the priest in the church.

...for they knew that the **mounted police** <note>original: *haiyâleh*, literally "cavalry" or gendarmerie</note> were approaching the village to collect taxes. <margin>Villagers were often terrified when the gendarmerie arrived because the soldiers collected taxes heartlessly and forced the people to pay for many unnecessary expenses.</margin> Women used to vow a hen to the saint if a member of their family was sick. A few hairs from the saint's head <note>Likely from the saint's tomb or a relic</note> were always taken when the hen was presented, and these hairs were used to **fumigate** the patient. <note>original: *Aberglaube*, p. 72, n. 5. Fumigation involves burning the hair so the sick person can inhale the smoke for healing.</note>

Sometimes vows are made to objects or places that are not connected with any known saint. As soon as someone believes they have witnessed a spontaneous, miraculous sign, the place is regarded as being inhabited by a supernatural power—likely anonymous pious men <note>original: *ṣullâḥ*</note>—to whom vows can then be made. The following example is a good illustration of this:

To the left of the main road <note>original: "carriage road"</note> leading from Jerusalem to Kolôniâ, and opposite the last house of Liftâ (which is located some distance from the main village and directly below the road), there is a cave where some peasants from Liftâ used to live during the summer months. <margin>The place is called el-Ḥômeh. The same family mentioned here still lives in the house opposite the cave.</margin> While staying in this cave, the family lost one member after another. Eventually, the father noticed a mysterious light in the cave. This made it clear to him that the place was inhabited by a superhuman power who had punished him for **defiling its home** <note>original: "defiled its habitation," meaning to make a holy place spiritually unclean through mundane use</note>. He immediately moved away from the spot and vowed to offer a sheep and light an oil lamp once a week. <note>A similar story is told about **Sheikh Ḥusên** near ed-Dâhrîyeh. After a mysterious light was seen one night under a **lote-tree** (original: *sidr* tree), the tree was immediately regarded as growing on the site of a saint (original: *welî*), and a small shrine (original: *maqâm*) was built there.</note>

There is another set of vows that have no connection with the types described so far. In these cases, offerings are made to objects that are not associated with any holy man, holy place, or sacred item. The best illustration is the following custom: sterile women who go to the **festival** <note>original: *môsam*</note> of el-Ḥusên near Ascalon take a bath in the sea and promise: 

> "If I become pregnant, O sea, I shall sacrifice a sheep in your honor." <note>original: *In ḥbilt yâ baḥr la-aḍbaḥlak ḫarûf*</note>

During the festival the following year, a woman who has received the blessing of motherhood kills the sheep on the shore, in...

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on vows made to natural elements like the sea and transitions into the theological distinction between vows made to God versus those made to saints. # CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

...such a way that the blood flows into the sea. While throwing the **offal** <term>saqaṭ</term>¹ <note>original: internal organs and extremities</note> of the sheep into the water, she exclaims:²

> “Take your vow, O sea.”³ <note>original: *Ḥôd niḏrak yâ baḥr*</note>

Although such customs are actually very rare, they carry us back to past ages when the sea was honored and worshipped as a divinity.

A connecting link between the two previous opposing groups—that is, between objects and places with no religious association and those directly connected with saints—are places where, according to local belief, dervish music or prayers have been heard, a greenish light has been seen, or the smell of burning incense has been detected. The person who first discovers such a place makes the initial vow. A good illustration of this belief is the story of Djum’ah (mentioned previously on pages 59–60).

In closing this section, we may highlight the following two points:

1. In many cases, vows are made to God without mentioning any saint at all.⁴

> “If my child gets well, O God, I shall sacrifice a sheep for you.” <note>original: *In ṭâb ibnî yâ allâh la-aḏbaḥlak ḫarûf*</note>

In such a case, the sheep may be slaughtered in any location, and the meat is given to the poor. However, vows made directly to God are becoming less and less frequent.

2. Most of the Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note> I questioned assured me that it is an irreligious act to make a vow solely in the name of a saint. Everything comes from the Almighty and must therefore be offered in His name. *Whoever makes a vow to a **saint** <term>welî</term> without mentioning God, his sacrifice becomes carrion,* <note>original: *Illî biynder lâ-walî min dûn ḏikr allâh ṣârat ḏbîḥtu fṭîṣeh*</note>. A "dead beast" <note>an animal not ritually slaughtered</note> is, of course, not accepted by either God or a saint and should not be eaten by people.⁵ The only correct formulas are of the following type: *If my child gets well, O God, You will have [this offering], O Hauwâṣ...* <note>original: *in ṭâb ibnî yâ allâh ilak yâ Hauwâṣ*</note>

> “If my child gets well, O God, you will have, O Hauwâṣ ...”

This principle is followed more rigidly by the Bedouin than by the village peasants, which is well illustrated in the formula recited when a **sacrifice** <term>dbîḥah</term> is killed by a Bedouin. He says: *From you and to you, O...* <note>original: *minnak u ilak yâ*</note>

¹ **Offal** <term>Saqaṭ</term> refers to the head, intestines, limbs, lungs, and other internal organs. It will be described more fully later on.
² "Take your vow, O sea."
³ See *Superstition* <note>original: *Aberglaube*</note>, pages 75, 76.
⁴ "If my child gets well, O God, I shall slaughter a sheep for you."
⁵ Refer to the **Quran** <note>original: *Qorân*</note>.

It transitions from the theological necessity of mentioning God to the specific formulas and categories of vows used by the people.</meta>

# CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

“...it is from Thee and to Thee, O God; reward and recompense for our lord Moses; God is great.”¹ <note>original: *allâh, adjr u tawâb la-saiydnâ Mûsâ allâh akbar*</note> While this is the formal religious rule, popular folk religion is in many ways at odds with the official doctrines of the Quran and the Bible.

The formulas used when making a vow vary. Usually, the word *nidr* <note>original: *niḏr*, meaning "vow"</note> is used: “A vow is upon me, O Prophet Moses; if my son recovers, I shall offer you a sheep.” <note>original: *nidrun 'alaiy yâ nabî Mûsâ in ṭâb ibnî la-aqaddimlak ḥarûf*</note> The word “vow” may sometimes be omitted: “O my lady Badriyeh, I owe you a jar of oil if my son returns healthy from America.” A third common form is: “If my brother is cured, I will light a candle the length of his height for you, O Green Saint.”² <note>original: *in ṭâb aḫûy la-aḍwîlak šam'ah ṭûluh yâ Ḥaḍr el-Aḫḍar*. The "Green Saint" or *al-Khadr* is a popular figure often identified with St. George or Elijah.</note>

All these examples are officially known as “bound vows” <term>nudûr muqayyadah</term>,³ which are vows tied to a specific condition that must be fulfilled once the request is granted. There is another class of vows called “free” or “unconditional” vows <term>nudûr muṭlaqah</term>, where no condition is attached to the fulfillment: “I impose upon myself for your sake, O Messenger of God, the obligation of fasting for two months.” <note>original: *ilak 'alaiy yâ rasûl allah an aṣûm šahrên*</note> In some parts of Palestine, specific expressions are used when vowing an animal; these will be described later. A beautiful formula is found in the following verse, recited by a mother visiting St. George’s church with her two children, asking the saint to keep them alive:

> “O Green Saint, two birds I bring to you today;
> One fully fledged, the other with kohl-lined eyes <note>original: *imkaḥḥal el-'êni*, referring to the dark, bright eyes of a very young infant</note>.
> I vow that if these two should live,
> I will offer sacrifices and pay my debt to the Green Saint.”⁴

---

¹ These two words, "reward and recompense," clearly express a very important fact regarding animal sacrifices <term>dhabā'ih</term>: namely, that they are performed for the spiritual benefit of the saint to whom they are offered.
² Heard from a Bedouin of the Adwan tribe.
³ At times, a very indefinite promise is made: “I vow to you, O Saint Michael, if my son walks, [I will give] whatever my soul prompts me to give.” <note>original: *ilak 'alaiy yâ mâr Miḫâ'îl in mišî ibnî illî biyṭla' min nafsî*</note>
⁴ Averroes <note>original: Ibn Rušd</note>, *The Beginning of the Prudent and the End of the Frugal* <note>original: *Bidâyatu l-Mudjtahid ua Nihâyatu l-Muqtaṣid*</note>, vol. I, pp. 341, etc.
⁵ From the town of Beit Jala.

"I take this vow upon myself: if these two remain alive, I shall offer sacrifices and pay my dues to <term>Ḥaḍr</term>."¹ <note>original: *al-Khidr*, a holy figure in Palestinian tradition often associated with Saint George or Elijah, known as the "Green Saint."</note>

Another verse of this sort, also used by Christians, is:

->O Virgin Mary, the silk of Damascus I will give as your belt;<-
->I vow that if those who are absent return, I shall need you (that is, I shall fulfill my promise).²<- 
<note>original: *Yâ ‘Adrâ Mariam ḥarîr eš-Šâm zunnârik; nidrun ‘alaiya in adjû l-ghuyâb la-aḥtâdjik*</note>

Sometimes, a person in great difficulty goes to a sanctuary, prays with devotion, begs for help, and makes a vow which they then write on the wall of the shrine. In this way, they bind themselves doubly to the promise, while the <term>welî</term> <note>original: *walī*, a Muslim saint or "friend of God" who acts as a protector of a locality.</note> is continually reminded by the writing. In the <term>maqâm</term> <note>original: *maqām*, a sacred shrine or tomb of a saint.</note> of <term>eš-šêḫ ‘Ôkâšeh</term> <note>original: *Sheikh Okasha*, a shrine in Jerusalem dedicated to a companion of the Prophet Muhammad.</note>, I found the following inscription, which is an excellent illustration of this custom: 

"I entrust to this place my testimony: there is no God but <term>Allâh</term> and Mohammed is God’s apostle. O my lord <term>‘Ôkâšeh</term>, if <term>Aḥmad</term>, the son of <term>Ḥadîdjeh</term>, the son of <term>Zakâriâ</term>, is released from jail with God’s help and your help, I will bring to you, O my lord ‘O, three <term>oqîyeh</term> <note>original: *uqiyyah*, a traditional unit of weight. Three oqîyeh is approximately 1.6 lbs or 750 grams.</note> of oil, and I shall come to visit you. Pray to your God to hear my prayer, O my lord ‘O..."

In offering the promised vow, one of the following expressions may be used: 
* "Your vow has come to you, O..." <note>original: *adjâk nidrak yâ ...*</note>
* "Take your sacrifice, O..." <note>original: *ḥôḍ d̠bîḥtak yâ ...*</note>
* "Here is your candle, O..." <note>original: *haiy šam‘tak yâ ...*</note>

Vows may be made at any time. In some regions, such as <term>Benî Zêd</term>³ <note>original: *Bani Zeid*, a group of villages north of Jerusalem.</note>, the night is specifically chosen for this purpose. For instance, a woman whose child is dangerously ill, and who is therefore in great anxiety, will choose the night to make her vow if possible.

shortly after midnight. She goes out of the house to speak directly with her God. Uncovering her chest,^1 and lifting up her arms, she makes a vow. Sometimes she goes entirely naked^2 and implores the Almighty or some saint for help, promising him an offering. It is believed that at such a time—when all human beings are at rest^3—the angels fill the atmosphere and hear human wishes better than in the daytime.^4 They carry them to heaven at once and bring them to the desired saint.^5

Whoever makes a vow must keep it: "Every vow is an obligation." <note>original: *kullu nidrin fard*</note> Both the Bible^6 and the Quran^7 give special rules for their fulfillment. The sooner a promise is carried out, the better. The saints sometimes remind a person who has not kept his promise. A peasant promised <term>Sheikh Ibrahim</term>^8 <note>original: *eš-šêh Ibrâhîm*</note> an offering. As a long period passed without an attempt on the part of the man to carry out his obligation, the saint appeared to him in a dream and warned: "Pay your vow at once; if you are unable to do all that you have promised, bring part of it." Generally, the <term>awliya</term> <note>original: *awliâ*, the plural of *wali*, meaning "friends of God" or saints.</note> are not so gracious, but are greatly irritated by such conduct and will punish the culprit severely. A woman vowed to give <term>Sheikh Hussein</term>^9 an offering and did not keep her word. The angry saint <note>original: *welî*</note> threw a <term>bannay</term>^10 at her and she had a miscarriage. Some think that saints try to remind a man who has not fulfilled his promise in a gentle, but sometimes also in a severe way. Thus, when the clothes of a person are caught in

thistles, or if he falls ill, he may be asked by his friends,<sup>1</sup> "Have you bound yourself by a promise which you have not yet fulfilled?"<sup>2</sup>

It is often possible—in case a man is unable to fulfill his promise exactly—to change some part of it or to substitute another for it. If, for example, someone vows to sacrifice a sheep, it is of course preferable to sacrifice it at the saint's shrine; but if he is unable to go himself, he may send money to the <term>caretaker</term> <note>original: *qaiym*, a guardian of a sanctuary</note> or to a friend, who will buy the animal and offer it in his name. The sheep may even be slaughtered in the village in the name of the prophet.

Similarly, in case a woman promises to walk barefoot to a sanctuary and tries to do so but is unable to continue the journey for some physical reason, she may be released from her promise by doing something else, such as offering a sum of money in the name of the saint, giving it to the poor, or depositing it at the shrine. The Prophet Mohammed is reported to have said:<sup>3</sup> "Whoever vows to walk to a place and cannot do so, it is not permissible for him to fulfill his vow by walking."<sup>4</sup>

Whenever a person cannot fulfill their vows, they go to a <term>sheikh</term> <note>original: *šêḫ*, a religious leader or elder</note> and ask for advice; he is generally told how to change the vow and what to offer as a substitute. It is a widespread belief among the people of Palestine that, while every <term>vow</term> <note>original: *nidr*</note> may be replaced by another, a vow of fasting a certain number of days or weeks (besides <term>Ramadan</term> <note>original: *Ramaḍân*</note>) cannot be changed. This vow cannot be "bought"<sup>5</sup> in any way. According to the regulations of the Old Testament, every vow could be changed to a payment of money except for a sacrificial animal.<sup>6</sup>

It very often happens that during the fulfillment of a promise one binds himself to making continual offerings. A father once promised the prophet Moses that if his only son, who was dangerously sick, should recover—

<sup>2</sup> Everyone who is reminded in one of the above-mentioned ways will at once say: "Your vow, O [Saint's Name], will surely reach you." <note>original: *lâzim nidrak yṣalak yâ . . .*</note>
<sup>3</sup> Muslim theologians have discussed the subject of vows very minutely in their books. I shall mention only the following question which may happen often in daily life: Is a person allowed to bind themselves to abstain from <term>permitted things</term> <note>original: *mubâḥât*</note>? Yes, is the answer, provided that matrimonial duties are never neglected.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the performance and types of religious vows (nidr) in Palestinian folk practice. It moves from the general principles of substitution to specific examples of costly vows and a systematic classification of what people promise to saints.</meta>

he promised he would give him a sheep. While the father—after the recovery of the child—was offering the sheep at the shrine of the prophet, he exclaimed:¹
> “Here is your vow, O Interlocutor of God <note>original: *Kalîm Allâh*, a title for Moses</note>, and if you keep my son well, I vow you a sheep every year.”
Not only Muslims <note>original: Mohammedans</note> but also Christians may bind themselves in such a way.

Most vows are not expensive, but some are truly costly. A man of Abu Dis who fell seriously ill during his stay in America vowed:²
> “O God, if I return to my family in perfect health, I shall build a minaret for the mosque of my village.”
He recovered, and as soon as he returned to his country, he built the minaret in question.³ Another even more expensive promise was that of a <term>sheikh</term> <note>original: *šēḫ*, a religious leader or elder</note> of the el-Imâm family in Jerusalem.⁴ During his final illness, he promised:
> “If I get well, O Prophet of God (Muhammad), I will build a <term>mosque</term> <note>original: *djâmiʿ*</note> with a minaret. But in case I die, I beg you, my relatives, to clothe forty orphans in my name.”

The things which may be vowed are so numerous that it appears impractical to give them in a single list. They may be divided according to the type of vow into (1) material offerings,⁵ and (2) promised work. However, a better classification is the following, which arranges them according to their purposes:

1. Things which serve directly for the upkeep of the <term>maqâm</term> <note>original: *maqâm*, a shrine or sacred tomb</note>:
    a) Offerings which serve to preserve and beautify the sanctuary.
    b) Material for repairs.
    c) Personal work.
2. Vows of food made in the name of a saint and offered (mostly) in his shrine. The shrine itself derives no direct benefit from the offering, but in most cases, the poor receive a portion:
    a) Animal sacrifices.
    b) <term>Sacrifice</term> <note>original: *Qurbân*</note> or a <term>banquet for God</term> <note>original: *walîmeh lil-lâh*</note>.
    c) Meatless food.

² <note>original: *In irdjiʿt bis-salâmeh la-ʿyâlî yâ Allâh la-abnî mēdaneh ladjâmiʿ baladî.* Translation: "If I return in safety to my family, O God, I will build a minaret for my town's mosque."</note>

### I. Things which serve for the upkeep of the <term>maqâm</term> <note>original: *maqâm*, a shrine or sacred tomb of a saint</note>

Things vowed and offered to a saint which serve directly for the upkeep, decoration, or repair of the shrine make up by far the largest number of all vows. Most of the offerings belonging to this group are so simple and inexpensive that even the poorest peasant is able to offer something. This is the reason for their popularity.

a) Offerings which are used to maintain and to beautify the shrine

The two most important items in this category—oil and incense—are held in special favor today, just as they were among the ancient peoples of the Near East. Olive oil is vowed and offered more than anything else. Peasants and city dwellers, Christians and Muslims <note>original: Mohammedans</note>, rich and poor alike vow oil, and it may be offered to any sort of sanctuary. The olive tree—called *šadjaret en-nûr* <note>original: *šadjaret en-nûr*, "the tree of light"</note> in the <term>Quran</term> <note>original: Qorân</note>—is regarded as holy. It demonstrates its supernatural power through its living spirit <note>original: animation</note>. 

The best example of this is the *zêtûnet en-nabî* <note>original: "the olive tree of the Prophet"</note> among Muslims, and the following belief among Christians: it is thought that olive trees kneel down on the night of the Feast of the Holy Cross because the heavens are believed to open on that very night. <note>Muslims believe that heaven opens once every year on the "Night of Power" (original: *lêlatu l-Qadr*).</note> A peasant from Bethlehem told me the following story. One night he lost his mule. He searched through the dark night in vain for his animal; but as he was searching in an olive grove, he observed that the ground was...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the sacred nature of the olive tree, concluding an anecdote about trees kneeling in reverence before moving into the scriptural and legendary origins of the tree in Islamic and Christian traditions.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Muslim <note>original: Mohammedan</note> Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

...covered with branches of trees. He did not trust his own eyes, and since he could find no explanation, he tore off a piece of his clothing and fastened it to a branch, marking the spot so he could find it early the next day and investigate this mystery in the light of day. Returning home, he told his relatives what had happened. No one could explain it. An old man, sitting in the corner, shook his head solemnly and reminded everyone that this was the night of the Feast of the Holy Cross, when all the trees reverently kneel down before their Master. The peasant went the next day to the scene of his nighttime adventure and saw all the trees standing upright, while the piece of cloth he had fastened to a branch was floating high up in the air at the very top of the tree.²

The Quran³ and the Bible mention oil and the olive tree very often. According to the Quran <note>original: Sûrah XCV, 1</note>, God swears⁴ by this tree, and it is therefore called "the blessed tree" <note>original: *eš-šadjarah el-mubârakeh*</note>. It was given to Adam and Eve after their expulsion from Paradise, and it was the first plant to appear after the Great Flood.⁵ The Arabs say that Adam suffered from a very severe skin condition. He begged the Almighty for help, and God sent the angel Gabriel with an olive sprig. The angel commanded Adam: "Take this sprig, plant it, and prepare an oil from its fruit which will cure all diseases except poisoning."⁶ Palestinian peasants sometimes swear by the olive tree, saying: "By the life of the Tree of Light" <note>original: *wiḥyât šadjret en-nûr*</note>.⁷ Oil is still used to light private homes as well as churches, mosques, and shrines. It remains as popular today in folk medicine, religious ceremonies, and magical practices as it was in the ancient Near East.⁸

The 27th of Ramadan <note>the "Night of Power" in the Islamic calendar</note> is especially famous for this.
² On the Feast of the Ascension of the Virgin, the trees are also believed to kneel.

⁸ In the Bible, oil is very often used to symbolize power, joy, light, life, and wisdom.

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the sacred use of olive oil in Palestinian folk religion, moving from the tree's legendary origins to the specific ritual of offering oil at the shrines of saints.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

This oil—which is always *zêt zêtûn*¹ <note>original: olive oil</note> and never *sîridj* <note>original: sesame oil</note>—is used for the small oil lamps, which will be described below. According to the ability of the person making a vow, they may promise a *jarrah*² <note>original: djarrah; a large jar</note>, half a *jarrah*, a *ratl* <note>original: raṭl; a unit of weight</note>, or a *qazâzeh* (also called a *qannîneh*, a bottle of 800–1000 ccm). Very often the quantity is not specified, and many *fellahin* <note>original: fellâḥîn; Palestinian peasants</note> offer only a partly filled bottle. In villages far from cities, an *ibrik* <note>original: ibrîq</note> (jug) of oil is given instead of a *qannîneh*. The vessels in which the oil³ is taken are left in the sanctuary. This is the reason why we find dozens of such bottles or jugs scattered about a typical shrine. Good examples of this practice are seen at *Qubbet el-Arba'in* <note>The Dome of the Forty</note>, *Sheikh 'Anbar*, *el-'Azeirat*, *es-Sidri*, etc. In *el-Badriyeh* (*Sharafat*), there are two large jars quite full of oil.⁴ Empty bottles, broken jars, and jugs are not removed from the shrine, and thus we encounter many potsherds scattered throughout the place. In large *maqams* <note>original: maqâms; shrines</note>, the one who makes the vow gives the oil to the *khaddam* <note>original: ḥaddâm; servant or attendant</note> of the shrine and tells him plainly that it belongs to the saint, and that it should be used exclusively in the shrine. "I have removed the vow from my neck (that is, I remove all obligations from myself) and place it on your neck (and impose it on you)." <note>original Arabic: *Qimt en-nidr min raqbatî uḥaṭṭêtuh fî raqbatak*</note> Everyone believes that if such an offering is not used in the shrine of the saint, the *wali* <note>original: welî; Muslim saint</note> will punish the servant of the shrine and not the one who pays the vow. Expressions like the one above are especially common among Christians who have vowed an offering to a prophet or *wali* whose shrine is in Muslim <note>original: Mohammedan</note> hands: *Nabi Musa* <note>Prophet Moses</note>, *Nabi Dahud* <note>Prophet David</note>, *Ibrahim el-Khalil*⁵ <note>Abraham the Friend of God</note>, etc.

---

¹ Also called *zêt hilu* <note>sweet oil</note>. Kerosene <note>original: petroleum</note> is *zêt kâz*, and not *zêt murr* <note>bitter oil</note>, as Kahle states in *PJB VIII*, 139.
² A *jarrah* contains from 5–7 *ratl*, depending on the different districts of Palestine.
³ The following story illustrates the belief that olive oil strengthens the body more than melted butter (*samneh*). A wife had a son of her own and a stepson. Both were shepherds. Every day before they drove the animals into the fields, she gave her own son—who was always preferred—bread and *samneh*, while the other received nothing but bread dipped in oil. After finishing their meals, both used to wipe their hands by rubbing them on their wooden walking sticks. The stick of the woman's own son was soon hollowed out by weevils <note>because of the animal fat</note>, while the stick of his stepbrother became gradually harder and stronger <note>preserved by the olive oil</note>. (Taken from the written notes of my father, Rev. B. Canaan.)
⁴ Not all the oil found in a shrine comes from offerings. In large *maqams*, some of it comes from the *waqf* <note>religious endowment</note> of the *wali* or from the department of religious endowments (*dairat el-auqaf*).
⁵ Heard from a woman of Taiybeh and from Imm Elyas.

<summary>This page details the ritualistic offering of olive oil at Palestinian shrines, explaining the types of vessels used, the transfer of religious obligation to shrine attendants, and the folklore regarding the strengthening properties of olive oil compared to butter.</summary>
<keywords>olive oil, maqam, Palestinian folklore, religious vows, Nabi Musa, waqf, fellahin, ethnographic study</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual offerings at Palestinian shrines, specifically focusing on the practice of vowing and presenting olive oil and matches to the saints.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

Two customs must still be mentioned in this connection. Very often a person takes a vow to offer a quantity of oil every year to a *wali* <note>original: welî; a Muslim saint</note>: "I will give you, O Sheikh Salman the Persian <note>original: šêḫ Salmân el-Fârsî</note>, a *ratl* <note>original: raṭl; a traditional unit of weight</note> of olive oil every year if you cure my son." Although this custom may be followed with any offering, it is more common with oil. Many peasants take some oil with them as a gift every time they go to visit the shrine, even if they are not bound by a vow. In such cases, only half a bottle is presented. It is believed that this act pleases the saint, who then favors the giver. When I visited Isawiya <note>original: 'Êsâwîyeh</note>, I asked Sheikh Mohammed Ali <note>original: šêḫ Mohammed 'Alî</note>—the title *sheikh* here means "old man" <note>original: šêḫ</note>—to send someone to guide me to the shrine of Sheikh Anbar. The young lad brought a bottle half-filled with oil and placed it in the *maqam* <note>original: maqâm; a sanctuary or shrine</note>. On my questioning whether he or one of his relatives had made a vow to offer oil, he answered: "No, but every time we come to the neighborhood of the saint, we bring some oil with us."

Most of those who offer oil take a matchbox (*ilbit kabrit* or *kahhateh*) with them and leave it there. Thus, an opportunity is given to every visitor to light the oil lamps. A great number of full and empty boxes are found in the niches <note>original: ṭaqât</note> of some shrines.

It is expected that everyone fulfills their promise exactly, since the saint takes every vow at its exact wording. Thus, a larger offering than what was mentioned in a vow might not be accepted by the saint. A very poor widow, whose only child was dangerously ill, promised in her excited state and without knowing what she was uttering: "O Saint George, if my son gets well, I shall offer you oil in an eggshell" <note>original Arabic: yâ Ḥaḍr ed-Djerîd in ṭâb ibnî la-aqaddimlak zêt fî qišret bêḍah</note>. As soon as her child recovered, she hastened with a pitcher of oil to fulfill her vow. She filled the oil lamp which hung in front of Saint George’s picture, but an unseen power tilted the lamp and the oil was spilled. The same thing happened every time the lamp was filled. The priest who observed this inexplicable happening asked the woman, "What was your promise?" And when she told him, he answered very earnestly: "The saint accepts only what is due to him, and not a grain more" <note>In Arabic, the word "grain" (*qamḥah*) specifically refers to a grain of wheat</note>.

---

¹ *Sheikh* here does not mean a saint (*welî*) but "old man."
² In Arabic, they use the word *qamhah*, "grain of wheat," also in this sense.
³ Related by Abu Elyas, Jerusalem.

<sig>10</sig>
<vocab>vow, oil, wali, sheikh, ratl, maqam, matchbox, niches, St. George, grain of wheat</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the traditions of offering olive oil and matchboxes at Palestinian shrines, noting that peasants often bring small gifts even without a formal vow. It highlights the folklore belief that saints demand the literal and exact fulfillment of a vow, illustrated by a story of Saint George rejecting an offering that exceeded a widow's specific promise.</summary>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual offerings at Palestinian shrines, focusing on the specific use of fats—such as clarified butter—and various types of candles and lamps used to fulfill religious vows.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

The oil presented is used only to light the oil lamps. Instructions regarding the use of oil taken from the lamps of sanctuaries will be given later. Since olive oil is not as abundant in Transjordan <note>modern-day Jordan</note>, melted butter <term>samneh; clarified butter</term>^1 takes its place in vows and for lighting lamps in shrines. Candles <term>šam'i, plural of šam'ah</term> and kerosene oil <term>kâz or zêt kâz</term> are also promised as vows. In the case of candles, not only the number but very often the quality and the length are specified.^2 "O Prophet Moses <note>original: Nebî Mûsâ</note>, if I find my boy in good health, I will light you a beeswax candle the same length as him."^3 Beeswax candles <term>šam'i naḥl</term>^4 are finer than tallow candles <note>candles made from animal fat</note> and are considered more fitting as offerings. The length of candles is specified only in cases where children are sick. When no specific detail is provided, the following expression is used: "O [Saint] ..., I shall light you a candle" <note>original: yâ ... la-aḍwîlak šam'ah</note>. Sometimes the weight of the candles is specified. In the shrine of el-Badriyeh, heaps of such candles may be seen,^5 mostly offered by Christians from Beit Jala <note>original: Bêt Djâlâ</note> and Bethlehem.^6 I have rarely found candles in less important sanctuaries.

In the case of the Church of St. Mary near Gethsemane, the vow may specify the number of candles and the way the vow must be fulfilled: "O my lady Mary, if my son Elias <note>original: Elyâs</note> walks, I shall light your staircase on both sides."^7 For every step, two candles are lighted. A few minutes later, the priest puts the light out, gathers the candles, and keeps them in the sanctuary for future use.^8 A vow of this sort must be paid on the first day of the Feast of the Virgin Mary. A still more comprehensive vow is the following: "If my son Ibrahim <note>original: Ibrâhîm</note> walks, I shall light a candle in every shrine which he enters."

The most common lamps used in shrines are small, crudely prepared pottery lamps, which resemble ancient Canaanite lamps in many respects. These pottery lamps <term>surdj; plural: srâdj</term> may be specifically mentioned in vows.

^3 Promised by a father who receives news of the illness of his child while he is away.
^4 The expression literally means "beeswax."
^5 I saw some of them hanging from the ceiling and others lying on the so-called tombs of her children.
^6 Christians place great trust in the help of this female saint <term>welî</term>.

^8 Reported by the mother of Elias <note>original: Imm Elyâs</note> in Jerusalem.

<meta>This page continues the description of lamp offerings in Palestinian shrines, specifically detailing where lamps are placed within the sanctuary and the various materials—from humble tin cans to expensive silver—used to construct them.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Muslim <note>original: Mohammedan</note> Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

Lamps are placed in various locations: in one of the cupboards of the tomb, in a niche in the wall, on the tomb itself or around it (at the shrines of Sheikh <note>original: eš-šêḫ</note> Hamad and the Dome of the Forty <note>original: Qubbet el-Arbaʿîn</note>), on a ledge in the wall (Sheikh Anbar), on a wooden bracket fastened to the wall <margin>1</margin> (Sultan Ibrahim el-Adhami, el-Badriyeh), in a cave (Ahmad el-Hwes), under a tree (Sheikh Abdallah), in a stony enclosure (Sheikh Fredj <margin>2</margin>), or in an open area (el-Umari <margin>3</margin>).

Sometimes we find tins crudely shaped by bending their sides upward into a lamp-like vessel which serves to hold the oil. <margin>4</margin> A lamp of this inferior type is not presented as a formal gift but is made on the spot due to the lack of a pottery lamp. <margin>5</margin>

The wick <margin>6</margin> is made in two ways:
1. A piece of cotton thread, or even a thin strip of cloth, is thoroughly dipped into the oil, with one end protruding above the surface of the liquid.
2. A thin wooden stick, about twice as long as a match, is wrapped well in cotton. The upper end of the cotton is allowed to project over the wood. After dipping the whole thing in oil, it is placed upright in the lamp and lighted.

Some people vow to provide a glass lamp, which may or may not have an outer silver casing. A <term>qandîl min fuddah; silver lamp</term> may be suspended by a silver chain in front of the saint's picture <margin>7</margin>, above the tomb <margin>8</margin>, or in front of the <term>mihrâb; a prayer niche indicating the direction of Mecca</term>. Wealthy people vow brass or silver candlesticks, or even an expensive chandelier <note>original: lustre</note> for candles or glass lamps. <margin>9</margin>

---

<margin>1</margin> These holders may be slightly more complex, looking like a small, elongated, narrow box without a top or front side. Occasionally, a top may be present.
<margin>2</margin> Located in Beit Hanina <note>original: Bêt Ḥanînâ</note>.
<margin>3</margin> Located in Jericho.
<margin>4</margin> In some places, empty sardine or other tins were used as lamps.
<margin>5</margin> At the shrine of the Prophet Danian <note>original: en-nabî Dâniân</note>, I found an old pan used as an oil lamp. I want to correct here the statement made on page 62 regarding the location of this sanctuary. It lies to the southwest of el-Khader <note>original: el-Ḥaḍer</note>, and not between that village and Artas <note>original: Arṭâs</note>. It is situated on a high mountain, commanding a magnificent view. The shrine is surrounded by ruins, and the simple niche is built in the midst of oak trees.
<margin>6</margin> <term>Ftîleh; wick</term>.
<margin>7</margin> Muslims <note>original: Mohammedans</note> never have human or animal pictures in their mosques or shrines. <note>The author likely refers to calligraphic depictions of the Prophet's name or the "Hand of Fatima" which sometimes serve a similar focal purpose, as Islamic law generally prohibits representational images.</note>

<margin>9</margin> I have not found pottery lamps in churches. For illustrations of pottery lamps, see McCown, *Annual of the American School*, vol. II—III, p. 28.

<language>English</language>
<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

Incense (<term>bakhur</term> <note>original: baḫḫûr</note>) is very often promised through a vow: 
> “O Sheikh <note>original: šêḫ</note> Sabbah, if my boy is released from jail, I shall burn incense for you.” 
Much more often, the phrase *I shall burn incense in your shrine* (<term>ad'aq bakhur fi maqamek</term>) is used. This incense may be burned in the shrine immediately, or it may be given to the servant (<term>khaddam</term>) or priest. In minor shrines, the incense is burned in an old dish, a piece of tin, or on a pottery shard. These objects are left behind in the shrine afterward. This is the case in all the shrines of Jericho, such as Sheikh Husen <note>original: eš-šêḫ Ḥusên</note>, the Dome of the Forty <note>original: Qubbet el-Arb'în</note>, ed-Daware <note>original: ed-Dawâ'rî</note>, and others. Sometimes the specific quantity of incense is stipulated in the vow (<term>nidr</term>). Incense may be offered in all types of shrines.<margin>2</margin>

Burning incense is a very ancient custom found in all religions and connected with many ceremonies.<margin>3</margin> It is not only done in shrines, but also in places believed to be inhabited by demons. In the first case, it is thought that one pleases the holy men by this act, while in the second instance, it drives away evil spirits. It is supposed that what pleases holy men and God is disliked by devils, demons, and evil spirits. For this reason, it is employed by sorcerers to expel evil spirits from those thought to be possessed by demons.

Other votive objects belonging to this category include: mats (<term>hasireh</term>, plural <term>husur</term>), carpets (<term>sidjjadeh</term>—<term>sidjjad</term>), brooms<margin>4</margin> (<term>mukunseh</term>—<term>makanes</term>), jars (<term>djarrah</term>—<term>djrar</term>, <term>hishsheh</term>—<term>hishash</term>), pails (<term>satl</term>—<term>stuleh</term>), tins<margin>5</margin> (<term>tanakeh</term>—<term>tanak</term>), ropes<margin>6</margin> (<term>habil</term>—<term>hbal</term>), water skins<margin>7</margin> (<term>qirbeh</term>—<term>qirab</term>; small ones are called <term>si'in</term>—<term>s'uneh</term>), wood (<term>hatab</term>), and so on. With the exception of the carpets, all these objects are vowed to all sorts of shrines, especially the simple ones. Carpets and *hudjur* (the plural of *hudjrah*)—long woolen—

---

<margin>1</margin> The word *da'aqa* (<term>Da'aqa</term>) in the sense of "burn" is not found in the standard Arabic dictionaries of Belot, Wahrmund, Kassab and Hammam, or in the *Muhit el-Muhit*.
<margin>2</margin> If a menstruating woman goes down to the Spring of Sheikh Husen <note>original: 'Ên eš-Šêḫ Ḥusên</note> (in Kolonia, also called the Spring of the Nut Tree <note>original: 'Ên ed-Djôz</note>), she must purify the place at once by burning incense. If she fails to do it, the servant of the saint <note>original: welî</note> (described as an *abd*, or "servant/slave") will appear and inflict a disease upon her.
<margin>3</margin> In the Old Testament, burning incense was one of the official religious rites (see Exodus 30:7–8, Leviticus 16:12, etc.).
<margin>4</margin> There are two kinds of brooms: the usual one with a broad sweeping surface, and a round, short one that ends in a small brush.
<margin>5</margin> These are used for storing or drawing water; they are sometimes specified as kerosene tins (<term>tanaket kâz</term>), as they were originally used for petroleum.
<margin>6</margin> For drawing water.
<margin>7</margin> For the transport and storage of water.

<summary>This page describes the ritual use of incense and the donation of household items as votive offerings in Palestinian shrines, explaining their role in both honoring holy figures and repelling evil spirits. It highlights how common objects like water skins and brooms are repurposed for sacred use in local sanctuaries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of votive offerings at Palestinian shrines, moving from carpets and decorative objects to specific types of jewelry and agricultural offerings like grain and grapes, highlighting the shared practices between Muslim and Christian communities.</meta>

# CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine <note>original title: "Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine"</note>

Carpets woven by women are presented to the more important sanctuaries.

Decorative objects are also sometimes promised through a vow and fastened to the wall of the <term>maqâm</term> <note>sanctuary or shrine</note> or to the tomb itself. Quranic verses, a <term>kaff qamih</term> <note>a "hand" of grain</note>, or jewels are often seen. Besides verses from the holy book, one sometimes finds the genealogical tree of the Prophet Muhammad, as well as representations of the Kaaba <note>the sacred building at the center of Islam's most important mosque in Mecca</note> and the sanctuary of Medina. A <term>kaff qamiḥ</term> is made of ears of grain with long stems woven together so that the ears appear on three sides—the two sides and the bottom—while the top is left without ears. This "hand" of grain is generally offered as a sign of thanks for a good harvest. In Beit Jala, every vineyard owner <margin>1</margin> used to bring a small basket of grapes (<term>sallit ‘inab</term> <margin>3</margin>) to the church on the Feast of the Transfiguration <margin>2</margin>. After blessing the grapes, the priest would distribute some of the fruit to those present. <margin>4</margin> These last two customs <margin>5</margin> are mentioned because they point to surviving traces <margin>6</margin> of ancient thanksgiving offerings. <margin>7</margin>

Jewels—or rather, ornaments like the <term>znâq</term> (necklace), <term>halaq</term> (earrings), <term>shakleh</term> (brooch), <term>hatim</term> (ring), <term>asawir</term> (bracelets), <term>shatweh</term> (the distinctive headdress of women from Bethlehem, Beit Jala, and Beit Sahour), and <term>saffeh</term> (the headdress of women from Ramallah and surrounding villages)—are also vowed. These items are either hung on the <term>stârah</term> <note>curtain or veil</note> of the tomb (such as at the shrine of al-Badriyyeh), placed around or on the <term>qûneh</term> <note>icon or religious picture</note> of a Christian saint, or sold with the proceeds used for the upkeep of the sanctuary. As soon as a Christian woman from Beit Jala <margin>8</margin> makes such a vow, she hangs the promised jewel on the icon of the saint. If the person for whom the vow (<term>nidr</term>) was made dies, the objects are taken back; however, if they recover, the items remain the property of the...

<margin>1</margin> Members of the Greek Orthodox Church.
<margin>2</margin> August 6th in the Julian Calendar (August 19th in the Gregorian Calendar).
<margin>3</margin> A small basket is also called a <term>qirtalleh</term>.
<margin>4</margin> Taken from the notes of my father.
<margin>5</margin> Many peasants in the villages around Nazareth set aside some barley and wheat for <term>Sheikh Shihab ed-Din</term> as soon as they finish harvesting. This grain is sold, and the money is used to host a picnic in the name and honor of the <term>wali</term> <note>Muslim saint</note>.
