<summary>This page details the variety of physical objects—ranging from woven carpets and "grain hands" to expensive jewelry and agricultural produce—offered by both Muslims and Christians at Palestinian shrines as acts of thanksgiving or fulfillment of vows.</summary>

<keywords>votive offerings, Palestinian shrines, Beit Jala, agricultural rituals, jewelry, religious syncretism, icons, Islamic art, Sheikh Shihab ed-Din, Saint Nicholas</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of votive offerings in Palestinian shrines, moving from general decorative objects to specific articles of jewelry, tomb coverings, and banners dedicated to the saints.</meta>

shrine.<sup>1</sup> Most of these ornaments, especially the <term>znaq</term> <note>original: znâq; a chin chain or necklace</note>, the <term>shatweh</term> <note>original: šaṭweh; a traditional bridal headdress</note>, and the <term>saffeh</term> <note>original: ṣaffeh; a row of coins used as an ornament</note> are costly pieces. They are offered by women who believe that by giving up such valuable items, they will secure the saint’s favor and sympathy. A description of the <term>shatweh</term> is important to illustrate the value of such a head-dress. It is cylindrical and solid, covered on the outside with red—or sometimes green—cloth. The top has a rigid projection covered with the same fabric. The front is decorated with several rows of gold and silver coins, while the back features only silver ones. The <term>shatweh</term> is fastened to the head by a band running below the chin. From both sides of the <term>shatweh</term>, the <term>znaq</term> hangs down.<sup>2</sup>

Vows that are offered specifically to the tomb itself include the <term>stara</term> <note>original: stârah</note> (or <term>ghata</term> <note>original: ghaṭâ</note>), which is a covering, and the <term>agal</term> <note>original: ‘aqâl</note> or <term>laffeh</term> (types of head-dresses). The <term>stara</term><sup>3</sup> is a large piece of cloth that covers the entire <term>cenotaph</term> <note>a tomb-like monument erected in honor of a person whose remains are elsewhere</note>. In less important shrines, it is made of a single colored cloth, while in the more significant ones, a thick, high-quality wool cloth (<term>juh</term><sup>4</sup> <note>original: djûh</note>) is presented. Red and green are the most common colors; white and yellow are less popular. Sometimes the cloth is bordered or even embroidered with Quranic verses. Very often, a combination of these colors is found in the same <term>stara</term>, created by sewing strips of different colored cloth onto the main cover. It is not uncommon for one tomb to be covered with several <term>starat</term> <note>plural of stara</note>, with the topmost one being the most recently vowed (such as at the shrines of the Prophet Moses, Badriyeh, etc.).<sup>5</sup>

Occasionally a <term>beyraq</term> <note>original: bêraq</note> (banner) is vowed, which is generally beautifully embroidered. The name of the saint, the names of the four <term>aqtab</term> <note>original: aqṭâb; spiritual leaders or "poles" in Sufism</note> (see below), and a verse from the Quran are usually embroidered on it.<sup>6</sup> The color of the turban (<term>agal</term>) and that of the <term>laffeh</term><sup>7</sup> (the dervish head-dress) must match the specific religious order to which the <term>sheikh</term> <note>original: šêh</note> belongs.

<sup>1</sup> From the written notes of my father.
<sup>2</sup> *Superstition* <note>original: Aberglaube</note>, p. 74, n. 6.
<sup>3</sup> Among the <term>welis</term> <note>saints</note> who possess a <term>stara</term> are Salman el-Farsi, ‘Anbar, Abd es-Salam, Badr, Our Lady el-Hadra, el-Bedriyeh, el-Halili, etc.
<sup>4</sup> In Sharafat, I heard the term <term>hirmzeh</term> used for the cloth of the <term>stara</term>.
<sup>5</sup> The <term>stara</term> does not always lie directly on the tomb monument; sometimes it is supported by a wooden frame that encloses the tomb (as seen with Beyram Shawish and el-Badriyeh).
<sup>6</sup> Such presents may be seen in the shrines of Lut <note>Lot</note>, Musa <note>Moses</note>, Dahud <note>David</note>, etc.
<sup>7</sup> The head-dress of some villages around Jerusalem is also a <term>laffeh</term>, which differs slightly from the one described in the text.

<summary>This page describes the specific types of valuable jewelry and textiles offered as vows to Palestinian shrines, including the elaborate "shatweh" headdress and the "stara" tomb coverings. It details the materials, colors, and religious significance of these offerings, noting how they reflect the donor's devotion and the saint's status.</summary>
<keywords>shatweh, znaq, stara, cenotaph, votive offerings, Palestinian shrines, textile arts, Quranic embroidery, dervish orders, Prophet Moses shrine</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of votive offerings, specifically focusing on tomb coverings (stara) and headdresses. It transitions into how natural resources like trees and land are dedicated to saints, as well as the practice of decorating shrines with henna and other pigments as an act of devotion.</meta>

belonged.<sup>1</sup> The <term>stara</term> <note>original: stârah; a decorative tomb cover</note> and the headdress are promised as vows only to saints whose tombs are located inside a building, and never to tombs located in the open air. However, in the case of some Bedouin saints, a headdress can be found on an exposed tomb. Our Lord al-Husayn <note>original: Seyidnâ el-Ḥusên</note>, located southeast of ed-Jorah (near Ascalon), has no physical tomb, but inside the <term>shrine</term> <note>original: maqâm</note>, a fragment of a pillar marks the spot where the head of al-Husayn was buried. The top of this pillar is adorned with a green <term>turban-wrap</term> <note>original: laffeh</note>, and a red cloth is placed below it.<sup>2</sup>

Some peasants (especially Christians) vow one or several trees to a saint. The fruit of these trees then belongs to the holy man. If the trees are cut down, the wood is given to the shrine, but the saint loses all further rights to that specific plot of land, since it was the trees and not the ground itself that were vowed. The land then returns to the previous owner or their descendants. Of course, a piece of land with its trees, or even a house, may also be donated to a shrine. Such property remains <term>waqf</term> <note>original: waqf; religious property or an endowment dedicated to God for charitable or religious purposes</note>.<sup>3</sup>

Vowing to decorate a shrine serves as a link between giving physical gifts and promising to perform labor. The most common material used for this is <term>henna</term> <note>original: ḥinnâ</note>, but we also find indigo <note>original: nîleh</note> and red lead <note>original: sirâqûn</note>, which have already been described. Common expressions of this vow include: "I come to you for protection, O my Lady of Damascus, if you grant me a son, I will sacrifice a sheep for you and dye your shrine with henna." <note>original: anâ dâḥil ‘alêkî yâ sittî eš-Šâmiyeh in arzaqtînî ṣabî la-adbaḥlik ḥarûf wa-aḥannîkî</note> When the two other colors are used, the word "embellish" <note>original: azauwiq</note> is used. Such vows are made exclusively by women.<sup>4</sup>

### b) Vowing material for the repair of the shrine

Vows promising materials for the repair of the <term>shrine</term> <note>original: maqâm</note> or its complete reconstruction are also very common. If such vows did not exist, a great number of sanctuaries would be in ruins, and the locations of some would have been lost completely. The

---

<sup>1</sup> Sheikh Rihan, Sheikh Ahmad at-Tori, Sheikh Anbar, Sheikh Abd as-Salam, etc.
<sup>2</sup> The large shrine is on the top of a hill about 20–30 minutes from the sea. There are no tombs or caves in its neighborhood. Two mulberry trees and a vineyard are his property.
<sup>3</sup> This is the reason why some churches have extensive property holdings.
<sup>4</sup> Even the stones of a holy <term>enclosure</term> <note>original: ḥuwêṭiyeh; a low wall or simple stone enclosure around a grave</note>—as in the case of Sheikh Said in Idna—may be painted with henna.

<summary>This page describes how various assets, including tomb covers, trees, and land, are dedicated to Palestinian saints, and explains the practice of women decorating shrines with henna or pigments as a form of answered prayer.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of votive offerings, moving from decorative items and land to physical building materials. It explains how communal promises are made to repair or build shrines (maqams) and the specific types of stone and mortar used in Palestinian villages.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

The following are the most important materials that may be vowed: **hewn stones** <note>original: *ḥadjârah*</note>, **rough stones** <note>original: *dabš* or *djabš*; unhewn, irregular stones</note>, **stones for vaulting** <note>original: *'uqqâd*</note>, **lime** <note>original: *šîd*</note>, **fine stone chips** <note>original: *nḥâteh*; the small pieces left over from stone-cutting</note>, water for making the **mortar** <note>original: *madjbûlîyeh*</note>,<sup>1</sup> **iron** <note>original: *ḥadîd*</note>, and **the door** <note>original: *el-bâb*; the wooden or sometimes iron door</note>. Generally, several people while gathered in the **village guest house** <note>original: *maḍâfeh*</note> agree that each person will bring something. The **sheikh** <note>original: *šêḫ*; the village leader or elder</note>, discussing the condition of the shrine, makes the first promise. One after another follows, saying, "I take it upon myself to offer..." <note>original: *anâ 'alaiyî...*</note>. Such a statement is considered a formal vow, and it must be fulfilled exactly.

Even when the **shrine** <note>original: *welî*; the tomb or sanctuary of a holy person</note> is not in a ruined state, many people vow lime, which is deposited in the immediate neighborhood of the shrine or even inside it. Such vows are made, primarily, by people who are burning lime. In this way, they hope to receive the assistance of the **saint** <note>original: *welî*</note> for the successful completion of their work.<sup>2</sup> A visitor to Muslim shrines will often find in them heaps of lime<sup>3</sup> generally covered with a coating of earth (for example, at the shrines of **Sheikh Anbar**, **Sultan Ibrahim**, **Sheikh Hamad**, and **Ahmad el-Karaki**, among others).

Another occasion when vows of this sort are made is when a wealthy peasant builds a house and promises some building material for the same reason: "If I finish building my house in safety, I will bring you, O [Saint]... three loads of lime" <note>original: *in ḥallaṣt ibnâit bêtî bis-salâmeh la-armîlak yâ . . . talât ḥmâl šîd*</note>.<sup>4</sup> Such vows are also made by Palestinians who become severely ill while abroad, as we have seen in the case of the man from Abu Dis. Sometimes a saint with no shrine, or one whose sanctuary is damaged, appears in a vision to someone in the village and orders him to build or repair the **shrine** <note>original: *maqâm*</note>. This person will then share his vision with his fellow villagers, and soon the necessary material is gathered.

***

<sup>1</sup> A mixture of lime (one-third) and earth (two-thirds) used for mortar.
<sup>2</sup> In 'Awartah, many of those who burn lime will offer to the **shrines of Ezra** <note>original: *el-'Azêrât*; referring to the tombs of the priests Eleazar and Itamar near Nablus</note> one **sack** <note>original: *fardeh*; about 30–50 kg</note> of lime. In this village, I heard the word *kubbâr* used for a small **lime kiln** <note>original: *lattôn*</note>.
<sup>3</sup> The lime may be deposited outside the shrine, in a nearby cave, in the shrine itself, or on its roof.
<sup>4</sup> The loads may be specified: "a camel's load," "a mule's load," or "a donkey's load" <note>original: *ḥiml djamal, ḥ. baghl, or ḥ. ḥmâr*</note>.

<summary>This page describes the communal practice of vowing building materials—such as stones, lime, and mortar—to repair or maintain Palestinian village shrines (maqams), often motivated by successful construction projects or recovery from illness.</summary>
<keywords>votive offerings, building materials, lime burning, maqam, Palestinian folklore, communal vows, 'Awartah, Abu Dis, religious architecture</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of materials donated to shrines, moving from the physical items like stone and lime to the communal labor and personal vows required for construction and repair.</meta>

### c) Personal labor

As soon as the materials are ready, the people of the village—men and women, adults and children—offer their help with the labor. One person provides two days of labor, another vows to hew some of the stone, a third promises to carry water, and so on; in a short time, the work is finished. Even the wealthy and the elderly consider it a special honor and a blessing to help build such a structure. The entire community joins in to help when it is time to build the vaulted ceiling <note>original: *el-ʻaqd*</note>. Everyone works busily to finish the shrine. However, only in rare cases does a sanctuary need to be built from scratch; generally, they only need to be repaired. For example, the roof might be leaking, the plaster <note>original: *qṣârah*</note> has fallen off, the door has been burnt, or the tomb has lost its whitewash. In these circumstances, a person generally makes a vow to perform a specific repair. 

"If you save my lime-kiln from ruin, O my lord, O *sheikh* <note>original: *šêḫ*</note> Abd es-Salam, I will bring you two loads of lime and will whitewash your tomb and the shrine."
<note>original Arabic: *ḥallaṣtillî el-lattôn min il-ḥarâb yâ sîdî yâ šêḫ ʻAbd es-Salâm la-armîlak ḥimlên šîd u la-aṭruš qabrak.*</note>

This vow was made by Mohammed of Anata, who had built a lime-kiln with some relatives. He had already been heating it for four time periods <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: *fṣûleh*, the plural of *faṣl* (season), used here to mean a half-day or twelve-hour shift</note>, when suddenly part of the kiln began to collapse. The owner, afraid of losing all his hard work, made the vow mentioned above. At that moment, the holy man appeared in the middle of the flames and began to extinguish the fire. They repaired the lime-kiln, lit the fire again, and the project was saved. Some examples of shrines repaired in this way include: a stone casing was built for the entrance of *esh-Sheikh es-Sidri*; the shrine of Anbar received an iron door; a tomb was built for *esh-Suwan*; the mosque <note>original: *djâmiʻ*</note> in Abu Dis received a minaret; the tombs of *ed-Dawari* were whitewashed; and a hall was built next to the mosque of Sultan Ibrahim in Beit Hanina.

Besides these expensive vows, there are others that are much simpler and cheaper. For instance, a woman may commit herself to sweeping a sanctuary for several days, weeks, or even longer. In the latter case, the shrine is—

<note>1</note> *Fasl* <note>original: *faṣl*</note> has the following meanings:
* *fasl es-saneh*: "a season of the year."
* *fasl maiyeh*: "twelve hours of water." This term is used when the water from a spring is divided among many gardens.
* A *fasl* of a day represents the twelve daylight hours or the twelve nighttime hours. Thus, a full twenty-four-hour day consists of two *fṣûleh* <note>plural of *fasl*</note>.

<summary>This page describes how Palestinian villagers provide communal labor and fulfill personal vows to build or repair local shrines (maqams), often in exchange for a saint's protection over their livelihoods.</summary>

...swept once weekly. Another person may promise to light the lamps for a period of time. This is usually done every Thursday evening. Still another will commit himself to filling the public water fountain <note>original: *sabîl*</note> with water. Some offer to work for three days or more in the sanctuary property <note>original: *waqf*</note> of the saint. Many women from the town of Beit Jala <note>original: *Bêt-Djâlâ*</note> vow to help harvest the olives at the monastery of Mar Elias <note>original: *Mâr Elyâs*</note>, while others vow to plow the vineyards of el-Khader <note>original: *el-Ḥaḍr*, the shrine of Saint George</note>.

## II. Food vows

These are generally offered at a shrine. The shrine itself does not benefit directly from these offerings, but in most cases, the poor receive a portion. This category can be divided into:

a) Animal sacrifices <note>original: *dbîḥah*, plural *dabâyiḥ*</note>
b) An offering to God <note>original: *qurbân*</note>, or a banquet for God <note>original: *walîmeh lil-lâh*</note>
c) Meatless food

All three of these categories were well known in the ancient Near East. In describing each class, we shall refer to the corresponding Hebrew practices found in the Bible.

### a) Animal sacrifices

By the term sacrifice <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note>, the ritual slaughter of an animal is always understood. The word itself is a feminine form <note>original: *dbîḥah* is the feminine of *dabîḥ*</note> and means "that which is slain as an offering." As was noted by the scholar Jaussen, the term originally referred specifically to a female animal. Today, however, the word has lost that specific distinction and is used for any sacrificial animal regardless of gender. From the same linguistic root <note>original: *dabaḥa*</note>, we get the word for altar <note>original: *madbaḥ*</note>, which originally signified the place where the sacrificial animal was killed <note>original: the term also refers to the neck where the animal is cut</note>.

It is interesting that the word for "banquet table" <note>original: *smâṭ*</note> is used in some parts of Palestine exclusively to mean an animal sacrifice <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note>. This is common in the districts of Beni Zeid <note>original: *Benî Zêd*</note> and Beni Malik <note>original: *Benî Mâlik*</note>. Meanwhile, in other parts—such as Jerusalem and the surrounding villages—this same word is used to refer to a cooked meal <note>original: *tabḥah*</note>, and not...

---

² Jaussen, page 338.
³ This word also means "the neck."
⁴ According to the dictionary <term>The Encompassing Ocean</term> <note>original: *Muḥîṭ el-Muḥîṭ*</note> (I, 994), a *smâṭ* is a table or a large round tray on which food is presented. I have no doubt that the current meaning of "food offering" originated from the idea of offering a table of food <note>original: *walîmeh*</note> for the saint <note>original: *welî*</note>.
⁵ A district with Deir Ghassaneh <note>original: *Dêr Ghassâneh*</note> as the main village and former administrative center.

<meta>This page continues the detailed classification of food-based vows, focusing specifically on the ritual slaughter of animals and the various cultural and religious contexts in which these sacrifices occur.</meta>

...for an animal sacrifice. Whenever the word for a "banquet" <note>original: *smât*</note>¹ is used in this section regarding animal sacrifices, it refers to a ritual sacrifice <note>original: *dbîhah*</note>. The specific ritual formulas used in these ceremonies originate from the villages of Deir Ghassaneh or Abu Ghosh.

I do not intend to provide a complete list of all the different types of sacrifices <note>original: *dabâiyḥ*</note> known in Palestine in this study. The scholar Jaussen, in his book *Customs of the Arabs* <note>original: *Coutumes des Arabes*</note>, has already provided a list of twenty-nine different types practiced in the region of Moab.²

Sacrifices <note>original: *dabâiyḥ*</note> may be divided into three distinct groups, and it is a mistake to confuse one with another:
1. Sacrifices connected directly with a religious idea.
2. Those connected with the spirits <note>original: *djinn*</note>.
3. Those connected with family circumstances, such as invitations, family feasts, and social obligations.

Only the animal offerings belonging to the first group will be described in detail here. Some of those belonging to the second category will be mentioned only for the sake of completeness. Occasions belonging to the third category include: the installation of a village headman <note>original: *muḥtâr*</note>,³ the arrival of an honored guest,⁴ or family events such as circumcision, betrothal, marriage, and the dedication of a new house. Important legal or social agreements are also often not considered complete until they are sealed with a sacrifice.⁵ However, the most frequent occasion for making a sacrifice is the fulfillment of a religious vow.⁶ These particular sacrifices belong to the first, or religious, group.

The custom of vowing an animal to a deity or saint is not a new practice; all religions of the ancient Near East practiced it. The types of animals that may be used for this purpose include a young camel <note>original: *djamal djazûr*</note>, a young she-camel <note>original: *nâqah djazûr*</note>, a yearling ox <note>original: *tôr ḥôlî*⁷</note>, a cow, a sheep <note>original: *harûf*⁸</note>, a young goat <note>original: *saḥl*⁹</note>, or a mature goat <note>original: *djidi*</note>.

In different parts of Palestine, different animals are preferred. For instance, the Bedouin nomads often vow a camel, while the local peasants <note>original: *fellaḥîn*</note> prefer a...

³ Compare with 1 Samuel 11:15.

⁹ A female goat is called an *'anzeh* and the male a *tés*; both may be offered. Sometimes the vow specifies which gender must be sacrificed.

sheep.<sup>1</sup> No unclean animal (such as a pig) will ever be vowed. A vowed animal must possess special characteristics which will be described later. It often happens that only a portion of an animal is offered. This is only the case when the sacrifice vow involves a camel, an ox, or a cow. One-half, one-third, or one-fourth of a cow may be vowed. In such a case, a corresponding fraction of the price of the cow is given to the sanctuary. This money is paid as soon as the cow is sold. Abû Ṭâlib, a man from the village of Bêt Ḥanînâ, told me that not only should one-half of the price of the animal be given, but as long as it is not sold, half of its labor, half of its milk, and half of all the calves the cow produces after the vow is made (and before it is sold) belong to the saint <term>welî: a Muslim saint or holy person who acts as a protector</term>. From the moment the vow is spoken, Abû Ṭâlib explained, half the cow—and thus one-half of its work and products—belongs to the owner and the other half belongs to the man of God. At present, usually only half the price of the animal is given, and all other rights of the saint are withdrawn. Abû Ṭâlib continued: "Everyone is consuming the rights of the saints" <note>original: *ed-djamî' byâklû haqq el-awliâ*</note>; in other words, not everything that belongs to a saint, and that should be given to him, is actually given. Very conscientious persons, therefore, expressly vow only half the price of an animal: "If I receive news of his recovery <note>The 'h' at the end of the Arabic word for recovery (*šifâh*) is meant to be pronounced.</note>, O Prophet of God, O Mohammed, I shall buy, for half the price of the ox, clothes for the poor" <note>original: *in adjânî habar šifâh yâ nabîy allâh yâ Muḥammad la-ašt rî ibnuṣṣ taman et-tôr kisweh lal-fuqarâ*</note>.<sup>4</sup>

A Christian from the village of Ṭaiybeh informed me that it is customary in the vicinity of his village, when the calves of a cow die one after another, to vow a portion of the next one born to Saint George <term>el-Ḥaḍr: "The Green One," a figure widely venerated by both Christians and Muslims, often identified with St. George</term>. "Accept my vow, O Green One; a quarter of what she (the cow) brings is yours."

By far the most common animal vowed is a sheep.<sup>5</sup> Whenever the animal is not specified—that is, when only the words "sacrifice" <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note> or

<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal sacrifices, transitioning from the division of animal ownership to the specific requirements for the quality and health of the sacrifice, as well as the common linguistic formulas used when making a vow.</meta>

<page-num>157</page-num>

When the term <term>smâṭ: an animal offering</term> (used in the Benî Zêd and Benî Mâlik regions) is used, it means a sheep¹ is intended to be offered.

An absolutely healthy and faultless animal must be offered. No lame, blind, or sick animal should be promised.² An animal which has accidentally broken a limb or has been wounded by a gunshot is not suitable for the fulfillment of a vow.³ The Old Testament provides the same regulations.⁴ The word *dbîḥah* <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note> carries the meaning of "slaughtering." Therefore, an animal which has lost some of its blood in any way other than by being butchered does not fulfill the true purpose of the vow.

Animal sacrifices are often taken from one's own herd. Stolen animals are not accepted by any <term>welî: a Muslim saint or holy person</term>. I cannot verify for Palestine the observation made by Doutté in North Africa,⁵ which is that a sacrifice <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note> must be a male animal.

The expressions used when making a vow for a sacrifice are very numerous. I shall only mention the most important ones: "Your animal offering, O Hauwâṣ, if my son recovers" <note>original: *Smâṭak yâ Hauwâṣ in râq ibnî*</note>;⁶ "I take a vow upon myself, O Prophet Moses, if my husband returns safely, I shall offer you an animal sacrifice" <note>original: *nidrun ‘alaiy yâ nabî Mûsâ in ridji‘ djôzî bis-salâmeh la-aqaddimlak dbîḥah*</note>;⁷ "If I am blessed with a boy, O Friend of God <note>Abraham</note>, I shall sacrifice a sheep to you every year" <note>original: *in adjânî ṣabî yâ Halîl Allâh la-adbaḥlak kull saneh ḥarûf*</note>.⁸

All the above expressions contain the assumption that the animal being promised will be killed. But it is not at all necessary—

This was first because their meat is considered better than that of ewes, and also because it was more important to spare the ewes for breeding. > with the fat of lambs and rams, the herds of Bashan," Deuteronomy 32:14.

...to slaughter the animal; some promise to send a living animal to the sanctuary: "O St. George, I owe you a healthy sheep if my child recovers," <note>original: *yâ mâr Djirius ilak ‘alaiy in ṭâb ibnî ḥarûf wâqif*</note> (meaning a living sheep). After such a vow, the sheep is sent to the convent of St. George in al-Khader, and the <term>rayis: director or head</term> of the convent has the right to do with it as he pleases.

Animal sacrifices are made mostly on important occasions: during the illness or absence of a family member; in times of great impending danger; when a man has no male children; or when a disease attacks a flock of sheep. The expression used in the last case is: "If you take the disease <margin>The word *el-wabâ* also means "plague" or "epidemic."</margin> from my flock, <margin>The word *ghanam* refers to a flock regardless of whether it consists of sheep or goats.</margin> the largest of them is your offering, O Rifa'i," <note>original: *in rafa‘t el-wabâ min ghanamî ikbîrhâ smâṭak yâ Rfâ‘î*</note>. In the case of <term>Sheikh Anbar: a saint whose shrine is near Isawiya</term>, <margin>Sheikh Anbar's shrine is located on the southeastern ridge of the eastward continuation of the Mount of Olives, not far from Isawiya. It is a <term>maqām: shrine</term> containing Sheikh Anbar's tomb in the main sanctuary and his wife's tomb in a small northern room. It is said that Anbar was the slave of an Egyptian master. The miraculous story of Anbar’s journey will be mentioned later.</margin> a flock attacked by an epidemic is brought in its entirety to visit the shrine, <margin>The original term is *bizauwrûhâ*, meaning "they make them visit."</margin> where the door is kept open. While the sheep pass the <term>weli: saint's shrine</term>, the first one that tries to enter the shrine is dedicated to the Sheikh. It is said, "He has chosen it" <note>original: *iẖtârha*</note>. In this case, as well as when a specific sheep is chosen, the top of the ear is cut while saying, "I have cut its ear" <note>original: *šaraḥt dânuh*</note>. <margin>The word *qaṭaš* is also used for "cutting."</margin> Such a sheep remains with the others and is well cared for until it is sacrificed; "it remains in his name" <note>original: *biybqâ ‘alâ ismuh*</note> (referring to the saint to whom it belongs). 

Sometimes a man promises the firstborn sheep <margin>According to the law of Moses, it was forbidden to vow the firstborn of any beast, as it was already devoted to God (Leviticus 27:26).</margin> of his flock to a saint, hoping that this man of God will bless the flock and keep it safe. Others vow one of the first twins. In both these cases, as well as when a lamb is brought to fulfill a vow, the young animals are well-fed and cared for until they grow up and the time for their sacrifice arrives. They are called <term>rbībeh: a well-fed animal</term>, <margin>A term also used for any well-fed sheep.</margin> an expression used for any animal that has been specially raised and fattened.

<summary>This page describes the rituals and linguistic formulas for vowing animal sacrifices to Palestinian saints, particularly for the healing of children or the protection of flocks from disease.</summary>
<keywords>animal sacrifice, St. George, al-Khader, Sheikh Anbar, maqam, vows, Rifa'i, Palestinian folklore, religious traditions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal sacrifice traditions in Palestinian folk religion, specifically addressing what happens when a vowed animal dies before sacrifice and how vows are fulfilled by proxy or at large shrine festivals.</meta>

# CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

<page-num>159</page-num>

...brought up under specially favorable conditions. If an animal dies from a disease, some people replace it with another. Others believe that since God Almighty permitted such a loss, the <term>welî: saint</term> has no right to another one, and they feel released from their obligation. If an animal that has been vowed falls ill, it may be sold and the money used to buy another (but younger) animal. Others slaughter it and distribute the meat among the poor.

It is forbidden to change an animal once it has been dedicated to a <term>welî: saint</term>. The peasants believe that the holy man will surely not accept such a sacrifice and will punish the person who does it.

It is not at all necessary to raise every animal promised for a <term>dbîḥah: sacrifice</term>; it may be bought at the sanctuary or in the market. In some shrines, there is a large market held at the time of the <term>môsam: religious festival</term>, giving everyone the opportunity to buy as many animals as they wish.<sup>1</sup> It may even happen that the person who has bound themselves by a vow cannot go to the sanctuary, making them unable to fulfill their promise in person. In that case, they entrust the fulfillment of the obligation to a friend by giving them the animal or the money to buy one. Sometimes, but not often, a sum of money may be sent to the <term>qaiym: custodian</term> of the holy place to buy a sheep, which he then slaughters in the name of the donor.

We will see later why it is very important, even obligatory, that everyone should be present in person or be represented by a delegated friend or relative while their <term>dbîḥah: sacrifice</term> is being performed. Offering an animal for someone else without their permission or authority breaks the desired spiritual connection between the person and their holy intercessor.<sup>2</sup>

Generally, the one who made the vow and the person for whom it was taken, along with some relatives and friends, go to the sanctuary to fulfill it. If the <term>nider: vow</term> was made for a woman who happens to be menstruating <note>original: "impure by menstruation"</note> at the time of the fulfillment, she does not accompany the procession and cannot attend the sacrifice. As a rule, young children and babies may not join such a feast, especially when the <term>welî: saint's shrine</term> is far away.

A <term>dbîḥah: sacrifice</term> is usually slaughtered at the <term>maqâm: shrine</term> of the saint to whom it was promised, but this is not a binding rule. A <term>dbîḥah: sacrifice</term> for...

<sup>1</sup> Nebi Musa (Prophet Moses), Rubin, Saleh, etc.
<sup>2</sup> See also Doutté, page 466.

# Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society

<page-num>160</page-num>

A sacrifice intended for <term>Ibrâhîm el-Ḥalîl: Abraham the Friend of God</term> (in Hebron) may be offered in Jerusalem, and one for <term>en-nabî Dâhûd: the Prophet David</term> (Jerusalem) may be offered in Nablus. In such a case, the animal <note>original: "btindbiḥ 'alâ ismuh"</note> "is slaughtered in his name" (that is, in the name of the saint). This may happen when no one is able to travel to the specific shrine in question. A Bedouin of the <term>el-'Idwân: Adwan</term> tribe told me that most of the sacrifices made for Moses are slaughtered in their camp (in Transjordan) and not at the shrine itself. However, it is considered more proper to offer the animal at the sanctuary, because a visit to such a place is in itself a <term>barakeh: blessing</term>.

When a sheep is taken to a shrine, it is sometimes decorated with flowers and colored ribbons. A small round mirror <note>original: "mrây"</note> is sometimes hung from its forehead, and the horns are dyed with <term>ḥinnâ: henna</term>. During the <term>môsam: religious festival</term> of <term>Nebî Mûsâ: the Prophet Moses</term>, one finds many sheep whose foreheads, backs, and tails are dyed with red lead <note>original: "sîraqûn"</note> and gold tinsel <note>original: "madhab"</note>.¹

Anyone who knows how to slaughter a sheep may do it. Generally, it is done by the people who bring the offering. In exceptional cases, it is performed by the <term>ḥaddâm: servant/attendant</term> of the shrine. In important places of pilgrimage with well-known <term>mawâsim: festivals</term>, there are butchers who usually perform the <term>dbîḥah: ritual slaughter</term>, receiving a quarter or half of a <term>madjîdî: Medjidie coin</term> for their work. However, no one is obligated to hand over their sheep to such a person. Women never perform this act. Doutté's² observation that a <term>muqaddim: offerer</term> slays the sacrifice in most shrines has a parallel in Palestine only once, at the North African <term>zâwieh: Sufi lodge</term> of Abû Madian.³

In a large <term>maqâm: sanctuary</term>, like Nebî Mûsâ,⁴ there is a special area designated for slaughtering.⁵ In smaller ones with a kitchen, the animal is slaughtered in or in front of this room (such as at <term>en-Nabî Ṣâleḥ: the Prophet Saleh</term>, in the village of en-Nabî Ṣâleḥ). There are sanctuaries which have no proper kitchen but have an open enclosure or gallery <note>original: "rwâq"</note> adjacent to the wall of the shrine which serves as a kitchen; there, the <term>dabâyḥ: sacrifices</term> are slaughtered and the food is cooked (such as at the shrines of er-Rfâ'î and Rdjâl Ṣûfeh). Not infrequently, especially in Transjordan, the animals are slaughtered on the roof of the shrine so that some of the blood runs over the front wall. In all other shrines, the...

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of animal sacrifices at Palestinian sanctuaries, moving from the decoration and selection of the animals to the specific locations and rituals of the slaughter itself.</meta>

<page-num>161</page-num>

### CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

An animal may be killed in any place in the direct neighborhood of the <term>maqâm: shrine or sanctuary</term>: under a tree, on a large flat rock, or in front of the shrine itself. If the specific location for the slaughter is designated in the <term>nider: vow</term>, then that condition must be fulfilled. For example, a person might say: <note>original Arabic: "smâṭak yâ Hauwâṣ adbaḥu 'alâ 'atbatak"</note> "Your sheep-offering, O <term>Hauwâṣ</term> <note>a local saint</note>, I shall slay on the threshold of your door." The animal is more often killed on the threshold of the courtyard door than on the door of the shrine itself. Another vow might be: <note>original Arabic: "Dbîḥtak yâ Ḥâtim adbaḥḥâ 'alâ tabûtak"</note> "Your <term>dbîḥah: ritual sacrifice</term>, O <term>Ḥâtim</term>, I shall slay on your coffin." In this last case, the animal is slain inside the shrine, beside the tomb and not directly on it. Great care is taken so that neither the floor nor the tomb is polluted with blood. A basin <note>original: "lakan"</note> is placed so that all the blood flows into it.¹ Unlike Kahle,² I have not seen nor heard of any case where animals are killed in such a way that their blood flows into the water of a holy spring or into a holy cave, as Curtiss³ mentions.

No pregnant animal—if this condition happens to be known—is ever sacrificed. However, such an animal may be vowed to a saint; in that case, both the mother and its offspring belong to the saint from then on. The owner waits until after the birth to fulfill the sacrifice. If such an animal is offered without knowing it was pregnant, the fetus is thrown away.⁴

The animal is thrown on the ground with its head turned eastward and its face southward. It is interesting to note that both deceased persons and sacrificed animals are laid so that the face looks toward <term>Mecca</term>⁵ (which is to the south in Palestine). The difference between them is that the human lies on his right side with the head to the west, while the animal is laid on its left side with the head pointing eastward. The person who kills the animal says: <note>original Arabic: "bism allâh—allâh akbar"</note> "In the name of God—God is greatest;"⁶ or <note>original Arabic: "bism illî qaddar 'alêki ed-dabḥ—allâh akbar"</note> "In the name of the One who decreed your sacrifice—God is greatest." In <term>Bîr Sabi': Beersheba</term> I heard the following expression: <note>original Arabic: "bism allâh—allâh akbar ḥall 'alêki ed-dabîḥ rabbî innahâ mink wa ilêk fidâ"</note> "In the name of God—God is greatest—slaughter is permitted upon you; my Lord, truly it is from You and for You as a ransom," you (the...

² *PJB* <note>Palästinajahrbuch</note> VIII, 155.

⁵ Christians lay their dead on their backs from west to east, with the head on the west side looking toward the east. <note>The original OCR says "head on the east side," but Christian burial traditionally places the head at the west, facing east.</note>

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

sheep) are lawfully slaughtered. "My Lord, it is from You and is a ransom for You." The name of the <term>welî: saint</term> to whom the animal was vowed may be added to the name of God. For example, I heard the people of Jericho say, "In the name of God and that of <term>Sheikh Salah</term>." The ordinary formula of the <term>Fatihah</term> <note>the opening chapter of the Quran</note>, which is used in almost all other cases—<note>original Arabic: bism allâh er-raḥmân er-raḥîm</note> "In the name of God, the most gracious, the most merciful" <gloss>1</gloss>—is never used during slaughtering. This is because the adjective "most merciful" is considered contradictory to the act of killing. Some even believe that an animal slaughtered while using these words should not be eaten. 

Although the following practice does not fall directly under the category of vows, it is valuable for illustrating these concepts. A person suffering from shock or fright (<term>mardjuf</term> or <term>mahdud</term> <gloss>2</gloss>) must undergo special treatment to counteract the evil results of "fright." One of the many ways to achieve this is to eat the neck (<term>raqabeh</term>) of a sheep, which is cooked with <term>shaddit</term> (or <term>hawayidj</term>) <term>et-tarbeh</term> <gloss>3</gloss>. When slaughtering such an animal, the following formula is pronounced: <note>original Arabic: bism allâh u bism rauwâḥ er-radjfeh</note> "In the name of God and in the name of the One who removes the fright." <gloss>4</gloss> When a person is attacked with night-blindness, which is known by the name of <term>hidbad</term>, <gloss>5</gloss> he will

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Referring to George Sale’s 1734 translation of the Quran.
<gloss>2</gloss> From <term>haddah</term>, which is another name for <term>el-hofeh</term> (also <term>radjfeh</term>), all terms for a state of shock or terror. See Canaan, *The Cup of Fear* (Tasit er-radjfeh), JPOS III, p. 130. In his work *Aberglaube* (Superstitions), p. 35, other less common names are mentioned.
<gloss>3</gloss> A yellow powder made from several strong spices.
<gloss>4</gloss> A very famous <term>prophylactic: preventative</term> measure against <term>el-haddah</term> (fright) is described in the following story. My friend and neighbor Ibrahim Djirius was very irritated and frightened by some bad news he had received. A relative of his, fearing that this fright might result in illness, cooked a black hen with various spices. She kept the vessel tightly covered so that the steam could not escape. When she thought the hen was fully cooked, she threw herself on the ground in a room next to his and began to cry and wail in a most heartbreaking way. Alarmed, he got up, went to her, and asked the reason for her distress. She wept louder: "My son, my dear son, why did you die? My joy, my fortune has ended—my son, my dear son!" My friend became even more anxious about her trouble. When she felt his new alarm was sufficient to cancel out the first shock, she told him the truth and forced him to put his face over the opened vessel so that the steam clouded his face. He then had to eat the entire hen by himself (Canaan, *Superstitions*, pp. 68, 69). A white hen is used if the fright occurred during the day, and a black one if it occurred during the night.
<gloss>5</gloss> In the lexicons *Muhit* (p. 2163) and Hava (p. 811), this word means simply "weakness of sight."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of folk remedies for ailments like night-blindness and details the specific religious formulas and rituals used during animal sacrifice in Palestine.</meta>

<page-num>163</page-num>

<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

...can only be cured if he eats the <term>zawâyid</term> <note>original: zawâyid; meaning "extra parts," referring here to specific entrails</note>.^1 The lungs^2 have to be prepared without salt. In killing such an animal, the formula <note>original Arabic: bism allâh u ism il-hidbâd</note> "in the name of God and the name of <term>el-hidbad</term> <note>the spirit or condition of night-blindness</note>," must be pronounced, otherwise the cure will have no effect.^3

Not only in slaying a <term>nidr dbiḥah</term> <note>vowed sacrifice</note> is the name of God invoked, but whenever the Mohammedans kill an animal for food they always say <term>bism allâh</term> <note>"In the name of God"</note>. If this is neglected, they think it unlawful to eat of such an animal. This is done because it is ordered in the Holy Book, Sûrah V, verse 4:^4 "You are forbidden to eat that which dies of itself, and blood, and swine's flesh, and that on which the name of any besides God has been invoked."^5 This was ordered because the idolatrous Arabs were accustomed, when killing any animal for food, to consecrate it to their idols by saying: "In the name of <term>Allat</term> or <term>al-'Uzza</term> <note>pre-Islamic Arabian goddesses</note>."

Doutté's^6 observation in the <term>Maghrib</term> <note>Northwest Africa</note> that a special knife is used in killing a sacrifice is unknown in Palestine. The <term>Tarâbîn</term>^7 Bedouin will not kill an animal with a knife whose sheath has three nails. With the words <note>original Arabic: bism allâh allâh akbar</note> "In the name of God, God is most great," the one who kills the animal says, <note>original Arabic: itqabbal yâ... nidr...</note> "Accept, O [name of saint], the vow of [name of person]." Those who have fulfilled the vow feel freed from their obligation. This is well expressed in what is said while the animal is being slain: <note>original Arabic: wafênâ smâṭak yâ šêh Yûsif</note> "We have paid your sheep, O Sheikh Yusif;" <note>original Arabic: haiy nidrak yâ nabî Rûbîn</note> "Here is your vow, O Prophet Rubin."

In some places, we find the following custom which shows the relation between the person (in this case a child) for whom the sacrifice was slain and the offering itself. If the child is very young, he is carried three times (rarely four times) around the animal. This circling is called <term>ṭwâf</term>: <note>original Arabic: biṭauw fû l-walad ḥôl el-ḥarûf</note> "the child is carried around the sheep." When the child is somewhat older, he...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal sacrifice in Palestinian folk religion, moving from the invocation of God's name to the specific rituals performed with the animal, such as children riding or stepping over the sacrifice, and the distinctions between various types of offerings like the vow (nidr) and the festival sacrifice (ed-dhiyeh).</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

...rides on the sheep. This latter custom is not nearly as widespread as the other one. Both of these two customs are practiced when the child cannot accompany the procession to offer the <term>nidr</term> <note>vowed sacrifice</note> at the shrine, and they are always performed before the animal is killed. When the child is able to go to the <term>maqâm</term> <note>shrine or sacred tomb of a saint</note> and attend the ceremony, he steps over the <term>harûf</term> <note>lamb or sheep</note> after it is killed, or over the flowing blood.^1 According to others, he first walks three times around the sheep and then steps over the blood. While he encircles the animal, the <term>fâtiḥah</term> <note>the opening chapter of the Quran, used as a prayer</note> is read three times. Even Christians in some villages practice this custom of stepping over the blood when a sheep is offered to <term>Ḥaḍr el-Abḍar</term> <note>the "Green One," the local name for Saint George</note>.^2 This crossing over the blood is called <term>fašaga</term>. In the village of Ṭaiybeh, the sheep is led three times around the sanctuary of Saint George, and during every round, the Lord’s Prayer is recited once.

This custom of stepping over a vowed sheep or over its blood is also practiced with the <term>ḍbîḥit el-fadwâ</term> <note>a "redemption sacrifice" made to ransom a person from bad luck or illness</note> and, more rarely, with the <term>eḍ-ḍḥîyeh</term> <note>the sacrifice performed during the Feast of Sacrifice, or Eid al-Adha</note>. A few words about these last two sacrifices are necessary to illustrate the difference between them and a vow sacrifice. The best animal for the <term>eḍ-ḍḥîyeh</term> is a sheep, although a poor person may offer a goat. Bedouins prefer a camel or an ox, just as they do for a vow. A man from the village of ar-Râm assured me that the <term>eḍ-ḍḥîyeh</term> must be a cow (<term>râs baqar</term> <note>literally "a head of cattle"</note>) three years of age (<term>tlêteh</term>)^3 or more. A poor man may offer seven goats instead of one cow.^4 This belief seems to be local, as I could not verify it in other villages. The sheep is laid on its left side with its head turned to the east and its face to the south.^5 The man who slays it turns his face toward Mecca.^6 The person for whom it is offered says: "O God, accept it from me" <note>original Arabic: allâhumma itqabbalha minni</note>. Such an animal is slain on the <term>Qurbân Beyram</term> feast <note>the Great Feast or Eid al-Adha</note>.^7 On this day, wealthy people may slay one sheep for each member of their family; the sheep must be well-developed and faultless.

<meta>This page continues the description of sacrificial rituals, specifically focusing on the requirements for the festival sacrifice (ed-ḍḥîyeh) and the folk belief that these animals serve as spiritual mounts to carry the faithful across the Bridge of Sirat on Judgment Day.</meta>

<page-num>165</page-num>

<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine 165</header>

...has passed the age of one year. <note>1</note> Every member of the family then steps over the blood of his sacrifice, which counts in his favor on the Day of Judgment. <note>2</note> The poor offer one animal for the whole family. Some women <note>3</note> attribute miraculous curative powers to the blood of such a sacrifice. If a woman washes herself with the blood of a <term>dbîḥah</term> <note>an animal slaughtered for sacrifice</note> mixed with water, she will be able to have children. <note>4</note> One third of the sacrifice is eaten by the person who offered it and his family, one third is given to relatives, and the last third is distributed among the poor. <note>5</note> Some—but not many—will not accept this division. For instance, the <term>šêḫ</term> <note>sheikh; a local leader or elder</note> of 'Imwâs <note>the village of Emmaus</note> assured me that the above statement is not correct and that nobody should eat of his own sacrifice. As many inhabitants of one village offer a sheep each, people will share the meat of the sacrifice offered by someone else and distribute all of their own sheep.

The Palestinians believe that a sacrificial sheep will appear on the Day of Judgment well-dressed, well-decorated, and with <term>imkaḥḥaleh</term> <note>meaning "painted with kohl"; eyes darkened with antimony</note> eyes, and it will carry the person for whom it was offered over the <term>surâṭ</term> <note>the Bridge of Sirat; in Islamic tradition, a bridge thinner than a hair and sharper than a sword spanning over Hell to Paradise</note> to Paradise. Therefore, the common saying is *ḍaḥayânâ maṭayânâ*, <note>6</note> "our sacrificial animals are our riding beasts."

In slaughtering the victim of the <term>ed-ḍḥîyeh</term> <note>the sacrifice performed during the "Great Festival" of Eid al-Adha</note>, the utmost care is taken not to break any bones, so that the animal may appear whole and faultless on Judgment Day. It is thought that these sheep render a great service to their offerer on the last day by joining his good deeds in the balance, thus outweighing his faults and sins. <note>7</note>

The <term>Dbîḥet el-fadwâ</term> <note>a "ransom sacrifice" intended to protect a person or community</note> is a "sheep of ransom" offered on special occasions. When plague attacks part of the country, its inhabitants, as well as those of the surrounding area, may make use of such—

---

1. The animal must be absolutely faultless; that is, not blind, partially blind, lame, or weak. Even its bleating must be loud and clear. Some people, though not many, prohibit even the sacrifice of an animal which has been branded (<term>inkawat</term> or <term>makwîyeh</term>) as a curative measure. This method of treatment is very widespread in the Orient.
2. Heard in Liftâ.
3. Imm Moḥammad of 'Imwâs.
4. If the blood is taken from a sheep sacrificed on 'Arâfât <note>the mountain near Mecca where Hajj pilgrims stand in prayer</note>, it will be more effective.
5. A <term>ḥadîṯ</term> <note>a recorded tradition or saying of the Prophet Muhammad</note> allows eating from one's own sacrifice for the festival.
6. Curtiss, chapter 19.
7. Heard in Bêt Ḥanînâ and verified elsewhere.

offerings. The people of the infected area expect that God will accept these offerings and free them from the plague. Others hope that the sacrifice <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note> will prevent the spread of the disease to their region. A ransom sacrifice <note>original: *fadwâ*</note> may also be performed for a single person. <note>1</note> M., the only male child of a family well-known to me, returned from a long journey. His parents slaughtered a sheep in front of the house, and the son had to step over the flowing blood before he entered the home. <note>2</note>

Even when a flock of sheep is attacked by a deadly plague, one of the sheep—generally the best—is offered as a ransom for the whole flock. All the sheep of the infected flock are then marked with the blood of the slaughtered animal. <note>3</note> Sometimes the following expression is used while performing a ransom sacrifice: "A ransom for the family and the property." <note>original Arabic: *fidâ* (or *fadwâ*) *‘an el-ʿiâl wil-mâl*</note>

The meat of this sacrifice <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note> is generally all distributed to the poor. What the scholar Jaussen states about the Negev <note>original: *Negeb*</note> is also true in some parts of Palestine: specifically, that "festival sacrifice" <note>original: *ḍḥîyeh*</note> and "ransom sacrifice" <note>original: *fadwâ*</note> are sometimes used to mean the exact same thing.

In continuing our examination of the vowed sacrifices <note>original: *nidr dabâyḥ*</note>, we notice that the blood of the sacrifice flows onto the ground. In some shrines where a special place inside the main enclosure surrounding the sanctuary <note>original: *maqâm*</note> is designated for slaughtering, the blood is led through a special channel to the outside. The blood may also be used to decorate the shrine. Large sanctuaries <note>original: *maqâms*</note>, such as the Shrine of the Prophet Moses <note>original: *en-nabî Mûsâ*</note>, are exceptions. The door lintel <note>original: *šâšîyeh*</note> and the doorposts <note>original: *ṣdâghât*</note> are first of all smeared with blood. Generally, an impression of a hand is made. The Christians of Taybeh <note>original: *Ṭaiybeh*</note> smear the entrance <note>original: *‘atabah*</note> <note>4</note> of the sanctuary of Saint George <note>original: *Ḥaḍr*</note> with blood, making the sign of the cross. They take some blood in a small earthen vessel <note>original: *kêkarah*</note> and sprinkle the lintel and the doorposts. <note>5</note> This same custom prevailed in early Biblical times when Aaron's sons sprinkled the blood of the offering "upon the altar round about." <note>6</note> Instead of blood, the—

Christians in the village of Tayyiba <note>original: Ṭaiybeh</note> very often whitewash <note>original: *biyutrušû*</note> stones with a lime preparation shortly after an animal is slaughtered. This is often done using a stick with a tassel of cloth ribbons fastened to the end. The white color is believed to bring good luck. Most door frames of newly built houses are smeared with the blood of sheep killed while the arched roofs <note>original: vaulting</note> are being constructed; this sheep is sacrificed in the name of "Our Lord Abraham, the Friend of God" <note>original: *saiyidnâ Ibrâhîm el-Ḫalîl*</note>. The same practice is followed for the "sacrifice of ransom" <note>original: *dbîḥit el-fidâ*</note>.

The forehead of the child—or less often, the adult—for whose sake the vow <note>original: *nidr*</note> was made, is also smeared with the blood of the sacrifice <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note>. This custom is observed at the shrines of the Prophet Moses <note>original: Nabî Mûsâ</note> and Abraham the Friend <note>original: Ibrâhîm el-Ḫalîl</note>. The Christians of Tayyiba and Bir Zeit <note>original: Bîr Zêt</note> have the same custom, where the sign of the cross is made on the child's forehead. In Beersheba <note>original: Bîr Sabi‘</note>, camels <note>original: *ibl*</note> are smeared with the blood of a sacrifice as a sign that a vow has been fulfilled.

The shrine custodian <note>original: *qaîym*</note> receives a specific portion of the animal. This generally consists of the skin and the offal <note>original: *saqaṭ*, also called *eṭ-ṭrâf* (extremities), or in some places *zawâyd* (extras)</note>. By these terms, the peasants mean the extremities, the head, the abdominal organs—with the exception of the fatty membrane <note>original: *omentum*; some also include the intestines attached to it</note>—and the "slaughter-piece" <note>original: *madbaḥ*</note>. The *madbaḥ* <note>derived from the verb *dabaḥa*, meaning "to slaughter an animal with a knife"</note> is the specific part of the neck where the knife cuts the throat. This piece is cut off and given to the custodian. Generally, he also receives a good piece of meat in addition and joins the party for the meal.

What has been said of the custodian is also true of the sheikhs <note>original: *šiyûḫ*</note> connected with shrines, such as those at the Shrines of the Prophets <note>original: *al-Anbiâ*</note> or the "Green One" <note>original: *el-Ḥaḍrâ*, referring to Saint George or Elijah</note>. The scholar Curtiss's statement that one-fourth of the animal is given to the custodian is only occasionally true in Palestine. This custom is an ancient one, illustrated in the biblical story of the children of Eli <note>1 Samuel 2:12-16</note>. In places like the Shrine of the Prophet Moses <note>original: Nabî Mûsâ</note>, the butcher <note>original: *laḥḥâm*</note> receives either the...

the skin or a quarter of a *medjîdî* <note>original: a Turkish silver coin</note>,¹ or less often, half of one. The other parts of the animal are cooked and eaten. Such a holy sacrifice <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note> should not be prepared as a *zarb* <note>original: meat roasted in a sealed earthen oven</note>.² Very often rice is also cooked and served with the meat. Bread and the other materials necessary for the preparation of the meal are brought by the group. Everyone present takes part in the meal, and often many bystanders join the party, for as soon as people are seen going with one or more sheep to a sanctuary, everyone knows that a vow <note>original: *nidr*</note> will be fulfilled. During large festivals <note>original: *mawâsim*</note>, a portion of the raw meat <note>original: *laḥm aḥdar* or *laḥm naiy*</note> is divided among those present. Often vowed animals are given whole to the kitchen of the Prophet Moses <note>original: Nabî Mûsâ</note> or some other saint, where the meat is cooked with the other food and distributed to the visitors. Such an act is thought to bring additional blessing.

The preparation of the food takes place in the kitchen, if there is one, otherwise near the shrine <note>original: *maqâm*</note> in a place protected from the winds. Often the food is cooked under a tree. The copper pots <note>original: *tanâdjir*, plural of *tandjarah*</note> or the copper troughs <note>original: *dsût*, plural of *dist*</note> containing the food are placed on the hearths <note>original: *mawâqid*, plural of *môqadeh*</note>. These are either well-built stone hearths or improvised ones. The latter are constructed by placing two elongated stones of the same height parallel to each other, with a space of about 12 to 24 inches <note>original: 30—60 cm.</note> between them, where the fire is made. Many shrines possess a number of copper pots, which are always used on such occasions. At shrines <note>original: *welîs*</note> where there are none, the people who come to offer their vow <note>original: *nidr*</note> bring them along. The wood is taken from the property of the saint <note>original: *welî*</note>, or is brought by the visitors. In cooking for a vow <note>original: *nidr*</note>, all the dry wood which has fallen from the holy trees may be used, but no twigs may be cut, even if they are dead. This fallen brushwood is never used on any other occasion.

Everyone joins in eating the sacred meal. Each person recites the opening chapter of the Quran <note>original: *fâtiḥah*</note> for the soul of the saint <note>original: *welî*</note> for whom the offering has been brought. It is considered that all present are guests of the saint, for the sacrifice <note>original: *dbîḥah*</note>...

¹ One Turkish *medjîdî* = 20 *ṣâgh* piasters <note>standard currency</note> (and 23 *šurk* piasters <note>market rate currency</note>), normally one-fifth of a Turkish pound.
² *Zarb* is a roasted sheep, where the whole animal (when the animal is young) or a part of a sheep is placed in a small, freshly-prepared, cave-like oven. This oven is heated very strongly; the meat is salted and placed in it, after which the oven is hermetically sealed.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the communal preparation of sacrificial food at Palestinian shrines, emphasizing that the meal is shared with the saint and the community.</meta>

<page-num>169</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

The food belongs to the saint, and it is cooked in his honor. Therefore, everyone who attends the sacrifice or happens to pass by on this occasion is welcome to share in the sacrificial meal. The saint <note>original: *welî*</note> receives the most important part of the animal—the soul, which resides in the blood,¹ as we shall see later. The same practice was known in the Old Testament.² A sacrificial meal followed the sacrifice. The flesh of the animal was eaten at the sanctuary by the person offering the sacrifice and his family (1 Samuel 1:3-7) or by representatives of the community (1 Samuel 9:22-25). The underlying idea was "that of sharing a common meal with the deity.³ The worshippers were the guests (Zephaniah 1:7) of God at His sanctuary."⁴ They rise from the feast happily and, joining in games, they pass the time in enjoyment. Satisfied with their actions, they leave the shrine, certain that the saint <note>original: *welî*</note> has blessed them for fulfilling their obligation.

These sacrifices followed by feasts are well known in the Bible (1 Samuel 20:4, etc.). We know from 1 Kings 3:3 and other passages⁵ that these sacrifices took place in the "high places" <note>Ancient places of worship, often on hilltops</note>. In the search for his father’s donkeys, Saul came to Samuel and was taken by the Prophet to a sacrificial meal.

Before we leave the subject of animal sacrifices <note>original: *dbâyḥ*</note>, we may mention some irregular or unusual customs practiced in some parts of Palestine. It very rarely happens that a sheep which has been slaughtered in or near a sanctuary is carried back to the village and cooked in the house of the person who made the vow. Sometimes the condition or the location of the shrine prevents people from offering the sheep in or near it. In such a case, the animal may be sold and the proceeds are either given directly to the shrine <note>original: *maqâm*</note>, or jewelry is bought with the money; this jewelry is then offered to the shrine and hung on the icon of the saint. Sacrifices <note>original: *dabâyiḥ*</note> vowed to Saint Mary on Calvary are not offered as live animals. Instead, the sheep is sold, jewelry is bought with the money, and it is offered to the icon <note>original: *qûneh*</note>.

Some vows in this group form a link to meatless food vows. *El-msarwaleh* is a dish made of milk and rice,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Palestinian sacrificial practices, moving from communal meals at shrines to specific rituals where animals are sacrificed as substitutes to protect humans or livestock from evil influences.</meta>

<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

...mixed with another of meat and rice. Such an offering—like all meatless ones—is cooked in the house and then carried to the shrine, where it is partly distributed and partly eaten.

Among the Bedouin and some peasants, the cooking done in connection with a *môlad* <term>original: *môlad*; a festival celebrating a birth or the birthday of a saint</term> vow (which will be discussed later) is more important than the *môlad* itself. This festival may be celebrated with or without meat. There are still several subsidiary practices connected with sacrifices to be considered. Since they touch upon the fundamental principles of making vows, we may be able, by studying them, to explain the concept connecting the sacrificial animal with the person for whom it is vowed. I shall first give two examples which, although they do not belong to the animal sacrifices described above, nevertheless vividly illustrate the underlying idea.

When a dead person is carried from the place where he died to his own village, the Bedouin and the peasants load the body onto a camel. The peasants hold the superstition that the camel may die from the *kabseh*¹ <term>original: *kabseh*; a harmful spiritual "compression" or influence</term> exerted by the dead. In order to safeguard against such a heavy loss, they kill a cock on the camel's back before the corpse is loaded. The blood of the cock, which flows over the camel's body, is thought to remove the impending danger. Thus, the cock, in giving his life, has saved that of the more precious camel.

Another illustration demonstrates the same idea from a different perspective. When a child suffers from convulsions, it is believed that a specially malignant *djinn* <term>original: *djinn*; a spirit or demon in Islamic folklore</term> has attacked him, and that the evil spirit will likely not depart without taking the child's life. In order to save the precious life of the child, the demon must be satisfied with some other life. Therefore, the head of a pigeon is inserted as deeply as possible into the rectum of the patient and held there until the animal dies. A life is given...

---

¹ It is not difficult to explain how the *kabseh* works in this case. A dead body is always surrounded by the evil spirits that caused its death and is therefore unclean. These spirits prefer to leave this environment and inhabit some living object. In doing so, they may cause the same harmful effects to their new host. The same explanation applies to the *kabseh* which may affect a sick person if another sick person is brought into the room. The evil spirits causing the illness of the second person (as most diseases are believed to be caused by demons) may leave their host and attack the first person, thereby increasing his disorder. For other examples, see *Superstition* <note>original: *Aberglaube*; a reference to Taufiq Canaan's 1914 book "Aberglaube und Volksmedizin im Lande der Bibel"</note>, pages 37 and 39.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of substitutionary sacrifice in Palestinian folk religion, specifically how the life of an animal is offered to a supernatural power to redeem or protect a human life.</meta>

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

...to save another, and the spirit <term>original: *djinn*; supernatural beings in Islamic mythology that can be benevolent or malevolent</term> is satisfied by the offering it has received, and leaves the child.

These two examples show three points clearly:
1. To save the life of an important being, that of another less important one must be sacrificed.
2. The sacrifice is made to please that supernatural power which is thought to be the cause of the danger, or which has the power to prevent it.
3. The animal sacrificed must come into direct external contact with the being for whom or for which it has given its life.

A close examination of the practices connected with vowing and sacrificing animals, as we have followed them, shows that the same ideas underlie them, with some modification.

**Regarding 1 <note>original: "Ad 1"; Latin for "To/Regarding 1"</note>.** The Palestinian thinks that everything, especially disease, affliction, and misfortune, comes "from God" <note>original: "min allâh"</note>. He has permitted their occurrence, exactly as He allowed Satan to tempt Job and afflict him with all sorts of visitations.1 Therefore, whenever the peasant is attacked with afflictions, he tries to escape them by directing the wrath of the superior power against some other being. An animal is offered in his place, to redeem the human being with its life. Thus, the peasant still follows the paths of his ancestors, believing that "life shall go for life"2 and that "blood makes atonement,"3 and that the life of an animal is accepted by the Divine Power instead of that of the offender, who himself deserves death.4 This is the only explanation for the widespread custom of sacrificial vows; and while other vows are not so strictly carried out, a promise of an animal offering is treated much more seriously. This idea of redemption is well-founded in all Semitic religions.

**Regarding 2 <note>original: "Ad 2"</note>.** The Almighty God is difficult to access. This is another fundamental idea in Palestinian folk religion.5 This is why the Palestinian prefers to call on saints <note>original: "welîs"; Muslim local saints or holy people</note> for help. They were once human beings and thus understand human difficulties and temptations. A saint is always ready to assist, even more so if one shows—

---

1 Job 1:6–12.
2 Deuteronomy 19:21.
3 Leviticus 17:11.
4 Compare the sin offerings of the Mosaic law <note>The religious laws attributed to Moses in the Hebrew Bible</note>.
5 Exodus 19, etc.

<summary>This page analyzes the logic of animal sacrifice in Palestinian folk religion, explaining it as a form of "life for life" redemption where animals are substituted for humans to appease divine or supernatural wrath.</summary>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of sacrificial logic in Palestinian folk religion, moving from the concept of substitution to the specific ways a person must interact with a sacrifice to secure a saint's favor or protection.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

...his humility in some way. Just as no one would approach a deity without a gift in his hands, or a promise of one, so a peasant would not ask a <term>welî: a Muslim saint or holy man</term> for help without at the same time vowing a sacrifice.¹ This conception already prevailed in Biblical times: "None shall appear before me empty."² The object of a sacrifice, reduced to its simplest terms, is threefold: to secure and retain the favor of the saint; to remove his displeasure; and thirdly to express gratitude for benefits received. Although these things may be said about any offering, nothing pleases these holy men as much as an animal sacrifice: firstly, because so many of the poor can be fed in their name and at their tables; secondly, because everyone who partakes of the feast says a prayer in the name of the *welî*. These two acts are credited by the Almighty to the account of the *welî*.

Regarding point 3 <note>original: "Ad 3"; Latin for "To/Regarding 3"</note>. The study of the connections between the animal to be offered and the person for whom it was offered is very interesting. For the purpose of analysis, the following summary <note>original: "resumé"</note> of the customs described above may be given:

*   A young child—for whom the <term>nidr: a religious vow or promise</term> was made—is carried three times around the animal. When the child is older, he rides on the sheep.³
*   He may step over the sacrifice or over the flowing blood.
*   The forehead of the person is sometimes smeared with the blood of the <term>dbîḥah: a ritual animal sacrifice</term>.
*   The flock or herd is touched with the blood of the animal killed for their safety.

All these actions show clearly that the sacrifice must have direct contact with the person for whom it is offered. Therefore, the person for whom an animal is slain to safeguard his life is marked with the blood of the victim. The shrine of the saint to whom the animal has been promised is smeared or sprinkled in many cases with the blood of the *dbîḥah*.⁴ This is done to assure the man of God that his *nidr* has been fulfilled; for the mere dedication of the animal during the act of slaying, with the words...

⁴ Exactly as in Leviticus 1:5, 17:11, etc.

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the ritualistic use of blood in Palestinian folk religion, moving from the physical act of sacrifice to the spiritual and superstitious significance of blood as the "abode of the soul."</meta>

<page-num>173</page-num>
<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

"Take your promise, O..." does not suffice. One asks why blood plays such an important role <note>original: "rôle"</note> in these religious rites? An investigation of this question discloses that blood plays a very interesting and complicated part in Palestinian superstition. Since I believe that the study of these practices will bring us to a clearer conception of the underlying ideas, I shall describe some of them.

Blood is believed by the Palestinian to be the home of the "soul." The same idea existed in the earliest periods of Biblical history: "For the blood is the life."¹ Therefore, wherever human blood is shed or lost, a part of human life and the soul is lost. This makes blood a highly respected, but at the same time a highly dangerous thing. The following superstitious customs will throw light on this idea:

1.  When two children desire to enter into a fraternal relation with each other (<term>yithâwû: becoming brothers</term>), that is, to become intimate friends, each pricks one of his fingers with a needle and sucks a little of the blood of the other. Thus, each takes some of the soul of his friend, and they enter into a blood relation with each other.

2.  In every place where a person is killed (and only when his blood is shed), the soul appears at nighttime as a <term>rasad: a guardian spirit or ghost</term>² and cries out the last words spoken by the dead;³ compare Genesis 4:10, *"The voice of your brother's blood cries to me from the earth."*

3.  In case a wife is not much beloved by her husband, she tries to inflame his affection by giving him a few drops of her blood, mixed with some other liquid, to drink.⁴ She is certain that the part of the soul drunk with the blood by her husband knits him more strongly to her.

4.  Supernatural powers hover over every place and person where blood is found. They may even be harmful. This is the cause of the fear of menstrual blood held by the inhabitants of Palestine.⁵

---

¹ Deuteronomy 12:13 <note>Likely referring to Deut. 12:23: "the blood is the life."</note>.
² Also called <term>mfâwil: a spirit or omen</term>.
³ *Superstition* <note>original: "Aberglaube," referring to Canaan’s 1914 book, *Aberglaube im Glauben der Araber* (Superstition in the Belief of the Arabs)</note>, page 17.
⁴ Blood always used to be the sign of a covenant, Exodus 12:7, 13.
⁵ This is not the place to dwell on this phase of superstition, which is discussed fully in *Superstition* <note>original: "Aberglaube"</note>.

<summary>This page explores the Palestinian belief that blood is the dwelling place of the soul, explaining rituals like blood-brotherhood and the spiritual dangers associated with shed blood.</summary>
<keywords>blood sacrifice, soul, Palestinian superstition, rasad, yithâwû, blood covenant, folk religion, Canaan</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ritual significance of blood in Palestinian folk religion, transitioning from the concept of blood as the seat of the soul to its role in animal sacrifice (dbîḥah) and offerings made directly to God (qurbân).</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society.</header>

By offering an animal, we are offering a life—a soul in place of another, the human life. The meat of the *dhibah* <term>dbîḥah: a slaughtered animal sacrifice</term> is not the most important part of the sacrifice. It serves only to satisfy the human guests. The gods (saints, who in practice act as lower deities) do not eat or drink; they are satisfied only by the sacrifice of the soul.¹ A Christian woman from Jifna <note>original: "Djifnah"</note>, in describing a sacrifice, said: *"To al-Khidr belongs the pouring of the blood (the soul), and to the people belongs the sacrificial animal."* <note>original: "lil-Ḥaḍr fêḍet ed-dam u lan-nâs ed-dbîḥah"</note> <note>al-Khidr, "The Green One," is a figure in Islamic and Levantine tradition often identified by local Christians with Saint George.</note> Blood plays an important role <note>original: "rôle"</note> as the symbol of life. This shedding of blood is the essential part of an animal sacrifice.² No animal that has been previously killed will be accepted by any *wali* <term>welî: a Muslim saint or holy man</term> as a sacrifice. The blood must be shed in his shrine, or at least in his name. It is the act of atonement.

### b) Qurban or Walimeh Lillah

A *qurban* <term>qurbân: an offering or sacrifice</term> also involves the *dhibah*. This type of vow belongs partly to this category and partly to a later one. It is described fully here because it involves animal offerings. In these cases, the vow is made to God alone, and not to any saint or *wali*. Thus, it is clear that a *qurban* is a true sacrifice in the name of the Almighty, often performed in cases of sickness. As soon as the sick person recovers, the animal must give its life for the person who was saved. The animal belongs entirely to God. However, because God does not need food, the meat is distributed entirely among the poor. The person making the vow receives none of it—unlike the practice in all the animal offerings described up to now. Such an animal may be slaughtered in any place, and its meat is distributed *ahdar* <note>original: "aḥḍar"</note>, meaning in a "raw" or uncooked condition,³ or it may be cooked and then given to the poor. From this custom, the name *walimeh lillah* <term>walîmeh lillâh: a feast for God</term> is derived.

*Qurban* (derived from the Arabic roots *qaruba* or *qarraba*) denotes anything offered to God that brings the person making the offering nearer to the Deity.⁴ *Walimeh lillah*, or "a feast for God," perfectly expresses the idea explained elsewhere and shows that the sacrifice is, in reality, nothing but a feast given to the needy in the name of God.

***

¹ 1 Samuel 14:32-34; Deuteronomy 12:16; Leviticus 7:10 ff., 7:27.
² Nathan Söderblom, *The Nature of Belief in God* <note>original: "Das Wesen des Götterglaubens"</note>, p. 81; Curtiss; Kahle.
³ Also called *naiy* <term>raw</term>.
⁴ *The Comprehensive Dictionary* <note>original: "Muḥîṭ el-Muḥîṭ"</note> II, 1681.

It distinguishes between sacrifices to saints and the "qurban," a vow-offering made exclusively to God where the meat is given entirely to the poor.</summary>
