In many cases when such a vow is made, the mother or the father takes an open knife (*mûs*, *ḥûṣah*) <note>original: "mûs, ḥûṣah"—different regional terms for a knife</note> and sticks it into the door or the outer wall of the sickroom. While doing so, the following words are spoken:

> "An offering for God’s sake <note>original: "qurbân la widjh allâh"—literally "a sacrifice for God’s face"</note> if my child recovers <note>original: "in ṭâb ibnî"—if my son gets well</note>."

Such a knife may remain in its place until the sheep is sacrificed. <note>This custom is especially prevalent in the Benî Zêd region.</note>

A similar custom involves vowing a candle to God:

> “If my child speaks, I will light a candle for You, O God.” <note>This type of vow is made when a child reaches an age where they should be speaking but remains silent.</note>

According to local Christian belief, such a candle must be lit in the open air and not inside a shrine. Imm Elyas of Jerusalem <note>Imm Elyas: "Mother of Elias"</note> once made such a vow and lit the candle on her roof. She spent the night guarding it so that it would not be stolen. As the candle did not burn down completely, it had to be lit again on the second night. According to Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> custom, a light of this kind may be brought to the nearest *wali* <term>welî: a Muslim saint or holy man</term> and burned there in the name of God. <note>According to an informant from Bettîr in the Benî Ḥasan district.</note>

Before leaving this subject of animal sacrifices, we may note a *dhibah* <term>dbîḥah: a slaughtered animal sacrifice</term> custom that has nothing to do with vows. In cases where a saint has been offended by a mortal, he may punish the wrongdoer—and perhaps all the inhabitants of the village—in a very severe way. A sheep is generally offered to reconcile with the saint. I have already shared the story of Sheikh Saleh <note>original: "eš-šêḫ Ṣâleḥ"</note>, who dwells in Khirbet Nuta <note>original: "Ḥirbet Nûtâ"</note>, which illustrates this point perfectly.

### c) Meatless food

In Jerusalem and the immediate neighborhood, these vows are called *smat* <term>smâṭ: a banquet or formal meal, often involving a communal dish</term>, while in the regions of Beni Zeid and Beni Malik, the word *smat* is used only for an animal sacrifice, as we have seen. I have been unable to find the same explanation for this word that Jaussen <note>Antonin Jaussen, a French Dominican priest and ethnologist who wrote "Coutumes des Arabes au pays de Moab" (1908)</note> provided, which he derived from the use of the expression in Transjordan. Everywhere, vows of this sort are also known by the name *tabhah* <term>ṭabhah: literally "a cooking" or "a cooked dish"</term>, though it is a less common expression. Most inhabitants of Jerusalem and the adjacent Christian villages understand *smat* to refer only to a special dish, namely—

The priest says prayers over the loaves and asks for God’s blessing for all the members of the family who offered them. He then keeps four loaves for himself, and the person who made the offering takes one. The final loaf is divided among the family members as a blessing <term>barakeh: a spiritual blessing or grace believed to be reside in holy objects</term>. Some keep a piece of this blessed bread to use during illness, believing that eating it will cure disease. Once such a vow is taken, it must be continued every year until the man for whom it was made passes away. Such a holy bread offering <note>original: *ghirbnîyeh*; also called *quddâseh*</note> is always made one day before the feast day of the saint to whom it was vowed. The priest blesses the bread after the afternoon prayer <note>original: *ṣalât el-’aṣr*</note>. This vow provides material support to the priest. <margin>This custom is followed only by the Greek Orthodox Church.</margin> Many people also vow bread for the poor and needy in the name of a saint. I shall describe this practice more fully in the following section.

### III. Offerings given to needy persons in the name of the saint

In the previous section, we frequently mentioned vows fulfilled for the benefit of the poor. The communal feast <note>original: *smâṭ* or *ṭabḫah*</note> and especially the "banquet for God" <note>original: *walîmeh lillâh* or *qurbân*</note> fall under this category. All vows of food promised to God must be given to the poor, even if this was not specifically stated in the wording of the vow. In all other vows, the recipient is specified; for example: “O Prophet Saleh <note>original: *Nabî Șâleh*</note>, if you bring me safely through this matter, I shall distribute a basket of rice to the poor.” Either food or cloth may be vowed. The food may be offered in either a cooked or uncooked state. The “needy” may be grouped under the following headings:

1. The poor <note>original: *fuqarâ*</note>
2. Orphans and widows <note>original: *aytâm u arâmil*</note> <margin>These are often called by the collective name *maqṭâ‘în*, meaning those who have no one to help them.</margin>
3. Prisoners <note>original: *maḥâbîs*</note>
4. The sick, especially the mentally disturbed <note>original: *madjânîn*</note>.

The following are examples of such vows: 
> “If I recover and am able to walk, I shall, O Prophet of God, clothe thirty orphans;” “O friend of God, as soon as I am released from jail, I shall give two hundred loaves of bread to the prisoners;” “O Saint George, if my son returns to his right mind, I shall sacrifice a sheep for the mentally ill <note>original: *madjânîn*</note> at your shrine.”

In making a vow of clothing <term>kisweh: derived from the Arabic *kasâ*, meaning "to clothe"</term>, one must—according to most of those I have interviewed—supply all the garments necessary to fully dress a person. This includes a shirt <note>original: *qamîṣ*</note>, shoes <note>original: *ṣarâmî*; also called *markûb* or *maššâi*</note>, loose bag-trousers <note>original: *lbâs*; made mostly of white or blue cloth with wide sections above the knees, sometimes called *širwâl*</note>, a gown <note>original: *qumbâz* or *kibir*</note>, and a head-dress—either a fez <note>original: *ṭarbûš*</note> or a traditional headscarf and cord <note>original: *ḥaṭṭah* and *'uqâl*</note>. <margin>The *qumbâz* gown is worn over the shirt and trousers; it is made of colored linen, cotton, or silk, is open in the front, reaches just above the ankles, and is secured with a belt or *zunnâr*.</margin> Many people do not provide the full outfit, giving only a shirt and a gown, or the cloth required to make both. Generally, the fulfillment of such a vow is postponed until one of the major Muslim feasts: the Feast of Sacrifice <note>original: *'îd eḍ-ḍḥîyeh* or *'îd el-kbîr* (the Great Feast)</note> or the Feast of Breaking the Fast <note>original: *'îd eṣ-ṣghîr* or *'îd Ramaḍân*</note>.

The condition of prisoners used to be pitiful. It very often happened that an accused or suspected person remained in prison for months before their trial took place. Most of these victims would implore God and all the saints for help, promising to offer a sheep or bread for the other prisoners if they were released from their misery. Even prisoners who had already been convicted would take vows that as soon as they were released, they would offer various gifts to the other inmates of the jail. Even if these vows are made in the name of a saint <note>original: *welî*</note>, the offerings do not reach his sanctuary, but are distributed directly to the needy in the prison.

It is advised by many in Jerusalem <margin>This information was provided by Abû 'Osmân of Jerusalem.</margin> that the best way to fulfill a food vow for the poor is to give it to the *dkîyeh* <term>dkîyeh: a charitable soup kitchen where food is cooked daily and distributed to the poor</term>. Generally, the person who vows a sheep will remain in the kitchen until the meat is cooked and distributed. Bread may also be brought to the kitchen. Orphanages and hospitals are sometimes, but not often, remembered in these vows. Donations of flour, rice, melted butter, beans, and lentils are commonly presented. The leper asylum "Jesus Help" <note>original: *Jesus Hilf*</note> receives such vows at long intervals.

> “My Lord, if I live to see my son return in good health, I will bequeath my lower house to the *Dkîyeh*.”

The yearly rental income of such a house goes to the general funds of the *dkîyeh* religious endowment <term>waqf: a religious or charitable trust under Islamic law</term> as religious...

endowment. I know of a man who transferred all his property to a relative as a *waqf* <term>waqf: a charitable endowment under Islamic law, where property is held in trust for religious or social purposes</term>, making it a condition in the *waqfîyeh* <term>waqfîyeh: the legal deed or document establishing an endowment</term> that a sum of money should be given to the poor by the heir in the donor's name. It may happen that someone has no money to vow to the poor. In such a case, he promises to devote the wages of three or more days’ work to the purchase of food for the poor.¹

## IV. Religious vows

By “religious vows,” I mean that a person takes it upon themselves to fulfill some religious act or obligation beyond what they would normally perform. Religious vows are practiced by both Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note> and Christians. A very interesting fact is that a member of one religious community sometimes takes on the obligations of another.

Perhaps the most popular example of this category is the promise to recite the story of the Prophet’s nativity, known as *qrâyet môlad* <note>original: "reading of the nativity"</note>. “If my daughter recovers, I shall recite a *môlad*, O Prophet of God.” The *môlad* is the legendary story of the Prophet’s birth; it is a poem that describes not only the birth of the Prophet but also names his ancestors, all the previous prophets, and his own deeds. A large part of the *môlad* is devoted to the praise of Muhammad <note>original: "Mohammed"</note>. There are different versions of the *môlad*, and one of the most important is that of Imam <note>original: "el-Imâm"</note> Abdallah bin Mohammad el-Manawi. According to the biographical dictionary *The Obituaries of Eminent Men* <note>original: *Wafiyât el-A‘yân*</note> by Ibn Khallikan <note>original: "Ibn Ḥallikân"</note>, volume I, the first person to attach great importance to the *môlad* was Muzaffar ad-Din <note>original: "Muẓẓaffar ed-Dîn"</note>, the Prince of Irbil. During his time, in the year 604 A.H. <note>604 A.H.: approximately 1207–1208 AD in the Islamic calendar</note>, al-Hafiz Omar Abu al-Khattab wrote the best *môlad* poem.

The fulfillment of the vow may be simple or more elaborate. In the first case, the person making the vow gives a sum of money—one to one and a half *medjîdî* <term>medjîdî: an Ottoman silver coin named after Sultan Abdülmecid I</term> in the villages, or half a pound to two pounds in the cities—to a sheikh <note>original: *šêḫ*</note> who must recite the *môlad* from a minaret, in a mosque, or at a *welî* <term>welî: a saint's shrine or sanctuary; literally "friend [of God]"</term> in the name of the giver. Among these places, the minaret is the one usually chosen, and often a specific one is named: “at the Red Minaret” <note>original: *‘alâ el-mêdaneh el-Ḥamrâ*</note>,² or “at the minaret of the Prophet Moses” <note>original: *‘alâ mêdanet en-nabî Mûsâ*</note>.³ The muezzin <note>original: *muaddin*</note> of that minaret may perform this service.

---

¹ Heard from Mother <note>original: "Imm"</note> Bisharah Tlejeh.
² Inside the city in the northeast quarter.
³ A *môlad* is not generally recited in small sanctuaries, nor from the minaret of unimportant shrines. The shrines of Ibrahim al-Hawwas, the Prophet Moses <note>original: "en-nabî Mûsâ"</note>, the Prophet Salih <note>original: "en-nabî Ṣâleḥ"</note>, Ali ibn Alem, and the Prophet Reuben <note>original: "en-nabî Rûbîn"</note>, etc., are the preferred places.

<meta>This page continues the description of the religious vow to recite the story of the Prophet Muhammad's birth (the mawlid). It details the social customs, the specific times the ceremony occurs, and the use of blessed items like barley, eggs, and water for healing and protection.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

The *muadhdhin* <term>muadhdhin: the person who calls the faithful to prayer from a minaret</term> or another *sheikh* <term>sheikh: a religious leader or scholar</term> who reads the *mawlid* <note>original: "môlad"; the recitation of the story of the Prophet Muhammad’s birth</note> takes several others with him to help him sing or recite parts of the text. It is always performed in the evening, generally on the night between Thursday and Friday, or between Sunday and Monday. Even more significant is the reading of a *mawlid* inside a home. Many friends are invited, and a large meal is prepared. After everyone has finished their supper, they gather in a large room where the ceremony is held. One or more sheikhs are chosen to recite this prayer, while the audience may repeat refrains in a chorus. The ceremony takes about two to two and a half hours, during which there is no smoking or conversation. People may enter or leave the room at any time. After the ceremony concludes, sweets are served. Thus, we see that the religious ceremony is both preceded and followed by a celebration. When a woman has made a vow to recite the *mawlid*, she fulfills it by inviting her female relatives and friends and asking a *sheikhah* <term>sheikhah: a female religious leader</term> or a blind sheikh <note>original: "šêḥ darîr"</note> to recite it.

In some villages, one to two *rotls* <term>rotls: a traditional unit of weight, roughly 5-6 pounds depending on the region</term> of barley and three raw eggs (though some bring only one) are placed in front of the sheikh who recites the story of "Muhammad's Nativity." The eggs are eaten by the sheikh to keep his voice clear, while the barley is kept by the people as a blessing and as a curative medicine for illness. It is believed that the barley receives supernatural power through this religious ceremony. If a child falls sick, they are treated with the smoke from this barley <note>original: "fumigated with this barley"; the practice of burning the grain so the child breathes the smoke for healing</note>.

An old woman from Jerusalem told me that a dish of flour and a glass of water are placed before the sheikh who recites this prayer. As soon as he finishes, the contents of the dish are distributed among those present. It is kept as an amulet <note>original: "hirz"</note> for small children. They either carry it in a small bundle around the neck as a protective measure against evil spirits, or they are treated with its smoke in case of disease. The water is drunk by those present, in the belief that it cures shortness of breath.

Although this custom is purely Islamic <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note>, it sometimes happens (though very rarely) that a Christian woman vows that she will recite—

<margin>2. I could find no other explanation for the eggs. The sheikh does not have the right to take them away with him.</margin>
<margin>3. I heard of this custom from Mansur of Lifta.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of religious vows, specifically the recitation of the Prophet Muhammad's birth story (mawlid). It transitions into other types of vows, such as fasting and the protective baptism of Muslim children in Christian churches.</meta>

# CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine 181

A *mawlid* <note>original: "môlad"; a recitation of the story of the Prophet Muhammad’s birth</note> is often promised as a vow. For example, the wife of el-Qari <margin>The el-Qari family belongs to the Greek Orthodox Church.</margin> once promised: "If my child recovers, I shall recite a *mawlid*." To fulfill this, she held the ceremony in her own home. Generally, Christians prefer to give a sheikh <term>sheikh: a Muslim religious leader or scholar</term> a sum of money and ask him to recite the *mawlid* on their behalf.

Besides the *mawlid*, fasting is very often vowed. It is a religious law that every Muslim must fast during the month of Ramadan. <note>Menstruating women, the sick, and those traveling are excused from this rule, but they must make up the missed fast days later that same year.</note> During this time, nothing is eaten, drunk, or smoked during daylight hours. A first meal, the *iftar* <note>original: "fṭûr"; the meal that breaks the fast</note>, is taken at a specific hour in the late evening, and a second meal, the *suhur* <note>original: "shûr"; the pre-dawn meal</note>, is eaten a few hours after midnight. 

No one may vow to fast during Ramadan itself, as it is already a mandatory duty. However, fasts on other days or weeks are often vowed, generally by women. The specific number of days or weeks to be observed is defined when the vow is made. These voluntary fasts are kept in the same way as the Ramadan fast. Rajab and Sha'ban are the preferred months for these extra fasts, and Monday and Thursday are the preferred days of the week. Unlike some other vows, this type cannot be "bought off" or changed to a different obligation. A person who has vowed to fast for a month must keep that promise exactly, whereas someone who has vowed to sacrifice a sheep or offer oil may give a sum of money instead. No woman is allowed to fast while she is ritually impure <note>original: "unclean"; referring to the period of menstruation</note>.

Christians may also vow to fast for more days or weeks than are required by their church. In these cases, they completely abstain from animal products—sometimes including fish, and sometimes excluding it. It is more unusual, but not unheard of, for a Christian to promise to observe the month of Ramadan, or part of it. Similarly, Muslim women sometimes bind themselves to observe the fast for the Feast of the Virgin Mary <note>original: "‘îd el-Ad’râ"</note> or a portion of it.

Often, a Muslim mother who has lost several children in succession will vow to baptize her next child. She believes that placing the infant under the protection of Christ will guard him against death by driving away the evil spirit *el-Qarina* <note>original: "el-Qarînî"; in Middle Eastern folklore, a female demon or "double" who haunts pregnant women and infants</note>. The *Qarina* is a demon believed to attack children and pregnant women, causing childhood diseases and miscarriages. <note>See the author's work on "Superstition" (Aberglaube) for more on the *Qarina*.</note> For instance, Watfeh, the wife of Ibrahim Aql from the village of Lifta, lost all of her sons during their infancy. While she was pregnant again, an old neighbor advised her to vow to have her child baptized...

<summary>This page describes various religious vows in Palestine, including voluntary fasting and the cross-religious practice of Muslim mothers baptizing children to protect them from the demon el-Qarina.</summary>
<keywords>fasting, Ramadan, mawlid, baptism, el-Qarina, interfaith practices, Palestine folklore, Lifta, Rajab, Sha'ban</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of religious vows in Palestine, focusing on interfaith practices where Muslim mothers seek Christian baptism for their sons as a protective measure, and the specific rituals surrounding circumcision vows at Muslim shrines.</meta>

...offspring baptized by a Christian priest. She did it with the words:
> "O my Lord, if You grant me a boy, I vow to have him baptized by the Christians and to give him a Christian name." <note>original: "yâ rabbî in razaqtnî ṣabî niḍrun ‘alay la-a‘mmduh ‘ind en-naṣârâ u la-asammîh ‘alâ ism en-naṣârâ"</note>

She bore two sons after this vow and both were baptized. One was named John <note>original: "Ḥannâ"</note> and the other George <note>original: "Djirius"</note>. A sheikh <term>sheikh: a Muslim religious leader or local elder</term> of Shu'fat <note>original: "Šu‘fâṭ"</note> told me that a barren woman may vow:
> "By God, if I get a boy I shall call him Elijah <note>original: "Eliâs"</note>."

By this expression, she means that she will give him a Christian name, thus dedicating him to a Christian saint. She must therefore baptize him, since baptism puts him under Christ’s protection, and giving him the name of a Christian saint helps to keep him safe. Therefore, only names of popular Christian saints are chosen, like John <note>original: "Ḥannâ"</note>, George <note>original: "Djirius"</note>, and others. St. George’s name is preferred since he is a connecting link between Christians and Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note>. It is said that the priest conducts the ceremony of baptism just as he would for a Christian child, except that holy oil <term>holy oil: original "mêrôn," or chrism, used in the sacrament of baptism</term> is not used.

There are also a few Christian women who vow to circumcise a child and to give him a Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> name, such as Darwish <note>original: "Darwîš"</note> or Hasan <note>original: "Ḥasan"</note>. Such vows are taken in the same circumstances as the ones mentioned previously—specifically, when a mother has lost all her earlier male children. When such a woman makes a vow, she may specify the place where the child is to be circumcised. The baptism of Muslim children is much more common than the circumcision of Christian ones; the latter is only found among a few villagers.

Circumcision is a rule for every Muslim, and therefore the act itself cannot be vowed as a voluntary obligation. However, a Muslim may bind himself to perform this act at a specific shrine:
> "I entreat you, O Prophet Moses, if you cure my child, I will circumcise him in your sanctuary."

Such a vow is thought to be beneficial for both parties. The child is protected by the saint, and the confidence shown in the man of God increases the saint's reputation. Very often, a special and additional vow is made, namely:
> "I will take it upon myself to circumcise a certain number of poor children together with my own boy."

This means that the person making the vow has to pay for the expenses of the operation and also give each child some clothing as a gift.

Some vow the service of a person in a sanctuary:
> "If you, O man of God, grant me a child, I shall let him serve you for one month..."

<summary>This page details the practice of "protective" interfaith vows in Palestine, where Muslim and Christian parents adopt each other's rituals—such as baptism or circumcision—to ensure the survival of their children. It also describes the practice of vowing circumcision at specific holy sites or sponsoring the circumcision of poor children as an act of devotion.</summary>

# CANAAN: Muslim Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

...time.)" Such a custom—although also found among Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note>—is more widespread among Christians. A vow of direct service to a sanctuary for a certain period is fulfilled by helping the <term>qandalaft</term> <note>original: "sexton"</note> with his work.<sup>1</sup> In such cases, Muslims sweep the shrine and light the lamps. This type of vow resembles the vow made by Hannah, the mother of Samuel (1 Samuel 1:11). *I have vowed him to Saint Anthony* <note>original: "Nadartuh la-mâr Anṭôn"</note> means that a boy must wear the habit of that religious order for one year. The priest puts the garments on the child, and one year later, the priest must take them off. 

The parents offer a candle at each of these ceremonies. The convent provides the girdle-rope, the rosary (<term>el-masbaḥah</term>), and the cap (<term>eṭ-ṭaqîyeh</term>), while the parents provide the cloth, which is cut by the priest (or at the convent). This priest prays over the clothing as well as over the child. Thus, the child wears priestly clothes for one year. Members of the Roman Catholic <note>original: "Latin"</note> church, as well as the Greek Orthodox, follow this practice. The latter often ask a Roman Catholic priest to perform this ceremony.

Sometimes a sick person, or a specific part of them, is vowed to a holy man: "O man of God, O <term>Hauwâṣ</term><sup>2</sup> <note>Hauwâṣ is a specific local saint</note>, if my child recovers, you will get half of him." This means that half the "price" of the person is offered as a gift to the saint. The monetary "price" of a person is usually only discussed in cases of murder. The <term>diyeh</term>, or "blood price," must be paid by the murderers to the family of the victim, and it typically amounts to 33,000 piasters <note>a historical unit of currency used in the Ottoman Empire and the Levant</note>. Vowing half or a quarter of a person means paying half or a quarter of those 33,000 piasters<sup>3</sup> (meaning 16,500 or 8,250 piasters respectively). 

This sum is given to the <term>qaiym</term> <note>the caretaker or warden of a sanctuary</note> of the shrine with the understanding that it is for the <term>welî</term> <note>a Muslim saint or "friend of God"</note>. In cases where such a vow is made for a girl, her price is not calculated based on the blood price, but on the <term>mahr</term> <note>the marriage gift or dowry paid by a groom to a bride</note>. The girl's wedding ceremony cannot take place until her vow is fulfilled.<sup>4</sup> Even in the Old Testament, a <term>Nazarite</term> <note>a person consecrated to God by a vow, often involving abstaining from certain foods or cutting their hair</note> could be...

...redeemed at a valuation according to age and sex, on a special scale. <note>original: Leviticus 27:1-7. The valuation for males ranged from 5 to 50 shekels, and for females from 3 to 30 shekels.</note>

Finally, we must mention a custom which, although not belonging directly to this group of vows, seems more closely associated with it than to others. The wife of F. (a Greek Orthodox Christian), whose son was very sick, fastened a silk handkerchief on the <term>Nabî Mûsâ</term> <note>The Prophet Moses; this refers to the banner used during the annual Palestinian festival of the same name</note> flag. This was done on the first day of the feast, while the procession moved from the Mosque of Omar <note>The Dome of the Rock</note>. While doing so, she exclaimed: “If my son gets well, O prophet Moses, I shall fasten another handkerchief on the day your flag returns.” <note>Information provided by Imm Elyâs (Mother of Elias).</note> This example shows us again the honor paid to Muslim <note>original: "Mohammedan"</note> saints by non-Muslims. Such a vow is exceedingly rare. In explanation of this custom, we may point to the Muslim custom: “If my son recovers, O <term>Hauwâṣ</term> <note>a local saint</note>, I shall tie for you an <term>ell</term> <note>original: "pic," a traditional unit of measurement</note> of white cloth as a white flag.” <note>original Arabic: "in ṭâb ibnî la-arbuṭlak yâ Hauwâṣ drâʿ baft râyeh bêḍah"</note> To hoist a white flag for someone means to proclaim their ability, their excellent character, their charity, and so on.

### V. Bodily chastisement and vows to be fulfilled on the body of the person making the vow or the person for whom the vow was taken

> “If my child gets well, O my Lady, O Saint Mary, I shall visit you (meaning your shrine) barefoot.”

Walking barefoot to a sanctuary is a vow made by both Muslims and Christians. Christians of Jerusalem prefer the sanctuaries of Saint Mary and <term>Mâr Eliâs</term> <note>Saint Elijah</note>. Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note> also bind themselves to <term>Sittî Maryam</term> <note>Our Lady Mary</note> by a similar vow. This visit must be performed, if at all possible, on the saint’s feast day. Generally, the women rise very early—long before daybreak—and while everyone is sleeping, they walk barefoot to the sanctuary, say a prayer, and return before the other visitors begin their pilgrimages to the shrine. The <term>hadith</term> <note>a traditional record of the sayings or actions of the Prophet Muhammad</note> says: “If a man vows to walk to a place and he cannot do it, he may not fulfill his vow by walking.” <note>original Arabic: "Man naḏara an yamšî li-maḥallin walam yaṣtaṭiʿ an yaqûma bihî falâ yadjûzu an yukmilan-niḏra mašyan."</note>

<summary>This page describes interfaith religious practices in Palestine, such as Christians participating in the Nabî Mûsâ festival and the shared tradition of walking barefoot to the shrines of Mary and Elijah.</summary>
<keywords>Nabî Mûsâ, Saint Mary, Mâr Eliâs, religious vows, barefoot pilgrimage, interfaith traditions, Hadith, Palestinian folklore</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of religious vows in Palestine, moving from symbolic acts of service to specific physical rituals involving hair-cutting and acts of humility performed at both Muslim and Christian sanctuaries.</meta>

Another vow of humiliation is the sweeping of a sanctuary with one’s head-dress.
> “I take a vow to sweep Sêf ed-Dîn <note>A local sanctuary in the village of Liftâ</note> with my head-cloth if my brother gets well.” <note>Heard from a woman of Liftâ.</note>

To throw or to place a head-dress on the ground is always regarded as a dishonoring action.

Very often, it is vowed that the hair of a sick child will be cut in a sanctuary. The hair is allowed to grow from the moment of the vow until the promise is fulfilled. In the case of a Christian, the child is taken to the church where the fulfillment of the vow must take place. If the Holy Sepulchre (known as the Tomb of Salvation <note>original: Qabr el-Ḥalâṣ</note>) is chosen, the act is performed behind the tomb of Christ. The priest, after saying some prayers, cuts a lock of hair from the forehead, another from the back of the head <note>original: occipital region</note>, and one from each side, thus marking the sign of the cross. The barber then cuts the rest of the hair. In other churches, the child approaches the altar <note>original: el-maḍbaḥ</note> and the ceremony takes place. Friends and relatives are invited to attend the ceremony, and upon returning home, everyone joins in a feast. 

In the case of Muslims <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note>, the child enters the sanctuary and the hair is cut in the prayer niche <note>original: miḥrâb</note> or near the tomb. At the Sanctuary of the Prophet Moses <note>original: Nebî Mûsâ</note>, it is done outside the actual shrine. In the district of Jenin <note>original: Djenîn</note>, I was told that a vow to cut the hair may be performed in most of the local saints' shrines <note>original: welîs</note>. This practice of shaving the head or cutting the hair at the end of a votive period is a very old custom. In the Bible, we have references to it. <note>Acts 18:18, 21:24</note>

In all cases, the weight of the hair that was cut off—calculated in gold, silver, or money—is presented to the sanctuary. The money is given to the priest or to the caretaker <note>original: qaiym</note>.

### VI. Vows not connected with any holy person or shrine

Such vows may be vestiges or remnants of primitive religious practices.
> “If my brother returns safely, I shall make you a pit-roasted feast <note>original: zarb; a traditional method of cooking meat in an underground earth oven</note>, which we will eat in the vineyards.”

This vow is made for friends. At other times, it is a thank-offering to a person. Thus we meet—

<keywords>vows, hair-cutting rituals, Sêf ed-Dîn, Holy Sepulchre, Nebî Mûsâ, zarb, Liftâ, Jenin, religious customs, Palestinian folklore</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Palestinian religious vows, explaining how the concepts of atonement and "breaking evil" manifest in daily life through symbolic sacrifices and verbal formulas, before transitioning to offerings made to the dead and spirits.</meta>

<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

There are many vows <note>original: ndûrah</note> of this category made for physicians, nurses, teachers, and others. Most of these vows are never fulfilled.

In connection with the subject of vows, I wish to draw attention to the following concept. There are some expressions that seem to indicate a completely different idea, but which actually originate from the same belief system as these vows. The Palestinian believes that the death of a child or animal, the breaking of an object, or the spilling of a liquid may atone for the loss of someone or something more important. "The evil is broken" <note>original: Inkasar eš-šarr</note> is said when a glass of water, a tea cup, or a coffee cup falls from a visitor’s hand and breaks. "The evil has been poured out" <note>original: Inkabb eš-šarr</note> is used on similar occasions.

When a horse dies or a house collapses, people express their feelings by saying: "It is a ransom for you and your family" <note>original: fidâk u fidâ 'iyâlak</note>. The idea underlying these expressions is that some evil was destined to befall the person who suffered the loss, but Divine Providence directed it toward a less important object, thereby preventing a greater loss.

### VII. Vows for the dead and the *djinn*

I cannot conclude the discussion of vows without calling attention to vows offered to the *djinn* <note>Spirits or demons in Islamic and Middle Eastern folklore</note> and the dead. Although this category of offerings differs from those brought to the saints, it shows many points of resemblance and serves to illustrate some interesting points. It is difficult to find examples of sacrifices brought to spirits and the dead that are as characteristic as those offered to saints. Nevertheless, a careful investigation reveals many customs that belong here. A comparison between sacrifices offered to saints and those brought to the dead and the *djinn* shows the same idea at different stages of development. I shall describe some customs and superstitious beliefs which suggest—

#### a) Offerings to demons

The "sacrifice of the house" <note>original: Dbîḥet ed-dâr</note> among the peasants—which corresponds to the "sacrifice of the tent" <note>original: dbîḥet bêt eš-ša'r; literally "sacrifice of the house of hair"</note> <margin>Jaussen, p. 339</margin> among the Bedouin—is the most characteristic example of this type. Besides the "sacrifice for Abraham" <note>original: dbîḥet el-Halîl; a sheep must be offered to the Prophet Abraham whenever a new house is built</note>, we encounter another kind of animal sacrifice,

<summary>This page explores the Palestinian folk belief in "ransom" (fidâ), where minor losses like broken pottery or animal deaths are seen as protective substitutes for human life, and introduces the practice of making offerings to spirits (djinn) and the dead.</summary>
<keywords>Palestinian folklore, vows, atonement, djinn, animal sacrifice, folk medicine, Bedouin customs, Abraham, domestic rituals</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of sacrifices and offerings, moving from general vows to specific rituals performed for the "jinn" (spirits) during the construction of houses and the settlement of new locations.</meta>

<header>CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine</header>

This type of sacrifice is practiced by many peasants in connection with the completion of a house. Some will not even occupy a newly built dwelling until a sheep is offered as a sacrifice <note>original: immolated</note> to please the *jinn* <note>original: djinn; spirits or supernatural beings in Islamic mythology</note> who have already taken up residence in it. The sides of the door are painted with the blood as an external sign of the offering. Others may even slay a sheep in the foundation trenches. Whenever a Bedouin tribe changes the location of its camp, the sheikh <note>original: šēḫ; a leader or head of a tribe</note> and others sacrifice a sheep to the spirits of that spot with the words *dastūr yā ṣāḥib el-maḥall*, "By your permission, O owner of the place."¹

According to an old belief, which is currently dying out, some buildings—especially baths and houses erected near a spring <note>OCR error: spriug</note>—will not be fortunate and prosperous unless the foundation stone has been laid upon shed blood. In the case of a Turkish bath, it is even thought that a human being—and curiously enough, a Sudanese person—must be offered before the first stone is laid. Some old women assured me that the head of a Black man <note>original: negro</note> is sufficient if buried below the threshold. The following expressions point to this tradition of human sacrifice: *el-ḥammām mâ bidûr illâ ‘alâ ‘abd*, "The bath does not work except on a Black man"; *qâym ‘alâ ‘abd*, "it is erected on a Black man"; *mabnî ‘alâ ‘abd*, "it is built on a Black man."² Excavations show that these foundation sacrifices were very widespread in the ancient Orient. In 1 Kings 16:34 we read:
> "In his days, Hiel the Bethelite built Jericho: he laid its foundation at the cost of Abiram his firstborn, and set up its gates at the cost of his youngest son Segub."

Whenever a house is thought to be inhabited by demons—perhaps because every family which occupies it loses one or more of its members to death—nobody else will move into it before sacrificing one or more sheep. Such a house is thought to be *maskûn*, "haunted," literally, "inhabited (by evil spirits)."

The spring of Djifnâ is supposed to be inhabited by a spirit <note>original: djinnîyeh; a female jinn</note> which appears in the form of a charming bride. Once, a woman of the village approached the spring during menstruation. This careless act provoked the anger of the spirit, and the spring dried up. A priest had to go to the place and burn incense, thereby reconciling the spirit.³

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of offerings made to spirits, shifting from animal sacrifices to "sugaring" rituals used to heal illnesses and appease the jinn, followed by a transition into rituals performed for the deceased.</meta>

<page-num>188</page-num>
<header>Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society</header>

The custom known as "sugaring" <note>original: tiḥlây</note> also belongs to this category. Superstition holds that the spirits <note>original: djinn; supernatural beings in Islamic folklore</note> are pleased and quieted by sweets and sugar offerings.¹ The following example will illustrate the procedure. A mother once beat her child on the threshold <note>original: ʿatabeh</note>. This careless act resulted in a severe punishment for the child and mother: the first developed a fever and the second suffered a slight attack of facial paralysis. These afflictions were believed to be caused by the spirits living in that place. To appease them, the woman was advised to strew sweets on the threshold.

More complicated is the following belief. A child had a fever, and the mother was told that this was caused by a fright. She took the sick boy to "the Forgotten Stone" <note>original: el-Ḥadjar el-Mansî; likely a local sacred site or megalith</note>, where she washed his hands and feet, after which she scattered sweets and returned home. The explanation of this act is as follows: The fever excited by the fright is caused by demons. Taking the child to the grave of a holy man and washing his extremities there drives the devils out by the power of the man of God. The sweets are an offering to appease the demons, who are irritated because they are forced to leave their prey.

In analyzing all these customs, we find that the idea of offering something to the spirits is represented in every one. I have only mentioned a few examples of each sort. The underlying idea is to gain the favor of the spirits through these sacrifices, to please them, and to atone for mistakes which may have provoked them. According to popular belief, the spirits always react favorably to such offerings. Many references in the Bible point to offerings made to spirits and demons.²

### b) Offerings to the Dead

With regard to sacrifices to the dead, I shall only mention practices which bear a special connection to our subject. While the corpse is being ritually washed, the relatives distribute some money "for the soul of the dead" <note>original: ʿan rûḥ el-maiyet</note>. This is called "the payment for the prayer" <note>original: sqûṭ eṣ-ṣalây; literally 'the falling of the prayer,' a ritual payment to compensate for the deceased's missed religious obligations</note>. In the cemetery, and when the burial is over, money is distributed again, this time to the sheikhs <note>original: šêhs; religious leaders or elders</note> who have been repeating "God is the greatest" <note>original: allâhu akbar</note> as a prayer for the dead during the entire procession. In the—

¹ See *Superstition* <note>original: Aberglaube</note>.
² Leviticus 16:8, 17:7; 2 Chronicles 11:15; 2 Kings 23:8. In the latter, "spirits" <note>original: śeʿîrîm; meaning goat-demons or satyrs</note> should be read instead of "gates" <note>original: šeʿarîm</note>, as has been suggested by scholars.

<summary>This page describes "sugaring" rituals used to appease spirits (jinn) that cause illness and begins detailing the charitable payments made for the souls of the deceased during funeral rites.</summary>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of rituals performed for the deceased, specifically focusing on the acts of charity and commemorative prayers that follow a burial.</meta>

->189<-
->CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine<-

At the graveyard, bread and dried figs are given to the poor for the soul of the deceased <note>original: 'an rûḥ el-maiyit</note>. Those who can afford it may even have a new wooden coffin <note>original: tâbût, ṣaḥlîyeh</note> made, in which the deceased is carried to his burial place. This coffin is then left to be used for the poor who have died, and this act of helping the poor, even after their death, is believed to bring a special spiritual merit <note>original: ṯawâb</note>, or "reward," for the soul of the departed.

All who go to the house of the mourners to offer condolences receive coffee, with or without a piece of cake. After drinking the coffee—and sometimes upon entering as well as leaving—they say: "May God be merciful to him" <note>original: allâh yerḥamu</note>.1 In the third night, a ceremony of praising God <note>original: tahlîleh</note> is held. Sweets are distributed to all the guests, who are chosen from the upper class as well as from the poor. Afterward, the phrase "There is no god but God" <note>original: lâ ilâha illâ allâh</note> is recited by those present, with everyone repeating this phrase over and over again until the total number of prayers reaches one thousand in the cities, and 75,000 in some villages.2 Following this, the complete recitation of the Quran <note>original: al-Ḥitmeh</note> is performed, and those reading it say at the end: "We have presented its merit"—that is, the merit of reading the Holy Book—"for the soul of [Name]" <note>original: wahabnâ ṯawâbahâ li-rûḥ fulân</note>. The meeting ends with a meal. Everyone recites the opening chapter of the Quran <note>original: fâtiḥah</note> for the soul of the dead. The meal on this night is also called the "comforting meal" <note>original: unîsah (el-Mâlḥah)</note>.

On the first Thursday, the relatives distribute a type of cake fried in oil to those who happen to be at the cemetery. This is called the "loosening of the jaw" <note>original: fakket ḥanak</note>.3 On the second Thursday, a specific Thursday ceremony <note>original: ḥmêsîyeh</note> is held in some villages. All friends go to the cemetery to visit the tomb. The relatives distribute dried figs, sweets, raisins, and other treats among those present, especially to children, also offered "for the soul of the deceased." As on other occasions, everyone asks for God’s mercy for the soul of the departed. At the end of forty days, another supper <note>original: 'ašâ</note>4 is prepared. On the "Thursday of the Dead" <note>original: Ḥamîs el-Amwât</note>, the peasants send fruit to the village guest house <note>original: maḍâfeh</note>, while in the cities, the relatives of the dead go to the cemetery and distribute sweets, dyed eggs, and, very rarely, cooked food among the poor as an act of charity.

1 Other common expressions of condolence include: "The safety of your heads" <note>original: salâmet rûskum</note>, "May the remainder of his days be added to your life" <note>original: el-bâqiyeh fî 'umrak</note>, and "May God preserve your children" <note>original: allâh yḫallî wlâdak</note>.
2 This specific number was recorded in the village of el-Qubêbeh.
3 "The visit of the lonely one" <note>original: fakket el-wiḥdeh</note> is the expression used for the first visit made on the day after the body is buried.
4 A person who does not prepare this supper for his deceased father is looked down upon by his fellow villagers, who say: "You have no goodness in you for your father; you didn't even make a supper for him, you dog" <note>original: mâ ilak ḫêr fî abûk mâ 'amiltiloš 'ašâ yâ kalb</note>.

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Islamic funerary customs in Palestine, specifically detailing how food, pilgrimages, and Quranic readings are dedicated as charity for the souls of the deceased.</meta>

Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society

Charity for the deceased <note>original: ṣadaqah ‘an el-maiyet</note>. Food may be sent at any time by the peasants to the guest-house and is known by various names: the charity <note>original: eṣ-ṣadaqah</note>, the visitation <note>original: el-fiqdeh</note>, the mercy <note>original: er-raḥmeh</note>, and the atonement <note>original: kaffârah</note>. City-dwellers generally give food to the poor during every religious festival, such as the Islamic New Year, Ramadan <note>original: Ramaḍân</note>, and the Great Feast <note>original: ’Îd el-Kbîr</note>. While any sort of food—even fruit—may be sent on any occasion to the guest-house <note>original: maḍâfeh</note>, it is customary in the villages to sacrifice a sheep during the ceremony of praising God <note>original: tahlîleh</note>, or sometimes on the Thursday of the Dead <note>original: Ḥamîs el-Amwât</note>.^1

From the examples above, we see that the peasants prefer to send these charitable offerings <note>original: ṣadaqah</note> to the guest-house, as all visitors, travelers, and strangers stop there for rest and refreshment. Every time the relatives of the deceased bring food to the guest-house <note>original: maḍâfeh</note>, they say: "This is for the soul of [so-and-so]" <note>original: hadâ an rûḥ fulân</note>. Everyone who eats from it must say a prayer for the deceased. In this way, many prayers ascend to the Almighty, pleading for the salvation of the one who has died.

Some relatives of a deceased person will give a sum of money to a poor person, requiring them to journey to Mecca and perform the pilgrimage <note>original: ḥadj</note> on behalf of the soul of the dead. When the traveler has finished the official ceremony there, he must say: "Accept, O God, the merit of this pilgrimage for the soul of [so-and-so]" <note>original: itqabbal yâ allah ṭawâb hal-ḥadj ’an rûḥ fulân</note>. However, no sacrifice of the Great Feast <note>original: dbîḥet eḍ-ḍḥîyeh</note> can be offered specifically in the name of the deceased.^2

The Bedouins of the South <note>original: el-Qibleh</note> (referring to the Sinai Peninsula) bring all their cattle once a year to visit their cemetery. While the sheep pass the burial place, every animal that enters the cemetery or remains standing near it is thought to have been chosen by the dead and is sacrificed there. I have been told that very many sheep are killed on such occasions, as all Bedouins, even the poor, bring their sheep to visit the dead so that the departed may choose an offering.

There are some who hire a religious leader <note>original: šêh</note> to read the complete Quran <note>original: el-Hitmeh</note>^3 for the benefit of the dead once or several times a year. As in the case of the pilgrimage, the reader says as soon as he finishes the recitation: "I have offered its merit for the soul of [so-and-so]" <note>original: wahabtu ṭawâbuhâ la-rûḥ fulân</note>.

There are other somewhat similar customs which may be passed over for now. I wish, however, to emphasize the importance of those already mentioned.

---

^1 In the villages, this day is also known as the Thursday of Eggs <note>original: Ḥamîs el-Bêḍ</note>.
^2 This information was gathered from several women in Jerusalem, who provided several examples.
^3 Some believe that the Islamic months of Rajab, Sha'ban, and Ramadan <note>original: Radjab, Ša‘bân, and Ramaḍân</note> are the most suitable months for reading the Holy Book.

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of Islamic charitable prayers for the deceased and transitions into a comparison with similar Christian funerary folk customs in Palestine.</meta>

# CANAAN: Muslim <note>original: Mohammedan</note> Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine 191

repeatedly, that every person who receives money or food in the name of the deceased recites a prayer—generally the *Fatiha* <note>original: fatihah; the opening chapter of the Quran</note>—for the person's soul. In every case, they add the words "May God's mercy be upon him" <note>original: rahmet allah 'aleh</note> or "May God have mercy on him" <note>original: allah yirhamuh</note>.

While all the customs described above are practiced by Muslims <note>original: Mohammedans</note>, the Christians have other traditions based on the same concepts. I do not intend to describe the various masses and formal prayers for the dead, as these are established religious institutions of the different churches. Instead, I will limit myself to folk practices, mentioning only those that differ from the customs described previously.

On the third, ninth, and fortieth days, and at the six-month and one-year anniversaries after a death, <note>1</note> a *shuniyeh* <note>original: şûniyeh</note> and a *widjih* are prepared by the family of the deceased and distributed in the cemetery. A *shuniyeh* refers to a dish of boiled wheat with sugar, decorated with almonds, candy, and other sweets. A *widjih* is a large, disk-like loaf of bread, about 40 to 60 centimeters in diameter, coated with sesame seeds. The priest says several prayers, and then the food is distributed to those present. Many families offer these things only once or twice. <note>2</note>

Some Christians distribute cakes (either sesame cakes <note>original: ka'k ibsimsim</note>, hard biscuits <note>original: biqsmât</note>, or "mercy" cakes <note>original: rahmeh</note>) <note>3</note> on the first Festival of the Dead <note>original: 'Îd el-Amwât</note>. Members of the Greek Orthodox Church may also bring wine on such occasions. The priest blesses the wine and gives everyone a cup. The deceased also receives a portion, as the priest pours a little wine on the tomb, marking a circle with it.

Just as with the Muslims, every Christian who receives an offering asks for God's mercy for the deceased.

An analysis of these customs shows the following main points:

1. Food and other offerings are made in the name of the dead and for the benefit of the dead.
2. These offerings are accompanied by many prayers and good wishes, which are intended for the spiritual benefit of the deceased.

***

<note>1</note> Some Christians give those who come to offer condolences a cup of coffee with a hard biscuit. In some cases, simple sesame cakes <note>original: ka'k ibsimsim</note> are distributed instead of the hard biscuits <note>original: biqsmât</note> mentioned above.
<note>2</note> This custom is common among the Greek Orthodox community.
<note>3</note> "Mercy" <note>original: Rahmeh</note> refers to round cakes made of semolina <note>original: smîd</note>, butter, and sesame seeds.

<summary>This page compares Muslim and Christian funerary folk customs in Palestine, highlighting the shared practice of distributing specific foods and wine at cemeteries to secure prayers and divine mercy for the deceased.</summary>
<keywords>Palestine, funerary customs, Greek Orthodox, Fatiha, shuniyeh, widjih, Festival of the Dead, semolina cakes, folk religion</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Palestinian folk customs regarding death, transitioning from a description of specific Christian rituals to a general theological explanation of why both Muslims and Christians provide charity and prayers for the deceased.</meta>

# Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society 192

When we question the peasants about the purpose of these customs, we find that they reason as follows. When help (food, money, etc.) is given to the poor, strangers, and children in the name of the deceased, and prayers are said for his soul, God will credit all these acts to his favor on the day of judgment. The more a soul can rely upon such acts, the better off it is, since all of them will be added to the *pan of the scales* <note>original: kaffet el-mîzân; the balance used to weigh souls on the Day of Judgment</note> in which his good actions are placed, and the likelihood of outweighing his evil actions will increase. In other words, we see that these actions are sacrifices made by the living for the benefit of the dead. This thought is beautifully expressed in the sentence, "Were it not for the actions of the living, the dead would have perished" <note>original: lôlâ el-ahyâ la-hilkat el-amwât</note>. This is the fundamental idea in all sacrifices to the dead, as we shall see later on.

A close examination of the goal at which these offerings and prayers aim removes every doubt of their sacrificial character. The latter becomes still clearer if we remember the words said every time the <term>fâtiḥah</term> <note>the opening chapter of the Quran</note> and <term>hitmeh</term> <note>a complete recitation of the entire Quran</note> are read, namely: "Accept, O God, its reward for the soul of . . ." <note>original: itqabbal yâ allah ṭawâbhâ ‘an rûḥ fulân</note>. These customs are illustrated by the fact that the Hebrew word for "pray" <note>original Hebrew: עתר; atar</note> is related to the Arabic root *atara* <note>original Arabic: عتر</note>, which means "to sacrifice a sheep."^1 Prayer and sacrifice were so intermingled that every prayer was an offering, and every sacrifice a prayer.

Even the simplest phrases, "May God have mercy on him" <note>original: allah yirḥamuh</note> or "The mercy of God be upon him" <note>original: raḥmet allah ‘alêh</note>, seem to have been originally <term>ad‘iyâ</term> <note>formal supplications or prayers</note> offered for the benefit of the dead, rather than just polite expressions.^2

There is another motive for this custom to which I wish to draw attention, as it has not usually been connected with sacrifices for the dead. The underlying idea of all these sacrifices, including those offered to saints, is the same. Neither saints nor other dead people eat the food themselves, although meals are offered in their names. To their tables many are invited, and when satisfied, they say a prayer for the—

---

^1 See the dictionary *Muḥîṭ el-Muḥîṭ* <note>a 19th-century Arabic dictionary by Butrus al-Bustani</note> and Belot <note>Jean-Baptiste Belot, author of a famous Arabic-French dictionary</note>.
^2 Whenever someone is about to begin a journey, he asks his friends: "Pray for me" <note>original: id‘û lî</note>. They at once recite phrases like "God be with you," "God protect you," "God make you prosper," etc. All these simple expressions are considered as prayers <note>original: du‘â</note>.

<summary>This page explains the theological reasoning behind Palestinian folk customs of charity and prayer for the dead, framing them as sacrifices that tip the scales of divine judgment in the deceased's favor. It also highlights the linguistic link between prayer and sacrifice in Semitic languages.</summary>
<keywords>Day of Judgment, scales of justice, kaffet el-mîzân, Fatiha, Quran recitation, etymology of prayer, Arabic, Hebrew, Taufiq Canaan, folk Islam, funerary sacrifices</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of death rituals, specifically how providing for the living (hospitality) serves as a sacrifice for the soul of the dead. It then transitions to the topic of annual religious festivals (mawâsim) at Palestinian sanctuaries.</meta>

...soul of the dead, who then acts as their true host. In this way, a person is granted even after death the greatest virtue in the eyes of Middle Eastern people: hospitality. <note>original: "the Oriental"; the author uses this term to refer to the local inhabitants of the Levant.</note>

In the Bible, we find parallels to the practice of offering sacrifices for the dead (see Deuteronomy 26:14 and Sirach 30:18). The most significant passage is 2 Maccabees 12:43–45:

> "After taking a collection, he sent twelve thousand silver drachmas to Jerusalem to provide a sin offering for the dead. He did this with a very good and noble thought, keeping the resurrection in mind. For if he had not expected that those who had been killed would rise again, it would have been unnecessary and foolish to pray for the dead."<gloss>1</gloss>

## 7. CELEBRATION OF FEASTS (*mawâsim*)

The word *môsam*<gloss>2</gloss> (plural: *mawâsim*) means "season, marketplace, fair, or a time for pilgrims to gather."<gloss>3</gloss> In our context, it refers to the "season for visiting a sanctuary." For instance, *êmtâ môsam en-nabî Rûbîn* <note>original Arabic: When is the season of the Prophet Reuben?</note> means "When is the time for visiting (or celebrating the feast of) the Prophet Reuben?" Whenever people in Jerusalem and the surrounding villages speak of "the *môsam*," everyone understands it to mean the festival of the Prophet Moses (*môsam en-nabî Mûsâ*).

Many sanctuaries have regular festivals (*mawâsim*). In some places, the feast is celebrated very elaborately and lasts for several days; in others, it is very simple and lasts only one day. During these times, people flock from every direction to visit the holy place and share in the joys of the festival. At these times, they also fulfill their religious vows, perform their spiritual duties, and have their children circumcised.

A description of the feast of the Prophet Moses (*Nebî Mûsâ*)<gloss>4</gloss> provides an excellent example of a *môsam*. All other festivals are similar to it in most respects. The Prophet Moses is highly honored by all Muslims of Palestine. People travel to the feast from every village in southern Palestine and Samaria, and many also come from the north.

---

<gloss>3</gloss> Originally used in reference to Mecca; see *The Encompassing Ocean* <note>original Arabic dictionary: Muhît el-Muhît</note> volume 2, page 2250.

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of sacred sites in Palestine, specifically detailing the architecture and layout of the shrine of the Prophet Moses (Nabi Musa) and its associated cemetery.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>

-> Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society <-

The shrine is located seven kilometers south-southwest of Jericho, just south of the road leading from Jerusalem to Jericho. It consists of an extensive complex of buildings with large gates. The site can be divided into two parts: the sanctuary itself and the rooms surrounding it. These rooms are separated from the sanctuary on three sides by an open courtyard.^1 The sanctuary itself is surrounded on the north and east by porches and covers the supposed tomb of the prophet. Many rags are tied to the bars of the windows <note>A common folk practice where pilgrims tie pieces of clothing to a shrine to leave a part of themselves or their prayers behind.</note>. Above the door, an inscription states that Abdallah Pasha rebuilt the site in 1235 A.H. <term>1235 After Hijra; this corresponds to approximately 1819–1820 AD in the Islamic calendar.</term> The shrine <note>original: *maqâm*</note> itself is opened only on special occasions and during festival days.

The majority of the complex surrounding the shrine consists of rooms used to house visitors. The minaret is located in the northwest corner of this section and is not directly connected to the sanctuary. Two mosques are attached to this complex. One of these, the Women’s Mosque <note>original: *djâmi en-niswân*</note>, has a prayer niche <term>original: *miḥrâb*; a niche in the wall that points toward Mecca.</term> The other mosque has no niche, as it appears to have been converted from a stable into a prayer room.^2 The second story opens almost everywhere onto a terrace that overlooks the courtyard. The lower story contains two large kitchens, as well as stables, storage rooms, and woodsheds, alongside many rooms for pilgrims.

Although this building is constructed on the top of a hill, two other shrines—Hasan the Shepherd <note>original: *Ḥasan er-Râ‘î*</note> and the Mosques of Our Lady Aisha <note>original: *Masâdjid sittnâ ‘Âišeh*</note>—offer better views, as they are located on even higher ground. East of the main building is a cemetery where those who die during festival days are buried. Important figures who die in Jericho are often brought here for burial, and the Adwan tribesmen of Transjordan also bury some of their dead in this cemetery. They consider it a special blessing to be buried near this "man of God."^3

***

^1 The courtyard is paved with stone slabs.
^2 This conversion is still evident, as the door leading outside has been closed, and the fixtures for tying animals are still visible.
^3 In addition to the three shrines mentioned on page 8 where children are buried nearby, I have found two other sanctuaries with the same tradition, making five in total:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Sheikh Ahmad at the Prophet Samuel <note>original: *eš-šêḥ Aḥmad at Nebî Ṣamwîl*</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Sheikh Mansur at Hizmah <note>original: *eš-šêḥ Manṣûr at Ḥizmah*</note>. The three previously mentioned shrines are:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Sheikh Nuran near Shallalah <note>original: *eš-šêḥ Nûrân near Šallâlah*</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Sheikh Ahmad al-Sarrisi at Abu Ghosh <note>original: *eš-šêḥ Aḥmad es-Sarrîsî at Abû Ghôš*</note>, and

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the shrine of the Prophet Moses (Nabi Musa), focusing on the surrounding landscape, the unique local stones, and the schedule of the annual pilgrimage festival.</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>

CANAAN: Mohammedan Saints and Sanctuaries in Palestine

Not a single tree can be seen anywhere near the building, but large cisterns¹ gather rainwater for use during the feast days. These cisterns appear to date from an earlier period than the current building. Some have suggested they were originally the cisterns of the Monastery of Saint Euthymius <note>Saint Euthymius the Great (377–473 AD) was a foundational figure in Judean Desert monasticism.</note>.²

The ridge where the sanctuary is built is composed of a bituminous stone <note>A rock containing hydrocarbons that can be ignited.</note>, which burns easily and releases an unpleasant, asphalt-like smell. Local people believe this natural phenomenon—which is not found at the site of any other saint—is clear proof of the Prophet's greatness. The phrase *Nâruh min hdjâruh*³ <note>original: "his fire comes from his stones"</note> has become a well-known proverb. I have never heard the sayings mentioned by Spoer⁴ regarding this bituminous stone: that it appears white on the outside (representing the Angel of Light⁵) and black on the inside (representing the Angel of Death⁶).

The feast itself begins on the Friday preceding the Greek Orthodox Good Friday⁷ and ends on Maundy Thursday. This Friday is called *djum'et en-nazleh* <note>original: "the Friday of Descent"</note>, while the Friday before it is known as *djum'et el-mnâdât* <note>original: "the Friday of Calling"</note>, since it is on this day that the festival <term>original: *môsam*; a seasonal religious pilgrimage or festival.</term> of the Prophet is officially announced to begin the following week. Thursday evening and the night between Thursday and Friday is named *lêlatu l-waqfeh*⁸ <note>original: "the Night of Standing"</note>; in other words, the night preceding the feast. Everyone who intends to take part in the festival prepares during the preceding days. Friends and families gather to discuss their plans. In some respects, this time resembles the Jewish preparations for the Passover. The night of the—

¹ Because so much depends on the amount of water these cisterns hold during the feast, the people believe they are often filled miraculously. It is said that when the winter rainfall is low and the cisterns are not full, the Prophet Moses always sends a heavy downpour of rain—which falls only over his sacred area—shortly before the feast begins.

The night between the following Wednesday and Thursday is called the "Night of Departure" <note>original: *lêlat eš-šêl*</note>, as this is when most of the pilgrims make arrangements to leave.

Before describing the start of the festival processions, we must point out that six Fridays during this period are considered particularly important, as they are associated with special celebrations and specific privileges. The Nebî Mûsâ festival <term>The annual pilgrimage to the tomb of the Prophet Moses near Jericho.</term> forms the center of these events. The following list <gloss>1</gloss> shows the names as they are known in the areas surrounding Jerusalem. Some of these Fridays are called by different names in other locations:

| Order of Fridays | Compared with the Greek Orthodox Easter | Names of Fridays in Jerusalem and the surrounding areas | Names used by the peasants and Bedouin |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| First | 14 days before Good Friday | Friday of the Proclamation <note>original: *djum‘et el-mnâdât*</note> | Thursday, or Friday of the Greenery <note>original: *hamîs*, or *dj. en-nabât*</note> |
| Second | 8 days before Good Friday | Friday of the Descent, Friday of the Banner, or Friday of the Procession Leader <note>original: *dj. en-nazleh*, *dj. el-bêraq*, or *dj. es-siddârî* (some say *sindârî*)</note><gloss>2</gloss> | Thursday of the Dead, or Friday of the Eggs <note>original: *hamîs el-amwât*, or *dj. el-bêd*</note> |
| Third | Maundy Thursday | Thursday of the Ascent, or Day of Departure <note>original: *hamîs et-ṭal‘ah*, or *yôm eš-šêl*</note> | |
| | Good Friday | Friday of the Standards, or Friday of the Banners <note>original: *dj. el-maghrî*, or *dj. el-‘elêmât*</note> | Friday of the Standards, or Friday of the Animals <note>original: *dj. el-maghrî*, or *dj. el-ḥayawânât*</note> |
| Fourth | 8 days after Good Friday | Friday of Desires, Friday of Sweets,<gloss>3</gloss> or the Long Friday <note>original: *dj. er-raghâyb*, *dj. el-ḥalâwî*, or *ed-dj. et-ṭawîlî*</note> | |
| Fifth | 14 days after Good Friday | Friday of the Strangers <note>original: *dj. el-ghurabâ*</note> | |
| Sixth | 21 days after Good Friday | Friday of the Mourners <note>original: *dj. el-ḥazânâ*</note> | |

<gloss>1</gloss> Slightly adapted from Canaan, *Folklore of the Seasons*, Journal of the Palestine Oriental Society III, p. 23.
<gloss>2</gloss> Some locals use the pronunciation *sindârî*.
<gloss>3</gloss> The Christians of Bethlehem, Beit Jala, and Beit Sahour call December 24 the "Day of Sweets" <note>original: *yôm el-ḥalâwî*</note>. On this day, they flock to Bethlehem to attend the official procession of the Patriarch, taking the opportunity to buy traditional sweets. According to my father's written notes, this custom is slowly disappearing.
